Introduction

Cohomology Theories are one of the most powerful tools in Geometry. In this P.h.D. thesis we will concentrate in particular on one of them: the
L^{2}
-cohomology.

The
L^{2}
-cohomology of a complete Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
is defined as

H^{l}_{2}(M,g):=\frac{ker(d_{l})}{im(d_{l-1})}

(1)

where
d_{l}
is the closure in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
of the exterior derivative defined on compactly supported differential
l
-forms. Moreover one can also define the reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology as

\overline{H}^{l}_{2}(M,g):=\frac{ker(d_{l})}{\overline{im(d_{l-1})}}.

(2)

It’s a well-known fact that if two metrics
g
and
h
on
M
are quasi-isometric, i.e. there are two constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that

C_{1}h\leq g\leq C_{2}h,

(3)

then the (un)-reduced
L^{2}
-cohomologies coincides. In particular if
M
is a compact manifold we have that both, reduced or not,
L^{2}
-cohomologies coincide with the de Rham Cohomology and so they don’t depend on the choice of the metric
g
in
M
.

Differently from the case of de Rham cohomology, the pullback along a differentiable map
f
between Riemannian manifolds, in general, doesn’t induce a morphism between the
L^{2}
-cohomology groups (see Subsection
1.3.4
in Chapter 1).

Given a uniformly proper, lipschitz map between manifolds of bounded geometry we shall define an operator
T_{f}
such that it induces a morphism in (un)-reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology. In particular, we will show that the usual functorial properties hold and that if
f^{*}
does induces a map in (un)-reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology, then

f^{*}[\alpha]=[T_{f}\alpha]

(4)

for each square-integrable differential form.

Let us suppose, moreover, that there is a group
\Gamma
of isometries that acts on the manifolds. Then, if
f
is
\Gamma
-equivariant, also
T_{f}
will be
\Gamma
-equivariant and the same will hold for the morphism in
L^{2}
-cohomology induced by
T_{f}
.

The idea of how to define
T_{f}
comes from the operator
T_{f}
defined by Hilsum and Skandalis in
[
13
]
. The authors use it in order to build a perturbation for the signature operator of the disjoint union of compact manifold which makes it invertible. We can understand our
T_{f}
as a
bounded geometry
version of the operator of Hilsum and Skandalis.

As consequence of our analysis, it follows that (un-reduced )
L^{2}
-cohomology is a lipschitz-homotopy invariant for manifolds of bounded geometry, i.e. if a map
f
is a homotopy equivalence such that

•

f
is lipschitz,

•

there is a homotopy inverse
g
of
f
which is lipschitz and

•

the homotopies between
g\circ f
and
f\circ g
with their respective identities are lipschitz,

then
T_{f}
induces an isomorphism between the (un)-reduced
L^{2}
-cohomologies.

In Chapter 2 we see an application of the existence of
T_{f}
. Our setting is the same as above. We have two Riemannian manifolds
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
of bounded geometry, a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence
f
which preserves the orientation between them and a group
\Gamma
acting by isometries so that
\frac{M}{\Gamma}
and
\frac{N}{\Gamma}
are manifolds of bounded geometry.

Our goal, in this second Chapter, will be to prove that, given the Roe algebras
C^{*}(M)^{\Gamma}
and
C^{*}(N)^{\Gamma}
, then

f_{\star}(Ind_{Roe}(D_{M}))=Ind_{Roe}(D_{N}).

(5)

In words: the Roe Index of the signature operator is a lipschitz-homotopy invariant for manifolds of bounded geometry.

In order to establish (
5
) we check the existence of the Hilsum-Skandalis perturbation for the signature operator in our setting and we will adapt the work of Piazza and Schick
[
17
]
, the work of Wahl
[
23
]
, of Fukumoto
[
5
]
and Zenobi
[
24
]
to the non-compact case.

Finally we will define a class

\rho(M,f)\in K_{n+1}(D^{*}(N)^{\Gamma})

(6)

that only depends on
M
and on the class of lipschitz-homotopy of
f
.

In what follows there is a more detailed description of the Sections of this Thesis.

Maps between manifolds of bounded geometry

In the first Section of the first Chapter, we introduce the setting of this Thesis.

The objects of our study are manifolds of bounded geometry, which are manifolds with strictly positive injectivity radius and some uniform bounds on the covariant derivatives of the curvature. Some examples of manifolds of bounded geometry can be compact manifolds, coverings of manifolds of bounded geometry (so, for example, the euclidean space and the hyperbolic space),
and products of manifolds of bounded geometry. Moreover Greene, in
[
8
]
, proved that every differential manifold admits a metric of bounded geometry. The main results that we will use about manifolds of bounded geometry can be found in
[
4
]
and in
[
20
]
.

On the other hand, the maps that we consider are the
uniformly proper lipschitz
maps between metric space. These are lipschitz maps such that the diameter of the preimage of a subset
A
is controlled by the diameter of
A
itself. We show, in particular, that every lipschitz-homotopy equivalence is an uniformly proper map.

We conclude the first section by introducing a particular family of actions of a group
\Gamma
on a metric space. We call these
uniformly proper, discontinuous and free
actions (or
FUPD
actions). We will see that if the metric space is a manifold of bounded geometry, then an action of a group
\Gamma
is FUPD if and only if the quotient is a manifold of bounded geometry.

C^{k}_{b}
-maps

In this second section of first Chapter we introduce the notion of
C^{k}_{b}
-map between manifolds of bounded geometry. One can also find the definition and the main properties of these maps in
[
4
]
. A
C^{k}_{b}
-map is a map such that, in local normal coordinates, has uniformly bounded derivatives of order
l\leq k
. In particular one can check that
C^{1}_{b}
-maps are lipschitz maps. The most important property, for us, about the
C^{k}_{b}
-map is proved in subsection
1.2.2
: we use a result of Eldering
[
4
]
to prove that every lipschitz map
f
between manifolds of bounded geometry can be approximated by a
C^{\infty}
-map
F
which is a
C^{k}_{b}
-map. Moreover if we have two FUPD actions of a group
\Gamma
on
M
and
N
and
f
is
\Gamma
-equivariant, then also the approximation is
\Gamma
-equivariant. In particular, we have that the morphisms induced between the K-theory groups of the Roe algebra by
f
and
F
are the same. This means that, in the next sections, we consider
f
as a smooth
C^{k}_{b}
-map for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

L^{2}
-cohomology and pull-back

In this third section of the first Chapter we introduce the notions of
L^{2}
-cohomology and reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology. In subsection
1.3.4
we show that the pullback along a map
f
, in general, doesn’t induce a map in (un)-reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology, even if it is a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence. Then, given a map
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
, we define the
Fiber Volume
of
f
as the Radon-Nicodym derivative

Vol_{f}:=\frac{\partial f_{\star}\mu_{M}}{\partial\mu_{N}},

(7)

where
\mu_{M}
and
\mu_{N}
are the measures on
M
and
N
induced by their metrics. We call
R.-N.-lipschitz
map, a lipschitz map
f
which has bounded Fiber Volume. Then we show that the pullback along a R.-N.-lipschitz map induces an
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator. In particular, we also show that R.-N.-lipschitz maps are exactly the
v.b.-maps
defined by Thomas Schick in his Ph.D. Thesis.

We conclude the third section of first Chapter by showing that the Fiber Volume of a submersion can be expressed as the integration along the fibers of some particular differential forms. In particular we choose the name
Fiber Volume
because in the case of a totally geodesics Riemannian submersion we have that
Vol_{f}
in a point
q
in
N
is exactly the volume of its fiber
F_{q}
respect to the metric induced by
g
.

Submersion related to lipschitz maps

This is the fourth section of first Chapter. Consider a uniformly proper lipschitz map
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
, where
(N,h)
is an oriented manifold of bounded geometry and
(M,g)
is an oriented manifold with bounded scalar curvature. Let
\Gamma
be a group which acts by isometries on
M
and
N
and suppose that
f
is
\Gamma
-equivariant. Let us define
T^{\delta}N
as the subset of
TN
given by the vectors with norm less or equal to
\delta:=inj_{N}
.

The main goal of this section is to define a
\Gamma
-equivariant lipschitz submersion

p_{f}:(f^{*}T^{\delta}N,g_{S})\longrightarrow(N,h)

(8)

such that
p_{f}(x,0)=f(x)
.
The metric
g_{S}
on
f^{*}T^{\delta}N
is called
Sasaki metric
and it depends on the metrics
g
and
h
. Moreover we also have that if
f
, in local normal coordinates, has some uniform bounds on the derivatives of order less or equal
j
, then also
p_{f}
has some bounds on the derivatives of the same order. In particular these bounds are important in Chapter 2.

The pull-back functor

The last section of the first Chapter starts by proving that the submersion
p_{f}
has bounded Fiber Volume and so it is a R.-N.-lipschitz map. Then we define the operator
T_{f}:\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
as

T_{f}(\alpha):=\int_{B^{\delta}}p_{f}^{*}\alpha\wedge\omega,

(9)

where
\omega
is a particular Thom form of
f^{*}(TN)
. It follows the proof of the
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-boundedness of
T_{f}
: it will be easily proved since
p_{f}
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map.

In the subsection
1.5.5
we prove two important lemmas: the first one gives a formula which relates
f
and
p_{f}
when
f
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map. The second one gives a formula which relates the submersion
p_{f\circ g}
with the submersions
p_{f}
and
p_{g}
, where
f:(M,m)\longrightarrow(N,h)
and
g:(S,l)\longrightarrow(M,m)
are uniformly proper lipschitz map between manifolds of bounded geometry.

We conclude the section by showing that, for every
z
in
\mathbb{N}

\begin{cases}\mathcal{F}_{z}(M,g)=H^{z}_{2}(M)\\
\mathcal{F}_{z}((M,g)\xrightarrow{f}(N,h))=H^{z}_{2}(N)\xrightarrow{T_{f}}H^{z}_{2}(M)\end{cases}

(10)

is a controvariant functor between the category
\mathcal{C}
of the manifolds of bounded geometry with uniformly proper lipschitz maps and the category
Vec
of the vector spaces. In particular, we will show that if two maps
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are uniformly proper lipschitz maps such that they are homotopic with a lipschitz homotopy
H
, then
T_{f_{1}}=T_{f_{2}}
in
L^{2}
-cohomology. Moreover if
f
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map, then
f^{*}=T_{f}
in
L^{2}
-cohomology. Finally we show that the same holds considering the reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology instead of the
L^{2}
-cohomology.

Coarse geometry

In the first section of the second Chapter, we introduce the notions of coarse structure and coarse map. This definitions and the main properties come from the book
Analytic K-homology
of Higson and Roe
[
10
]
. In particular we use two coarse structures. The first one is a
metric
coarse structure, which is a coarse structure which can be defined on a metric space. It easily follows that uniformly proper lipschitz map are coarse maps respect to metric coarse structures.

The second one is a coarse structure which can be defined on the disjoint union of metric spaces
M
and
N
such that there is a map
f:M\longrightarrow N
. Indeed, in the following sections, we will need a coarse structure on
M\sqcup N
, which is not a metric space.

After that, given a coarse space
X
and given an action of a group
\Gamma
on
X
, we define the Roe (or coarse) algebras
C^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}
and the structure algebra
D^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}
. In the case
X=M\sqcup N
we will denote the algebras by
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
and by
D^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
to recall the dependence on
f
. We have that
C^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}
is an ideal of
D^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}
and so this means that the sequence

0\longrightarrow C^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}\longrightarrow D^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}\longrightarrow\frac{D^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}}{C^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}}\longrightarrow 0

(11)

is exact. Thus we obtain the long exact sequence in K-Theory

...K_{n}(C^{*}(X)^{\Gamma})\longrightarrow K_{n}(D^{*}(X)^{\Gamma})\longrightarrow K_{n}(\frac{D^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}}{C^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}})\longrightarrow K_{n-1}(C^{*}(X)^{\Gamma})\longrightarrow...

(12)

Finally, if
dim(M)=n
, we define the
fundamental class

[D_{M}]\in K_{n+1}(\frac{D^{\star}(M)^{\Gamma}}{C^{\star}(M)^{\Gamma}})
of the signature operator
D_{M}
of a Riemannian manifold and we conclude the section defining the Roe Index of a connected Riemannian manifold as the class

Ind_{Roe}(D_{M}):=\delta[D_{M}]

(13)

in
K_{n}(C^{*}(M)^{\Gamma})
, where
\delta
the connecting homomorphism in K-Theory.

Smoothing operators between manifolds of bounded geometry

This is the second section of second Chapter. Consider
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence with preserves the orientations. Then, in this section we prove that there is an
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded,
\Gamma
-invariant, operator
y
such that

1-T_{f}^{\dagger}T_{f}=dy+yd

(14)

on
dom(d)
where
T_{f}^{\dagger}=\tau_{N}T_{f}^{*}\tau_{M}
and
\tau_{X}
is the so-called
chiral operator
of the Riemannian manifold
X
.

After that we prove that
T_{f}
is a smoothing operator, i.e. an operator
A
such that for each smooth
\alpha

A\alpha(p)=\int_{N}K(p,q)\alpha(q)d\mu_{N},

(15)

where
K
is a smooth section of the fiber bundle
\Lambda^{*}(M)\boxtimes\Lambda(N)
. Then, using the results reported in Appendix
D
, we prove that also
dT_{f}
is a smoothing operator, and, in particular, we can see that if
f
is a
C^{k}_{b}
-map where
k\geq 2
, then
dT_{f}
is also
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded.

We conclude the section proving that if
f
is a
C^{k\geq 2}_{b}
-map, then the operators
T_{f}y
,
yT^{\dagger}_{f}
,
dT_{f}y
and all their adjoints and their algebraic combinations are all operators in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

Lipschitz-homotopy invariance of the Roe Index

In the third section of Chapter 2 we use
T_{f}
and
y
to define the Hilsum-Skandalis perturbation
\mathcal{P}
of
[
13
]
for the signature operator on the manifold of bounded geometry
M\sqcup N
. It follows, since
T_{f}
induces an isomorphism in
L^{2}
-cohomology, that the perturbed signature operator
D_{M\sqcup N}+\mathcal{P}
is
L^{2}
-invertible. Moreover, since
T_{f}
,
T_{f}y
,
dT_{f}y
etc.., are all operators in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
, we are able to prove that also
\mathcal{P}
is in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

Similarly to what we did for Riemannian connected manifolds, we define the
fundamental class

[D_{M\sqcup N}]
and the
Roe Index

Ind_{Roe}(D_{M\sqcup N})
. The using that
\mathcal{P}
is in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
and a result of
[
11
]
, we can find a class
\rho_{f}
in
K_{n+1}(D_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma})
such that its image in the long exact sequence in K-Theory coincides with
[D_{M\sqcup N}]
. This fact implies that

Ind_{Roe}(D_{M\sqcup N})=0,

(16)

and, using some lemmas proved in Appendix
C
, we conclude that

f_{\star}(Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{M}))=Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{N}).

(17)

Lipschitz-homotopy stability of
\rho_{f}
and
\rho(f,M)

In the last section of this Thesis we prove that the class
\rho_{f}
just depend on the class of lipschitz-homotopy of
f
, i.e. if
f
and
f^{\prime}
are two
C^{k}_{b}
lipschitz-homotopy equivalences and there is a lipschitz homotopy
H
between them, then

D^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}=D^{*}_{f^{\prime}}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}\mbox{ and }C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}=C^{*}_{f^{\prime}}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}.

(18)

Moreover we also have that

\rho_{f}=\rho_{f^{\prime}}.

(19)

In order to prove this fact we consider for each
s\in[0,1]
the
f_{s}:=H(\cdot,s)
and we prove that
\mathcal{P}_{s}
, which is the Hilsum-Skandalis perturbation related to
f_{s}
defines a curve in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

We conclude this Thesis defining the projected
\rho
-class in
K_{n+1}(D^{*}(N)^{\Gamma})

\rho(f,M):=f_{\star}[\rho_{f}]

(20)

and showing that it depends only on
M
and on the lipschitz-homotopy class of
f
.

Chapter 1
A pull-back functor for (un)-reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology

1.1
Maps between manifolds of bounded geometry

1.1.1
Lipschitz maps

In this section our goal is to introduce families of maps between metric spaces. The first one is the one of lipschitz maps.
Let
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
be two metric spaces. Let us consider a map
f:(X,d_{X})\longrightarrow(Y,d_{Y})
.

Definition 1.1.1
.

We say that
f
is
lipschitz
if for all
p
and
q
in
X
there is a constant
C_{f}
such that

d_{Y}(f(p),f(q))\leq C_{f}d_{X}(p,q).

(1.1)

Remark 1
.

If
f
is a smooth map between Riemannian manifolds there are also two other equivalent definitions: the first one is that
f
is a
lipschitz
map if there is a constant
C_{f}
such that for all
p
in
M
an for all
v_{p}
in
T_{p}M

||f_{\star,p}(v_{p})||\leq C_{f}||v_{p}||.

(1.2)

The second one is the following:
f
is a
lipschitz
map if there is a constant
C_{f}
such that for every
p
in
M
, fixed some normal coordinates
\{x^{i}\}
centered in
p
and
\{y^{j}\}
centered in
f(p)
, then

|\frac{\partial y^{j}\circ f}{\partial x^{i}}(p)|\leq C_{f}.

(1.3)

The above inequality doesn’t depend on the choice of normal coordinates, indeed we have that

|\frac{\partial y_{2}^{j}}{\partial y_{1}^{i}}(f(p))|=|\frac{\partial x_{2}^{j}}{\partial x_{1}^{i}}(p)|=1.

(1.4)

1.1.2
\Gamma
-Lipschitz-homotopy equivalence

Let us consider now a group
\Gamma
acting on
M
and
N
. We will concentrate on the maps which preserves the action of
\Gamma
.

Definition 1.1.2
.

Two maps
f_{0}
and
f_{1}:(X,d_{X})\longrightarrow(Y,d_{Y})
are
\Gamma
-lipschitz-homotopic
, denoted by

f_{0}\sim_{\Gamma}f_{1},

(1.5)

if they are
\Gamma
-equivariant maps and there is a
\Gamma
-invariant lipschitz homotopy
1
1

1

Here we are considering
d_{X}\times d_{[0,1]}
as the product distance between
d_{X}
and the euclidean distance on
[0,1]
.

H:(X\times[0,1],d_{X}\times d_{[0,1]})\longrightarrow(Y,d_{Y})
such that

H(x,0)=f_{0}(x)\mbox{ and }H(x,1)=f(x)

(1.6)

Definition 1.1.3
.

A map
f:(X,d_{X})\longrightarrow(Y,d_{Y})
is a
\Gamma
-lipschitz-homotopy equivalence
if
f
is
\Gamma
-equivariant, lipschitz and there is a
\Gamma
-equivariant map
g
such that

•

g
is a homotopy inverse of
f
,

•

g
is lipschitz,

•

f\circ g\sim_{\Gamma}id_{Y}
and
g\circ f\sim_{\Gamma}id_{X}
.

1.1.3
Uniformly proper maps

Let us introduce the notion of uniformly proper map.

Definition 1.1.4
.

Consider two metric spaces
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
. Let
f:(X,d_{X})\longrightarrow(Y,d_{Y})
be a map. Then
f
is
uniformly (metrically) proper
, if there is a continuous function
\alpha:[0,+\infty)\longrightarrow[0,+\infty])
such that for every subset
A\subseteq Y
we have

diam(f^{-1}(A))\leq\alpha(diam(A))

(1.7)

Remark 2
.

If
f
is a uniformly proper lipschitz map, then
\alpha
can be chosen such that for all
s,t\geq 0

\alpha(t)<\alpha(t+s).

(1.8)

Indeed if
A\subseteq B
then
f^{-1}(A)\subseteq f^{-1}(B)
. This means that the composition of two uniformly proper maps is still uniformly proper, indeed

\begin{split}diam((g\circ f)^{-1}(A))&=diam(f^{-1}(g^{-1}(A)))\\
&\leq\alpha(diam(g^{-1}(A)))\\
&\leq\alpha(\beta(diam(A)))=(\alpha\circ\beta)(diam(A)).\end{split}

(1.9)

Remark 3
.

Let
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
be two metric spaces and let
\Gamma
be a group acting on
M
and on
N
. Consider two lipschitz maps
F,f:(X,d_{X})\longrightarrow(Y,d_{Y})
such that
f\sim_{\Gamma}F
trought a
\Gamma
-equivariant lipschitz homotopy
h
. If
f
is a uniformly proper map, then also the homotopy
h
is uniformly proper. Then, in particular, also
F
is uniformly proper.

In order prove this fact we have to observe that

d(f(x),h(x,t))=d(h(x,0),h(x,t))\leq C_{h}\cdot t.

(1.10)

Then, if
A
is a subset of
Y
and
h_{t}:X\longrightarrow Y
is defined as
h_{t}(p):=h(p,t)
, we have that

\begin{split}h_{t}^{-1}(A)&=\{p\in X|h(p,t)\in A\}\\
&\subseteq\{p\in X|f(p)\in B_{C_{h}\cdot t}(A)\}\\
&=f^{-1}(B_{C_{h}\cdot t}(A))\end{split}

(1.11)

where
B_{C_{h}\cdot t}(A)
are the points
y
of
Y
such that
d(y,A)\leq C_{h}\cdot t
. Then we have that

\begin{split}h^{-1}(A)&=\bigsqcup\limits_{t\in[0,1]}h_{t}^{-1}(A)\times\{t\}\\
&\subseteq f^{-1}(B_{C_{h}}(A))\times[0,1]\end{split}

(1.12)

and so

diam(h^{-1}(A))\leq diam(f^{-1}(B_{1}(A)))+diam([0,1])\leq\alpha(diam(A)+2{C_{h}})+1.

(1.13)

Lemma 1.1.1
.

Let
f:(X,d_{X})\longrightarrow(Y,d_{Y})
be a
\Gamma
-lipschitz-homotopy equivalence between metric spaces and let us denote by
g
a lipschitz-homotopy inverse of
f
. Moreover we denote by
H
the lipschitz homotopy between
g\circ f
and
id_{X}
. Then
f
is uniformly proper.

Proof.

Since
H
is a lipschitz map we have that if
y
is a point in
Y
, then

\begin{split}d_{X}(x,g\circ f(x))&=d_{X}(H(x,0),H(x,1))\\
&\leq C_{H}d_{X\times[0,1]}((x,0),(x,1))=C_{H}.\end{split}

(1.14)

Now, let
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
be two points in
f^{-1}(A)
, then

\begin{split}d_{X}(x_{1},x_{2})&\leq d_{X}(x_{1},g\circ f(x_{1}))+d_{X}(g\circ f(x_{1}),g\circ f(x_{2}))+d_{X}(x_{1},g\circ f(x_{1}))\\
&\leq 2C_{H}+C_{g}d_{Y}(f(x_{1}),f(x_{2}))\\
&\leq 2C_{H}+C_{g}diam(A).\end{split}

(1.15)

∎

1.1.4
Manifolds of bounded geometry

In this section we will introduce the notion of manifolds of bounded geometry. All the definitions and propositions which follow can be found in
[
4
]
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold.

Definition 1.1.5
.

The Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
has
k
-bounded geometry
if:

•

the sectional curvature
K
of
(M,g)
and its first
k
-covariant derivatives are bounded, i.e. for each
i=0,...,k
there is a constant
V_{i}
such that
\forall x\in M

|\nabla^{i}K(x)|\leq V_{i}.

(1.16)

•

there is a number
C>0
such that for all
p
in
M
the injectivity radius
i_{g}(p)
satisfies

i_{g}(p)\geq C.

(1.17)

The maximal
C
which satisfies this inequality will be denoted by
r_{inj}(M)
.

When we talk about a manifold
M
with bounded geometry, without specifying the
k
, we mean that
M
has
k
-bounded geometry for all
k
in
\mathbb{N}
.

Proposition 1.1.2
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold of
k
-bounded geometry. Then there exists a
\delta>0
such that the metric up to its
k
-th order derivatives and the Christoffel symbols up to its
(k-1)
-th order derivatives are bounded in normal coordinates of radius
\delta

around each
x\in M
, with bounds that are uniform in
x
.

Proposition 1.1.3
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold of
k\geq 1
-bounded geometry.
For every
C>1
there exists a
\delta>0
such that the normal coordinate charts
\phi_{x}
are defined on
B_{\delta}(x)
for each
x\in M
and the Euclidean distance
d_{E}
on the normal
coordinates is uniformly
C
-equivalent to the metric distance
d
induced by
M
, that
is,
\forall x_{1},x_{2}\in B_{\delta}(x)

C^{-1}d(x_{1},x_{2})\leq d_{E}(\phi_{x}(x_{1}),\phi_{x}(x_{2}))\leq Cd(x_{1},x_{2}).

(1.18)

Proposition 1.1.4
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold of
k
-bounded geometry with
k\geq 2
. There
exists a
\delta
with
0<\delta<r_{inj}(M)
and a constant
C>0
such that for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in M
with
d(x_{1},x_{2})<\delta
we have that the coordinate transition map

\phi_{2,1}=exp^{-1}_{x_{2}}\circ exp_{x_{1}}:U\longrightarrow T_{x_{2}}M\text{ with }U=exp_{x_{1}}(B_{\delta}(x_{1})\cap B_{\delta}(x_{2}))\subset T_{x_{1}}M.

(1.19)

is
C^{k-1}
-bounded
2
2

2

it means that all the derivatives of degree less or equal to
k-1
are bounded.
with
|\phi_{2,1}|_{k-1}\leq C
.

Remark 4
.

Consider a Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
such that

Ric(M)\geq(n-1)K

(1.20)

where
K
is a constant. In particular, if
M
has
k_{\geq 0}
-bounded geometry we have that (
1.20
) is satisfied. Then, as consequence of Bishop-Gromov inequality, if
(M,g)
satisfies (
1.20
) for each
p\in M
and for each
r\geq 0
we have that

\mu_{M}(B_{r}(p))\leq C(r)

(1.21)

for some function
C:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. This means that if
A\subset M
has
diam(A)=r
, then, once we fix a
p
in
A
, we have that

\mu_{M}(A)\leq\mu_{M}(B_{diam(A)}(p))\leq C(diam(A)).

(1.22)

1.1.5
Uniformly proper and discontinuous actions

In this section we will introduce a particular family of actions of a group
\Gamma
over a metric space
(X,d_{X})
.

Definition 1.1.6
.

Consider
\Gamma
a group which acts by isometries on a metric space
(X,d_{X})
. We say that the action of
\Gamma
is
uniformly properly discontinuous and free (FUPD)
if

•

the action of
\Gamma
is free and properly discontinuous,

•

there exists a number
\delta>0
such that

d_{X}(p,\gamma p)\leq\delta\implies p=\gamma p.

(1.23)

Proposition 1.1.5
.

Let us suppose that
(M,g)
is a manifold of bounded geometry. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

\Gamma
induce a FUPD action,

2.

the quotient
\frac{M}{\Gamma}
has bounded geometry.

Proof.

1\implies 2

If
\delta
is the constant of the FUPD action of
\Gamma
, then for each
p
in
M
we have that
B_{\delta}(p)
is a trivializing open of
\frac{M}{\Gamma}
and so
inj_{\frac{M}{\Gamma}}\geq\min\{inj_{M},\delta\}
and the curvature of
\frac{M}{\Gamma}
has the same bounds of the curvature of
M
.

2\implies 1

Let us suppose that for each
\delta>0
there is a point
p
and a
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
d_{M}(p,\gamma p)\leq\delta
. Consider
\delta<\min\{inj_{M},inj_{\frac{M}{\Gamma}}\}
. Then there is a vector
v
in
T_{p}M
with norm less then
\delta
such that
exp_{p}(v)=\gamma p
. So, if we denote by
s
the Riemannian covering
s:M\longrightarrow\frac{M}{\Gamma}
, we can apply the formula

s\circ exp_{p}=exp_{s(p)}\circ ds

(1.24)

to
v
. We have that

s(p)=s\circ exp_{p}(v)=exp_{s(p)}\circ ds(v).

(1.25)

Then, since
ds
is an isometry, we have that the norm of
ds(v)
is less or equal to the injectivity radius of
\frac{M}{\Gamma}
. This means that
ds(v)
has to be null, then in particular
v
is null and so

\gamma p=exp_{p}(v)=exp_{p}(0)=p.

(1.26)

∎

1.2
C^{k}_{b}
-maps

1.2.1
Maps with bounded derivatives

Consider
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold of bounded geometry.

Definition 1.2.1
.

We define
\delta>0
to be

M
-small
if the Propositions
1.1.2
,
1.1.3
and
1.1.4
hold on all normal coordinate charts of
radius
\delta
.

Definition 1.2.2
.

Let
X,Y
be Riemannian manifolds of
k+1
-bounded geometry and
f\in C^{k}(X,Y)
. We
say that
f
is of class
C^{k}_{b}
when there exist
X,Y
-small
\delta_{X}
,
\delta_{Y}>0
such that for each
x\in X
we have
f(B_{\delta_{X}}(x))\subset B_{\delta_{Y}}(f(x))
and the composition

F_{x}=exp^{-1}_{f(x)}\circ f\circ exp_{x}:B_{\delta_{X}}(0)\subset T_{x}X\longrightarrow T_{y}Y

(1.27)

in normal coordinates is of class
C^{k}_{b}
and its
C^{l}
-norms (
l=0,...,k
) as function from
T_{x}X
to
T_{y}Y
are bounded uniformly in
x\in X
.

Remark 5
.

There are four remarks we wish to make:

•

The condition of bounded geometry is necessary to define
C^{k}_{b}
-maps. One can see more detail in
[
4
]
.

•

Composition of two
C^{k}_{b}
-maps is a
C^{k}_{b}
-map.

•

Lipschitz maps between manifolds of bounded geometry are
C^{0}_{b}
-maps.
In order to prove this, observe that, since
M
and
N
are manifolds of bounded geometry, then the exponential maps and their inverses are lipschitz maps. In particular their lipschitz constants are uniformly bounded (see Proposition 2.5 of
[
4
]
). Then, in local normal coordinates,

|F_{x}(y)|=|F_{x}(y)-F_{x}(0)|\leq C|y|\leq C\cdot\delta_{X}

(1.28)

where
C
is a constant which uniformly bounds in
x
the lipschitz constants of
F_{x}:=exp^{-1}_{f(x)}\circ f\circ exp_{x}
. This means that the
C^{0}_{b}
-norm of
F_{x}
is uniformly bounded in
x
.

•

we have that if
f
is a
C^{k}_{b}
-map, then, in particular, it is a
C^{k-1}_{bu}
-map.

1.2.2
C^{k}_{b}
-approximation of a lipschitz map

Consider a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
between manifolds of bounded geometry. Let us suppose, moreover, that there is a group
\Gamma
on
M
and
N
acting by isometries FUPD and assume that
f
is
\Gamma
-equivariant. We want to show that for all
k
there is a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence
F:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
which is
C^{k}_{b}
and that is
\Gamma
-lipschitz-homotopic to
f
.

In order to prove this fact we need the following two proposition of
[
4
]
.

Proposition 1.2.1
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold of
k
-bounded geometry for some
k
. Then for
\delta_{2}>0
small enough and any
0<\delta_{1}\leq\delta_{2}
,
M
has a countable cover
\{B_{\delta_{1}}(x_{i})\}
such that

•

\forall i\neq j\implies d(x_{i},x_{j})\geq\delta_{1}

•

There exists an explicit global bound
K
such that for each
x\in M
the ball
B_{\delta_{2}}(x)
intersects at most
K
of the
B_{\delta_{2}}(x_{i})
.

Proposition 1.2.2
.

Let
r,\delta r>0
,
g\in C^{k\geq 0}_{b}(B_{r+2\delta r}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
g
is uniformly continuous and fix
l>k
, and
\epsilon>0
. Consider now a mollifier
3
3

3

A mollifier is a compactly supported, smooth, positive function
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that its integral over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
equals to 1.

\phi
with support in the euclidean ball of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and let us define
\phi_{\nu}(x):=\nu^{-m}\phi(\frac{x}{\nu})
. Then
g
can be approximated by a function
G_{\nu}
such that

•

G_{\nu}=g
outside
B_{r+\delta r}(0)
;

•

G_{\nu}\in C^{l}_{b,u}\cap C^{\infty}
on
B_{r}(0)
and
G_{\nu}\in C^{l}_{b,u}\cap C^{\infty}
on an open
A
if
g\in C^{l}_{b,u}\cap C^{\infty}
on
A
;

•

|g-G_{\nu}|_{k}\leq\epsilon
;

•

|G_{\nu}|_{l}\leq C(\nu,l)|g|_{0}
on
B_{r}(0)
for some
C_{\nu,l}>0
.

Note that
C(\nu,l)
may grow unboundedly as
\nu\longrightarrow 0
or
l\longrightarrow+\infty
.

Remark 6
.

The approximation above is defined in the following way. Let us consider a smooth map
\chi:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow[0,1]
which is
1
if
x\leq r
and
0
if
x\geq r+\delta r
. Then we have that

G_{\nu}(x):=(1-\chi(||x||))g(x)+\chi(||x||)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}g(x-y)\phi_{\nu}(y)dy

(1.29)

where
\nu<\frac{\delta r}{2}
.

moreover
G_{\nu}
can be also written as

\begin{split}G_{\nu}(x)&=(1-\chi(||x||))g_{i,\gamma}(x)+\chi(||x||)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}g_{i,\gamma}(x-y)\phi_{\nu}(y)dy\\
&=(1-\chi(||x||))g_{i,\gamma}(x)+\chi(||x||)\int_{B^{m}}g_{i,\gamma}(x-\nu y)\phi(y)dy.\end{split}

(1.30)

Then, using this expression of
G_{\nu}
, it is also possible to consider
\nu=0
.

Remark 7
.

In particular following the proof of Lemma 2.34 of
[
4
]
,we obtain that if
g
is a lipschitz function of constant
C_{g}
then

d(g(p),G_{\nu}(p))\leq C_{g}\nu.

(1.31)

We are ready to prove the existence of a
C^{k}_{b}
-approximation of a lipschitz map.

Proposition 1.2.3
.

Consider two Riemannian manifolds
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
of bounded geometry and let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a lipschitz map. Fix
\nu>0
. Then there is map
F:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
such that

•

d(F(p),f(p))\leq K\nu
where
K
is a constant which depends on the lipschitz constant
C_{f}
and by the uniform covering we choose to define
F
,

•

for all
l\geq 1
the approximation
F
is a
C^{l}_{b,u}\cap C^{\infty}
-map,

•

Consider
\Gamma
a group that acts FUPD by isometries on
M
and
N
. Assume that
f
is
\Gamma
-equivariant. Then also
F
is
\Gamma
-equivariant.

•

f\sim_{\Gamma}F
.

Proof.

Observe that since
f
is lipschitz, then in particular is uniformly continuous. Then there are two positive numbers
\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2}
both
N
-small and there is a
M
-small number
\delta_{f}
such that
f(B_{\delta_{f}}(q)\subseteq B_{\sigma_{1}}(f(q))
.

Let us consider the Riemannian manifold
X:=\frac{M}{\Gamma}
. Let us define

R_{0}:=\frac{1}{4}min\{\delta_{f},r_{inj}(X)\}.

(1.32)

Following the Remark
1.1.5
, we can see that
R_{0}\leq inj_{M}
and
R_{0}\leq\delta_{0}
where
\delta_{0}
is the constant given by the FUPD action of
\Gamma
. Fix, then,
\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}<R_{0}
where
\delta_{2}
is
X
-small.

So, applying Proposition
1.2.1
, we know that there is a number
K
and a countable cover of
X
given by
\{B_{\delta_{1}}(x_{i})\}
such that for all
x
in
X
we have that
B_{\delta_{2}}(x)
intersect at most
K
of
\{B_{\delta_{2}}(x_{i})\}_{i}
.

Consider the preimage of this cover on
M
. Since
\delta_{2}<\delta_{0}
, it has the form
\{\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}B_{\delta_{2}}(\gamma x_{i})\}_{i}
, where
\gamma x_{i}
is an element of the fiber of
x_{i}
.

Moreover we can observe that
f(B_{\delta_{2}}(q))\subseteq B_{\sigma_{1}}(f(q))
for all
q
in
M
.

Now, let us define
F_{0}:=f
and for all
i
in
\mathbb{N}_{>0}
we have

F_{i+1}(p):=\begin{cases}\exp_{f(\gamma x_{i})}\circ G_{i,\gamma,\nu}\circ exp_{\gamma x_{i}}^{-1}\mbox{ if }p\in B_{R_{0}}(\bigcup_{\gamma}\{\gamma x_{i}\})\\
F_{i}(p)\mbox{ otherwise}\end{cases}

(1.33)

where
G_{i,\gamma,\nu}
is the
C^{k}_{b}
-approximation of
g_{i,\gamma}=\exp_{f(\gamma x_{i})}^{-1}\circ F_{i}\circ exp_{\gamma x_{i}}
using
\nu\leq(\frac{\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{1}}{4K})
. Observe that
F_{i}
is well-defined for all
i
using the gluing lemma.

Finally we can define

F(p):=\lim_{i\rightarrow+\infty}F_{i}(p).

(1.34)

Let us check all the properties of
F
:

•

F
is well-defined: consider
p
a point in
M
. Let
s
be the covering
s:M\longrightarrow X
, then
s(B_{\delta_{2}}(p))
intersect at most
K
ball
B_{\delta_{2}}(x_{i})
. Moreover we have that, since
\delta_{2}<\frac{1}{4}\delta
, then for all of these
x_{i}
there exists only a
\gamma
in
\Gamma
such that
B_{\delta_{2}}(p)
intersects
B_{\delta_{2}}(\gamma x_{i})
.

Then for all
p
there are at most
K
indexes
i_{j}
such that
F_{i_{j}}(p)\neq F_{i_{j}+1}(p)
and so the limit exists since the sequence
\{F_{i}(p)\}_{i\geq i_{K}+1}
is constant.

•

We have that

d(F(p),f(p))\leq C_{f}K\nu.

(1.35)

Indeed, applying the Proposition
1.2.2
and Remark
7
, we have that

d(f(p),F(p))\leq d(f(p),F_{i_{1}+1}(p))+...+d(F_{i_{K}}(p),F_{i_{K}+1}(p))\leq K\cdot C_{f}\nu

(1.36)

•

F
is a
C^{k}_{bu}
-map. Consider a point
p
. As consequence of Proposition
1.2.1
, there is a neighborhood
U
of
p
and there is a finite sequence
x_{i_{1}},...,x_{i_{k}}
of the
x_{i}
s such that
F_{i+1}\neq F_{i}
on
U
. So, we have to study the boundedness of the derivatives of

\begin{split}F_{p}:&=exp^{-1}_{F_{i_{k}+1}(p)}\circ F_{i_{k}+1}\circ exp_{p}\\
&=exp^{-1}_{F_{i_{k}+1}(p)}\circ\exp_{f(\gamma x_{i})}\circ G_{i_{k},\gamma,\nu}\circ exp_{\gamma x_{i}}^{-1}\circ exp_{p}.\end{split}

(1.37)

Now, we have that
exp^{-1}_{F_{i_{k}+1}(p)}\circ\exp_{f(\gamma x_{i})}
and
exp_{\gamma x_{i}}^{-1}\circ exp_{p}
are just a change of normal coordinates, and so they are
C^{k}_{bu}
-maps as consequence of Proposition
1.1.4
. Then it is sufficient to study
G_{i_{k},\gamma,\nu}
. Applying the Proposition
1.2.2
, we have that

|G_{i_{k},\gamma,\nu}|_{k}\leq C(\nu,k)|G_{i_{k-1},\gamma,\nu}|_{0}\leq C(\nu,k)\sigma_{2}.

(1.38)

•

F
is
\Gamma
-equivariant. In order to prove this we have to check that all the
F_{i}
s are
\Gamma
-equivariant. Observe that
F_{0}=f
is
\Gamma
-equivariant. Moreover if
F_{i}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant, then also
F_{i+1}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant. Then we conclude observing that

\gamma F(p)=\gamma F_{i_{k}+1}(p)=F_{i_{k}+1}(\gamma p)=F(\gamma p).

(1.39)

In order to conclude the proof we have to show that
F
and
f
are
\Gamma
-lipschitz-homotopic. Let us define for each
\epsilon\in[0,1]
the map

H_{i,\gamma,\epsilon}:=G_{i,\gamma,\epsilon\nu}

(1.40)

where
G_{i,\gamma,\epsilon\nu}
is the
C^{k}_{bu}
-approximation of
g_{i,\gamma}=\exp_{f(\gamma x_{i})}^{-1}\circ F_{i}\circ exp_{\gamma x_{i}}
using
\epsilon\nu
.

Let us define the map

\begin{split}H_{0}:(M\times[0,1],g+g_{[0,1]})&\longrightarrow(N,h)\\
(p,\epsilon)\longrightarrow f(p)\end{split}

(1.41)

and

H_{i+1}(p,\epsilon):=\begin{cases}\exp_{f(\gamma x_{i})}\circ H_{i,\gamma,\epsilon}\circ exp_{\gamma x_{i}}^{-1}\mbox{ if }p\in B_{r_{inj}(M)}(\bigcup_{\gamma}\{\gamma x_{i}\})\\
H_{i}(p,\epsilon)\mbox{ otherwise}\end{cases}

(1.42)

Then we define the map
H:(M\times[0,1],g+g_{[0,1]})\longrightarrow(N,h)
as

H(p,\epsilon)=\lim_{i\rightarrow+\infty}H_{i}(p,\epsilon).

(1.43)

Let us suppose that if
H_{i}
lipschitz implies
H_{i+1}
is lipschitz with a constant less or equal to
C\cdot C_{i}
where
C_{i}
is the costant of
H_{i}
. Then we will have that the constant of
H
will be
C^{K}C_{f}
.

To prove that if
H_{i-1}
is lipschitz then
H_{i}
is lipschitz it is sufficient to prove that
H_{i}
is lipschitz on
B_{\delta_{2}}(\gamma x_{i})
for all
\gamma
in
\Gamma
. Moreover we also know that the exponential map are lipschtiz with constant
C_{\sigma_{2}}
and
C_{\delta_{2}}
on
B_{\delta_{2}}(\gamma x_{i})
and on
B_{\sigma_{2}}(f(\gamma x_{i}))
. This means that we can study
H_{i,\gamma,\epsilon}
.

Let us consider

d(H_{i,\gamma,\epsilon_{1}}(x_{1}),H_{i,\gamma,\epsilon_{2}}(x_{2}))\leq d(H_{i,\gamma,\epsilon_{1}}(x_{1}),H_{i,\gamma,\epsilon_{1}}(x_{2}))+d(H_{i,\gamma,\epsilon_{1}}(x_{2}),H_{i,\gamma,\epsilon_{2}}(x_{2}))

(1.44)

One have to observe that
g_{i,\gamma}
is a lipschitz map with costant less or equal to
C_{\delta_{2}}\cdot C_{\sigma_{2}}\cdot C_{F_{i}}
.

Then we have

\begin{split}d(H_{i,\gamma,\epsilon_{1}}(x_{1}),H_{i,\gamma,\epsilon_{1}}(x_{2}))&\leq\int_{B^{m}}|g_{i,\gamma}(x_{2}-\epsilon_{1}y)-g_{i,\gamma}(x_{2}-\epsilon_{1}y)|\phi(y)dy\\
&\leq C_{\delta_{2}}\cdot C_{\sigma_{2}}\cdot C_{F_{i}}|x_{1}-x_{2}|\\
&\leq C_{\delta_{2}}^{2}\cdot C_{\sigma_{2}}\cdot C_{F_{i}}d((x_{1},\epsilon_{1}),(x_{2},\epsilon_{1})).\end{split}

(1.45)

and, moreover

\begin{split}d(H_{i,\gamma,\epsilon_{1}}(x_{1}),H_{i,\gamma,\epsilon_{2}}(x_{1}))&\leq\int_{B^{m}}|g_{i,\gamma}(x_{2}-\epsilon_{1}y)-g_{i,\gamma}(x_{2}-\epsilon_{2}y)|\phi(y)dy\\
&\leq C_{\delta_{2}}\cdot C_{\sigma_{2}}\cdot C_{F_{i}}||x_{2}-\epsilon_{1}y-x_{2}+\epsilon_{2}y|\\
&\leq C_{\delta_{2}}\cdot C_{\sigma_{2}}\cdot C_{F_{i}}|\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}|\\
&\leq C_{\delta_{2}}\cdot C_{\sigma_{2}}\cdot C_{F_{i}}d((x_{1},\epsilon_{1}),(x_{2},\epsilon_{2})).\end{split}

(1.46)

Then, we have that
H
is a lipschitz map and so
f
and
F
are lipschitz-homotopic. The
\Gamma
-equivariance of
H
follows exactly as for
F
.
∎

Corollary 1.2.4
.

Consider a lipschitz map
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
between manifolds of bounded geometry. Let us suppose that there is a closed set
C
such that
f_{|_{M\setminus C}}
is a
C^{k}_{bu}
-map. Then for all
\epsilon>0
there is a map
F:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
such that
F
is a
C^{k}_{bu}
-map and if
C_{\epsilon}
is the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
C
, then

F_{|_{M\setminus C_{\epsilon}}}=f_{|_{M\setminus C_{\epsilon}}}.

(1.47)

Moreover if
C
is
\Gamma
-invariant and
f
is
\Gamma
-equivariant then also
F
is
\Gamma
-equivariant and they are
\Gamma
-lipschitz homotopic.

Proof.

The proof is exactly the same of the previous proposition. Indeed it is sufficient to impose

\epsilon=2\delta_{2},

(1.48)

and to define the
F_{i}
s and the
H_{i}
s only on the
\bigsqcup\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}B_{\delta_{2}}(\gamma x_{i})
that intersect
C
.
∎

Lemma 1.2.5
.

Let
f,f^{\prime}:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be two
C^{k}_{b}
lipschitz-homotopy equivalences such that
f\sim_{\Gamma}f^{\prime}
, where
\Gamma
acts FUPD on
M
and
N
. Let us fix some normal coordinates
\{x^{i},s\}
on
M\times[0,1]
and
\{y^{j}\}
on
N
. Then there is a lipschitz homotopy
H:(M\times[0,1],g+g_{eucl})\longrightarrow(N,h)
such that all its derivatives in normal coordinates of order minus or equal of
k
are uniformly bounded.

Proof.

Let us denote by
h
the homotopy between
f
and
f^{\prime}
. Then we can define the lipschitz map
H_{0}:(M\times\mathbb{R},g+g_{0,1})\longrightarrow(N,h)
as

H_{0}(p,s):=\begin{cases}f(p)\mbox{ if }s\leq\frac{1}{4}\\
h(p,\frac{1}{2}(t+\frac{1}{2}))\mbox{ if }s\in[\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4}]\\
f^{\prime}(p)\mbox{ if }s\geq\frac{3}{4}.\end{cases}

(1.49)

Let us consider the action of
\Gamma
on
M\times\mathbb{R}
as the action
\gamma(p,t):=(\gamma(p),t)
. Then we can consider the
\Gamma
-equivariant closed subset given by
M\times[\frac{3}{8},\frac{5}{8}]
. Since
M\times[0,1]
is a manifold of bounded geometry, we can apply the Corollary
1.2.4
, and we obtain a map
\tilde{H}
which is a
C^{k}_{b}
-map such that
\tilde{H}(p,0)=f(p)
and
\tilde{H}(p,1)=f^{\prime}(p)
. Then it is sufficient to define

H:=\tilde{H}_{|_{M\times[0,1]}}.

(1.50)

∎

Corollary 1.2.6
.

Let
H:M\times[0,1]\longrightarrow N
be the homotopy in the Lemma
1.2.5
. If
p_{H}:f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)\times[0,1]\longrightarrow N
is the submersion related to
H
then
p_{H}
has, in normal coordinates, the derivatives of order
k
uniformly bounded.

Proof.

It is a consequence of Proposition
1.4.1
.
∎

1.3
L^{2}
-cohomology and pull-back

1.3.1
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-forms

Let us consider an orientable Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
and let us denote by
\Omega^{k}_{c}(M)
the space of complex differential forms with compact support. The Riemannian metric
g
induces for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
a scalar product on
\Omega^{k}_{c}(M)
as follows: consider
\alpha
and
\beta
in
\Omega^{*}_{c}(M)
then

<\alpha,\beta>_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)}:=\int\limits_{M}\alpha\wedge\star\bar{\beta},

(1.51)

where
\star
is the Hodge star operator given by
g
.

This hermitian product gives a norm on
\Omega^{k}_{c}(M)
:

\begin{split}\|\alpha\|^{2}_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)}:&=<\alpha,\alpha>_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)}\\
&=\int\limits_{M}\alpha\wedge\star\bar{\alpha}<+\infty.\end{split}

(1.52)

Definition 1.3.1
.

We will denote by
\mathcal{L}^{2}\Omega^{k}(M)
the Hilbert space given by the closure of
\Omega^{*}_{c}(M)
respect the norm
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}\Omega^{k}(M)}
. Moreover we can also define the Hilbert space
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
given by

\mathcal{L}^{2}(M):=\bigoplus\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Omega^{k}(M).

(1.53)

The norm of
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
it will be denoted by
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}
or
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)}
.

Remark 8
.

Since
\Omega_{c}^{k}(M)
is dense in
\mathcal{L}^{2}\Omega^{k}(M)
, then
\Omega_{c}^{*}(M)
is dense in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
.

Definition 1.3.2
.

Consider an orientable Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
with
dim(M)=m
. Then, the
chirality operator

\tau_{M}:\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
is the operator defined for each
\alpha
in
\Omega_{c}^{*}(M)
as

\tau_{M}(\alpha):=i^{\frac{m}{2}}\star\alpha

(1.54)

if
m
is even and

\tau_{M}(\alpha):=i^{\frac{m+1}{2}}\star\alpha

(1.55)

if
m
is odd.

Remark 9
.

The chirality operator is a bounded operator. In particular, respect to
||\cdot||_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}
it is an isometry and an involution and so we obtain that

\tau_{M}^{2}=1

(1.56)

and

||\tau_{M}||=1.

(1.57)

1.3.2
Exterior derivative

Let us consider two orientable Riemannian manifolds
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
and let
F
be a linear operator
F:dom(F)\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
.

Definition 1.3.3
.

The operator
F
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded
if
F
is a bounded operator respect to the
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-norms.

Moreover we say that
F
is a
closed
operator if for every
\alpha
in
dom(F)
and for each sequence
\{\alpha_{n}\}
converging to
\alpha
there is a
\beta
in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
such that

\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow+\infty}F(\alpha_{n})=\beta\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)

(1.58)

and, moreover,
F(\alpha)=\beta
.

Remark 10
.

Every bounded operator is closed. In particular we have that
F
is a closed operator if and only if its graph is a closed subset of
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)\times\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
.

Consider now a linear operator
F:\Omega^{*}_{c}(M)\longrightarrow\Omega^{*}_{c}(N)
. Since
\Omega_{c}^{*}(M)
is dense in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
, if
F
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded, then there is a unique extension
\overline{F}:\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
of
F
and it is bounded.

On the other hand, if
F
is unbounded, then there could be some different ways to extend
F
.

Let us denote by
d:\Omega_{c}^{*}(M)\longrightarrow\Omega_{c}^{*}(M)
the operator given by the exterior derivative of compactly supported complex differential forms. We introduce some
closed extensions
i.e. some extensions
\overline{d}
of
d
such that
\overline{d}
is a closed operator.

Definition 1.3.4
.

The
maximal extension of
d
is the operator
d_{max}:dom(d_{max})\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
defined on

\begin{split}dom(d_{max}):=\{&\alpha\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)|\exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)s.t.\forall\beta\in\Omega_{c}^{*}(N)\\
&\langle\alpha^{\prime},d(\beta)\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(\Omega^{*}(M))}=\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)}\}\end{split}

(1.59)

posing

d_{max}(\alpha)=\alpha^{\prime}.

(1.60)

Moreover we also have

Definition 1.3.5
.

The
minimal extension of
d
is the operator
d_{min}:dom(d_{min})\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
defined on

\begin{split}dom(d_{min}):=\{&\alpha\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)|\exists\{\alpha_{n}\}\in\Omega_{c}^{*}(N)\mbox{ and}\exists y\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)s.t.\\
&\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\alpha_{n}=\alpha\mbox{ and}\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow+\infty}d\alpha_{n}=y\}\end{split}

(1.61)

as

d_{min}(\alpha)=y.

(1.62)

Given two operators
F:dom(F)\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
and
G:dom(G)\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
we will say that

F\subseteq G\iff Graph(F)\subseteq Graph(G)

(1.63)

.
We can observe that

d\subseteq d_{min}\subseteq d_{max}.

(1.64)

Moreover every closed extension
\overline{d}
is such that

d_{min}\subseteq\overline{d}\subseteq d_{max}.

(1.65)

However if the Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
is complete then the following proposition of
[
6
]
holds.

Proposition 1.3.1
.

Let
(M,g)
a complete Riemannian manifold. Then the maximal and the minimal domain of the exterior derivative coincide and so

d_{max}=d_{min}.

(1.66)

As consequence of this Proposition we also have that there is a unique closed extension for
d^{*}:\Omega_{c}^{*}(M)\longrightarrow\Omega_{c}^{*}(M)
, the adjoint of
d
and also for the operator
d+d^{*}:\Omega_{c}^{*}(M)\longrightarrow\Omega_{c}^{*}(M)
.

When
(M,g)
is a manifold of bounded geometry, in particular is a complete Riemannian manifold and so, using an abuse of notation, we denote the closed extensions of these operators by
d
,
d^{*}
,
d+d^{*}
.

1.3.3
L^{2}
-cohomology and reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology

Let us consider a closed extension
\overline{d}
of the exterior derivative on an orientable Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
. Let us suppose that
\overline{d}(dom(\overline{d}))\subseteq ker(\overline{d})\subseteq dom(\overline{d})
, and so
\overline{d}\circ\overline{d}
is well defined and equals to zero. In particular, this condition is satisfied for each closed extension
\overline{d}
if
(M,g)
is a complete Riemannian manifold.

Let us consider now the sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(\Omega^{1}(M))\xrightarrow{\overline{d}}\mathcal{L}^{2}(\Omega^{2}(M))\xrightarrow{\overline{d}}\mathcal{L}^{2}(\Omega^{3}(M))\xrightarrow{\overline{d}}...

(1.67)

It is, following the definition given in
[
2
]
, an Hilbert complex.

Definition 1.3.6
.

We will define
i
-th group of
L^{2}
-cohomology
the group

H^{i}_{2,\overline{d}}(M):=\frac{ker(\overline{d}_{i})}{im(\overline{d}_{i-1})}.

(1.68)

Definition 1.3.7
.

We will define
i
-th group of reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology
the group

\overline{H}^{i}_{2,\overline{d}}(M):=\frac{ker(\overline{d}_{i})}{\overline{im(\overline{d}_{i-1})}}.

(1.69)

Remark 11
.

If
(M,g)
is a complete manifold, since
[
6
]
, the (reduced or not)
L^{2}
-cohomology is uniquely defined. In this case we denote the groups as
H^{i}_{2}(M)
and
\overline{H}^{i}_{2}(M)
.

Proposition 1.3.2
.

Let
(N,h)
and
(M,g)
be two orientable Riemannian manifolds. Let
B
and
K:\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
be two
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operators. Let us suppose that
B(\Omega^{*}_{c}(N))\subseteq\Omega^{*}_{c}(M)
and

dB=\pm Bd+K

(1.70)

over
\Omega^{*}_{c}(N)
. Then we have that

B(dom(d_{min}))\subseteq dom(d_{min})

(1.71)

and
d_{min}B=\pm Bd_{min}+K
on the minimal domain of
d
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha
be an element in
dom(d_{min})
. This means that there is a sequence
\{\alpha_{n}\}
in
\Omega^{*}_{c}(N)
such that

\begin{cases}&\alpha=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\alpha_{n}\\
&d\alpha=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow+\infty}d\alpha_{n}.\end{cases}

(1.72)

Using that
B
is continuous we have

B\alpha=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow+\infty}B\alpha_{n}

(1.73)

where
\{B\alpha_{n}\}
is a sequence in
\Omega^{*}_{c}(M)
. Moreover one can see that the limit of
dB\alpha_{n}
exists:

\begin{split}\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow+\infty}dB\alpha_{n}&=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\pm Bd\alpha_{n}+K\alpha_{n}\\
&=\pm B\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow+\infty}d\alpha_{n}+K\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\alpha_{n}\\
&=\pm Bd\alpha+K\alpha.\end{split}

(1.74)

So
d_{min}
is well defined in
B\alpha
and

d_{min}B\alpha=\pm Bd_{min}\alpha+K\alpha.

(1.75)

∎

Remark 12
.

Let us suppose
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
are complete, orientable, Riemannian manifolds. In general an operator
A:dom(A)\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
induces a map in
L^{2}
-cohomology if

•

dom(d_{N})\subseteq dom(A)
,

•

A(dom(d_{N}))\subseteq dom(d_{M})
,

•

A
and
d
commute.

Moreover if we also want that
A
induces a map in reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology, we need that
A
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded on
\overline{im(d)}
. Indeed, given
\alpha
in
dom(d_{N})
, we have that

\begin{split}A(\alpha+\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}d\beta_{k})&=A(\alpha)+A(\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}d\beta_{k})\\
&=A(\alpha)+\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}A(d\beta_{k})\\
&=A(\alpha)+\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}dA(\beta_{k})\end{split}

(1.76)

and so, in reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology,

[A(\alpha+\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}d\beta_{k})]=[A(\alpha)].

(1.77)

Corollary 1.3.3
.

Consider two operators
B
and
K
as in Proposition
1.3.2
. Let us suppose that
K=0
and that

dB=Bd

(1.78)

on
\Omega_{c}^{*}(M)
. Then
B
induces a map in (un)-reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology.
4
4

4

We are considering
\overline{d}=d_{min}
.

1.3.4
Problems with pullback

Let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a map between orientable Riemannian manifolds. In general the pullback
f^{*}
is not an
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator and it doesn’t induce an operator in
L^{2}
-cohomology (reduced or not). In this section we will see some examples.

Example 1.3.1
.

In this first example we will show that the pullback of a map
p:(E,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
between complete Riemannian manifolds is not a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator even if
p
is a proper submersion. Let us consider
(N,h)=(\mathbb{R},g_{eucl})
,
E=(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1},g)
where

g(x,\theta)=dx^{2}+e^{\frac{x^{2}}{2}}d\theta^{2}.

(1.79)

Consider
p:(E,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
given by the projection
(x,y)\longrightarrow x
. One can observe that
p
is a proper submersion, but
p^{*}
is not
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded.

Indeed if
\alpha=e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}dx
in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
, then

\begin{split}\|p^{*}(\alpha)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(E)}&=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\int\limits_{S^{1}}e^{-x^{2}}Vol_{\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}}\\
&=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{S^{1}}e^{-x^{2}}e^{x^{2}}d\theta dx\\
&=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}2\pi e^{-x^{2}}e^{x^{2}}dx=+\infty,\end{split}

(1.80)

and so
p^{*}(\mathcal{L}^{2}(N))
is not in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(E)
.

The pullback is not a bounded operator between the
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-spaces even if
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
are manifolds of bounded geometry and
f
is a smooth lipschitz-homotopy equivalence.

Example 1.3.2
.

Let us consider
\alpha:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined by

\alpha(x)=\begin{cases}\alpha_{0}e^{-\frac{1}{\tan^{2}(x)}}\mbox{ if $x\in[-1,1]$}\\
|x|\mbox{ otherwise}\end{cases}

(1.81)

where

\alpha_{0}:=e^{-\frac{1}{\tan^{2}(1)}}.

(1.82)

Consider the map
\psi:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

\beta(x)=\begin{cases}\alpha(x)\mbox{ if $x\geq 0$}\\
-\alpha(x)\mbox{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

(1.83)

Observe that for each
\epsilon>0
we have that
\beta
is a smooth map outside
C_{\epsilon}:=[-1-\epsilon,-1+\epsilon]\sqcup[1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon]
. Fix
\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Consider
\psi
a smooth approximation of
\beta
which is equal to
\beta
outside
C_{\epsilon}
. We know that such a approximation exists since Corollary
1.2.4
.

We have that
\psi
is also lipschitz-homotopic to
id_{\mathbb{R}}
, indeed the homotopy
h:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

h(x,t):=\psi(x)\cdot t+(1-t)\cdot x

(1.84)

is lipschitz.
We have that
\psi
is smooth, indeed it is clearly smooth if
x\neq 0
and

\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial^{k}x}\psi(0)=0,

(1.85)

for each
k\geq 1
. Moreover
\psi
is lipschitz-homotopic to the identity: the homotopy is
h:\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined as follow

h(x,s)=s\psi(x)+(1-s)x.

(1.86)

Consider now

f(x,y)=\begin{cases}(x,\psi(y-1))\mbox{ if $y\geq 1$}\\
(x,\psi(y+1))\mbox{ if $y\leq-1$}\\
(x,0)\mbox{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

(1.87)

We have that
f
is a uniformly proper and lipschitz map. In particular it is smooth since

\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial^{k}x}\psi(0)=\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial^{k}x}\beta(0)=0.

(1.88)

Finally
f
is lipschitz-homotopic to the identity: indeed the homotopy

H_{1}(x,y,s)=\begin{cases}(x,\psi(y-s))\mbox{ if $y\geq s$}\\
(x,\psi(y+s))\mbox{ if $y\leq-s$}\\
(x,0)\mbox{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

(1.89)

is a lipschitz homotopy between
f
and
(id_{\mathbb{R}},\psi)
and
H_{2}(x,y,s):=(x,h(y,s))
is a lipschitz homotopy between
(id_{\mathbb{R}},\psi)
and
id_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
.

Let us consider the
0
-form given by
\chi_{A}
, where

A:=\{(x,y)\in R^{2}|y\in[-\frac{e^{-x^{2}}}{2},+\frac{e^{-x^{2}}}{2}]\}.

(1.90)

We have that
\chi_{A}
is in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, but
f^{*}(\chi_{A})
is not squared-integrable.

Finally we will show that given a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence
\phi
between manifolds of bounded geometry, then
\phi^{*}(dom(d))\nsubseteq dom(d)
and so
\phi^{*}
doesn’t induce a morphism on
L^{2}
-cohomology (reduced or not).

Example 1.3.3
.

Let us consider
\phi:(\mathbb{R}^{4},g_{eucl})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{4},g_{eucl})
such that
\phi(x,y,z,w)=(\sigma(x),\sigma(y),\sigma(z),w)
where

\sigma(t):=\begin{cases}0\mbox{ if }|t|\leq 1\\
\psi(t-1)\mbox{ if }t\leq-1\\
\psi(t+1)\mbox{ if }t\geq 1.\end{cases}

(1.91)

where
\psi
is the map defined in Example
1.3.2
. Since the derivatives of each order of
\psi
in
0
are null, then
\sigma
is a smooth map. So
\phi
is a smooth map.

Consider a compactly supported
0
-form
\alpha
such that around the origin is
\frac{1}{|(x,y,z,w)|^{k}}
where
\frac{1}{2}<k<2
, then
\alpha
is in
dom(d)
, indeed we know that
\alpha
is in the Sobolev space
W^{1,2}
.

On the other hand
\phi^{*}\alpha
is not an
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-form, indeed we have that on
[-1,1]\times[-1,1]\times[-1,1]\times U
, where
U
is a sufficiently small neighborhood of
0
in
\mathbb{R}
, we have that

\phi^{*}\alpha(x,y,z,w)=\frac{1}{w^{k}}

(1.92)

and so

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}(\phi^{*}\alpha)^{2}d\mu_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}&\geq\int_{[-1,1]^{3}}\int_{U}\frac{1}{w^{2k}}d\mu_{\mathbb{R}}d\mu_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\\
&\geq 2^{3}\cdot\int_{U}\frac{1}{w^{2k}}d\mu_{\mathbb{R}}\\
&=+\infty,\end{split}

(1.93)

since
2k>1
.

Observe that
\phi
is a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence because there is a lipschitz homotopy
H
between
\phi
and the identity. This homotopy is defined as follow

\begin{split}H:(\mathbb{R}^{4}\times[0,1],g_{eucl}\times g_{[0,1]})&\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{4},g_{eucl})\\
(x,y,z,w,t)&\longrightarrow(h(x,t),h(y,t),h(z,t),w).\end{split}

(1.94)

where
h
is defined in the Example
1.3.2
.

1.3.5
Radon-Nicodym derivative and Fiber Volume

In this section we will introduce some tools of Measure Theory, that we will use to define a family of maps whose pullbacks are
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operators.

Definition 1.3.8
.

Let
X
be a set with a
\sigma
-algebra
\Sigma
(i.e. a
measurable set
). Let
\mu
and
\nu
two measures respect
\Sigma
. We will say that
\nu
is
abolutely continuous with respect to

\mu
if and only if for all measurable
A

\mu(A)=0\implies\nu(A)=0.

(1.95)

We will denote it by
\nu<<\mu
.

Definition 1.3.9
.

A measure
\mu
over a measurable set
(X,\Sigma)
is said
\sigma
-finite
if
X
is the countable union of substsets with finite measure.

Remark 13
.

The measure over a Riemannian manifold given by its metric is always
\sigma
-finite. It follows from para-compactness.

Theorem 1.3.4

(Radon-Nyikodym)
.

Let
(X,\Sigma)
be a meausurable set. Given two measures
\mu
and
\nu
such that
\nu<<\mu
and
\mu
is
\sigma
-finite, then there is a non-negative
\mu
-measurable function
f:X\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

\nu(A)=\int_{A}fd\mu.

(1.96)

We will call
f
the
Radon-Nicodym derivative
of
\nu
respect to
\mu
and we will denote it by
\frac{\partial\nu}{\partial\mu}
.

Remark 14
.

If
g
is an integrable function respect to
\nu
, then

\int_{M}gd\nu=\int_{M}gfd\mu.

(1.97)

Remark 15
.

The Radon-Nicodym derivative is unique (up to
0
-measure sets).

Remark 16
.

Let us consider on a measurable space
(X,\Sigma)
and consider three
\sigma
-finite measure
\lambda
,
\mu
and
\sigma
such that
\lambda<<\mu<<\sigma
. Then

\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial\lambda}=\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial{\mu}}\frac{\partial\mu}{\partial\sigma}

(1.98)

Let us consider now a measurable map
f:(M,\Sigma_{M},\nu)\longrightarrow(N,\Sigma_{N},\mu)

Definition 1.3.10
.

The
pushforward measure
is the measure
f_{\star}(\nu)
on
(N,\Sigma_{N})
defined for all
A
in
\Sigma_{N}
as

f_{\star}(\nu)(A):=\nu(f^{-1}(A)).

(1.99)

Remark 17
.

If
g
is an integrable function on
N
, then

\int_{M}f^{*}(g)d\nu=\int_{N}gd(f_{\star}\nu).

(1.100)

Definition 1.3.11
.

Let
(M,\Sigma_{M},\nu)
and
(N,\Sigma_{N},\mu)
be two measured spaces and let
f:(M,\Sigma_{M},\nu)\longrightarrow(N,\Sigma_{N},\mu)
be a function such that
f_{\star}(\nu)
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\nu
.
Let
(N,\mu)
be
\sigma
-finite, then the
Fiber Volume
is the function

Vol_{f,\nu,\mu}:=\frac{\partial f_{\star}\nu}{\partial\mu}.

(1.101)

1.3.6
Radon-Nicodym-Lipschitz maps

Definition 1.3.12
.

A
measured metric space
is a triplet
(M,d_{M},\nu_{M})
where
d_{M}
is a metric space,
M
is a topological space with the topology induced by
d_{M}
and
\nu_{M}
is a measure defined on the
\sigma
-algebra of Borellians of
(M,d_{M})
.

Definition 1.3.13
.

Let
(M,d_{M},\nu_{M})
and
(N,d_{N},\mu_{N})
be two measured and metric spaces. A map
f:(M,\nu)\longrightarrow(N,\mu)
is
Radon-Nikodym-lipschitz
or
R.-N.-lipschitz
if

•

f
is lipschitz

•

f
has bounded Fiber Volume.

Consider
f:(M,d_{M},\nu_{M})\longrightarrow(N,d_{N},\mu_{N})
an R.-N.-lipschitz map and let
C
be the supremum of
\frac{\partial f_{\star}\mu_{M}}{\partial\mu_{N}}
. Then for all measurable set
A\subseteq N
, we have that

\begin{split}\mu_{M}(f^{-1}(A))&=\int_{A}\frac{\partial f_{\star}\mu_{M}}{\partial\mu_{N}}d\mu_{N}\\
&\leq C\int_{A}d\mu_{N}=C\mu_{N}(A).\end{split}

(1.102)

A measurable, lipschitz map which satisfies the above inequality is called a
volume bounded map
or
v.b.-map
: these maps are defined by Thomas Schick in his Ph.D. thesis.

Proposition 1.3.5
.

Let us consider a map
f:(M,d_{M},\nu_{M})\longrightarrow(N,d_{N},\mu_{N})
between measured metric spaces. Then we have that
f
is a R.-N.-map if and only if it is a v.b.-map.

Proof.

If
f
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map, then it follows by
1.102
that it is a v.b.-map.
To prove the other implication we can start observing that the Fiber Volume is well-defined since
\mu_{N}<<\nu_{M}
. Consider
C
the constant such that
\nu_{M}(f^{-1}A)\leq C\cdot\mu_{N}(A)
: then

|\frac{\partial f_{\star}\nu_{M}}{\partial\mu_{N}}|<C.

(1.103)

Indeed if the above inequality is not satisfied, then there is
A\subseteq N
such that
\frac{\mu_{M}(f^{-1}A)}{\mu_{N}(A)}>C
. But this implies

\begin{split}\mu_{M}(f^{-1}(A))&=\int_{A}\frac{\partial f_{\star}\nu_{M}}{\partial\mu_{N}}\\
&\geq C\cdot\int_{A}d\mu_{N}\\
&=C\cdot\mu_{N}(A)\end{split}

(1.104)

and so we have the contradiction.
∎

Let us prove some properties of R.-N.-lipschitz maps.

Proposition 1.3.6
.

Consider
f:(M,d_{M},\mu_{M})\longrightarrow(N,d_{N},\mu_{N})
and
g:(N,d_{N},\mu_{N})\longrightarrow(W,d_{W},\mu_{W})
two R.-N.-lipschitz maps. Then
(g\circ f):(M,d_{M},\mu_{M})\longrightarrow(W,d_{W},\mu_{W})
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map.

Proof.

Since the equivalence of the definitions, we can check that the composition of two v.b.-maps is a v.b.-map. We can start observing that the composition of lipschitz map is lipschitz. Moreover we also have that

\mu_{M}((g\circ f)^{-1}A)\leq C_{f}\mu_{N}(g^{-1}(A))\leq C_{f}\cdot C_{g}\mu_{N}(A)

(1.105)

and so it means that
g\circ f
is a v.b.-map.
∎

Proposition 1.3.7
.

Let us consider two R.-N.-lipschitz maps
f:(M,d_{M},\mu_{M})\longrightarrow(X,d_{X},\mu_{X})
and
g:(N,d_{N},\mu_{N})\longrightarrow(Y,d_{Y},\mu_{Y})
. Then the map

(f,g):(M\times N,d_{M}\times d_{N},\mu_{M}\times\mu_{N})\longrightarrow(X\times Y,d_{X}\times d_{Y},\mu_{X}\times\mu_{Y})

(1.106)

is a R.-N.-lipschitz map.

Proof.

Again we will show that
(f,g)
is a v.b.-map. We can observe that
(f,g)
is lipschitz. Moreover we can also consider a subset
A\times B
of
X\times Y
. Then we have that

\begin{split}\mu_{M\times N}((f,g)^{-1}(A\times B))&=\mu_{M}(f^{-1}(A))\cdot\mu_{N}(g^{-1}(B))\\
&\leq C_{f}\cdot C_{g}\mu_{X}(A)\cdot\mu_{Y}(B)\\
&\leq C_{f}\cdot C_{g}\mu_{X\times Y}(A\times B).\end{split}

(1.107)

Since the sets
\{A\times B\}
are generators of the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
X\times Y
we can conclude.
∎

Remark 18
.

We can also prove the Proposition
1.3.7
checking that the Fiber Volume of
(f,g)
in a point
(p,q)
is given by
Vol_{f}(p)\cdot Vol_{g}(q)
.

Proposition 1.3.8
.

Let
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
be Riemannian manifolds. Let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a R.-N.-lipschitz map. Then
f
induces an
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded pullback
f^{*}:\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
. In particular the norm of
f^{*}
is less or equal to
K_{f}\cdot\sqrt{C_{Vol}}
, where
C_{Vol}
is the maximum of the Fiber Volume.
5
5

5

In order to prove this Proposition we will use some inequalities between the norms of differential forms that can be found in Appendix
A
.

Proof.

Let
\omega
be a smooth form with compact support in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
. Then

\begin{split}||f^{*}\omega||_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)}^{2}&=\int_{M}||f^{*}\omega||^{2}d\mu_{g}\\
&\leq\int_{M}K_{f}^{2}f^{*}(||\omega||^{2})d\mu_{g}\\
&=K_{f}^{2}\int_{N}||\omega||^{2}d(f_{\star}\mu_{g})\\
&=K_{f}^{2}\int_{N}||\omega||^{2}Vol_{f,g,h}d\mu_{h}\\
&\leq K_{f}^{2}C_{Vol}\int_{N}||\omega||^{2}d\mu_{h}\\
&=K_{f}^{2}C_{Vol}||\omega||_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)}.\end{split}

(1.108)

∎

Definition 1.3.14
.

Consider a map
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
between Riemannian manifolds. We say that
f
is a
L^{2}
-map
, if it is a smooth, uniformly proper, R.-N.-lipschitz map.

Proposition 1.3.9
.

Consider a
L^{2}
-map
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
between complete Riemannian manifolds. Then
f^{*}
induces a morphism between the (un)-reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology.

Proof.

It is a consequence of Remark
12
.
∎

1.3.7
Quotients of differential forms

In next sections we will focus on submersions. In particular we will study their Fiber Volumes. In order to do this we need the notion of quotient of differential forms

Definition 1.3.15
.

Let us consider a differentiable manifold
M
. Given two differential forms
\alpha\in\Omega^{k}(M)
,
\beta\in\Omega^{n}(M)
we define
a quotient between
\alpha
and
\beta
, denoted by
\frac{\alpha}{\beta}
as a section of
\Lambda^{k-n}(M)
such that for all
p
in
M

\alpha(p)=\beta(p)\wedge\frac{\alpha}{\beta}(p).

(1.109)

Remark 19
.

There are no condition about the continuity, or smoothness, of
\frac{\alpha}{\beta}
.

In general, given two differential forms, there isn’t a quotient between them: for example we can consider
dx^{1}
and
dx^{2}\wedge dx^{3}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Moreover if there is a quotient between
\alpha
and
\beta
it may not be unique: for example if we consider
\alpha=dx^{1}\wedge dx^{2}
and
\beta=dx^{1}+dx^{2}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
then
\frac{1}{2}(dx^{2}-dx^{1})
and
-dx^{1}
are both quotients.

However there are some useful formulas concerning the quotients.

Proposition 1.3.10
.

Let us consider a differentiable manifold
M
and let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\Omega^{*}(M)
.
Then, if
\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}
,
\frac{\alpha}{\delta}
,
\frac{\beta}{\gamma}
, and
\frac{\beta}{\delta}
are well-defined, then the following formulas hold
6
6

6

in these formulas the
=
have to be read as
”exists and one of the possible quotient is given by”

1.

\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\gamma}=\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\frac{\beta}{\gamma}
,

2.

if
\gamma
as a
g
-form,
\beta
a
b
-form and
\delta
a
d
-form. Then
\frac{\alpha\wedge\beta}{\gamma\wedge\delta}=(-1)^{d(a-g)}\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}\wedge\frac{\beta}{\delta}.

3.

If
\gamma
is closed, and
\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}
is a smooth form, then
\frac{d\alpha}{\gamma}=d(\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}).

4.

if
\alpha
is an
a
-form and
\beta
is a
b
-form then
\frac{\alpha\wedge\beta}{\beta\wedge\gamma}=(-1)^{ab}\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}
.

5.

if
\frac{\frac{\alpha}{\delta}}{\gamma}
exists then
\frac{\alpha}{\delta\wedge\gamma}=\frac{\frac{\alpha}{\delta}}{\gamma}.

6.

if
\frac{\alpha}{\delta\wedge\gamma}
exists, then
\frac{\frac{\alpha}{\delta}}{\gamma}=\frac{\alpha}{\delta\wedge\gamma}.

Proof.

1.

It follows since

\gamma\wedge(\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\frac{\beta}{\gamma})=\gamma\wedge\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\gamma\wedge\frac{\beta}{\gamma}=\alpha+\beta

(1.110)

2.

We have that the degree of
\frac{\beta}{\delta}
is
(b-d)
.

Then we have that

\gamma\wedge\delta\wedge(-1)^{d(a-g)}\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}\wedge\frac{\beta}{\delta}=\gamma\wedge\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}\wedge\delta\wedge\frac{\beta}{\delta}=\alpha\wedge\beta.

(1.111)

3.

Since
\alpha=\gamma\wedge\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}
, then

d\alpha=\gamma\wedge d(\frac{\alpha}{\gamma})+d\gamma\wedge\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}=d(\frac{\alpha}{\gamma})\wedge\gamma.

(1.112)

4.

Observe that

\beta\wedge\gamma\wedge(-1)^{ab}\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}=((-1)^{ab}\beta\wedge\alpha=\alpha\wedge\beta.

(1.113)

5.

We have that

\delta\wedge\gamma\wedge\frac{\frac{\alpha}{\delta}}{\gamma}=\delta\wedge\frac{\alpha}{\delta}=\alpha.

(1.114)

6.

In oreder to prove this, observe that

\frac{\alpha}{\delta}=\gamma\wedge\frac{\alpha}{\delta\wedge\gamma}
, indeed

\delta\wedge(\gamma\wedge\frac{\alpha}{\delta\wedge\gamma})=(\delta\wedge\gamma)\wedge\frac{\alpha}{\delta\wedge\gamma}=\alpha,

(1.115)

and then we have that

\frac{\frac{\alpha}{\delta}}{\gamma}=\frac{\gamma\wedge\frac{\alpha}{\delta\wedge\gamma}}{\gamma}
is a form
F
such that
\gamma\wedge\frac{\alpha}{\delta\wedge\gamma}=\gamma\wedge F
and so, by the definition of quotient we have that

\frac{\frac{\alpha}{\delta}}{\gamma}=F=\frac{\alpha}{\delta\wedge\gamma}.

(1.116)

∎

Consider two oriented differentiable manifolds
X
and
Y
.
As proved in
[
3
]
, in particular in Proposition 16.21.7, when we have a submersion
f:X\longrightarrow Y
, then if
F_{q}
is the fiber of
f
in
q
and
i_{q}:F_{q}\longrightarrow X
is the immersion of the fiber, then
i_{q}^{*}(\frac{\beta}{f^{*}\alpha})
doesn’t depend on the choice of the quotient. Moreover in
[
3
]
it is proved that if
\beta
is a smooth form in
X
, then
i_{q}^{*}(\frac{\beta}{f^{*}\alpha})
is a smooth form on the fiber
F_{q}
.
It means that for all
p
in
Y
we obtain the orientation of
F_{p}
defined imposing

\int_{F_{p}}i_{p}^{*}\frac{Vol_{X}}{f^{*}Vol_{Y}}>0.

(1.117)

We can also observe that if
f:(X,g)\longrightarrow(Y,h)
is a submersion between Riemannian manifolds, then it’s possible to define a
locally smooth
quotient between
Vol_{X}
and
f^{*}Vol_{Y}
. Indeed it is sufficient to consider local fibered coordinates
{y^{j},x^{i}}
on
X
and
\{y^{j}\}
in
Y
and we obtain that locally

\frac{Vol_{X}}{f^{*}Vol_{Y}}=\frac{det(g_{ij}(y,x))}{det(h_{rs})(y)}dx^{1}\wedge...\wedge dx^{n},

(1.118)

where
g_{ij}
and
h_{rs}
are the matrix related to the metrics
g
and
h
. As consequence of this fact we have that the Projection Formula holds also for quotients of volume forms, i.e. given a differential form
\alpha
in
Y
we have that

\int_{F}f^{*}\alpha\wedge\frac{Vol_{X}}{f^{*}Vol_{Y}}=\alpha\wedge\int_{F}\frac{Vol_{X}}{f^{*}Vol_{Y}}.

(1.119)

In order to prove this we have to consider a cover of coordinate charts and to decompose the integral using a partition of unity. Remember that, since
[
3
]
, the integral over the fiber in
p
of a quotient between a form
\alpha
and a form
f^{*}\beta
doesn’t depend on the choice of the quotient. This means that in each chart one can choose a smooth quotient and apply the usual Projection Formula (see Appendix
B
).

Proposition 1.3.11
.

Let
p
be a submersion
p:X\longrightarrow Y
. Consider
\alpha\in\Omega_{vc}^{*}(X)
and
\beta\in\Omega^{*}(X)
. Let us suppose that there is a well-defined
\frac{\int_{F}\alpha}{\beta}
which is locally smooth. Then we have that
7
7

7

again
=
have to be read as
”exists and one of the possible quotient is given by”: this property holds for all possible choice of the quotient.
, unless
\alpha
and
\beta
are top-degree forms: in that case the
=
actually means the equality as differential forms.

\frac{\int_{F}\alpha}{\beta}=\int_{F}\frac{\alpha}{p^{*}\beta}.

(1.120)

Proof.

We have that

\beta\wedge(\int_{F}\frac{\alpha}{p^{*}\beta})=\int_{F}p^{*}\beta\wedge\frac{\alpha}{p^{*}\beta}=\int_{F}\alpha.

(1.121)

∎

1.3.8
Fiber Volume of a submersion

In this section we study the Fiber Volumes of lipschitz submersions between orientable manifolds. We use the notions of
oriented fiber bundle
,
integration along the fibers
and the
Projection Formula
: we introduce them in Appendix
B
.

Proposition 1.3.12
.

Let
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
two orientable, Riemannian manifolds. Let
p:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a proper lipschitz submersion. Then we have that

Vol_{p,g,h}(q)=\int_{F}\frac{Vol_{M}}{p^{*}Vol_{N}}(q)

(1.122)

if
q
is in
p(M)
,
0
otherwise.

Proof.

Let
A
a be measurable set of
N
. Then we have that

\begin{split}f_{\star}\mu_{M}(A)&=\int_{p^{-1}(A)}1d\mu_{M}\\
&=\int_{p^{-1}(A)}Vol_{M}\\
&=\int_{p^{-1}(A)}p^{*}(Vol_{N})\wedge\frac{Vol_{M}}{p^{*}Vol_{N}}\\
&=\int_{p^{-1}(A\cap p(M))}p^{*}(Vol_{N})\wedge\frac{Vol_{M}}{p^{*}Vol_{N}}+\int_{A\cap[p(M)]^{c}}0d\mu_{N}\\
\\
&=\int_{A\cap p(M)}Vol_{N}(\int_{F}\frac{Vol_{M}}{p^{*}Vol_{N}})+\int_{A\cap[p(M)]^{c}}0d\mu_{N}\\
&=\int_{A\cap p(M)}(\int_{F}\frac{Vol_{M}}{p^{*}Vol_{N}})Vol_{N}+\int_{A\cap[p(M)]^{c}}0d\mu_{N}\\
&=\int_{A\cap p(M)}(\int_{F}\frac{Vol_{M}}{p^{*}Vol_{N}})d\mu_{N}+\int_{A\cap[p(M)]^{c}}0d\mu_{N}\end{split}

(1.123)

∎

Remark 20
.

The Fiber Volume doesn’t depend on the choice of
\frac{Vol_{M}}{p^{*}Vol_{N}}
. It is coherent with the uniqueness of the Radon-Nicodym derivative.

Remark 21
.

If the submersion
f:X\rightarrow Y
is in particular a diffeomorphism between oriented Riemannian manifold, then we have that the integration along the fibers of
f
is the pullback operator
(f^{-1})^{*}
with a
+
if
f
preserves the orientation and a
-
otherwise. Indeed the orientation of the fibers is given posing

\int_{f^{-1}(p)}\frac{Vol_{X}}{f^{*}Vol_{X}}>0

(1.124)

and we know that the integration over a
0
-chain is just the evaluation on the oriented points. So

\begin{split}f_{\star}(\alpha)_{p}(v_{1,p},...,v_{k,p})&=\pm\alpha_{f^{-1}(p)}(df_{f^{-1}(p)}^{-1}(v_{1,p}),...,df_{f^{-1}(p)}^{-1}(v_{k,p}))\\
&=\pm(f^{-1})^{*}\alpha(v_{1,p},...,v_{k,p}),\end{split}

(1.125)

where we have a
+
if
f
preserves the orientation and
-
otherwise. Then, this means that

f_{\star}=\pm(f^{-1})^{*}.

(1.126)

and so, in particular, the Fiber Volume of
f
is given by

\pm(f^{-1})^{*}\frac{Vol_{X}}{f^{*}(Vol_{Y})}=|(f^{-1})^{*}\frac{Vol_{X}}{f^{*}(Vol_{Y})}|.

(1.127)

Proposition 1.3.13
.

Given a fiber bundle
p:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
where
M
and
N
are orientable, we have that if
p^{*}
induces a map between the
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-space, then the same is true for
p_{\star}
, the operator of integration along the fibers of
p
. Moreover, if we denote by
(p_{\star})^{*}
the adjoint of
p_{\star}
, we have that, if
\tau_{X}
and
\tau_{Y}
are the chiral operators of
X
and
Y

(p_{\star})^{*}=\tau_{X}\circ p^{*}\circ\tau_{Y}.

(1.128)

Proof.

Let
\alpha
be in
\Omega_{vc}^{*}(X)
and let
\beta
be in
\Omega^{*}(Y)
, if
n=dim(Y)
we have that

\langle p_{\star}\alpha,\beta\rangle_{Y}=\int\limits_{Y}[\int\limits_{F}\alpha]\wedge\star_{Y}\overline{\beta}

(1.129)

Now, applying the Projection Formula, we have that

\begin{split}\langle p_{\star}\alpha,\beta\rangle_{Y}&=\int\limits_{X}\alpha\wedge p^{*}(\overline{\star_{Y}\beta})\\
&=i^{-|\beta|(n-|\beta|)}\int\limits_{X}\alpha\wedge(\overline{p^{*}\tau_{Y}\beta})\\
&=i^{-|\beta|(n-|\beta|)}i^{|\beta|(n-|\beta|)}\int\limits_{X}\alpha\wedge\star_{X}(\overline{\tau_{M}p^{*}\tau_{Y}\beta})\\
&=\langle\alpha,\tau_{X}\circ p^{*}\circ\tau_{Y}(\beta)\rangle_{X}.\end{split}

(1.130)

∎

Remark 22
.

The norm of
p_{\star}
as operator between
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-spacesis the same of the norm of
p^{*}
.

Corollary 1.3.14
.

Consider
p:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
a R.-N.-lipschitz submersion. Then the operator
p_{\star}
is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator.

We conclude this section giving a formula which allows to compute the Fiber Volume of the composition of two submersions.

Proposition 1.3.15
.

Let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
and
g:(N,h)\longrightarrow(W,l)
be two submersions between oriented Riemannian manifolds. Then we have that

Vol_{g\circ f,\mu_{M},\mu_{W}}(q)=\int_{g^{-1}(q)}(\int_{f^{-1}g^{-1}(q)}\frac{Vol_{M}}{f^{*}(Vol_{N})})\frac{Vol_{N}}{g^{*}Vol_{N}}

(1.131)

Proof.

We can observe that, as quotients, we have that

\frac{Vol_{M}}{(g\circ f)^{*}Vol_{W}}=\frac{Vol_{M}}{f^{*}Vol_{N}}\wedge\frac{f^{*}Vol_{N}}{(g\circ f)^{*}Vol_{W}}

(1.132)

and, in particular one can observe that we can choose as quotient

\frac{f^{*}Vol_{N}}{(g\circ f)^{*}Vol_{W}}=f^{*}(\frac{Vol_{N}}{g^{*}Vol_{W}}),

(1.133)

indeed

(g\circ f)^{*}Vol_{W}\wedge f^{*}(\frac{Vol_{N}}{g^{*}Vol_{W}})=f^{*}(g^{*}Vol_{W}\wedge\frac{Vol_{N}}{g^{*}Vol_{W}})=f^{*}Vol_{N}.

(1.134)

Then we can conclude by applying the Projection Formula. Indeed

\begin{split}Vol_{g\circ f,\mu_{M},\mu_{W}}(q)&=\int_{(g\circ f)^{-1}(q)}\frac{Vol_{M}}{(g\circ f)^{*}Vol_{W}}\\
&=\int_{(g\circ f)^{-1}(q)}\frac{Vol_{M}}{f^{*}Vol_{N}}\wedge f^{*}(\frac{Vol_{N}}{g^{*}Vol_{W}})\\
&=\int_{g^{-1}(q)}(\int_{f^{-1}g^{-1}(q)}\frac{Vol_{M}}{f^{*}(Vol_{N})})\frac{Vol_{N}}{g^{*}Vol_{N}}.\end{split}

(1.135)

∎

1.4
Submersion related to lipschitz maps

1.4.1
The Sasaki metric

Let us consider a Riemannian manifold
(N,h)
of dimension
n
,
\pi_{E}:E\longrightarrow N
a vector bundle of rank
m
endowed with a bundle metric
H_{E}\in\Gamma(E^{*}\otimes E^{*})
and a linear connection
\nabla_{E}
which preserves
H_{E}
. Fix
\{s_{j}\}
a local frame of
E
: we have that if
\{x^{i}\}
is a system of local coordinate over
U\subseteq N
, then we can define the system of coordinates
\{x^{i},\mu^{j}\}
on
\pi_{E}^{-1}(U)
, where the
\mu^{j}
are the components respect to
\{s_{j}\}
.

Then we can denote by
K
the map
K:TE\longrightarrow E
defined as

K(b^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}|_{(x_{0},\mu_{0})}+z^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{j}}|_{(x_{0},\mu_{0})}):=(z^{l}+b^{i}z^{j}\Gamma_{ij}^{l}(x_{0}))s_{l}(x_{0}),

(1.136)

where the
\Gamma_{ij}^{l}
are the Christoffel symbols of
\nabla_{E}
.

Definition 1.4.1
.

The
Sasaki metric
on
E
is the Riemannian metric
h^{E}
defined for all
A,B
in
T_{(p,v_{p})}E
as

h^{E}(A,B):=h(d\pi_{E,v_{p}}(A),d\pi_{E,v_{p}}(B))+H_{E}(K(A),K(B)).

(1.137)

Remark 23
.

Let us consider the system of coordinate
\{x^{i}\}
on
N
and
\{x^{i},\mu^{j}\}
on
E
. We have that the components of
h^{E}
are given by

\begin{cases}h^{E}_{ij}(x,\mu)=h_{ij}(x)+H_{\alpha\gamma}(x)\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\beta i}(x)\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\eta j}(x)\mu^{\beta}\mu^{\eta}\\
h^{E}_{i\sigma}(x,\mu)=H_{\sigma\alpha}(x)\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\beta i}(x)\mu^{\beta}\\
h^{E}_{\sigma\tau}(x,\mu)=H_{\sigma,\tau}(x),\end{cases}

(1.138)

where
i,j=1,...,n
and
\sigma,\tau=n+1,...,n+m
. Consider a point
x_{0}=(x^{1}_{0},...,x^{n}_{0})
in
M
. We have that all the Christoffel symbols are in
x_{0}
are zero, then, in local coordinates the matrix of
h^{E}
in a point
(x_{0},\mu)
is given by

\begin{bmatrix}h_{i,j}(x)&&0\\
0&&H_{\sigma,\tau}(x)\end{bmatrix}.

(1.139)

Consider a fiber bundle
E
over a Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
. Fix a bundle metric
H
and a connection
\nabla
on
E
. Then the projection
p:(E,g_{S})\longrightarrow(M,g)
, where
g_{S}
is a Sasaki metric related to
g
,
H
and
\nabla
is a Riemannian submersion.

Example 1.4.1
.

Let us consider a Riemannian manifold
(N,h)
. We can consider as
E
the tangent bundle
TM
and as
h_{E}
the metric
g
itself. In this case the connection
\nabla_{E}
is the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla_{g}^{LC}
.

1.4.2
Pull-back bundle and pull-back connection

Let us consider a smooth map
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
between Riemannian manifolds.

Definition 1.4.2
.

The
pullback bundle

f^{*}E
is a bundle over
(M,g)
given by

f^{*}(E):=\{(p,v)\in M\times E|\pi_{E}(t)=f(p)\}.

(1.140)

Remark 24
.

Given a smooth map
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
, then there is a bundle map
F:f^{*}E\longrightarrow E
induced by
f
. This map is defined as

F(p,v)=(f(p),v).

(1.141)

Using this map it’s possible to define a metric bundle on
f^{*}(E)
as follows

F^{*}(H)(A_{p},B_{p}):=H(F(A_{p}),F(B_{p}))

(1.142)

Given a section
\sigma
of
E
, one can define the section

f^{*}\sigma:=f^{*}\sigma(p)=\sigma\circ f(p).

(1.143)

A consequence of the existence of the pullback of a section of
E
is that it is possible to
pullback
also a connection
\nabla_{E}
.

Definition 1.4.3
.

The
pullback connection

f^{*}\nabla_{E}
on
f^{*}(E)
is uniquely defined imposing that

(f^{*}\nabla_{E})f^{*}\sigma=f^{*}(\nabla_{E}\sigma).

(1.144)

Remark 25
.

The condition (
1.144
) is sufficient to uniquely define a connection on
f^{*}(E)
. An equivalent condition is to impose that the local Christoffel symbols
\tilde{\Gamma}^{\alpha}_{\beta,i}
are given by

\tilde{\Gamma}^{\alpha}_{\beta,i}:=\frac{\partial f^{l}}{\partial x^{i}}f^{*}(\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\beta,l}).

(1.145)

Remark 26
.

Let us consider a map
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
and consider, in particular, the bundle
\pi:f^{*}(TN)\longrightarrow M
.
Fix on
f^{*}(TN)
the bundle metric
f^{*}h
and
f^{*}\nabla^{LC}_{h}
. Consider the Sasaki metric on
f^{*}(TN)
induced by
g
,
f^{*}h
and
f^{*}\nabla^{LC}_{h}
. Fix some normal coordinates
x^{i}
around a point
p
on
M
and fix a frame
E_{j}
of
f^{*}(TN)
around
f(p)
. Then we can consider the fibered coordinates
\{x^{i},y^{j}\}
of
f^{*}(TN)
. Let us observe that in
(p,w_{f(p)})
we have the orthogonal decomposition

\begin{split}T_{(p,w_{f(p)})}f^{*}(TN)&=Span\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}|_{(p,w_{f(p)})}\}\oplus ker(d\pi_{(p,w_{f(p)})})\\
&=Span\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}|_{(p,w_{f(p)})}\}\oplus Span\{\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}|_{(p,w_{f}(p))}\}\end{split}

(1.146)

and, moreover, we have that
Span\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}|_{(p,w_{f(p)})}\}
is isometric to
T_{p}M
, while

Span\{\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}|_{(p,w_{f}(p))}\}
is isometric to
T_{f(p)}N
. This fact follows by (
1.138
) and (
1.145
) and observing that the Christoffel symbols of
\nabla^{LC}_{N}
respect to normal coordinates around
f(p)
are null in
f(p)
.

This fact, in particular, implies that the projection
\pi:f^{*}TN\longrightarrow M
is a Riemannian submersion.

Remark 27
.

Consider
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
two manifolds of bounded geometry and let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a
C^{k}_{bu}
-map for each
k
in
\mathbb{N}
. Fix on
f^{*}(TN)
the Sasaki metric
g_{S}
induced by
g
,
f^{*}h
and
f^{*}\nabla^{LC}_{g}
. Then, if we denote by
\nabla:=\nabla^{LC}_{g_{S}}
and by
R
the Riemann tensor on
f^{*}(TN)
, we have that for each
i
in
\mathbb{N}
there is a continuous function
C_{i}:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

|\nabla^{i}R(v_{p})|\leq C_{i}(||v_{p}||).

(1.147)

1.4.3
The map
p_{f}

In this subsection we will define a submersion
p_{f}:f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)\longrightarrow N
where

f^{*}(T^{\delta}N):=\{(p,w_{f(p)})\in f^{*}(TN)||w_{f(p)}|\leq\delta\}.

(1.148)

Lemma 1.4.1
.

Let us consider
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
a smooth lipschitz map between two oriented Riemannian manifolds. Suppose that
(N,h)
has bounded geometry.

Let us denote by
F:f^{*}TN\longrightarrow TN
the bundle morphism induced by
f
between
f^{*}(TN)
and
TN
. Fix on
f^{*}TN
the Sasaki metric
g_{s}
defined using
f^{*}\nabla^{L}C_{h}
,
f^{*}h
and
g
. Then there is a map
p_{f}:(f^{*}(T^{\delta}N),g_{s})\longrightarrow(N,h)
such that:

1.

p_{f}
is a submersion,

2.
