{p}_{f}(x,0)=f(x)
,

3.

{p}_{f}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant,

4.

{p}_{f}={p}_{id_{N}}\circ F
,

5.

Fix some local normal coordinates
\{x^{i}\}
around a point
p
in
M
and some local normal coordinates
\{y^{j}\}
around
f(p)
on
N
. Let us suppose that for each
k
in
\mathbb{N}

\sup\limits_{s=0,...,k}|\frac{\partial^{s}y^{j}\circ f}{\partial x^{i}_{1}...\partial x^{i_{s}}}(x)|\leq L_{k}

(1.149)

for some
L_{k}
. Consider the frame
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}\}
around
f(0)
and define the fibered coordinates
\{x^{i},\mu^{j}\}
related to
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}\}
on
f^{*}T^{\delta}N
. Then for each
k
in
\mathbb{N}
there is a constant
C_{k}
such that

\sup\limits_{s+t=0,...,k}|\frac{\partial^{s}y^{j}\circ p_{f}}{\partial x^{i}_{1}...\partial x^{i_{s}}\partial\mu^{j_{1}}...\partial\mu^{j_{t}}}(x,\mu)|\leq C_{k}

(1.150)

where
C_{k}
doesn’t depend on
x
or
j
.

Proof.

We can define

\begin{split}p_{f}:(f^{*}(T^{\delta}N),g_{s})&\longrightarrow(N,h)\\
(p,w_{f(p)})&\longrightarrow exp_{f(p)}(w_{f(p)}).\end{split}

(1.151)

Then we have that

1.

p_{f}
is a submersion, indeed for each fixed
p
in
M
we have that
{p}_{f}(p,\cdot):f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)_{p}=T^{\delta}_{f(p)}N\longrightarrow N
is the exponential map in
f(p)
. We know that the exponential map is a local diffeomorphism and so
\tilde{p}_{f}
is a submersion,

2.

p_{f}(p,0_{f(p)})=f(p)
: this follows immediately by the definition of exponential map,

3.

p_{f}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant, indeed, since
\Gamma
acts by isometries,

\begin{split}p_{f}(\gamma p,d\gamma w_{f(p)})&=exp_{f(\gamma p)}d\gamma w_{f(p)}\\
&=exp_{\gamma f(p)}d\gamma w_{f(p)}\\
&=\gamma exp_{f(p)}w_{f(p)}=\gamma{p}_{f}(p,w_{f(p)}).\end{split}

(1.152)

4.

It’s obvious: we have that
F(p,w_{f(p)})=w_{f(p)}
,

5.

Because of the last point it is sufficient to show the assertion just for
p_{id}
.

Consider
V\subseteq N
a normal coordinate chart. Let
\{x^{i}\}
be the coordinates system on
V
. Consider
\pi:TN\longrightarrow N
and let
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\}
be an orthonormal frame on
V
. Then we have the fibered coordinate system
\{x^{i},\mu^{j}\}
on
\pi^{-1}(V)
. Let us study now
p_{id}
restricted to
\pi^{-1}(V)
. We have that it can be seen as
\pi\circ\phi(x,\mu)
where
\pi(x,\mu)=x
and
\phi
is the flow of the system of differential equations given by

\begin{cases}\dot{x}^{k}=c^{k}\\
\dot{c}^{k}=-\Gamma^{k}_{ij}(x)x^{i}x^{j}\end{cases}

(1.153)

Then, applying the Lemma 3.4 of
[
20
]
we have that the partial derivatives of
\phi
are uniformly bounded. Then we conclude that the derivatives of
p_{id}
are uniformly bounded.

∎

Corollary 1.4.2
.

We have that
\tilde{p}_{f}
is a lipschitz map.

Proof.

It’s a direct computation. Observe that
||\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}||
,
||\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}||
and
||\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{l}}||
are uniformly bounded. Moreover we also have that

dp_{f}(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}})=\frac{\partial p_{f}^{j}}{\partial x^{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}

(1.154)

and

dp_{f}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{l}})=\frac{\partial p_{f}^{j}}{\partial\mu^{l}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}.

(1.155)

∎

1.5
The pull-back functor

1.5.1
The Fiber Volume of
p_{f}

Lemma 1.5.1
.

Consider
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
a smooth lipschitz map between Riemannian manifolds of bounded geometry. Then the map
{t}_{f}:f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)\longrightarrow M\times N
defined as

{t}_{f}(p,w_{f(p)})=(p,p_{f}(p,w_{f(p)}))

(1.156)

is an R.-N.-lipschitz diffeomorphism with its image.

Proof.

We start proving that
{t}_{f}
is a diffeomorphism with its image. Observe that

dim(f^{*}(T^{\delta}N))=m+n=dim(M)+dim(N)=dim(M\times N).

(1.157)

Fix some normal coordinates
\{x^{i}\}
around a point
p
in
M
and let
\{y^{j}\}
be some normal coordinates around
f(p)
in
N
. Consider the frame
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}\}
and define the fibered coordinates
\{x^{i},\mu^{j}\}
related to
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}\}
on
f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)
. On
M\times N
we consider the normal coordinates
\{x^{i},y^{j}\}
. Then the Jacobian of
\tilde{t}
is given by

Jt_{f}(x,\mu)=\begin{bmatrix}1&&\star\\
0&&Jexp_{x}(\mu)\end{bmatrix}

(1.158)

Then, since the exponential map is a diffeomorphism for each
x_{0}
, we have that
Jt_{f}
is invertible. Moreover
{t}_{f}
is also injective, indeed if
(p,w_{f(p)})
and
(q,v_{f(q)})
have the same image, then
p=q
and

exp_{f(p)}w_{f(p)}=exp_{f(p)}v_{p}\implies w_{p}=v_{p},

(1.159)

since their norm is less than
\delta
and
\delta\leq inj_{N}
. We proved that
{t}_{f}
is a diffeomorphism with its image.

We know that
{t}_{f}
is a lipschitz map because
p_{f}
is a lipschitz map.

So, in order to prove that
{t}_{f}
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map, we have to show that it has bounded Fiber Volume.

Consider a point
(p,q)
in
M\times N
. Then its fiber is empty or it is a singleton
\{(p,w_{f(p)})\}
. This means, following the Remark
21
that the Fiber Volume of
{t}_{f}
is given by

|{t_{f}}^{-1^{*}}\frac{Vol_{T^{\delta}N}}{{t_{f}}^{*}Vol_{M\times N}}|

(1.160)

on the image of
t_{f}
and it null otherwise.

Then if
\frac{Vol_{T^{\delta}N}}{{t}^{*}Vol_{M\times N}}
is a bounded function, which is a-priori not clear, then we can conclude that
{t}_{f}
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map.

Consider the fibered coordinates
\{x^{i},\mu^{j}\}
on
f^{*}(TN)
and the coordinates
\{x^{i},y^{j}\}
on
M\times N
. Since the definition of exponential map we have that the image of
{t}_{f}
is contained in a
\delta
-neighborhood of the
Graph(f)\in M\times N
. This means that we can cover all the image of
{t}_{f}
using the normal coordinates
\{x^{i},y^{j}\}
around
(p,f(p))
. Out of the image of
{t}_{f}
we already know since Proposition
1.3.12
that the Fiber Volume is null.

Observe that, in this coordinates, we have

{t}_{f}(0,\mu^{j})=(0,\mu^{j}).

(1.161)

Consider

Vol_{f^{*}T^{\delta}N}(x,\mu)=\sqrt{det(G_{ij})}(x,\mu)dx^{1}\wedge...\wedge d\mu^{n}

(1.162)

and

Vol_{M\times N}(x,y)=\sqrt{det(H_{ij})}(x,y)dx^{1}\wedge...\wedge dy^{n},

(1.163)

where
G_{ij}
is the matrix of
g_{S}
on
f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)
with respect to
\{x^{i},\mu^{j}\}
and
H_{ij}
is the matrix of the metric on
M\times N
. Then we have that

\frac{Vol_{T^{\delta}N}}{t^{*}Vol_{M\times N}}(x,\mu)=\frac{\sqrt{det(G_{ij})}}{t_{f}^{*}(\sqrt{det(H_{ij})})}(x,\mu)\cdot\frac{1}{det(Jexp_{f(x)}(\mu))}.

(1.164)

Observe that in
(0,\mu)
we have that
Jexp_{0}(\mu)
is the identity matrix. Moreover we also have that

G_{ij}(0,\mu)=\begin{bmatrix}1&&0\\
0&&1\end{bmatrix}

(1.165)

and so
\sqrt{det(G_{ij})}(0,y)=1
. Finally we have that

H_{ij}(0,\mu)=\begin{bmatrix}1&&0\\
0&&h_{ij}(0,\mu)\end{bmatrix}

(1.166)

where
h_{ij}
is the matrix related to the Riemannian metric
h
in normal coordinates. Then we have that

det(H_{ij})^{-1}(0,y)=det(h_{ij})^{-1}(0,y)\leq C

(1.167)

because
N
is a manifold of bounded geometry
[
20
]
. This means that

\frac{Vol_{T^{\delta}N}}{t^{*}Vol_{M\times N}}(0,\mu)=\sqrt{\frac{det(G_{ij})}{t_{f}^{*}det(H_{ij})}}(0,\mu)\leq C

(1.168)

and so the Fiber Volume of
t_{f}
is bounded.
∎

Corollary 1.5.2
.

Let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a smooth uniformly proper lipschitz map between Riemannian manifolds of bounded geometry. Then
p_{f}
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map and
p_{f}^{*}
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded.

Proof.

We already know, since Lemma
1.5.1
, that
t_{f}
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map. Observe that
p_{f}=pr_{N}\circ t_{f}
.

Consider
\overline{f}:f^{*}T^{\delta}N\longrightarrow N
defined as

\overline{f}(w_{f(p)}):=f(p).

(1.169)

We have that
p_{f}\sim_{\Gamma}\overline{f}
. Then, using Remark
3
, in particular (
1.11
), we have that there is a
C>0
such that

p_{f}^{-1}(q)\subset A_{q}:=\overline{f}^{-1}(B_{C}(q))=\pi^{-1}f^{-1}(B_{C}(q)),

(1.170)

where
\pi:f^{*}T^{\delta}N\longrightarrow M
is the projection of the bundle.

This means that if we fix a
q
in
N
, then the Fiber Volume of
t_{f}
in a point
(p,q)
can be different from zero only if
p\in f^{-1}(B_{C}(q))
.

Then, using the Proposition
1.3.15
, we have that the Fiber Volume of
p_{f}
in a point
q
is given by

\begin{split}Vol_{p_{f}}(q)&=\int_{M}Vol_{t_{f}}(p,q)d\mu_{M}\\
&=\int_{f^{-1}(B_{C}(q))}Vol_{t_{f}}(p,q)d\mu_{M}\\
&\leq K\cdot\mu_{M}(f^{-1}(B_{C}(q))).\end{split}

(1.171)

where
K
is the maximum of the Fiber Volume of
t_{f}
. Let us observe that since
f
is uniformly proper, then the diameter of
f^{-1}(B_{C}(q))
is uniformly bounded and so there are a point
x_{0}
in
M
and a radius
R
such that

f^{-1}(B_{C}(q))\subseteq B_{R}(x_{0}).

(1.172)

Then, since the Ricci curvature of
M
is bounded, we also have that (applying Remark
4
) there is a constant
V
such that

\mu_{M}(f^{-1}(B_{C}(q)))\leq\mu_{M}(B_{R}(x_{0}))\leq V

(1.173)

and so

Vol_{p_{f}}(q)\leq K\cdot V

(1.174)

and
p_{f}
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map.
∎

Remark 28
.

Consider a smooth map
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
which is lipschitz and uniformly proper. Let us suppose, moreover, that
(N,h)
is a manifold of bounded geometry and
(M,g)
has bounded Ricci curvature. Eventually suppose
M
incomplete or
\partial M\neq\emptyset
. Then we can observe that the map
p_{f}:(f^{*}(T^{\delta}N),g_{S})\longrightarrow(N,h)
is well-defined and, moreover, using the same arguments we used in this section, it is also R.-N.-lipschitz.

1.5.2
A Thom form for
f^{*}(TN)

Let
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
be two manifolds of bounded geometry, let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a smooth lipschitz map and consider
(f^{*}(TN),g_{S})
the pullback bundle
f^{*}(TN)
endowed of the Sasaki metric defined in the subsection
1.4.1
. Let us introduce the notion of Thom form.

Definition 1.5.1
.

Let
\pi:E\longrightarrow M
be a vector bundle. We say that
\omega
in
\Omega_{cv}^{*}(E)
is a
Thom form
if it is closed and its integral along the fibers of
\pi
is equal to the constant function
1
.

It’s a well known fact that for each oriented vector bundle
(E,\pi,M)
there is at least a Thom form
\omega
(see for example
[
1
]
). This, in particular, implies that the tangent bundle
(TN,\pi,N)
always admits a Thom form. Then, if we consider a smooth map
f:M\longrightarrow N
, the same holds for
(f^{*}TN,\pi_{1},M)
.

Given a Thom form
\omega
of
f^{*}TN
such that
supp(\omega)
is contained in a
\delta_{0}<\delta
neighborhood of the null section, let us define an operator
e_{\omega}:\Omega^{*}(f^{*}(T^{\delta}N))\longrightarrow\Omega^{*}(f^{*}(T^{\delta}N))
, for every smooth form
\alpha
as

e_{\omega}(\alpha):=\alpha\wedge\omega.

(1.175)

Our goal, in this subsection, is to find a Thom form
\omega
such that the operator
e_{\omega}
is
L^{2}
-bounded. To this end we follow the work of Mathai and Quillen
[
16
]
. In their work, indeed, they compute a Thom form for a vector bundle endowed with a connection and a metric bundle.

First we construct the Thom form of
f^{*}(TN)
when
f=id_{N}
.

Consider the bundle
\pi:(TN,h_{S})\longrightarrow(N,h)
and let
\pi^{*}(TN)
be the pullback bundle over
TN
. Consider the bundle metric on
\pi^{*}(TN)
given by
\pi^{*}h
and we can also consider the connection
\pi^{*}\nabla^{LC}_{h}
. Then we can denote by
\Omega^{i,j}
the algebra

\Omega^{i,j}:=\Omega^{i}(TN,\Lambda^{j}\pi^{*}TN)=\Gamma(TN,\Lambda^{i}T^{*}(TN)\otimes\Lambda^{j}\pi^{*}TN).

(1.176)

Let us consider the following section of
TN

\begin{split}X:TN&\longrightarrow\pi^{*}TN\\
v_{p}&\longrightarrow(v_{p},v_{p}).\end{split}

(1.177)

Observe that, identifying
\pi^{*}TN
and
\Lambda^{1}(\pi^{*}TN)
we obtain that
X
is in
\Omega^{0,1}
.

Let us fix some normal coordinates
\{x^{i}\}
on
N
and the coordinates
\{x^{i},\mu^{j}\}
on
TN
, where
\{\mu^{j}\}
are referred to the frame
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\}
. Then we have that

X(x,\mu)=\mu^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}.

(1.178)

Let us consider

\begin{split}\pi^{*}g(X,X)=|X|^{2}:TN&\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\\
v_{p}&\longrightarrow h_{p}(v_{p},v_{p}).\end{split}

(1.179)

This map can be see as a differential form in
\Omega^{0,0}
. In coordinates it can be expressed as follow

|X|^{2}(x,\mu)=h_{i,j}(x)\mu^{i}\mu^{j}.

(1.180)

Consider
\pi^{*}\nabla^{LC}_{h}(X)
: this is a form in
\Omega^{1,1}
and, in local coordinates, it is given by

\begin{split}\pi^{*}\nabla^{LC}_{h}(X)&=d\mu^{i}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}+\mu^{i}\nabla\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\\
&=d\mu^{i}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}+\mu^{i}\Gamma^{k}_{ij}(x)dx^{j}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}\\
&=(\delta^{k}_{j}+\mu^{i}\Gamma^{k}_{ij}(x))dx^{j}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}\end{split}

(1.181)

Finally let us consider
\Omega
the curvature form of
TN
: this is a
2
-form on
N
with values in
\Lambda^{2}(TN)
. We know that
\Omega
, in coordinates is given by

\Omega(x)=R^{ij}_{kl}(x)dx^{k}\wedge dx^{l}\otimes(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}})

(1.182)

where
R^{ij}_{kl}=g^{is}R^{j}_{kls}
and
R^{j}_{kls}
are the components of the Riemann tensor of
N
. Pulling back
\Omega
along
\pi
, we obtain
\pi^{*}\Omega
which is a differential form in
\Omega^{2,2}
.

Let
\phi:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function which support is contained in
[\delta_{0},\delta_{0}]
. Then we define

\overline{\omega}:=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{\phi^{(k)}(\frac{|X^{2}|}{2})}{k!}(\pi^{*}\nabla X+\pi^{*}\Omega)^{k},

(1.183)

where
(\pi^{*}\nabla X+\pi^{*}\Omega)^{k}
is the
k
-times wedge of
\pi^{*}\nabla X+\pi^{*}\Omega
.

Observe that the support of
\overline{\omega}
is strictly contained in a
\delta_{0}
-nighborhood of the zero section since
supp(f)\subseteq[-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}]
.

Let us consider
I,J,K
some multi-index and let us denote
dx^{I}:=dx^{i}_{1}\wedge...\wedge dx^{i}_{|I|}
,
d\mu^{J}:=d\mu^{i}_{1}\wedge...\wedge d\mu^{i}_{|J|}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{K}}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i_{1}}}\wedge...\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i_{|K|}}}
. Since
N
is a manifold of bounded geometry, we have that
\overline{\omega}
in local fibered coordinate
\{U,x^{i},\mu^{j}\}
is given by

\overline{\omega}(x,\mu):=\alpha_{IJ}^{K}(x,\mu)dx^{I}\wedge d\mu^{J}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{K}}

(1.184)

where
\alpha_{IJ}

|\alpha_{IJ}^{K}(x,\mu)|\leq C

(1.185)

and
C
doesn’t depend on the choice of
U
. Indeed the bounded geometry of
N
implies that the components of the metric
h
, the Christoffel symbols of
\nabla
and also the components of
h_{S}
are uniformly bounded in the fibered coordinates
\{x^{i},\mu^{j}\}
.

We have to introduce an important tool: the
Berezin integral

B
. This is the isometry

\begin{split}\textbf{B}:\Lambda^{n}(TN)&\longrightarrow N\times\mathbb{R}\\
\alpha_{p}&\longrightarrow(p,Vol_{p}(\alpha_{p}))\end{split}

(1.186)

where
n=dim(N)
and
Vol_{p}
is the volume form of
N
in a point
p

8
8

8

Actually the definition of Berezin integral is much more general: this is the definition of Berezin integral for the fiber bundle
TN
.
. We can observe that the Berezin integral can be extended to
\Omega^{i,j}

\mathcal{B}:\Omega^{i,j}\longrightarrow\Omega^{i}(TN)\\

(1.187)

posing
\mathcal{B}(\alpha\otimes\beta):=\textbf{B}(\beta)\alpha
if
j=n
,
\textbf{B}(\alpha\otimes\beta):=0
otherwise.

As showed in
[
7
]
and in
[
16
]
, we can define our Thom form as the differential form given by

\omega:=\mathcal{B}(\overline{\omega}).

(1.188)

We can observe that, in coordinates
\{x,\mu\}
on
TN
,

\mathcal{B}(\alpha^{I}_{J,L}(x,\mu)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{I}}\otimes dx^{J}\wedge d\mu^{L})=\alpha^{0}_{J,L}(x,\mu)dx^{J}\wedge d\mu^{L},

(1.189)

where
I
,
J
,
L
are multi-index and
0
is the multi-index
(1,...,n)
. This fact implies that, in local coordinates
(x,\mu)
we can express

\omega(x,\mu)=\alpha_{IJ}(x,\mu)dx^{I}\wedge d\mu^{J},

(1.190)

where
|\alpha_{IJ}|
are uniformly bounded. Moreover the support of
\omega
is contained in a
\delta_{0}
-neighborhood of the null section. The differential form
\omega
is a Thom form for
TN
: a proof of this fact can be find in
[
16
]
or in
[
7
]
.

Proposition 1.5.3
.

Consider
(N,h)
a manifold of bounded geometry. Let
\alpha
be a differential form on
(T^{\delta}N,h_{S})
. If in fibered coordinates
\{x^{i},\mu^{j}\}
, where
\mu^{j}
are referred to
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\}
the coefficients of
\alpha
are uniformly bounded, then the punctual norm
|\alpha_{p}|
is uniformly bounded.

Proof.

It is a direct computation. We know that the norm of
dx^{i}
and
d\mu^{j}
are uniformly bounded in
f^{*}T^{\delta}N
. Moreover, applying the Hadamard-Schwartz inequality (
[
19
]
or Lemma
A.0.2
in Appendix A) we have that there is a number
K
such that
|dx^{I}\wedge d\mu^{J}|_{p}\leq K
. Then, since

|\alpha_{p}|\leq\sqrt{|\alpha_{IJ}(x,\mu)|^{2}\cdot|\alpha_{RS}(x,\mu)|^{2}\cdot|dx^{I}\wedge d\mu^{J}|_{p}^{2}\cdot|dx^{R}\wedge d\mu^{S}|_{p}^{2}}\leq C.

(1.191)

∎

Let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a smooth lipschitz map and consider
(f^{*}(TN),g_{S})
where
g_{S}
is the Sasaki metric defined as in the previous section. Observe that if
\omega
is the Thom form on
TN
defined in (
1.188
) and if we consider the map
F:f^{*}(TN)\longrightarrow TN
given by
F(p,w_{f(p)})=(f(p),w_{f(p)})
, then it’s easy to check that
F^{*}\omega
is a Thom form for
f^{*}(TN)
and moreover, applying Lemma
A.0.3
in Appendix A, we also have a uniform bound on the norm of
|F^{*}\omega|_{p}
.

Proposition 1.5.4
.

Let us consider a form
\alpha
on a Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
. Suppose that there is a number
C
such that

|\alpha_{p}|\leq C

(1.192)

for each
p
in
(M,g)
. Then the operator
e_{\alpha}(\beta):=\beta\wedge\alpha
defines an
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator.

Proof.

It’s again a direct consequence of Hadamard-Schwartz inequality. Indeed

\begin{split}||e_{\alpha}(\beta)||_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)}^{2}&=\int_{M}|\alpha\wedge\beta|_{p}^{2}d\mu_{M}\\
&\leq\int_{M}C\cdot|\alpha|_{p}^{2}\cdot|\beta|_{p}^{2}d\mu_{M}\\
&\leq C_{\alpha}\int_{M}|\beta|_{p}^{2}d\mu_{M}=C_{\alpha}||\beta||_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)}^{2}.\end{split}

(1.193)

∎

1.5.3
The
T_{f}
operator

Let
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
be two manifolds of bounded geometry and let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a uniformly proper, smooth, lipschitz map.

Let us denote by
pr_{M}
the projection
pr_{M}:(f^{*}(T^{\delta}N),g_{S})\longrightarrow(M,g)
, let
\omega
be a Thom form defined as in the previous subsection and consider
p_{f}:(f^{*}(T^{\delta}N),g_{S})\longrightarrow(M,g)
the submersion related to
f
that we introduced in the previous section. If
f
is differentiable, then we can define the operator
T_{f}
for each smooth
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-form as

\begin{split}T_{f}(\alpha)&=pr_{M,\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ p_{f}^{*}(\alpha)\\
&=\int_{B^{\delta}}p_{f}^{*}(\alpha)\wedge\omega.\end{split}

(1.194)

where
B^{\delta}
denotes the fibers of
pr_{M}
. If
f
is not differentiable, then we can consider a differentiable lipschitz map
f^{\prime}
which is lipschitz-homotopic to
f

9
9

9

we know that such a
f^{\prime}
exists as consequence of Proposition
1.2.3
and we impose

T_{f}:=T_{f^{\prime}}.

(1.195)

Actually, if
f
is not differentiable, the definition of
T_{f}
depends on the choice of
f^{\prime}
. We will not denote the choice of the
f^{\prime}
because, as we will see later,
T_{f}
induces an operator in (un)-reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology which doesn’t depend on the choice of
f^{\prime}
.

Proposition 1.5.5
.

Let us consider a uniformly proper lipschitz map
f
between Riemannian manifolds of bounded geometry. Then the operator
T_{f}
is a bounded operator.

Proof.

Let us suppose
f
is smooth.
We know, since Corollary
1.5.2
, that
p_{f}^{*}
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded.

Because of since Corollary
1.5.4
, we also know that
e_{\omega}
is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator.

We just have to prove that
pr_{M,\star}
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded.
Observe that
pr_{M}
is a Riemannian submersion (Remark
23
) and so, in particular, it is a lipschitz map. Moreover it’s easy to check that its Fiber Volume is the Volume of an euclidean ball of radius
\delta
in each point of
M
. Then, applying Proposition
1.3.12
, we have that
pr_{M}
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map and, in particular
pr_{M}^{*}
is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator. Then, beacause of Remark
22
,

||pr_{M,\star}||=||pr_{M}^{*}||=L<+\infty.

(1.196)

Then we can conclude since
T_{f}
is composition of bounded operators.
∎

Remark 29
.

In particular the previous Proposition holds if
f
is a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence.

Corollary 1.5.6
.

Given a uniformly proper lipschitz map
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
between two Riemannian manifolds of bounded geometry, then we have

T_{f}(dom(d_{min}))\subseteq dom(d_{min})

(1.197)

and
T_{f}d=dT_{f}
. This, in particular, means that
T_{f}
induces a morphism in
L^{2}
-cohomology. Moreover, since
T_{f}
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded, it also induces a morphism between the reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology groups.

Proof.

Let us suppose, again, that
f
is smooth.
In order to conclude the proof we will prove that the operator
T_{f}
satisfies the hypothesis of Proposition
1.3.3
. We already know that
T_{f}
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded. Then, in order to conclude the proof, we just have to prove that
T_{f}(\Omega^{*}_{c}(N))\subseteq\Omega^{*}_{c}(M)
and
dT_{f}\alpha=T_{f}d\alpha
for each
\alpha
in
\Omega^{*}_{c}(N)

Since
p_{f}
is uniformly proper we have that for each smooth form
\alpha
in
L^{2}(N)

diam(supp(e_{\omega}\circ p_{f}^{*}(\alpha))\leq C_{\alpha}.

(1.198)

Moreover, since
supp(pr_{M,\star}\beta)\subset pr_{M}(supp(\beta))
for each smooth
\beta
in
L^{2}(f^{*}T^{\delta}N)
, we have that
supp(T_{f}\alpha)
is bounded in
M
. This means that
supp(T_{f}\alpha)
is compact since
(M,g)
is complete and
T_{f}(\Omega_{c}(N))\subseteq\Omega_{c}(M)
.

We have that
e_{\omega}\circ p_{f}^{*}(\Omega^{*}_{c}(N))\subseteq\Omega^{*}_{vc}(f^{*}(T^{\delta}N))
, where in
\Omega^{*}_{vc}(f^{*}(T^{\delta}N))
we are considering the vertically compactly supported smooth forms related to the projection on the first component
pr_{M}:f^{*}(T^{\delta}(M))\longrightarrow M
.

Then we can apply the Proposition
B.0.3
of
[
9
]
, which allow us to say
pr_{M,\star}(\Omega^{*}_{vc}(f^{*}T^{\delta}N)\subseteq\Omega_{c}(M)
and that

\int_{B^{\delta}}d\eta=d\int_{B^{\delta}}\eta,

(1.199)

if
\eta
is in
\Omega_{vc}^{*}(f^{*}T^{\delta}N)
. Then, using that
T_{f}
is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator we conclude applying Corollary
1.3.3
.
∎

Remark 30
.

Since
p_{f}
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant map, then
p_{f}^{*}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant and, in particular
T_{f}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant.

1.5.4
R.-N.-lipschitz equivalences of vector bundles

Let us consider two smooth lipschitz maps
f_{0},f_{1}:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
between Riemannian manifolds and consider a vector bundle
E
over
N
. Fix a group
\Gamma
acting on
M
and on
N
. Then it’s a known fact (see for example
[
22
]
) that if
f_{0}\sim_{\Gamma}f_{1}
with a lipschitz homotopy
H
, then

f_{0}^{*}(E)\times[0,1]\cong f_{1}^{*}(E)\times[0,1]\cong H^{*}(E)

(1.200)

and

f_{0}^{*}(E)\cong f_{1}^{*}(E)

(1.201)

where with
\cong
we mean that they are isomorphic as fiber bundles. As consequence of this fact we also have that they are homeomorphic as manifolds.

Definition 1.5.2
.

Consider two vector bundles
E
,
F
over a Riemannian manifold
(N,h)
. Let us suppose that
e
is a Riemannian metric on
E
and
f
a Riemannian metric on
F
. Let us denote by
E^{\delta}
and
F^{\delta}
the disk bundles of radius
\delta
. We say that
(E,e)
and
(F,f)
are
R.-N.-lipschitz equivalent
if there is a number
\delta>0
and there is a bundle isomorphism
\phi:E\longrightarrow F
such that
\phi(E^{\delta})=F^{\delta}
and
\phi
restricted to
E^{\delta}
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map.

Remark 31
.

If
E
and
F
are R.-N.-lipschitz equivalent, then if
A:(F^{\delta},f)\longrightarrow(M,g)
is an R.-N.-lipschitz map, then
\phi^{*}A
is again an R.-N.-lipschitz map. Moreover if
B:(E^{\delta},e)\longrightarrow(M,g)
is an R.-N.-lipschitz map it is also true that
(\phi^{-1})^{*}B
is an R.-N.-lipschitz map, indeed, since
\phi
is a diffeomorphism,

(\phi^{-1})^{*}B=\phi_{\star}B,

(1.202)

and the
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-norm of
\phi_{\star}
is the same of the norm of
\phi^{*}
.

Lemma 1.5.7
.

Let us consider two Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
and
(N,l)
, where
(N,l)
is a manifold of bounded geometry. Consider
f_{0},f_{1}:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,l)
two smooth lipschitz maps and suppose that there is a lipschitz-homotopy
H:(M\times[0,1],g\times g_{[0,1]})\longrightarrow(N,l)
such that
H(p,i):=f_{i}(p)
for
i=0,1
.

Let us suppose, moreover, that

d(f_{0}(p),f_{1}(p))\leq\frac{inj_{N}}{2}

(1.203)

where
\delta
is the radius of injectivity of
N
. Let us denote by
g_{S,f^{*}_{i}}
the Sasaki metric on
f_{i}^{*}(TN)
defined using
g
,
f^{*}_{i}(l)
and
f^{*}_{i}(\nabla^{LC}_{l})
where
i=0,1
.

Then
(f^{*}_{0}(TN),g_{S,f^{*}_{0}})
and
(f^{*}_{1}(TN),g_{S,f^{*}_{1}})
are R.-N.-lipschitz equivalent. In particular
\delta
can be choose less or equal to
inj_{N}
.

Proof.

We already know that
f^{*}_{0}(TN)
and
f^{*}_{1}(TN)
are homeomorphic. Then we will consider
f^{*}_{0}(TN)
and
f^{*}_{1}(TN)
as the same bundle, but with different metrics. Then we have to check that the identity map
Id:f^{*}_{1}(T^{\delta}N)\longrightarrow f^{*}_{0}(T^{\delta}N)
is lipschitz and that its Fiber Volume is bounded. Let us start with its Fiber Volume.

Fix some normal coordinates
\{U,x^{i}\}
on
M
. Fix, moreover, some normal coordinates
\{V,y^{j}\}
on
N
such that
f_{0}(U)\subseteq V
. We also suppose that
f_{0}(0)=0
in this coordinates.
Then we can consider
\{x^{i},\mu^{j}\}
the fibered coordinates on
f_{0}^{*}(TN)
related to the frame
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}\}
. Then we have that
Id(x,\mu)=(x,\mu)
and so

Vol_{Id}(x,\mu)=\sqrt{\frac{det(G_{ij})}{det(L_{jk})}(x,\mu)}

(1.204)

where
G_{ij}
is the matrix related to the metric
g_{S,f^{*}_{1}}
and
L_{jk}
the matrix related to
g_{S,f^{*}_{0}}
. Observe that in
(0,\mu)
we have that

det(L_{jk}(0,\mu))=1.

(1.205)

On the other hand we have that
det(G_{ij}(0,\mu))
is an algebraic combination of pullback of Christoffell symbols and terms of the metric on
N
along the lipschitz map
f_{1}
, first derivatives of
f_{1}
and
\mu^{j}\mu^{i}
. We obtain a uniform bound on
|h_{E,ij}|
and
|\Gamma^{k}_{E,ij}|
on a ball of radius less or equal to
inj_{r}N
. Moreover we have that
|\mu^{i}|\leq\delta
. Then we can conclude that the function

\sqrt{\frac{det(G_{ij})}{det(L_{jk})}}:U\times[0,1]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

(1.206)

is bounded and its bound doesn’t depend on the choice of
U
.

Let us verify that
Id
is lipschitz. This time we fix the coordinates
\{x^{i}\}
on
M
and we consider some normal coordinates
\{y^{j}\}
on
N
such that
f_{1}(0)=0
. Then, exactly as we did in the previous point, we choose the coordinates
\{x^{i},\mu^{j}\}
related to the frame
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}\}
.

Then we have to check that

\frac{|\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}|_{f_{0}^{*}(TN),(0,\mu)}}{|\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}|_{f_{1}^{*}(TN),(0,\mu)}}

(1.207)

and

\frac{|\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{j}}|_{f_{0}^{*}(TN),(0,\mu)}}{|\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{j}}|_{f_{1}^{*}(TN),(0,\mu)}}

(1.208)

are uniformly bounded. Observe that
\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}|_{f_{1}^{*}(TN),(0,\mu)}
and
|\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{j}}|_{f_{1}^{*}(TN),(0,\mu)}
are equal to
1
.

Let us concentrate on
|\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}|_{f_{0}^{*}(TN),(0,\mu)}
and on
|\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{j}}|_{f_{0}^{*}(TN),(0,\mu)}
.

Then, similarly to what we did proving the boundedness of the Fiber Volume, we can observe that the components of metric
g_{S,f^{*}_{0}}
, in these coordinates, are algebraic combinations of pullback of Christoffell symbols and terms of the metric on
N
along the lipschitz map
f_{0}
, first derivatives of
f_{0}
and
\mu^{j}\mu^{i}
. Then, since on a ball of radius less or equal to
\delta=inj_{r}N
we have a bound on
|h_{E,ij}|
and
|\Gamma^{k}_{E,ij}|
, we conclude that the lipschitz constant is bounded.
∎

Proposition 1.5.8
.

Let us consider
f_{0},f_{1}:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
two lipschitz maps such that
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
are lipschitz-homotopic. Then
(f^{*}_{0}(TN),g_{S,0})
and
(f^{*}_{1}(TN),g_{S,1})
are R.-N.-lipschitz equivalent. In particular
\delta
can be choose less or equal to
inj_{N}
.

Proof.

Let us consider the homotopy
H
between
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
. Consider a finite partition
\{[s_{i},s_{i+1}]\}
of
[0,1]
such that
s_{i+1}-s_{i}\leq\frac{inj_{N}}{2C_{H}}
, where
C_{h}
is the lipschitz constant of
h
. Let us define the maps
H_{s}:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
as
H_{i}(p):=H(p,s_{i})
then we can observe that

d(H_{i}(p),H_{i+1}(p))\leq d(H(p,s_{i}),(p,s_{i+1}))\leq C_{H}\cdot(s_{i+1}-s_{i})=\frac{inj_{N}}{2}.

(1.209)

Then it is sufficient to apply the previous lemma to
H_{i}
and
H_{i+1}
and observe that composition of R.-N.-lipschitz map is a R.-N.-lipschitz map.
∎

Corollary 1.5.9
.

Let us consider a lipschitz map
H:(M\times[0,1],g+g_{[0,1]}])\longrightarrow(N,h)
. For each
i
in
[0,1]
, let us denote by
f_{i}:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
the map defined as
f_{i}(p):=H(p,i)
. Then we have that the vector bundles
(H^{*}(TN),g_{S,H})
and
(f^{*}_{i}(TN)\times[0,1],g_{S}\times g_{[0,1]})
are R.-N.-lipschitz equivalent and
\delta
can choose less or equal to
inj_{N}
.

Proof.

Let us define the map
\overline{f}_{i}:M\times[0,1]\longrightarrow N
as
\overline{f}_{i}(p,s):=f_{i}(p)
. Observe that the Sasaki metric on
\overline{f}_{i}^{*}(E)
defined using
g
,
\overline{f}^{*}_{i}(h)
and
\overline{f}^{*}_{i}(\nabla^{LC}_{h})
is the product metric between the Sasaki metric on
f^{*}_{i}(E)
and the metric
g_{[0,1]}
on
[0,1]
.

Then we can conclude observing that the map

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}:(M\times[0,1]\times[0,1],g\times g_{[0,1]}\times g_{[0,1]})&\longrightarrow(N,h)\\
(p,s,t)&\longrightarrow H(p,i\cdot t+s\cdot(1-t))\end{split}

(1.210)

is a lipschitz-homotopy between
H
and
\overline{f}_{i}
.
∎

1.5.5
Lemmas about homotopy

In this section we study the
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-boundedness of the pullback of some homotopies.

Lemma 1.5.10
.

Let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a smooth lipschitz map and let
\delta\leq inj_{N}
. Consider the homotopy
H:(f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)\times[0,1],g_{S}\times g_{[0,1]})\longrightarrow(N,l)
defined as

H(p,w,t):=p_{f}(t\cdot w_{f(p)}).

(1.211)

Then, if
f
is a smooth R.-N.-lipschitz map, then also
H
and
(H,id_{[0,1]}):(f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)\times[0,1],g_{S}\times g_{[0,1]})\longrightarrow(N\times[0,1],l\times g_{[0,1]})
are R.-N.-lipschitz maps.

Remark 32
.

The map
H
above is important for us because it is a
\Gamma
-equivariant lipschitz-homotopy between
p_{f}
and
\overline{f}:f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)\longrightarrow N
. In particular, proving this Lemma, we have that the maps
p_{id}:TN\longrightarrow N
and the projection
pr_{N}:TN\longrightarrow N
are
\Gamma
-lipschitz-homotopic with an homotopy
H
which is a R.-N.-lipschitz map, and so
H
induces a morphism in (un)-reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology.

Proof.

Observe that
H=pr_{N}\circ(H,id_{[0,1]})
, where
pr_{N}:N\times[0,1]\longrightarrow N
. We have that
pr_{N}
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map. This means that if
(H,id_{[0,1]})
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map, then also
H
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map.

Moreover we can observe that
f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)\times\{0,1\}
is a set of null measure an so we can consider the interval
[0,1]
as open.

Let us define the map

\begin{split}(pr_{M},H,id_{(0,1)}):(f^{*}T^{\delta}N\times(0,1),g_{S}\times g_{(0,1)})&\longrightarrow(M\times N\times(0,1),g\times h\times g_{(0,1)})\\
(v_{f(p)},s)&\longrightarrow(p,p_{f}(s\cdot v_{p}),s).\end{split}

(1.212)

This map is a submersion since we are considering
s\neq 0
.
In particular we have that
(pr_{M},H,id_{(0,1)})
is lipschitz since it is composition of lipschitz maps.

Moreover we have that
(H,id_{(0,1)})=pr_{N\times(0,1)}\circ(pr_{M},H,id_{(0,1)})
and so, applying Proposition
1.3.15
, we have

Vol_{(H,id_{(0,1)})}(q,t)=\int_{M}Vol_{(pr_{M},H,id_{(0,1)})}(p,q,t)d\mu_{M}.

(1.213)

Let us calculate the Fiber Volume of
(pr_{M},H,id_{(0,1)})
.

Similarly to the Lemma
1.5.1
, we can consider for each
p
in
M
some normal coordinates
\{x^{i}\}
around
p
, some normal coordinates
\{y^{j}\}
around
f(p)
in
N
and, considering the frame
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\}
, we have the coordinates
\{x^{i},\mu^{j},t\}
on
f^{*}(TN)\times[0,1]
around
(p,0,0)
.

Moreover we can also consider the coordinates
\{x^{i},y^{j},t\}
on
M\times N\times[0,1]
.

Observe that, the coordinates
\{x^{i},y^{j},t\}
are enough to cover the image of
(id_{M},H,id_{[0,1]})
which is contained in a
\delta
-neighborhood of
Graph(f)\times[0,1]
.

Finally, respect to these coordinates, we have that

(pr_{M},H,id_{(0,1)})(0,\mu^{j},t)=(0,t\cdot y^{j},t).

(1.214)

Similarly as we did in Lemma
1.5.1
one can show that

Vol_{f^{*}(TN)\times[0,1]}(0,\mu,t)=dx^{1}\wedge...\wedge dx^{m}\wedge d\mu^{1}\wedge...\wedge d\mu^{n}\wedge dt

(1.215)

and that

Vol_{M\times N\times[0,1]}(0,y,t)=\sqrt{det(H_{ij}(0,y))}dx^{1}\wedge...\wedge dx^{m}\wedge dy^{1}\wedge...\wedge dy^{n}\wedge dt,

(1.216)

where

H_{ij}=\begin{bmatrix}1&&0&&0\\
0&&h_{l,s}(y)&&0\\
0&&0&&1\end{bmatrix}

(1.217)

and
h_{l,s}
are the components of the metric
h
on
N
. Then we have that
(pr_{M},H,id_{[0,1]})
is a diffeomorphism with its image and so applying Remark
21
, its Fiber Volume on
im(pr_{M},H,id_{[0,1]})
is given by

|[(pr_{M},\tilde{h},id_{[0,1]})^{-1}]^{*}\frac{Vol_{f^{*}TN\times[0,1]}}{(pr_{M},\tilde{h},id_{[0,1]})^{*}Vol_{M\times N\times[0,1]}}|

(1.218)

and it is null otherwise.

Observe that, similarly to Lemma
1.5.1
, in a point
(0,y^{j},t)
we have that the Fiber Volume of
(pr_{M},\tilde{h},id_{[0,1]})
is

\frac{Vol_{f^{*}TN\times[0,1]}}{(pr_{M},\tilde{h},id_{[0,1]})^{*}Vol_{M\times N\times[0,1]}}=\frac{1}{t^{n}}(1+C(t,y)),

(1.219)

where
C
is a bounded function.

Let us consider now the projection
pr_{N\times[0,1]}:M\times N\times(0,1)\longrightarrow N\times(0,1)
. Let us recall that, since the Proposition
1.3.15
, we have

Vol_{(H,id_{(0,1)})}(q,t)=\int_{M}Vol_{(id_{M},H,id_{[0,1]})}(p,q,t)d\mu_{M}.

(1.220)

We know that outside the image of
(id_{M},H,id_{[0,1]})
the Fiber Volume
Vol_{(id_{M},H,id_{[0,1]})}
is null. Let us denote by

\begin{split}H_{t}:M\times N&\longrightarrow N\\
(p,q)&\longrightarrow H(p,q,t).\end{split}

(1.221)

we have that
Vol_{(id_{M},H,id_{[0,1]})}
on
M\times\{q\}\times\{t\}
is null outside

pr_{M}(H^{-1}_{t}(q))\times\{q\}\times\{t\}\supseteq[im(id_{M},H,id_{[0,1]})\cap M\times\{q\}\times\{t\}]

(1.222)

Since
H
is lipschitz, applying Remark
3
, we have

H^{-1}_{t}(q)\subseteq\pi^{-1}(f^{-1}(B_{C_{H}\cdot t}(q)))\times\{t\}

(1.223)

where
\pi:f^{*}TN\longrightarrow M
is the projection of the bundle. So

pr_{M}(H^{-1}_{t}(q))\times\{q\}\times\{t\}\subset f^{-1}(B_{C_{H}\cdot t}(q))\times\{q\}\times\{t\}.

(1.224)

Then using that
f
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map, we have that

\mu(f^{-1}(B_{C_{H}\cdot t}(q)))\leq K_{0}\mu_{N}(B_{C_{H}\cdot t}(q))\leq K_{0}C_{H}^{n}t^{n}(1+L(t)),

(1.225)

and so, since
N
has bounded geometry,

\mu_{N}(B_{C_{H}\cdot t}(q))\leq C_{H}^{n}t^{n}(1+L(t))

(1.226)

where
L
is a bounded function which depends by
t
. Then we have that the Fiber Volume of
(H,id_{(0,1)})
is given by

\begin{split}Vol_{(H,id_{(0,1)})}(q,t)&=\int_{f^{-1}(B_{C_{H}\cdot t}(q))}Vol_{(id_{M},H,id_{[0,1]})}(p,q,t)d\mu_{M}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{t^{n}}(1+C(t,\tilde{y}))\cdot(K_{0}C_{H}^{n}t^{n}(1+L(t)))\\
&\leq K\cdot\frac{1}{t^{n}}\cdot t^{n}\leq K.\end{split}

(1.227)

and so
(H,id_{(0,1)})
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map and so also

H=pr_{N}\circ(H,id_{(0,1)})

(1.228)

is an R.-N.-lipschitz map.
∎

Let us consider two uniformly proper lipschitz maps
g:(S,v)\longrightarrow(M,m)
and
f:(M,m)\longrightarrow(N,l)
between manifolds of bounded geometry. Let us define the maps

\begin{split}\overline{g}:g^{*}T^{\sigma}M&\longrightarrow M\\
v_{g(p)}&\longrightarrow g(p).\end{split}

(1.229)

and

\begin{split}p_{g}:g^{*}T^{\sigma}M&\longrightarrow M\\
v_{g(p)}&\longrightarrow p_{g}(v_{g(p)}).\end{split}

(1.230)

Consider the compositions
f\circ\overline{g}
and
f\circ p_{g}:g^{*}T^{\sigma}M\longrightarrow M
. Observe that these maps are lipschitz-homotopy equivalent. Because of Proposition
1.5.8
, we have that the pullback bundles

(f\circ\overline{g})^{*}TN\longrightarrow S

(1.231)

and

(f\circ p_{g})^{*}TN\longrightarrow S

(1.232)

are R.-N.-lipschitz homotopy equivalent
10
10

10

for
\delta\leq inj_{N}
.
. Denote by
h
the homotopy

\begin{split}h:g^{*}(T^{\sigma}M)\times[0,1]&\longrightarrow N\\
(v_{g(p)},t)&\longrightarrow f\circ p_{g}(t\cdot v_{g(p)})\end{split}

(1.233)

between
f\circ p_{g}
and
f\circ\overline{g}
. Because of Corollary
1.5.9
we have that
(h^{*}(TN),g_{S,h})
and

(f\circ\overline{g}^{*}(T^{\delta}N)\times[0,1],g_{S}\times g_{[0,1]})

(1.234)

are R.-N.-lipschitz equivalent. Then we have that

p_{h}:((f\circ\overline{g})^{*}(T^{\delta}N)\times[0,1],g_{S}\times g_{[0,1]})\longrightarrow(N,l)

(1.235)

is a R.-N.-lipschitz map because
h
is uniformly proper and lipschitz map and because of Remark
28
.

Observe that
p_{h}
is a lipschitz homotopy between the maps

p_{f\circ\overline{g}}:f\circ\overline{g}^{*}(T^{\delta}N)\longrightarrow(N,l)

(1.236)

and

p_{f\circ p_{g}}:f\circ\overline{g}^{*}(T^{\delta}N)\longrightarrow(N,l).

(1.237)

Applying the point 4. of Proposition
1.4.1
, we can obtain that

\begin{split}p_{f\circ p_{g}}&=p_{id_{M}}\circ F\circ P_{g},\\
&=p_{f}\circ P_{g}\end{split}

(1.238)

where
F:f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)\longrightarrow T^{\delta}N
is the bundle map induced by
f
and
P_{g}:(f\circ\overline{g})^{*}T^{\delta}N\longrightarrow f^{*}T^{\delta}N
is the bundle map induced by
p_{g}
.

Proposition 1.5.11
.

The map
H

\begin{split}H:((f\circ\overline{g})^{*}T^{\delta}N\times[0,1],g_{S}\times g_{[0,1]})&\longrightarrow(N,l)\\
(v_{f\circ\overline{g}},t)&\longrightarrow p_{f}(P_{g}(t\cdot v_{f\circ\overline{g}}))\end{split}

(1.239)

is a R.-N.-lipschitz map.

Proof.

Observe that
H=p_{h}
defined above. Observe that since Remark
27
we have that
(f\circ\overline{g})^{*}T^{\delta}N\times[0,1]
has bounded Ricci curvature. Then, since
h
is a smooth, uniformly proper and lipschitz map and since Remark
28
, we have that
H=p_{H}
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map.
∎

1.5.6
Lipschitz-homotopy invariance of (un)-reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology

Consider the category
\mathcal{C}
which has manifolds of bounded geometry as objects and uniformly proper lipschitz maps as arrows. Consider, moreover, the category
Vec
which has complex vector spaces as objects and linear maps as arrows. In this section we will show that, for every
z
in
\mathbb{N}
, the association
\mathcal{F}_{z}:\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\textbf{Vec}
defined as

\begin{cases}\mathcal{F}_{z}(M,g)=H^{z}_{2}(M)\\
\mathcal{F}_{z}((M,g)\xrightarrow{f}(N,h))=H^{z}_{2}(N)\xrightarrow{T_{f}}H^{z}_{2}(M)\end{cases}

(1.240)

is a controvariant functor.

Moreover, we will show that if two maps
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are uniformly proper lipschitz-homotopic then
T_{f_{1}}=T_{f_{2}}
in (un)-reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology and that if
f
is a
L^{2}
-map
11
11

11

i.e. a smooth R.-N.-lipschitz map which is also uniformly proper
, then
f^{*}=T_{f}
in (un)-reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology.

This fact will imply that if
f
is a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence between manifolds of bounded geometry, then the
L^{2}
-cohomology groups are isomorphic. In other words, the
L^{2}
-cohomology is a lipschitz-homotopy invariant for manifolds of bounded geometry.

Let us introduce, the operator
\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}
: it will be the main tool that we will use to study homotopies.

Lemma 1.5.12
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold and consider
([0,1],g_{[0,1]})
. Then there is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator

\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}:\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\times[0,1])\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)

(1.241)

such that for all smooth
\alpha\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\times[0,1])
we have, in
\Omega^{*}(M)
,

i_{1}^{*}\alpha-i_{0}^{*}\alpha=\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}d\alpha+d\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}\alpha

(1.242)

Moreover
\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}
sends compactly-supported differential forms on
\Omega^{*}(M\times[0,1])
into
\Omega^{*}_{c}(M)
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha
be in
\Omega^{*}_{c}(M\times[0,1])
with compact support and let
p:M\times[0,1]\longrightarrow M
be the projection on the first component. Then we can decompose every differential form on
M\times[0,1]
as

\alpha=g(x,t)p^{*}\omega+f(x,t)dt\wedge p^{*}\omega,

(1.243)

for some
\omega
in
\Omega_{c}(M)
and for some
C^{\infty}
-class functions
g,f:M\times[0,1]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
. Then we can define the linear operator
\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}
as follow: if
\alpha
is a
0
-form with respect to
[0,1]
, then

\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}\alpha:=\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}g(x,t)p^{*}\omega=0

(1.244)

and, if
\alpha
is a
1
-form with respect to
[0,1]
,

\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}\alpha:=(\int_{0}^{1}f(x,t)dt)\omega.

(1.245)

This operator is very similar to the operator
p_{\star}
(the integration along the fiber of
p:M\times[0,1]\longrightarrow M
), but they are different for the signs. Indeed if we consider
\alpha=f(x,t)dt\wedge p^{*}\omega
we have that

\begin{split}p_{\star}\alpha&=\int_{[0,1]}f(x,t)dt\wedge p^{*}\omega\\
&=\int_{[0,1]}(-1)^{deg(p^{*}\omega)}p^{*}\omega\wedge f(x,t)dt.\end{split}

(1.246)

Then applying the Projection Formula we obtain

\begin{split}p_{\star}\alpha&=(-1)^{deg(p^{*}\omega)}\omega\cdot(\int_{[0,1]}f(x,t)dt)\\
&=(-1)^{deg(p^{*}\omega)}\omega\cdot(\int_{0}^{1}f(x,t)dt)\\
&=(-1)^{deg(p^{*}\omega)}\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}\alpha.\end{split}

(1.247)

Moreover the operator
\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}
doesn’t commute with the exterior derivative
d
on
\Omega_{vc}(M\times[0,1])
, indeed if we consider for example
M=U
an open set of
\mathbb{R}^{n}

\begin{split}d\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}\alpha&=d(\int_{0}^{1}f(x,t)dt)\\
&=(\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}dt)\wedge dx^{i}\end{split}

(1.248)

but

\begin{split}\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}d\alpha&=\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}dx^{i}\wedge dt)\\
&=\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}(-\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}dt\wedge dx^{i})\\
&=-(\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i}}dt)dx^{i}.\end{split}

(1.249)

Since the operator
\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}
, up to signs, is the operator of integration along the fibers, then we have that the norm is the same of
p_{\star}=1
. We know from Lemma 11.4 of
[
15
]
that
\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}:\Omega^{*}(M\times[0,1])\longrightarrow\Omega^{*-1}(M)
and that for all differential forms we have that

\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}d\alpha+d\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}\alpha=i_{1}^{*}\alpha-i_{0}^{*}\alpha.

(1.250)

∎

Remark 33
.

Consider a map
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
between manifolds of bounded geometry and let
H:(M\times[0,1],g+g_{[0,1]})\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a lipschitz map such that
H(x,0)=f(x)
.

Because of Corollary
1.5.9
, we have that the bundles
(H^{*}TN,g_{H})
and
(f^{*}(TN)\times[0,1],g_{S}\times g_{[0,1]})
are R.-N.-lipschitz equivalent. Then we have that the map

pr_{[0,1]}:(H^{*}T^{\delta}N,g_{H})\longrightarrow(f^{*}T^{\delta}N,g_{S})

(1.251)

which is the projection on the component in
[0,1]
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map. Then, using that,

||pr_{[0,1]}^{*}||=||pr_{[0,1],\star}||=||\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}||

(1.252)

we obtain that
\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}:\mathcal{L}^{2}(H^{*}T^{\delta}N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(f^{*}T^{\delta}N)
is an
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator.

Proposition 1.5.13
.

Let
id:(N,h)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be the identity map on a manifold of bounded geometry and consider
p_{id}:TN\longrightarrow N
the submersion related to the identity as in Theorem
1.4.1
. Then, if
pr_{N}:TN\longrightarrow N
is the projection of the tangent bundle, then there is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator
K_{1}:\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(T^{\delta}N)
such that for every smooth form
\alpha

p_{id}^{*}\alpha-pr_{N}^{*}\alpha=d\circ K_{1}\alpha+K_{1}\circ d\alpha.

(1.253)

Moreover, if
\alpha
is in
\Omega^{*}_{c}(N)
, then
K_{1}\alpha\in\Omega^{*}(T^{\delta}N)
has compact support.

Proof.

Observe that
pr_{N}
is a lipschitz submersion with bounded Fiber Volume and so
pr_{N}^{*}
is a bounded operator.

Then we have that
p_{id}
and the projection
pr_{N}:(TN,g_{S})\longrightarrow(N,h)
are lipschitz-homotopic. Indeed, if we consider

H(p,t,s)=p_{id}(p,st)

(1.254)

this is a lipschitz homotopy. Moreover, by Lemma
1.5.10
we know that
H
is R.-N.-lipschitz and so
H^{*}
is an
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator.

This means that for all
\alpha
in
\Omega^{*}_{c}(N)
, using Lemma 11.4. of
[
15
]
we have that

p_{id}^{*}-pr_{N}^{*}(\alpha)=i_{1}^{*}H^{*}\alpha-i_{0}^{*}H^{*}\alpha=\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}H^{*}d\alpha+d\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}H^{*}\alpha.

(1.255)

Then

K_{1}:=\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}\circ H^{*},

(1.256)

satisfies (
1.253
) and it is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded.
∎

Proposition 1.5.14
.

Consider
g:(M,m)\longrightarrow(N,h)
and
f:(N,h)\longrightarrow(S,r)
two smooth uniformly proper lipschitz maps between manifolds of bounded geometry. There is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator
K_{2}:\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}((f\circ\overline{g})^{*}TS)
such that for every smooth form
\alpha

p_{f\circ p_{g}}^{*}\alpha-p_{f\circ\overline{g}}^{*}\alpha=d\circ K_{2}\alpha+K_{2}\circ d\alpha.

(1.257)

Moreover, if
\alpha
is in
\Omega^{*}_{c}(S)
, then
K_{2}\alpha\in\Omega^{*}((f\circ\overline{g})^{*}T^{\delta}S)
has compact support.

Proof.

Consider
h:g^{*}f^{*}TS\times[0,1]\longrightarrow S
the lipschitz homotopy between
f\circ p_{g}
and
f\circ\overline{g}
. Let us define the submersion related to
h
defined in Theorem
1.4.1

p_{h}:h^{*}(TS)\longrightarrow S

(1.258)

Observe that
p_{h}
is the homotopy between
p_{f}\circ P_{g}=p_{f\circ p_{g}}
and
p_{f\circ\overline{g}}
defined in Lemma
1.5.11
and so
p_{h}
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map. Let us define the operator

K_{2}:=\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}\circ p_{h}^{*}.

(1.259)

It is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator because it is composition of
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operators. Then we can observe that for every smooth form
\alpha
we have

\begin{split}(p_{f\circ p_{g}})^{*}\alpha-p_{f\circ\overline{g}}^{*}\alpha&=(i_{0}^{*}-i_{1}^{*})p_{h}^{*}\alpha\\
&=(d\circ\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1})p_{h}^{*}\alpha+(\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}\circ d)p_{h}^{*}\alpha\\
&=d\circ K_{2}\alpha+K_{2}\circ d\alpha.\end{split}

(1.260)

Finally, since
p_{h}
is a proper map (it is composition of proper maps), if
\alpha\in\Omega^{*}_{c}(S)
the support of
K_{2}\alpha\in\Omega^{*}((f\circ\overline{g})^{*}TS)
is compact.
∎

Proposition 1.5.15
.

Let
f_{1}
and
f_{2}:(M,m)\longrightarrow(N,l)
be two smooth, uniformly proper and lipschitz maps between manifolds of bounded geometry. Let us suppose that
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are uniformly proper lipschitz-homotopic with a smooth lipschitz homotopy. Then there is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator
K_{3}:\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(p_{f_{1}}^{*}TN)
such that for all smooth form
\alpha

p_{f_{1}}^{*}\alpha-p_{f_{2}}^{*}\alpha=d\circ K_{3}\alpha+K_{3}\circ d\alpha.

(1.261)

Moreover if
\alpha\in\Omega^{*}_{c}(N)
then the support of
K_{3}\alpha\in\Omega^{*}(p_{f_{1}}^{*}TN)
is compact.

Proof.

Observe that
h
, the homotopy such that
h(p,0)=f_{1}(p)
and
h(p,1)=f_{2}(p)
, is a uniformly proper lipschitz map. Let us define the metric
g_{S}
on
f_{1}^{*}TN
as the Sasaki metric defined using the Riemannain metric
m
, the bundle metric
f_{1}^{*}l
and the connection
f^{*}\nabla^{LC,N}
.

Applying Corollary(
1.5.2
), we have that
p_{h}:(f_{1}^{*}TN\times[0,1],g_{S}\times g_{[0,1]})\longrightarrow(N,h)
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map and so
p_{h}^{*}
is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator.

Moreover
p_{h}
is a lipschitz-homotopy between
p_{f_{1}}
and
p_{f_{2}}
. This fact follows directly by the definition of submersion related to a lipschitz map in Theorem
.

So we can conclude exactly as we did in the Proposition
1.253
and Proposition
1.5.14
considering

K_{3}:=\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}\circ p_{h}^{*}

(1.262)

and using that
p_{h}
is a proper map.
∎

Proposition 1.5.16
.

Consider
(M,g)
,
(N,h)
and
(S,l)
three manifolds of bounded geometry and consider
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
,
F:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
and
g:(S,l)\longrightarrow(M,g)
three uniformly proper lipschitz maps, possibly non-smooth. Then, in
L^{2}
-cohomology, we have that

1.

T_{id_{M}}=Id_{H^{*}_{2}(M)}
,

2.

if
f
is not differentiable and
f^{\prime}
is a smooth lipschitz maps which is lipschitz-homotopic to
f
, then
T_{f}
doesn’t depend on the choice of
f^{\prime}
,

3.

if
f\sim_{\Gamma}F
then
T_{f}=T_{F}
,

4.

for each
\delta_{0}\leq\delta=inj_{N}
is possible to find a Thom form
\omega_{0}
of
f^{*}TN
with support in
f^{*}T^{\delta_{0}}N
. In particular if we denote by
T_{f}^{0}
the
T_{f}
operator defined using
\omega_{0}
instead of
\omega
, we obtain that

T_{f}=T_{f}^{0},

(1.263)

5.

T_{f\circ g}=T_{g}\circ T_{f}
,

6.

if
f
is a
L^{2}
-map then
f^{*}=T_{f}
,

Moreover, since the operator
T_{f}
is bounded, then the identities above also holds in reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology.

Proof.

Point 1.
Let us consider the standard projection
pr_{M}:TM\longrightarrow M
. Then, because of the previous proposition we have that for all smooth forms
\alpha
in
\Omega^{*}_{c}(M)
,

pr_{M}^{*}-p_{id}^{*}(\alpha)=d\circ K_{1}+K_{1}\circ d(\alpha).

(1.264)

Then we have the identity map in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
can be written as

1(\alpha):=\int_{B^{k}}pr^{*}_{M}\alpha\wedge\omega=pr_{M\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ pr^{*}_{M}(\alpha)

(1.265)

where
pr_{M\star}
is the operator of integration along the fibers of
pr_{M}
.

We have that for every
\alpha\in\Omega_{c}^{*}(M)

\begin{split}1-T_{id_{M}}(\alpha)&=pr_{M\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ pr^{*}_{M}(\alpha)-pr_{M\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ p_{id}^{*}(\alpha)\\
&=pr_{M\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ(pr^{*}_{M}-p_{id}^{*})\alpha\\
&=pr_{M\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ(d\circ K_{1}+K_{1}\circ d)\alpha.\end{split}

(1.266)

Observe that since
\omega
is closed, we have that
d(\alpha\wedge\omega)=(d\alpha)\wedge\omega
. Moreover
\alpha\wedge\omega
is in
\Omega^{*}_{vc}(TM)
. This means that the exterior derivative can be switched with
pr_{M\star}
and then we have

\begin{split}1-T_{id_{M}}(\alpha)&=d\circ pr_{M\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ K_{1}+pr_{M\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ K_{1}\circ d(\alpha)\\
&=d\circ Y_{1}+Y_{1}\circ d(\alpha),\end{split}

(1.267)

where

Y_{1}:=pr_{M\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ K_{1}.

(1.268)

Observe that
Y_{1}
is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator because it is composition of
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operators. Moreover applying Proposition
1.3.2
choosing
B=Y_{1}
and
K=1-T_{id_{M}}
we obtain that
Y_{1}(dom(d))\subseteq dom(d)
and

1\beta+T_{id_{M}}\beta=dY_{1}\beta+Y_{1}d\beta

(1.269)

for every
\beta
in
dom(d_{min})
. Then in
L^{2}
-cohomology we have that

Id_{H^{*}_{2}(M)}=T_{id_{M}}.

(1.270)

Point 2.
Consider two differentiable lipschitz approximation
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
of
f
. Then we have that
f_{1}\sim_{\Gamma}f_{2}
. In order to prove this fact let us denote by
h_{1}:M\times[0,1]\longrightarrow N
the lipschitz homotopy between
f_{1}
and
f
and by
h_{2}:M\times[0,1]\longrightarrow N
the lipschitz-homotopy between
f
and
f_{2}
. Then we can define a lipschitz homotopy
h:M\times[0,1]\longrightarrow N
between
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
as

h(p,t):=\begin{cases}h_{1}(p,2t)\mbox{ if }t\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]\\
h_{2}(p,2t-1)\mbox{ if }t\in[\frac{1}{2},1].\end{cases}

(1.271)

Let us consider, now, a differentiable lipschitz homotopy
H
between
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
as in Lemma
1.2.5
. We know, since Proposition
1.5.15
that there is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator
K_{3}
, such that for every
\alpha
in
\Omega_{c}^{*}(N)

p_{f_{1}}^{*}-p_{f_{2}}^{*}(\alpha)=d\circ K_{3}+K_{3}\circ d(\alpha).

(1.272)

This means that if we consider the
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator
12
12

12

it is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded because it is composition of
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operators

Y_{3}:=pr_{M\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ K_{3},

(1.273)

then for all
\alpha
in
\Omega_{c}^{*}(N)
we have that

\begin{split}T_{f_{1}}-T_{f_{2}}(\alpha)&=pr_{M\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ(p_{f_{1}}^{*}-p_{f_{2}}^{*})\\
&=pr_{M\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ(d\circ K_{3}+K_{3}\circ d)(\alpha)\\
&=d\circ Y_{3}+Y_{3}\circ d(\alpha).\end{split}

(1.274)

Again, applying Proposition 1.2.2. we obtain that the identity above holds for all
\beta
in
dom(d_{min})

Then, in
L^{2}
-cohomology,
T_{f}
doesn’t depend on the choice of the smooth lipschitz approximation of
f
.

Point 3.
Let us consider
f^{\prime}
and
F^{\prime}
two differentiable maps which are lipschitz-homotopic to
f
and
F
. Let
H
be a differentiable lipschitz homotopy between
f^{\prime}
and
F^{\prime}
. Then we can conclude as the previous point.

Point 4.
Let us consider
\omega
the Thom form on
f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)
defined in subsection
1.5.2
. Consider
\delta_{0}\leq\delta
and let
\phi
be the map

\begin{split}\phi:f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)&\longrightarrow f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)\\
(p,v_{f(p)})&\longrightarrow(p,\frac{\delta_{0}}{\delta}v_{f(p)}).\end{split}

(1.275)

Observe that
\phi
, respect to the Sasaki metric, is lipschitz. This means that the punctual norm of
\phi^{*}\omega
is uniformly bounded and so
e_{\phi^{*}\omega}
is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator. Then if use
\phi^{*}\omega
instead of
\omega
to define
T_{f}
we still have a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator.

Moreover we can also observe that
\phi^{*}\omega
is still a Thom form for
f^{*}(TN)
and
supp(\phi^{*}\omega)
is contained in
f^{*}T^{\delta_{0}}N
. The forms
\omega
and
\phi^{*}\omega
define on compactly supported cohomology on
f^{*}T^{\delta}N
the same class. This means that if alpha is a closed form, then

\begin{split}\int_{B^{\delta}}(\omega-\phi^{*}\omega)\wedge\alpha&=\int_{B^{\delta}}d\eta\wedge\alpha\\
&=\int_{B^{\delta}}d(\eta\wedge\alpha)+\int_{B^{\delta}}\eta\wedge d\alpha\\
&=\int_{\partial B^{\delta}}\eta\wedge\alpha+d\int_{B^{\delta}}\eta\wedge\alpha+\int_{B^{\delta}}\eta\wedge d\alpha\\
&=d\int_{B^{\delta}}\eta\wedge\alpha+\int_{B^{\delta}}\eta\wedge d\alpha\end{split}

(1.276)

This means that if there is a form
\eta
such that
\pi_{\star}\circ e_{\eta}
is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator and
\pi_{\star}\circ e_{\eta}(dom(d))\subseteq dom(d)
then we can conclude the proof of this point for both the unreduced and reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology
13
13

13

we are denoting by
\pi
the projection
\pi:f^{*}T^{\delta}N\longrightarrow M
.

Let us introduce the map

\begin{split}\Phi:f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)\times[0,1]&\longrightarrow f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)\\
(p,v_{f(p)},s)&\longrightarrow(p,[\frac{\delta_{0}}{\delta}s+\delta(1-s)]v_{f(p)}).\end{split}

(1.277)

Observe that

\omega\wedge\beta-\phi^{*}\omega\wedge\beta=\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}\Phi^{*}\omega\wedge d\beta+d\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}\Phi^{*}\omega\wedge\beta.

(1.278)

This means

\int_{B^{\delta}}\omega\wedge\beta-\int_{B^{\delta}}\phi^{*}\omega\wedge\beta=\int_{B^{\delta}}\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}\Phi^{*}\omega\wedge d\beta+d\int_{B^{\delta}}\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}\Phi^{*}\omega\wedge\beta.

(1.279)

We know that
\omega
, in local fibered coordinates, has the form
\alpha_{IJ}(x,\mu)dx^{I}\wedge d\mu^{J}
where
|\alpha_{IJ}|
uniformly bounded. Let us denote by

. Then we have that

\begin{split}\Phi^{*}\omega&=\alpha_{IJ}(x,A(s)\mu)\frac{\partial A}{\partial s}(s)\cdot A(s)^{|J|-1}dx^{I}\wedge d\mu^{J}\wedge ds\\
&+\alpha_{IJ}(x,A(s)\mu)\cdot A(s)^{|J|}dx^{I}\wedge d\mu^{J}\end{split}

(1.280)

Observe that
A
has bounded derivative. Then we have that

\begin{split}\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}\Phi^{*}\omega&=[\int_{0}^{1}\alpha_{IJ}(x,A(s)\mu)\frac{\partial A}{\partial s}(s)\cdot A(s)^{|J|-1}dx^{I}\wedge d\mu^{J}\wedge ds\\
&=\beta_{RS}(x,\mu)dx^{I}\wedge d\mu^{J}\end{split}

(1.281)

where
|\beta_{IJ}|
are uniformly bounded. This fact, as we showed in subsection

, implies
|\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}\Phi^{*}\omega|_{p,v_{f(p)}}
is uniformly bounded. Let us denote by

e_{\eta}

\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator. Consider the projection of the bundle
\pi:f^{*}T^{\delta}N\longrightarrow N
.
Then we have that the operator

\pi_{\star}\circ e_{\eta}

\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded. We have to prove that
\pi_{\star}\circ e_{\eta}(dom(d))\subseteq dom(d)
. Consider
\beta

\begin{cases}\beta=\lim\limits_{k}\beta_{k}\\

(1.283)

(1.284)

Moreover (

Point 5.
Since the previous points we can consider
f
and

as differentiable maps. Consider the submersions

(P_{g})^{*}\circ p_{f}^{*}-\overline{p}^{*}_{f\circ g}(\alpha)=d\circ K_{2}+K_{2}\circ d(\alpha).

(1.286)

Let us denote by
\omega
and

are Thom form in

and in

defined as in subsection

with their pullback on other vector bundles.
Recall that

\overline{g}(s,w_{g(s)}):=g(s)

.

Then we have that

\begin{split}T_{f\circ g}&=pr_{S\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ p_{f\circ g}^{*}\\
&=pr_{S\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ id_{(f\circ\overline{g})^{*}T^{\delta}N}\circ p_{f\circ g}^{*}\\
&=pr_{S\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ pr_{g^{*}TM,\star}\circ e_{\omega^{\prime}}\circ pr_{g^{*}TM}^{*}\circ p_{f\circ g}^{*},\end{split}

(1.288)

where

and

are the projections of the vector bundles. Observe that

p_{f\circ\overline{g}}=p_{f\circ g}\circ pr_{g^{*}TM},

(1.289)

and so we have that

T_{f\circ g}=pr_{S\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ pr_{g^{*}TM\star}\circ e_{\omega^{\prime}}\circ p_{f\circ\overline{g}}^{*}.

(1.290)

Now we will focus on
T_{g}\circ T_{f}
. We have that

T_{g}\circ T_{f}=pr_{S\star}\circ e_{\omega^{\prime}}\circ p_{g}^{*}\circ pr_{M\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ p_{f}^{*}.

(1.291)

It’s possible to apply the Proposition VIII of Chapter 5 in
[

to the fiber bundles

(\overline{g}^{*}f^{*}TN,pr_{g^{*}TM},g^{*}T^{\sigma}M,B^{\delta})

(1.292)

and
(TM,pr_{M},M,B^{\sigma})
and the bundle morphism

.

We obtain that

p_{g}^{*}\circ pr_{M\star}=pr_{g^{*}TM,\star}\circ P_{g}^{*}.

(1.293)

Finally we have that also

and

commute. This means that

\begin{split}T_{g}\circ T_{f}&=pr_{S\star}\circ e_{\omega^{\prime}}\circ p_{g}^{*}\circ pr_{M\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ p_{f}^{*}\\
&=pr_{S\star}\circ e_{\omega^{\prime}}\circ pr_{g^{*}TM,\star}\circ P_{g}^{*}\circ e_{\omega}\circ p_{f}^{*}\\
&=pr_{S\star}\circ e_{\omega^{\prime}}\circ pr_{g^{*}TM,\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ P_{g}^{*}\circ p_{f}^{*}\end{split}

(1.294)

This means that, on
dom(d_{min})
, because of Proposition

, we have

\begin{split}T_{f\circ g}-T_{g}\circ T_{f}&=pr_{S\star}\circ e_{\omega^{\prime}}\circ pr_{g^{*}TM,\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ(p_{f\circ\overline{g}}^{*}-(p_{f}\circ P_{g})^{*})\\
&=pr_{S\star}\circ e_{\omega^{\prime}}\circ pr_{g^{*}TM,\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ(K_{2}\circ d-d\circ K_{2}).\end{split}

(1.295)

Now, since for each smooth form
\alpha
we have that
e_{\omega}(\alpha)
is a vertically compact smooth form, we have that

pr_{S\star}\circ e_{\omega^{\prime}}\circ pr_{g^{*}TM,\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ d=d\circ pr_{S\star}\circ e_{\omega^{\prime}}\circ pr_{g^{*}TM,\star}\circ e_{\omega}.

(1.296)

Let us define the
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator

Y_{2}:=pr_{S\star}\circ e_{\omega^{\prime}}\circ pr_{g^{*}TM,\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ K_{2}.

(1.297)

Then we have that for all

in
\Omega_{c}^{*}(N)
the equality

T_{f\circ g}-T_{g}\circ T_{f}(\alpha)=d\circ Y_{2}+Y_{2}\circ d(\alpha)

(1.298)

holds. In order to conclude the proof we have to show that the equation above also holds for every

in

1\beta+T_{id_{M}}\beta=dY_{1}\beta+Y_{1}d\beta

(1.299)

for every

in

.
Then, in
