T_{f\circ g}=T_{g}\circ T_{f}.

(1.300)

Point 6.
In order to prove this statement we have to observe that, since Theorem
1.4.1
, we have that

p_{f}=p_{id}\circ F.

(1.301)

Let us consider a form
\alpha\in\Omega_{c}^{*}(N)
: we have that

\begin{split}T_{f}\alpha&=pr_{M\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ p_{f}^{*}\alpha\\
&=pr_{M\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ F^{*}\circ p_{id}^{*}\alpha\\
&=pr_{M\star}\circ F^{*}\circ e_{\omega}\circ p_{id}^{*}\alpha.\end{split}

(1.302)

Now, using the Proposition VIII of Chapter 5 of
[
9
]
we have that

\begin{split}T_{f}\alpha&=pr_{M\star}\circ F^{*}\circ e_{\omega}\circ p_{id}^{*}\alpha\\
&=f^{*}\circ pr_{N\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ p_{id}^{*}\alpha\\
&=f^{*}\circ T_{id_{N}}\alpha\end{split}

(1.303)

Since
f
is R.-N.-lipschitz, we have that
f^{*}
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded. This means that (
1.303
) implies that

T_{f}=f^{*}\circ T_{id_{N}}.

(1.304)

Then we have that on
dom(d_{min})
the following holds

\begin{split}f^{*}-T_{f}&=f^{*}\circ(1-T_{id_{N}})\\
&=f^{*}\circ(d\circ Y_{1}+Y_{1}\circ d).\end{split}

(1.305)

We can observe that
f^{*}(\Omega_{c}^{*}(N))\subseteq\Omega_{c}^{*}(M)
since
f
is proper and for all smooth form
\alpha
we have that
f^{*}d\alpha=df^{*}\alpha
. Then we have that on
dom(d_{min})

\begin{split}f^{*}-T_{f}&=f^{*}\circ(d\circ Y_{1}+Y_{1}\circ d)\\
&=d\circ W+W\circ d,\end{split}

(1.306)

where

W=f^{*}\circ Y_{1}.

(1.307)

And so in
L^{2}
-cohomology we have that

f^{*}=T_{f}.

(1.308)

In order to conclude the proof we have to show that all the identities above also holds in reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology. To this end it is sufficient to show that if
Q
is an operator such that on
dom(d)

Q=dZ+Zd

(1.309)

where
Z
is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator, then, in reduced
L^{2}
cohomology,
Q
is the null operator.

Consider a differential form
\alpha+\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}d\beta_{k}
on
ker(d)
. We have that

\begin{split}dZ+Zd(\alpha+\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}d\beta_{k})&=dZ+Zd(\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\alpha+d\beta_{k})\\
&=\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}dZ+Zd(\alpha+d\beta_{k})\\
&=\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}dZ(\alpha+d\beta_{k})\in\overline{im(d)}.\end{split}

(1.310)

This means that on reduced
L^{2}
-cohomology
Q=dZ+Zd
is the null operator.
∎

Remark 34
.

Consider the category
\mathcal{B}
which has manifolds of bounded geometry as objects and uniformly proper R.-N.-lipschitz maps as arrows and let us recall the category
\mathcal{C}
, the category of manifolds of bounded geometry with uniformly proper lipschitz maps as arrows.

We can define the functor
\mathcal{I}:\mathcal{B}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
, where defined as

\begin{cases}\mathcal{I}(M,g)=(M,g)\\
\mathcal{I}((M,g)\xrightarrow{f}(N,h))=(M,g)\xrightarrow{f}(N,h).\end{cases}

(1.311)

Consider moreover, for all
z\in\mathbb{N}
, the functor
\mathcal{G}_{z}:\mathcal{B}\longrightarrow\textbf{Vec}
defined as

\begin{cases}\mathcal{G}_{z}(M,g)=H^{z}_{2}(M)\\
\mathcal{G}_{z}((M,g)\xrightarrow{f}(N,h))=H^{z}_{2}(N)\xrightarrow{f^{*}}H^{z}_{2}(M).\end{cases}

(1.312)

As consequence of the last point we have that

\mathcal{G}_{z}=\mathcal{F}_{z}\circ\mathcal{I}.

(1.313)

All this also holds if we replace
H^{z}_{2}
with
\overline{H}^{z}_{2}
.

Corollary 1.5.17
.

Let
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
be two manifolds of bounded geometry. Let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence. Then
T_{f}
induces an isomorphism in (reduce or not)
L^{2}
-cohomology.

Proof.

We will prove this fact for the unreduced
L^{2}
-cohomology. The reduced case can be proved exactly in the same way.

Observe that if
f
is a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence: then it is a uniformly proper lipschitz map. For the same reason also its homotopic-inverse
g
is a uniformly proper lipschitz map. Moreover since
g\circ f
is lipschitz-homotopic to
id_{M}
then, using Lemma 7.2, we also have that
g\circ f
is uniformly proper lipschitz homotopic to
id_{M}
. Then we have that in
L^{2}
-cohomology.

1_{H^{*}_{2}(M)}=T_{id_{M}}=T_{g\circ f}=T_{f}\circ T_{g}.

(1.314)

Using the same arguments we also have that

1_{H^{*}_{2}(N)}=T_{g}\circ T_{f}.

(1.315)

∎

Chapter 2
Roe index and
\rho_{f}
-class

2.1
Coarse geometry

2.1.1
Coarse structures

In this section we introduce the notions of coarse structure, the Roe and the structure algebras of a coarse space. In particular we introduce the coarse
metric
structure and a particular coarse structure defined on the disjoint union of Riemannian manifolds. Finally we conclude this section defining the Roe Index of the signature operator of an orientable Riemannian manifold.

The next definitions can be found in
[
10
]
. Let
X
be a set

Definition 2.1.1
.

A
coarse structure
over
X
is a provision, for each set
S
of an equivalence relation on the set of maps form
S
to
X
. If
p_{1},p_{2}:S\rightarrow X
are in relation then they are said to be
close
and it is also required that if

•

if
p_{1}
and
p_{2}:S\longrightarrow X
are close and
q:S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
is another map then
p_{1}\circ q
and
p_{2}\circ q
are close too.

•

If
S=S_{1}\cup S_{2}
,
p_{1},p_{2}:S\longrightarrow X
are maps whose restrictions to
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are close, then
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are close,

•

two constant maps are always close to each other.

If
X
has a coarse structure, then is a
coarse space
.

Example 2.1.1
.

When
(X,d_{X})
is a metric space, one can define a
metric
coarse structure in the following way: let
p_{1}
e
p_{2}:S\longrightarrow X
be two functions, then

p_{1}\sim p_{2}\iff\exists C\in\mathbb{R}\mbox{ such that }\forall x\in S\mbox{ }d_{X}(p_{1}(x),p_{2}(x))\leq C.

(2.1)

If the domain of
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
is a metric space
(Y,d_{Y})
and if
\Gamma
is a group which acts on
X
and on
Y
, then

p_{1}\sim_{\Gamma}p_{2}\implies p_{1}\sim p_{2}.

(2.2)

Indeed

d_{X}(p_{1}(x),p_{2}(x))=d(h(x,0),h(x,1))\leq C_{H},

(2.3)

where
C_{H}
is the lipschitz constant of the homotopy equivalence
h
.

Example 2.1.2
.

Consider now a Riemannian manifold
X:=M\sqcup N
where
M
and
N
are two connected Riemannian manifolds and let
f:M\longrightarrow N
be a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence.

In this case
X
is not a metric space in a natural way and so it has not
a priori
a metric coarse structure. We will define a coarse structure using the map
f
.

Let
p_{1}
e
p_{2}:S\longrightarrow X
be two maps, then

p_{1}\sim p_{2}\iff\exists C\in\mathbb{R}\forall x\in Ss.t.d_{N}((f\sqcup id_{N})\circ p_{1}(x),(f\sqcup id_{N})\circ p_{2}(x))\leq C.

(2.4)

Definition 2.1.2
.

Let
X
be a coarse space and let
S\subseteq X\times X
. Then
S
is
controlled
if the projections
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}:S\subseteq X
are close. A family of subsets
\mathcal{U}
of
X
is
uniformly bounded
if
\cup_{U\in\mathcal{U}}U\times U
is controlled.

A subset
B
of
X
is
bounded
if
B\times B
is controlled.

Definition 2.1.3
.

Let
X
be a locally compact topological space. A coarse structure on
X
is
proper
if

•

X
has an uniformly bounded open covering,

•

every bounded subset of
X
has compact closure.

Definition 2.1.4
.

A coarse space
X
is
separable
if
X
admits a countable, uniformly bounded, open covering.

Remark 35
.

Given a metric space
(X,d_{X})
, then the metric coarse structure is proper and separable. Moreover if
M
and
N
are two connected Riemannian manifolds and
f:M\longrightarrow N
is a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence, then also the coarse structure defined in the Example
2.1.2
is proper and separable.

2.1.2
Roe and Structure Algebras

Consider a locally compact topological space endowed with a proper and separable coarse structure. Moreover, let
\rho:C_{0}(X)\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}(H)
be a representation of
C_{0}(X)
, where
H
is a separable Hilbert space. Then

Definition 2.1.5
.

The
support
of an element
v
in
H
is the complement in
X
of all open sets
U
such that
\rho(f)v=0
for all
f
in
C_{0}(U)
.

Definition 2.1.6
.

The
support
of an operator
T
in
\mathbb{B}(H)
is the complement in
X\times X
of the union of
U\times V
such that

\rho(f)T\rho(g)=0,

(2.5)

for all
f
in
C_{0}(U)
and for all
g
in
C_{0}(V)
. An operator is
controlled
or
finite propagation
if its support is a controlled set.

Example 2.1.3
.

Consider
(X,g)
a Riemannian manifolds. Let
H_{X}
be Hilbert space
H_{X}:=\mathcal{L}^{2}(X)
and let
\rho
be the representation of
C_{0}(X)
on
H_{X}
given by the point-wise multiplication. With respect to the metric coarse structure, an operator
T
is controlled if and only if

\exists C\in\mathbb{R}\forall\phi,\psi\in C_{0}(X)s.t.d_{X}(supp(\phi),supp(\psi))\geq C\implies\phi T\psi=0.

(2.6)

Example 2.1.4
.

Consider
M
and
N
as in Example
2.1.2
and let us impose
H_{X}:=\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)
. Fix the point-wise multiplication as representation of
C_{0}(X)
on
H_{X}
. Then an operator
T
is
controlled
if there is a number
C>0
such that for each
\phi,\psi\in C_{0}(X)
we have that

d_{N}((f\sqcup id_{N})(supp(\phi)),(f\sqcup id_{N})(supp(\psi)))\geq C\implies\phi T\psi=0.

(2.7)

The next two definition can be found in
[
21
]
.

Definition 2.1.7
.

Let
X
be a coarse space. Consider
H
be a Hilbert space equipped with a representation
\rho:C_{0}(X)\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}(H)
, a group
\Gamma
and a unitary representation
U:\Gamma\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}(H)
. We say that the the triple
(H,U,\rho)
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant
X
-module
or simply a
(X,\Gamma)
-module
if

U(\gamma)\circ\rho(f)=\rho(\gamma^{*}(f))\circ U(\gamma),

(2.8)

for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
f\in C_{0}(X)
.

Definition 2.1.8
.

A represention
\rho
of a group
\Gamma
in
\mathbb{B}(H)
for some Hilbert space
H
is
nondegenerate
if the set

\{\rho(\gamma)h\in H|\gamma\in\Gamma,h\in H\}

(2.9)

is dense in
H
.

Definition 2.1.9
.

A representation
\rho
of a group
\Gamma
in
\mathbb{B}(H)
for some Hilbert space
H
is
ample
if it is nondegenerate and
\rho(g)
is a compact operator if and only if
g=0
.

If the Hilbert space
H
is separable and the representation
\rho
is the countable direct sum of a fixed ample representation, then
\rho
is said to be
very ample
.

Example 2.1.5
.

Let us consider a Riemannian manifold
X
with
dim(X)>0
and consider the Hilbert space
H:=\mathcal{L}^{2}(X)
. Let us define the representation
\rho_{X}:C_{0}^{\infty}(X)\longrightarrow B(\mathcal{L}^{2}(X))
for each
\phi
in
C_{0}^{\infty}(X)
as

\rho_{X}(\phi)(\alpha):=\phi\cdot\alpha

(2.10)

where
\alpha
is a differential form
\alpha
in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(X)
. Observe that, because of Hadamard-Schwartz inequality
[
19
]
,
\rho_{X}(\phi)
is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator. In particular we have that
\rho
is an ample representation of
C_{0}^{\infty}(X)
.

Let us consider a subgroup of the isometries of
X
called
\Gamma
. Then, the representation

\begin{split}U:\Gamma&\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{L}^{2}(X))\\
\gamma&\longrightarrow\gamma^{*}.\end{split}

(2.11)

is well defined: as consequence of Proposition
1.3.12
and Remark
21
, we have that
\gamma
is a R.N.-lipschitz map and so
\gamma^{*}
is a bounded operator.

So the triple
(\mathcal{L}^{2}(X),U,\rho)
is a
(X,\Gamma)
-module.

Example 2.1.6
.

Consider
(X,g)
an oriented Riemannian manifold (possibly non connected). Consider the Hilbert space
H_{X}:=\mathcal{L}^{2}(X)\otimes l^{2}(\mathbb{N})
. We have that an element in
H_{X}
can be seen as sequence of
\{\alpha_{i}\}
where
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(X)
such that

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}||\alpha_{i}||^{2}<+\infty.

(2.12)

Consider, moreover,
\rho_{X}:C^{\infty}_{0}(X)\longrightarrow B(H_{X})
for each
\phi
in
C^{\infty}_{0}(X)
and for each
\{\alpha_{i}\}
as

\rho_{X}(\phi)(\{\alpha_{i}\}):=\{\phi\cdot\alpha_{i}\}.

(2.13)

Consider
\Gamma
as a subgroup of isometries of
X
. Then we can define a representation
U_{X}:\Gamma\longrightarrow B(H_{X})
as

U_{X}(\gamma)(\{\alpha_{i}\}):=\{\gamma^{*}\alpha_{i}\}.

(2.14)

Then
(H_{X},\rho_{X},U_{X})
defined in this way is a very ample
(X,\Gamma)
-module.

Example 2.1.7
.

Let
(X,g)
be an oriented Riemannian manifold such that
dim(X)
is even. Let us consider the chirality operator
\tau
.
We have that
\tau
defines an involution
\tau_{p}:\Lambda^{*}_{p}(X)\longrightarrow\Lambda^{*}_{p}(X)
in each point
p
of
X
. Let us denote by
V_{\pm}
as the bundles whose fibers are the
\pm 1
-eigenspaces of
\tau_{p}
.

Let us define the Hilbert space
\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{\pm})
as the closure of the space of compactly supported section
\Gamma_{c}(M,V_{\pm})
respect to the bundle metric induced by the Riemannian metric
g
.

We obtain an orthogonal splitting

\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)=\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{+})\oplus\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{-}).

(2.15)

Let us define the vector space

H_{X}:=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{<0}}\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{-})\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{+}).

(2.16)

An element in
H_{X}
is a sequence
\{\alpha_{i}\}
indexed by
i\in\mathbb{Z}
of
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{-})
if
i<0
and
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{+})
if
i>0
such that

\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}||\alpha_{i}||_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(X)}^{2}<+\infty.

(2.17)

Consider, moreover the representation
\rho_{X}:C^{\infty}_{0}(X)\longrightarrow B(H_{X})
defined for each
\phi
in
C^{\infty}_{0}(X)
as

\rho_{X}(\phi)(\{\alpha_{i}\}):=\{\phi\cdot\alpha_{i}\}

(2.18)

and, given a subgroup
\Gamma
of isometries of
X
, let
U:\Gamma\longrightarrow B(H_{X})
be

U_{X}(\gamma)(\{\alpha_{i}\}):=\{\gamma^{*}\alpha_{i}\}.

(2.19)

Then we have that
(H_{X},\rho_{X},U_{X})
is a very ample
(X,\Gamma)
-module.

Definition 2.1.10
.

Let us consider a coarse space
X
and let
(H,U,\rho)
be a
\Gamma
-equivariant
X
-module. Then an operator
T
in
\mathbb{B}(H)
is
pseudo-local
if for all
f
in
C_{0}(X)
we have that
[\rho(f),T]
is a compact operator.

Definition 2.1.11
.

An operator
T\in B(H)
is
locally compact
if
T\rho(f)
and
\rho(f)T
are compact operators for all
f\in C_{0}(X)
.

Fix a coarse space
X
and a
\Gamma
-equivariant
X
-module
(H,U,\rho)
. We can define the following algebras.

Definition 2.1.12
.

The algebra
D^{\star}_{c,\rho}(X,H)
is given by

\{T\in B(H)|T\textit{ is pseudo-local and controlled }\}.

(2.20)

Definition 2.1.13
.

We denote by
C^{\star}_{c,\rho}(X)
the algebra

\{T\in D^{\star}_{c,\rho}(X.H)|T\textit{ is locally compact }\}.

(2.21)

Definition 2.1.14
.

Let
X
be a coarse space,
(H,U,\rho)
a
\Gamma
-equivariant
X
-module. Then we will denote by
D^{\star}_{c,\rho}(X,H)^{\Gamma}
and
C^{\star}_{c,\rho}(X,H)^{\Gamma}
the operators of
D^{\star}_{c,\rho}(X,H)
and
C^{\star}_{c,\rho}(X,H)
which commute with the action of
\Gamma
on
H
.

Definition 2.1.15
.

The
C^{*}
-algebras
D^{\star}_{\rho}(X,H)
,
C^{\star}_{\rho}(X,H)
,
D^{\star}_{\rho}(X,H)^{\Gamma}
and
C^{\star}_{\rho}(X,H)^{\Gamma}
are the closure in
B(H)
, of
D^{\star}_{c,\rho}(X,H)
,
C^{\star}_{c,\rho}(X,H)
,
D^{\star}_{c,\rho}(X,H)^{\Gamma}
e
C^{\star}_{c,\rho}(X,H)^{\Gamma}
. The algebra
D^{\star}_{\rho}(X,H)^{\Gamma}
will be called
structure algebra
and
C^{\star}_{\rho}(X,H)^{\Gamma}
will be called
coarse algebra
or
Roe algebra
.

We have that
C^{*}_{\rho}(X,H)^{\Gamma}
is an ideal of
D^{*}_{\rho}(X,H)^{\Gamma}
and so the following sequence

{0\longrightarrow C^{\star}_{\rho}(X,H)^{\Gamma}\longrightarrow D^{\star}_{\rho}(X,H)^{\Gamma}\longrightarrow\frac{D^{\star}_{\rho}(X,H)^{\Gamma}}{C^{\star}_{\rho}(X,H)^{\Gamma}}\longrightarrow 0}

(2.22)

is exact.

Remark 36
.

Let us consider a Riemannian manifold
(X,g)
and let
\Gamma
be a subgroup of isometries of
X
. In particular consider on
(X,g)
the coarse metric structure if
X
is connected. If
X=M\sqcup N
and there is a lipschitz map
f:M\longrightarrow N
consider the coarse structure defined in Example
2.1.2
. Let us consider the
(X,\Gamma)
-modules defined in Exemple
2.1.5
and
2.1.6
. We can observe that
B(\mathcal{L}^{2}(X))
can be embedded in
B(H_{X})
in the following way: for each
A
in
B(\mathcal{L}^{2}(X))
, we define
\tilde{A}\in B(H_{X})
as follow

\tilde{A}(\{\alpha_{i}\}):=\{\beta_{j}\}

(2.23)

where
\beta_{j}=0
if
j\neq 0
and
\beta_{0}:=A\alpha_{0}
.

Observe that if
A
is in
C^{*}_{\rho}(X,\mathcal{L}^{2}(X))^{\Gamma}
then
\tilde{A}
is in
C^{*}_{\rho_{X}}(X,H_{X})^{\Gamma}
and that if
A
is in
D^{*}_{\rho}(X,\mathcal{L}^{2}(X))^{\Gamma}
then
\tilde{A}
is in
D^{*}_{\rho_{X}}(X,H_{X})^{\Gamma}
. In the following sections, with a little abuse of notation, we will denote
\tilde{A}
by
A
.

Remark 37
.

Let
(X,g)
be a Riemannian manifold and
\Gamma
be a subgroup of isometries of
X
. Let us suppose
dim(X)
is even. Again if
X
is connected we consider the metric coarse structure on
X
, if
X=M\sqcup N
and there is a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence
f:M\longrightarrow N
we consider the coarse structure defined in Example
2.1.2
.

Fix on
X
the
(X,\Gamma)
-modules defined in Example
2.1.5
and in Example
2.1.7
.

Let
A
be an operator in
B(\mathcal{L}^{2}(X))
such that
A(\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{+}))\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{-})
. Let us define the operator
\tilde{A}
as the operator

\tilde{A}(\{\alpha_{i}\}):=\{\beta_{j}\}

(2.24)

where
\beta_{j}:=\alpha_{j+1}
if
j\neq-1
and
\beta_{-1}:=A(\alpha_{0})
. Then if
A
is in
D^{*}_{\rho}(X,\mathcal{L}^{2}(X))^{\Gamma}
then
\tilde{A}
is in
D^{*}_{\rho_{X}}(X,H_{X})^{\Gamma}
. Moreover we also have that if
A
and
B
are operators in
D^{*}_{\rho}(X,\mathcal{L}^{2}(X))^{\Gamma}
such that
A-B
are in
C^{*}_{\rho}(X,\mathcal{L}^{2}(X))^{\Gamma}
, then we have that also
\tilde{A}-\tilde{B}
, which is the operator

[\tilde{A}-\tilde{B}](\{\alpha_{i}\}):=\{\beta_{j}\}

(2.25)

where
\beta_{j}=0
if
j\neq-1
and
\beta_{-1}=(A-B)\alpha_{0}
, is in
C^{*}_{\rho_{X}}(X,H_{X})^{\Gamma}
.

Moreover we also have that

||\tilde{A}-\tilde{B}||=||A-B||.

(2.26)

In the following sections, with a little abuse of notation, we will denote
\tilde{A}
with
A
.

Notation 2.1.1
.

Given a Riemannian manifold
(X,g)
, if we write
C^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}
or
D^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}
without specify
H
and
\rho
, we are considering
H
,
\rho
and
\Gamma
as in Example
2.1.5
.

Moreover, if
X=M\sqcup N
, as in Example
2.1.2
, then the coarse structure depends on
f
. Thus, for this reason, we will denote its algebras by
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
and by
D_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

2.1.3
Coarse maps

Let us introduce a class of maps between coarse spaces. The following definitions and properties can be found in
[
10
]
in Chapter 6.

Definition 2.1.16
.

Let
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
be coarse spaces. A function
q:X_{1}\longrightarrow X_{2}
is called a
coarse map
if

•

whenever
p
and
p^{\prime}
are close maps into
X_{1}
, then so are the composition
q\circ p
and
q\circ p^{\prime}
,

•

for every bounded (in the coarse sense of Definition 4.2) subset
B\subseteq X_{2}
we have that
q^{-1}(B)
is a bounded subset.

Remark 38
.

If
X_{i}
are complete metric space with the metric coarse structure, then lipschitz-homotopy equivalences and, more generally, uniformly proper lipschitz maps are coarse maps. Consider, indeed, a function
f:(X_{1},d_{2})\longrightarrow(X_{2},d_{2})
be a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence. Let
p
and
p^{\prime}:(Y,d_{Y})\longrightarrow(X_{1},d_{1})
be close maps, i.e.

d_{1}(p(y),p^{\prime}(y))\leq C

(2.27)

then also
f\circ p
and
f\circ p^{\prime}
are close, indeed

d_{2}(f(p(y)),f(p^{\prime}(y)))\leq C_{f}\cdot d_{1}(p(y),p^{\prime}(y))\leq C_{f}\cdot C,

(2.28)

where
C_{f}
is the lipschitz constant of
f
. Moreover recall that if
f
is a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence, then, in particular, it is also uniformly proper map, i.e. there is a function
\alpha:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that for each bounded subset
B

diam(f^{-1}(B))\leq\alpha(diam(B)).

(2.29)

This concludes the proof.

Our next step is to introduce a morphism between the structure algebras of coarse spaces related to a coarse map.

Definition 2.1.17
.

Let
X
and
Y
be proper separable coarse spaces and suppose that
C_{0}(X)
and
C_{0}(Y)
are non-degeneratly represented on Hilbert spaces
H_{X}
and
H_{Y}
. Consider a coarse map
q:X\longrightarrow Y
. A bounded operator
V:H_{X}\longrightarrow H_{Y}

coarsely covers
q
if the maps
\pi_{1}
and
q\circ\pi_{2}
from
supp(V)\subseteq X\times Y
to
Y
are close.

Remark 39
.

As showed in Remark 6.3.10 of
[
10
]
, if
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
are close maps and
V
coarsely covers
f_{0}
, then it also coarsely covers
f_{1}
.

Definition 2.1.18
.

Let
X
and
Y
be coarse spaces, let
\rho_{X}:C_{0}(X)\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}(H_{X})
and
\rho_{Y}:C_{0}(Y)\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}_{(}H_{Y})
be ample representations on separable Hilbert spaces, and let
\phi:U\longrightarrow Y
be a continuous proper map defined on an open subset
U\subseteq X
. An isometry
V:H_{X}\longrightarrow H_{Y}

topologically covers
\phi
if for every
f\in C_{0}(Y)
there is a compact operator
K
such that in
\mathbb{B}(H_{Y})

\rho_{Y}(f)=V\rho_{X}(\phi^{*}(f))V^{*}+K.

(2.30)

An isometry
V
which analytically and topologically covers a map
\phi
then it
uniformly covers
\phi
.

Let us recall the Proposition 2.13 of
[
21
]

Proposition 2.1.1
.

Let
H_{X}
be an ample
(X,\Gamma)
-module and let
H_{Y}
be a very ample
(Y,\Gamma)
-module. Then every equivariant uniform map
\phi:X\longrightarrow Y
is uniformly covered by an equivariant isometry
V:H_{X}\longrightarrow H_{Y}
.

As consequence of this fact we can prove the following Lemma.

Lemma 2.1.2
.

Let
X
and
Y
be proper separable coarse spaces and fix
\Gamma
a group. Let us consider a
\Gamma
-equivariant continuous coarse map
\phi:X\longrightarrow Y
and consider an equivariant isometry
V:H_{X}\longrightarrow H_{Y}
which uniformly covers
\phi
. Then the map

\begin{split}Ad_{V}:D_{\rho}^{*}(X,H_{X})^{\Gamma}&\longrightarrow D_{\rho}^{*}(Y,H_{Y})^{\Gamma}\\
T&\longrightarrow VTV^{*}\end{split}

(2.31)

is well defined, maps
C^{*}_{\rho}(X,H_{X})^{\Gamma}
in
C^{*}_{\rho}(Y,H_{Y})^{\Gamma}
. Moreover the induced morphisms between the K-theory groups don’t depend on the choice of
V
.

Proof.

Let us start by proving that
Ad_{V}(T)
is in
D^{*}_{\rho}(Y,H_{Y})^{\Gamma}
. In Lemma 6.3.11 of
[
10
]
, the authors prove that if
T
is a controlled operator, then
Ad_{V}(T)
is controlled. Moreover, since
T
and
V
are both
\Gamma
-equivariant, also
Ad_{V}(T)
is
\Gamma
-equivariant. Then we just have to prove that, given
f
in
C_{0}(Y)
, then

[\rho_{Y}(f),VTV^{*}]

(2.32)

is a compact operator. We can observe that since
V
topologically covers
\phi
, then

\rho_{Y}(f)=V\rho_{X}(\phi^{*}(f))V^{*}+K,

(2.33)

where
K
is a compact operator. This means that

\begin{split}[\rho_{Y}(f),VTV^{*}]&=[V\rho_{X}(\phi^{*}(f))V^{*},VTV^{*}]+[K,VTV^{*}]\\
&=V[\phi^{*}(f),T]V^{*}+[K,VTV^{*}],\end{split}

(2.34)

which is a compact operator.

That
Ad_{V}
maps
C^{*}_{\rho}(X,H_{X})^{\Gamma}
in
C^{*}_{\rho}(Y,H_{Y})^{\Gamma}
is proved in Lemma 6.3.11 of
[
10
]
. The
\Gamma
-equivariance, again, follows by the
\Gamma
-equivariance of
V
.

Since Proposition 6.3.12 of
[
10
]
, we have that the morphisms induced between
K_{\star}(C^{*}_{\rho}(X,H_{X})^{\Gamma})
and
K_{\star}(C^{*}_{\rho}(Y,H_{Y})^{\Gamma})
don’t depend on the choice of
V
. Applying the same arguments used in the proof of Lemma 5.4.2. of
[
10
]
, we obtain that the same holds for the morphisms between
K_{\star}(D^{*}_{\rho}(X,H_{X})^{\Gamma})
and
K_{\star}(D^{*}_{\rho}(Y,H_{Y})^{\Gamma})
. Let us consider, indeed, a projection (or a unitary)
T
in
D^{*}_{\rho}(X,H_{X})^{\Gamma}
and consider two isometries
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
that uniformly cover
f
.
Consider the matrices

\mathcal{V}_{1}:=\begin{bmatrix}V_{1}TV_{1}^{*}&&0\\
0&&0\end{bmatrix}\mbox{ and }\mathcal{V}_{2}:=\begin{bmatrix}0&&0\\
0&&V_{2}TV_{2}^{*}\end{bmatrix}

(2.35)

if
T
is a projection and

\mathcal{V}_{1}:=\begin{bmatrix}V_{1}TV_{1}^{*}&&0\\
0&&1\end{bmatrix}\mbox{ and }\mathcal{V}_{2}:=\begin{bmatrix}1&&0\\
0&&V_{2}TV_{2}^{*}\end{bmatrix}

(2.36)

if
T
is a unitary.
Observe that if we define the matrix

C:=\begin{bmatrix}0&&V_{1}V_{2}^{*}\\
V_{2}V_{1}^{*}&&0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&&1\\
1&&0\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}V_{1}V_{2}^{*}&&0\\
0&&V_{2}V_{1}^{*}\end{bmatrix}

(2.37)

then, we have that

\mathcal{V}_{1}=C\mathcal{V}_{2}C^{*}

(2.38)

and each entry of
C
is an isometry of
D^{*}_{\rho}(Y,H_{Y})^{\Gamma}
. As consequence of Lemma 4.1.10 of
[
10
]
, we have that there is a continuous curve of unitary elements connecting
C
and the matrix

\begin{bmatrix}0&&1\\
1&&0\end{bmatrix}.

(2.39)

Let us define the matrix
\mathcal{W}_{2}
as

\mathcal{W}_{2}:=\begin{bmatrix}V_{2}TV_{2}^{*}&&0\\
0&&0\end{bmatrix}

(2.40)

if
T
is a projection and

\mathcal{W}_{2}:=\begin{bmatrix}V_{2}TV_{2}^{*}&&0\\
0&&1\end{bmatrix}

(2.41)

if
T
is a unitary. As consequence of Lemma 4.1.10 there is a continuous curve of projection (or unitary) connecting
\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\mathcal{W}_{2}
.
Then we conclude observing that

[A_{V_{1}}T]=[\mathcal{V}_{1}]=[\mathcal{W}_{2}]=[A_{V_{2}}T].

(2.42)

∎

Definition 2.1.19
.

Consider two coarse spaces
X
and
Y
and let
(H_{X},U_{X},\rho_{X})
and
(H_{Y},U_{Y},\rho_{Y})
be a
(X,\Gamma)
-module and a
(Y,\Gamma)
-module. Suppose that
(H_{X},U_{X},\rho_{X})
and
(H_{Y},U_{Y},\rho_{Y})
are very ample. Then if
f:X\longrightarrow Y
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant continuous coarse map, then

f_{\star}:K_{n}(C^{*}(X)^{\Gamma})\longrightarrow K_{n}(C^{*}(Y)^{\Gamma})

(2.43)

is defined as the morphism induced by
Ad_{V}
in K-Theory. We will use the same notation to denote the maps induced by
Ad_{V}
between the K-theory groups of
D^{*}(\cdot)^{\Gamma}
and
\frac{D^{*}(\cdot)^{\Gamma}}{C^{*}(\cdot)^{\Gamma}}
.

Remark 40
.

Because of Example
2.1.6
, we know that if
X
is a coarse metric space or if
X=M\sqcup N
and it has the coarse structure defined in Example
2.1.2
, then they admit a very ample
(X,\Gamma)
-module. Then we have that if
f
is a coarse map between coarse spaces that have one of these two coarse structures, then there is a well-defined map
f_{\star}
in K-theory.

Remark 41
.

If we have two continuous coarse maps
f:X\longrightarrow Y
and
g:Y\longrightarrow Z
, an isometry
V:H_{X}\longrightarrow H_{Y}
which uniformly covers
f
and an isometry
W:H_{Y}\longrightarrow H_{Z}
then
W\circ V
uniformly covers
g\circ f
. This fact implies that

f\longrightarrow f_{\star}

(2.44)

respect the functorial properties.

Consider a coarse map
f:X\longrightarrow Y
between coarse spaces. As proved in Lemma 6.3.11 of
[
10
]
, to induce a map between the coarse algebras it is sufficient that the isometry
V
coarsely covers
f
. Moreover, as we said in Remark
39
, if
f
and
g:X\longrightarrow Y
are close maps, then
V
coarsely covers
f
if and only if it coarsely covers
g
.

We already know, because Exemple
2.1.1
, that if
f
and
g:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
are two lipschitz-homotopy equivalent maps between connected Riemannian manifolds, then they are close. Then we have

f\sim_{\Gamma}g\implies f_{\star}=g_{\star}.

(2.45)

where
f_{\star},g_{\star}:K_{n}(C^{*}(X)^{\Gamma})\longrightarrow K_{n}(C^{*}(Y)^{\Gamma})
.

Then the following Proposition holds.

Proposition 2.1.3
.

Consider
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
two Riemannian manifolds. If they are lipschitz-homotopy equivalent, then

K_{n}(C^{*}(M)^{\Gamma})\cong K_{n}(C^{*}(N)^{\Gamma}).

(2.46)

2.1.4
Roe Index of the signature operator

Consider
(M,g)
a connected, oriented and complete Riemannian manifold.

Definition 2.1.20
.

Let us denote by
d
the closed extension in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
of exterior derivative operator. The
signature operator

D_{M}:dom(d)\cap dom(d^{*})\subset\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
is the operator defined as

D_{M}:=d+d^{*}=d-\tau d\tau.

(2.47)

if
dim(M)
is even and as

D_{M}:=\tau d+d\tau

(2.48)

if
dim(M)
is odd.

Fix a group
\Gamma
of isometries on
(M,g)
. Our goal, in this subsection, is to define a class in
K_{\star}(C^{*}(M)^{\Gamma})
related to
D_{M}
. We will call this class the
Roe index
of
D_{M}
.

Observe that
D_{M}
is an unbounded, self-adjoint operator. Since all the operators in
C^{*}(M)^{\Gamma}
are bounded, we need to define a bounded operator related to
D_{M}
. In order to do this we need the notion of chopping function.

Definition 2.1.21
.

Let
\chi:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a smooth map. Then
\chi
is a
chopping function
if it is odd,
\lim\limits_{x\to+\infty}\chi=1
and
\lim\limits_{x\to-\infty}\chi=-1
.

Then, since
D_{M}
is selfadjoint, the operator

\chi(D_{M})\in B(\mathcal{L}^{2}(M))

(2.49)

is well-defined. In particular, we have that
\chi(D_{M})
is in
D^{*}(M)^{\Gamma}
(see
[
18
]
).

Let us denote by
C_{0}(\mathbb{R})
the vector space of continuous functions
h:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\pm\infty}h(t)=0
.

Because of Proposition 3.6
[
18
]
, if
h
is a function in
C_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, then

h(D_{M})\in C^{*}(M)^{\Gamma}.

(2.50)

Then, if
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
are two chopping functions, we have that

\chi_{1}-\chi_{2}(D_{M})\in C^{*}(M)^{\Gamma}

(2.51)

This means that, given a chopping function
\chi
, the operator

\chi(D_{M})\in\frac{D^{*}(M)^{\Gamma}}{C^{*}(M)^{\Gamma}}

(2.52)

doesn’t depend on the choice of
\chi
. Moreover, since

\chi^{2}-1\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}),

(2.53)

we also have that
\chi(D_{M})
is an involution of
\frac{D^{*}(M)^{\Gamma}}{C^{*}(M)^{\Gamma}}
.

Let us suppose that
dim(M)
is odd and consider

\frac{1}{2}(\chi(D_{M})+1)\in\frac{D^{\star}(M)^{\Gamma}}{C^{\star}(M)^{\Gamma}}.

(2.54)

This is a projection.

Let us suppose
dim(M)
is even. We have that
D_{M}
anti-commute with the chirality operator
\tau
. Then, considering the orthogonal splitting

\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)=\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{+1})\oplus\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{-1}),

(2.55)

we have that
D_{M}
can be written as

D_{M}=\begin{bmatrix}0&&D_{M-}\\
D_{M+}&&0\end{bmatrix}.

(2.56)

We have that

\chi(D_{M})=\begin{bmatrix}0&&\chi(D_{M})_{-}\\
\chi(D_{M})_{+}&&0\end{bmatrix}.

(2.57)

Since
\chi(M)
is a self-adjoint involution, then it is a unitary operator. The same holds also for
\chi(D_{M})_{+}
and
\chi(D_{M})_{-}
.

Consider
(H_{M},\rho_{M},U_{M})
the
(M,\Gamma)
-module defined in Example
2.1.7
: we have that

H_{M}=...\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{-1})\oplus\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{-1})\oplus\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{+1})\oplus\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{-1})\oplus\mathcal{L}^{2}(V_{-1})...

(2.58)

and, because of Remark
37
, we can see
\chi(D_{M})_{+}
as a bounded operator on
H_{M}
defined for each
\{\alpha_{j}\}
in
H_{M}
as

\beta_{i}=\alpha_{i+1}

(2.59)

if
i\neq-1
and as
\beta_{-1}=\chi(D_{M})_{+}(\alpha_{0})
. Then we can see
\chi(D_{M})_{+}
as an unitary operator in

\frac{D^{*}_{\rho_{M}}(M,H_{M})^{\Gamma}}{C^{*}_{\rho_{M}}(M,H_{M})^{\Gamma}}.

(2.60)

Definition 2.1.22
.

The
fundamental class of
D_{M}
is
[D_{M}]\in K_{n+1}(\frac{D^{\star}(M)^{\Gamma}}{C^{\star}(M)^{\Gamma}})
given by

[D_{M}]:=\begin{cases}[\frac{1}{2}(\chi(D_{M})+1)]\text{if $n$ is odd,}\\
[\chi(D_{M})_{+}]\text{if $n$ is even.}\par\end{cases}

(2.61)

Remark 42
.

The definition of fundamental class in the even case is well-given: we are considering the
(M,\Gamma)
-module defined in Example
2.1.7
(remember that the K-theory groups of Roe algebra, structure algebra and their quotient don’t depend on the Hilbert space or the representation).

Definition 2.1.23
.

The
Roe index
of
D_{M}
is the class

{Ind_{Roe}(D_{M}):=\delta[D_{M}]}

(2.62)

in
K_{n}(C^{*}(M)^{\Gamma})
, where
\delta
the connecting homomorphism in the K-Theory sequence.

Remark 43
.

The definition of Roe index in the even case is coherent with the definition 12.3.5. given in
[
10
]
. Indeed as the authors show in the proof of Proposition 12.3.7., our fundamental class is the image of the Kasparov class
[D]\in K_{p}(M)
under Paschke duality.

Our main goal, in this Chapter is to prove that given
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
two Riemannian manifolds of bounded geometry and given
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence which preserves the orientation, then we have that

f_{\star}[Ind_{Roe}(D_{M})]=Ind_{Roe}(D_{N}).

(2.63)

2.2
Smoothing operators between manifolds of bounded geometry

2.2.1
The operator
y

Let us consider two manifold of bounded geometry
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
. Consider a uniformly proper, lipschitz map
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
. Recall that in Proposition
1.4.1
we introduced an uniformly proper R.-N.-lipschitz submersion
p_{f}:(f^{*}(T^{\delta}N),g_{S})\longrightarrow(N,h)
such that
p_{f}(0_{v_{p}})=f(p)
.

Lemma 2.2.1
.

Let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence between two complete oriented Riemannian manifolds. Let us consider the bundle
1
1

1

We use the numbers
1
and
2
just to distingush the first and the second summands.

f^{*}(TN)_{1}\oplus f^{*}(TN)_{2}\longrightarrow M

(2.64)

and consider
\mathcal{B}\subset f^{*}(TN)_{1}\oplus f^{*}(TN)_{2}
given by

\mathcal{B}=\{(v_{f_{1}(p)},v_{f_{2}(p)})\in f^{*}(TN)_{1}\oplus f^{*}(TN)_{2}||v_{f_{1}(p)}|\leq\delta,|v_{f_{2}(p)}|\leq\delta\}.

(2.65)

Let us define on
f^{*}(TN)_{1}\oplus f^{*}(TN)_{2}
the Sasaki metric
g_{S}
induced by the metric
g
, the bundle metric
f^{*}h_{1}\oplus f^{*}h_{2}
and the connection
f^{*}\nabla^{N,LC}_{1}\oplus\nabla^{N,LC}_{2}
. Then we consider on
\mathcal{B}
the metric induced by
g_{S}
.

Consider for
i=1,2
the projections

pr_{i}:\mathcal{B}\longrightarrow f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)_{i}

(2.66)

and let
p_{f,i}
be the maps
p_{f,i}=p_{f}\circ pr_{i}
. Then
p_{f,i}^{*}
induce a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded map and there is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator
y_{0}
such that

p_{f,1}^{*}-p_{f,2}^{*}=dy_{0}+y_{0}d.

(2.67)

for all smooth forms in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
.

Proof.

We can start observing that

p_{f,i}^{*}=pr_{i}^{*}\circ p_{f}^{*}.

(2.68)

It’s easy to check that
pr_{i}
is a lipschitz submersion with Fiber Volume equal to the volume of a
\delta
-euclidean ball in each point
(p,t)
. Then it is a R.-N.-lipschitz map. Moreover, applying the Remark
3
of the previous Chapter, we can prove that
f
is uniformly proper lipschitz map and so
p_{f}
is R.-N.-lipschitz. Then since composition of R.-N.-lipschitz maps is R.-N.-lipschitz, we can conclude that
p_{f,i}^{*}
is R.-N.-lipschitz and so its pullback is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator.

To prove the second point we need a lipschitz-homotopy
H
between
p_{f,0}
and
p_{f,1}
such that
H^{*}
is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-continuous map:then the assertion will be proved considering

y_{0}:=\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}\circ H^{*}.

(2.69)

Let us define the map
a:B^{\delta}_{1}\times B^{\delta}_{2}\times[0,1]\longrightarrow B^{\delta}
as

a(t_{1},t_{2},s)=t_{1}(1-s)+st_{2}.

(2.70)

where
B^{\delta}
and
B^{\delta}_{i}
are euclidean balls of radius
\delta
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Now, since
B^{\delta}
and
[0,1]
are compact spaces, we have that
a
is a lipschitz surjective submersion with bounded Fiber Volume. Indeed we know that the Fiber Volume of a submersion is continuous on the image of the submersion (it is a consequence of Proposition
1.3.12
). Then
a
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map.

Let us define the map
A:\mathcal{B}\times[0,1]\longrightarrow f^{*}T^{\delta}N
as

A(v_{f(p),1},v_{f(p),2},s):=v_{f(p),1}\cdot(1-s)+s\cdot v_{f(p),2}

(2.71)

and consider the homotopy
H:\mathcal{B}\times[0,1]\longrightarrow N
defined as

H(v_{f(p),1},v_{f(p),2},s)=p_{f}\circ A(v_{f(p),1},v_{f(p),2},s).

(2.72)

One can easily prove that
A
is a lipschitz map and the Fiber Volume of
A
has the same bound of the Fiber Volume of
a
. Then
H
is an R.N.-lipschitz map because it is composition of R.-N.-lipschitz maps (Proposition
1.3.6
). This means that
H^{*}
is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator. So, in particular, since
\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}
is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator (Lemma
1.5.12
), also the composition

y_{0}:=\int_{0\mathcal{L}}^{1}\circ H^{*}

(2.73)

is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator.

Observe that
H^{*}(\Omega_{c}^{*}(N))\subset(\Omega^{*}(f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)_{1}\oplus f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)_{2}\times[0,1]))
. Since
d
and
H^{*}
commute for all smooth forms in
\mathcal{L}^{2}
, we can apply Proposition
1.3.2
and so the formula

p_{f,1}^{*}\alpha-p_{f,2}^{*}\alpha=dy_{0}\alpha+y_{0}d\alpha.

(2.74)

holds for every
\alpha
in
dom(d)
.
∎

Lemma 2.2.2
.

Consider an oriented manifold
(N,h)
of bounded geometry. Consider
p_{id}:TN\longrightarrow N
the submersion related to the identity map defined in Proposition
1.4.1
. Consider a Thom form
\omega
of the bundle
\pi:TN\longrightarrow N
, where
\pi(v_{p})=p
, such that
supp(\omega)\subset T^{\delta}N
. Then for all
q
in
N
we have that

\int_{F_{q}}\omega=1

(2.75)

where
F_{q}
is the fiber of the submersion
p_{id}:T^{\delta}N\longrightarrow N
defined in Proposition
1.4.1
.

Proof.

For all
q
in
N
the fiber
F_{q}
is an oriented compact submanifold with boundary. The same also holds for
B^{\delta}_{q}
which is the fiber of the projection
\pi:T^{\delta}N\longrightarrow N
defined as
\pi(v_{q}):=q
.

Let us consider the map
H:T^{\delta}N\times[0,1]\longrightarrow N
defined as

H(v_{p},s)=p_{id}(s\cdot v_{p}).

(2.76)

Since
H
is a proper submersion, we have that the fiber along
H
given by
F_{H,q}
is submanifold of
T^{\delta}N\times[0,1]
. Its boundary, in particular is

\partial F_{H,q}=B^{\delta}_{q}\times\{0\}\sqcup F_{q}\times\{1\}\cup A

(2.77)

where
A
is contained in

S^{\delta}N:=\{v_{p}\in TN||v_{p}|=\delta\}.

(2.78)

Then we have that, if
\omega
is a Thom form of
TN
whose support is contained in
T^{\delta}N
, then

0=\int_{F_{H_{q}}}d\omega=d\int_{F_{H_{q}}}\omega+\int_{\partial F_{H_{q}}}\omega.

(2.79)

We can observe that
\omega
is a
k
-form and
dim(F_{H_{q}})=k+1
. Then the first integral on the right side of
2.79
is
0
. Moreover, we can observe that
\omega
is null on
A
, and so

\int_{A}\omega=0.

(2.80)

Then, since
2.79
, we have that

0=\mp\int_{B^{\delta}}\omega\pm\int_{F_{q}}\omega.

(2.81)

and we conclude.
∎

Proposition 2.2.3
.

Let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)

such that
2
2

2

We have that if

\mathcal{L}^{2}

.

•

y(dom(d_{min}))\subseteq dom(d_{min})
,

•

on

1-T^{\dagger}_{f}T_{f}=dy+yd,

(2.82)

•

Proof.

Let us consider two smooth

-forms

-smooth

Using that
(T_{f}^{\dagger})^{*}=(\tau T_{f}^{*}\tau)^{*}
and that
\tau
is self-adjoint, we obtain

\begin{split}\langle T^{\dagger}_{f}T_{f}\alpha,\beta\rangle&=\langle T_{f}\alpha,\tau T_{f}\tau\beta\rangle\\
&=\int_{M}(\int_{B^{\delta}_{2}}p_{f,2}^{*}\alpha\wedge\omega_{2})\wedge(\int_{B^{\delta}_{1}}p_{f,1}^{*}\tau\beta\wedge\omega_{1})\\
&=\int_{f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)_{1}}(\int_{B^{\delta}_{2}}p_{f,2}^{*}\alpha\wedge\omega_{2})\wedge p_{f,1}^{*}\tau\beta\wedge\omega_{1}\\
&=(-1)^{(n+j)j}\int_{f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)_{1}}p_{f,1}^{*}\tau\beta\wedge\omega_{1}\wedge(\int_{B^{\delta}_{2}}p_{f,2}^{*}\alpha\wedge\omega_{2})\\
&=(-1)^{(n+j)j}\int_{\mathcal{B}}p_{f,1}^{*}\tau\beta\wedge\omega_{1}\wedge p_{f,2}^{*}\alpha\wedge\omega_{2}\\
&=(-1)^{(n+j)j}(-1)^{(n+j)j}\int_{\mathcal{B}}p_{f,2}^{*}\alpha\wedge p_{f,1}^{*}\tau\beta\wedge\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}\\
&=\int_{\mathcal{B}}p_{f,2}^{*}\alpha\wedge p_{f,1}^{*}\tau\beta\wedge\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}\end{split}

(2.83)

Consider the identity we proved in Lemma
2.2.1

p_{f,2}^{*}=p_{f,1}^{*}+dy_{0}+y_{0}d.

(2.84)

We obtain

\begin{split}\langle T^{\dagger}_{f}T_{f}\alpha,\beta\rangle&=\int_{f^{*}(TN)_{1}}p_{f_{1}}^{*}(\alpha\wedge\tau\beta)\wedge\omega_{1}\\
&+\int_{\mathcal{B}}(dy_{0}+y_{0}d)\alpha\wedge\omega_{1}\wedge p_{f,2}^{*}\tau\beta\wedge\omega_{2}.\end{split}

(2.85)

Since

is a top-degree form, then its closed and, in particular, the first integral can be written as

\begin{split}\int_{f^{*}(TN)_{1}}p_{f_{1}}^{*}(\alpha\wedge\tau\beta)\wedge\omega_{1}&=\int_{f^{*}(TN)_{1}}(f,id_{B^{k}})^{*}\circ p_{id}^{*}(\alpha\wedge\tau\beta)\wedge\omega_{1}\\
&=\int_{TN}p_{id}^{*}(\alpha\wedge\tau\beta)\wedge\omega_{1}\\
&=\int_{N}(\int_{F}\omega_{1})\alpha\wedge\tau\beta\\
&=\int_{N}\alpha\wedge\tau\beta\\
&=\langle 1(\alpha),\beta\rangle,\end{split}

(2.86)

where

.

Consider the second integral: let us denote by

&=\langle(d\circ Y\alpha+Y\circ d)\alpha,\beta\rangle\end{split}

\begin{split}Y:&=(-1)^{n(n-deg(\cdot))}p_{f,2\star}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}\circ pr_{1,\star}\circ e_{\omega_{1}}\circ y_{0}\\

, we conclude that

Consider now
y:=\frac{Y+Y^{\dagger}}{2}
. Then we have that
y
is an
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded operator. Observe that since

, on

satisfies the equation (
2.82
), then also

satisfies it. Moreover we have that
[-1+dy+yd]\alpha
is a
j
-form if

is a
j
-form.

Let us assume that
\alpha

-form and

is a

, we have that

\langle T_{f}^{\dagger}T_{f}\alpha,\beta\rangle=\langle(-1+dy+yd)(\alpha),\beta\rangle=0.

(2.89)

Now we just have to show that
y(dom(d_{min}))\subset dom(d_{min})
: using this fact the proof of the equation (
) for every
\alpha
in

will be immediate.

First we observe that if
\beta
is a differential form in

, then

is in
\Omega^{*}_{c}(M)
. Indeed if
K_{\beta}
is the compact support of

and

is the projection on the first component, then

supp(\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}\circ H^{*}\beta)\subseteq pr_{M\times B^{k}_{1}\times B^{k}_{2}}(H^{-1}(K_{\beta}))=:K_{\beta}^{\prime}

(2.90)

which is compact since
H
is proper. Then, if
pr_{2}:\mathcal{B}\longrightarrow f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)_{2}
is the projection on the second component,

supp(pr_{1\star}\circ e_{\omega_{1}}\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}\circ H^{*}\beta)\subseteq pr_{2}(K_{\beta}^{\prime})=:K_{\beta}^{\prime\prime}

(2.91)

which is compact because

(2.92)

which is compact because

is closed and

is continuous. This means that

is a closed subset of a compact set and so it is compact. We know that

is smooth because

is a composition of operators which preserve the smoothness of differential forms.

Moreover if

, then we also have that

is in

. Indeed we have that

Y^{\dagger}\beta=\pm(-1)^{n(n-deg(\beta))}H_{\star}\circ pr_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}\circ e_{\omega_{1}}\circ pr_{2}^{*}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}\circ p_{f,2}^{*}\beta

Then we can prove, as we did for

Consider

. Then we have, for every

Y\alpha=Y\lim\limits_{j\rightarrow+\infty}\alpha_{j}=\lim\limits_{j\rightarrow+\infty}Y\alpha_{j}

and

&=-Yd\alpha+\alpha+T_{f}^{\dagger}T_{f}\alpha\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\times B^{k}_{1}\times B^{k}_{2}).\end{split}

(2.96)

and so we have
Y(dom(d_{min})\subseteq dom(d_{min})
. Using exactly the same argument one can easily check that

and
y(dom(d_{min})\subseteq dom(d_{min})
.
Finally if in the equation (

) for all the elements of
