Lemma 2.2.4
.

Let
(M,g)
be an orientable Riemannian manifold and let
\Gamma
be a group of isometries which preserve the orientation. Let
A
un operator on
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
. If
A
commute with
\Gamma
, then

A^{*}\Gamma=\Gamma A^{*}

(2.97)

and

A^{\dagger}\Gamma=\Gamma A^{\dagger}.

(2.98)

Proof.

Let
\gamma
be an element of
\Gamma
. Then, since
\gamma
is an isometry which preserves the orientation, we have that

\gamma^{-1}=\gamma^{*}=\gamma^{\dagger}

(2.99)

Then we have that

A^{*}\gamma=(\gamma^{*}A)^{*}=(\gamma^{-1}A)^{*}=(A\gamma^{-1})^{*}=(\gamma^{-1})^{*}A^{*}=\gamma A^{*}

(2.100)

and

A^{\dagger}\gamma=(\gamma^{\dagger}A)^{\dagger}=(\gamma^{-1}A)^{\dagger}=(A\gamma^{-1})^{\dagger}=(\gamma^{-1})^{\dagger}A^{\dagger}=\gamma A^{\dagger}.

(2.101)

∎

Corollary 2.2.5
.

If there exists a group
\Gamma
of isometries which commute with
f
, then also
y
commute with
\gamma
for all
\gamma
in
\Gamma
.

Proof.

One can easily check that the operator
Y
commute with
\Gamma
: the previous lemma allows us to conclude.
∎

2.2.2
T_{f}
as smoothing operator

Proposition 2.2.6
.

Consider
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence between manifolds of bounded geometry. Then the operator
T_{f}
is a smoothing operator.

Proof.

We divide the proof in two steps: in the first one we will show that for all smooth forms
\alpha
in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
we have that

T_{f}\alpha(p)=\int_{N}K(p,q)\alpha(q)d\mu_{N}

(2.102)

with
K(p,q)\in\Lambda^{*}(M)\boxtimes\Lambda(N)_{(p,q)}
. In the second one we will prove that the section
K
is smooth.

1.
The proof of the first step is similar to the proof given by Vito Zenobi in his Ph.D. thesis
[
24
]
.

Let us denote the following maps as follows

•

p_{f}:f^{*}T^{\delta}N\longrightarrow N
the submersion related to
f
,

•

t_{f}:f^{*}T^{\delta}N\longrightarrow M\times N
is the map
t:=(\pi,p_{f})
defined in Lemma
1.5.1
,

•

\pi:f^{*}TN\longrightarrow M
is the projection of the bundle
f^{*}TN
,

•

pr_{M}
and
pr_{N}
the projections of
M\times N
over
M
and
N
.

We already proved that
t_{f}
is a diffeomorphism on its image, so, in particular, it is a submersion and the integration along the fibers of
t_{f}
is the pullback along
t_{f}^{-1}:im(t_{f})\longrightarrow f^{*}T^{\delta}N
.

Observe that

p_{f}=pr_{N}\circ t_{f}

(2.103)

and

\pi=pr_{M}\circ t_{f}.

(2.104)

Consider a smooth form
\alpha
in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
. Applying the Projection Formula, we obtain that

\begin{split}T_{f}\alpha&=\pi_{\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ p_{f}^{*}\alpha\\
&=pr_{M,\star}\circ t_{f,\star}\circ e_{\omega}\circ t_{f}^{*}\circ pr_{N}^{*}\alpha\\
&=pr_{M,\star}\circ e_{t_{f,\star}\omega}\circ pr_{N}^{*}\alpha,\end{split}

(2.105)

where
t_{f,\star}\omega
is the form
t_{f}^{-1}\omega
on
im(t_{f})\subset M\times N
and
0
in
im(t_{f})^{c}
. Since
supp(\omega)
is contained in
f^{*}T^{\delta_{1}}N
where
\delta_{1}<\delta
, then
t_{f,\star}\omega
is a well-defined, smooth form.

Observe that the Hodge star operator
\star_{N}
on
N
induces a bundle endomorphism on
\Lambda^{*}(M\times N)
imposing that, for each couple of orthonormal frames
\{W^{i}\}
of
\Lambda^{*}M
and
\{\epsilon^{j}\}
of
\Lambda^{*}N
, we have

\star_{N}(pr_{M}^{*}W^{I}\wedge pr_{N}^{*}\epsilon^{J}(p,q)):=pr_{M}^{*}W^{I}\wedge(\star_{N}\epsilon^{J})(p,q)

(2.106)

Consider
\Lambda^{\star,k}(M\times N)
the subbundle of
\Lambda^{*}(M\times N)
given by the forms which are
k
-forms with respect to
N
. Observe that

\Lambda^{*}(M\times N)=\bigoplus\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\Lambda^{\star,k}(M\times N).

(2.107)

Let us denote by
n
the dimension of
N
. We can define the bundle morphism

B:\Lambda^{*}(M\times N)\longrightarrow\Lambda^{\star,0}(M\times N)=pr_{M}^{*}(\Lambda^{*}M)

(2.108)

which is

B\alpha_{(p,q)}:=\star_{N}\alpha_{(p,q)}

(2.109)

if
\alpha_{(p,q)}\in\Lambda^{\star,n}(M\times N)_{(p,q)}
and it is zero if
\alpha_{(p,q)}
is in
\Lambda^{\star,q}(M\times N)_{(p,q)}
where
q\neq n
.

Then we have that for each

\begin{split}T_{f}\alpha(p)&=pr_{M,\star}\circ e_{t_{f,\star}\omega}\circ pr_{N}^{*}\alpha(p)\\
&=\int_{N}B\circ e_{t_{f,\star}\omega}(\alpha)(p,q)d\mu_{N}\end{split}

(2.110)

Let us define, for each
(p,q)
in
M\times N
the operators

\begin{split}E_{\omega,p,q}:pr_{N}^{*}(\Lambda^{*}N)_{(p,q)}&\longrightarrow\Lambda^{*}(M\times N)_{(p,q)}\\
\beta_{(p,q)}&\longrightarrow\beta_{(p,q)}\wedge t_{f,\star}\omega(p,q)\end{split}

(2.111)

and

\begin{split}B_{p,q}:\Lambda^{*}(M\times N)_{(p,q)}&\longrightarrow pr_{M}^{*}(\Lambda^{*}M)_{(p,q)}\\
\gamma_{(p,q)}&\longrightarrow B\gamma_{(p,q)}.\end{split}

(2.112)

Then the equation (
2.110
) can be read as

\begin{split}T_{f}\alpha(p)&=\int_{N}(B_{p,q}\circ E_{\omega,p,q})pr_{N}^{*}\alpha(q)d\mu_{N}\\
\int_{N}K(p,q)\alpha(q)d\mu_{N},\end{split}

(2.113)

where

K(p,q):=B_{p,q}\circ E_{\omega,p,q}:pr_{N}^{*}(\Lambda^{*}N)_{(p,q)}\longrightarrow pr_{M}^{*}(\Lambda^{*}M)_{(p,q)}

(2.114)

is, for each
(p,q)
in
M\times N
an element of
\Lambda^{*}M\boxtimes\Lambda TN_{(p,q)}
.

2.

To prove that
K
is a smooth section of
\Lambda^{*}M\boxtimes\Lambda N
it is sufficient to show that, in local coordinates
\{x^{i},y^{j}\}
, the kernel
K
has the form

K(x,y)=K^{J}_{I}(x,y)dx^{I}\boxtimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{J}}

(2.115)

where
K^{J}_{I}
are smooth functions.

Let us fix some normal coordinates
\{x,y\}
on
M\times N
. We have that

t_{f,\star}\omega(x,y)=\beta_{IR}(x,y)dx^{I}\wedge dy^{R}

(2.116)

for some smooth functions
\beta_{IR}
and that
\alpha
has the form

\alpha(y)=\alpha_{J}(y)dy^{J}.

(2.117)

Let us denote by
\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{I}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i_{1}}}\wedge...\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i_{k}}}
, the dual of
dx^{I}
.
Let
J^{c}
be the multindex such that, up to double switches,
(J,J^{c})
is the index
(1,...,n)
. We have that

\begin{split}K^{J}_{I}(x,y)&=K(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{I}},dy^{J})\\
&=[B_{x,y}(\beta_{LR}(x,y)dx^{L}\wedge dy^{R}\wedge dy^{J})](\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{I}})\\
&=[\beta_{LJ^{c}}(x,y)dx^{L}](\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{I}})\\
&=\beta_{IJ^{c}}(x,y).\end{split}

(2.118)

This fact implies that if
K
in local coordinates has the form

K(x,y)=\beta_{IJ^{c}}(x,y)dx^{I}\boxtimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{J}}

(2.119)

and so the kernel
K
is smooth.
∎

2.2.3
Some operators in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}

Proposition 2.2.7
.

The operator
T_{f}
is in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

One can observe that

supp(T_{f})\subseteq\{(x,x^{\prime})\in M\times N|B^{\delta}_{x}\cap p_{f}^{-1}(x^{\prime})\neq\emptyset\}

(2.120)

where
B^{\delta}_{x}
is the fiber of
\pi:f^{*}TN\longrightarrow M
.

So, if
\psi
and
\phi
are two compactly supported functions on
N
and
M
respectively we have that

supp(\psi T_{f}\phi)\subseteq\pi^{-1}(supp(\psi))\cap p_{f}^{-1}(supp(\phi)).

(2.121)

And so

d(\pi^{-1}(supp(\psi)),p_{f}^{-1}(supp(\phi)))>0\implies\psi T_{f}\phi=0.

(2.122)

Now, if
t\in p_{f}(\pi^{-1}(supp(\psi)))
then
t=p_{f}(s,q)
for some
(s,q)
. Then we have

d(t,f(supp(\psi)))\leq d(p_{f}(s,q),p_{f}(s,0))\leq C_{p_{f}}d((s,q),(s,0))\leq C_{p_{f}}.

(2.123)

Let
R>C_{p_{f}}
. We have that

\begin{split}&d(f(supp(\psi)),supp(\phi))>R\implies\\
&d(p_{f}(\pi^{-1}(supp(\psi))),supp(\phi))>0\implies\\
&d(\pi^{-1}(supp(\psi)),p_{f}^{-1}supp(\phi))>0\implies\\
&\psi T_{f}\phi=0.\end{split}

(2.124)

It means that
T_{f}
has propagation less or equal to
C_{p_{f}}
. Moreover if
\phi
has compact support then
T_{f}\phi
and
\phi T_{f}
are integral operator with smooth, compactly supported kernel. Then, in particular they are compact.

Since the
\Gamma
-invariance of
T_{f}
it follows that
T_{f}
is in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.
∎

Corollary 2.2.8
.

Since
T_{f}
is in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
, then also
T_{f}^{\dagger}
,
\tau T_{f}\tau
and
\tau T_{f}^{\dagger}\tau
are in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to observe that
\tau
is in
D_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
and that, since
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
is a
C^{*}
-algebra, if
T_{f}
is in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
then
T_{f}^{*}
is in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.
∎

Proposition 2.2.9
.

The operator
y
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant operator with finite propagation. Moreover the operators
T_{f}y
,
yT_{f}
,
T_{f}^{\dagger}y

yT_{f}^{\dagger}
are all operators in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

Let us consider the operator
Y
in Lemma
2.2.2
. If we show that
Y
is an operator with finite propagation, then one can easily check that the same holds for
Y^{\dagger}
and for
y:=\frac{Y+Y^{\dagger}}{2}
.

First, we have to observe that, up to signs,

Y:=p_{f,2\star}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}\circ pr_{1,\star}\circ e_{\omega_{1}}\circ\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}\circ H^{*}.

(2.125)

where
H
is the homotopy defined in Lemma
2.2.1
. Then its pullbcak is given by

H^{*}=A^{*}\circ pr_{1}^{*}\circ p_{f,2}^{*}.

(2.126)

Let
\alpha
be a compactly supported differential forms on
N
.

Since the formula in Remark
21
, we have that

supp(p_{f,2}^{*}\alpha)=p_{f,2}^{-1}(supp(\alpha))\subseteq\pi_{2}^{-1}(f^{-1}(B_{1}(supp(\alpha)))),

(2.127)

where
\pi_{2}:f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)_{2}\longrightarrow M
is the projection of the fiber bundle and where
B_{1}(supp(\alpha))
is the
1
-neighborhood of
supp(\alpha)
.

This means that

supp(pr_{1,\star}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}\circ e_{\omega_{1}}\circ\int_{0\mathcal{L}}\circ H^{*}(\alpha))\subseteq\pi_{2}^{-1}(f^{-1}(B_{1}(supp(\alpha)))).

(2.128)

and, in particular

supp(Y(\alpha))\subseteq p_{f,2}(\pi_{2}^{-1}(f^{-1}(B_{1}(supp(\alpha)))))

(2.129)

Now we can observe that if
(x,t)
is in
\pi_{2}^{-1}(f^{-1}(B_{1}(supp(\alpha))))
, then

d(p_{f}(x,t),B_{1}(supp(\alpha))\leq C_{p_{f}}

(2.130)

where
C_{p_{f}}
is the lipschitz costant of
p_{f}
. Indeed it is sufficient to observe that

p_{f}(x,0)=f(x)\subseteq B_{1}(supp(\alpha))

(2.131)

and

d(p_{f}(x,1),p_{f}(x,0))\leq C_{p_{f}}d((x,0),(x,1))=C_{p_{f}}.

(2.132)

Then we have that if
\phi
and
\psi
are two functions on
N
with

d(supp(\phi),supp(\psi))>C_{p_{f}}+1\implies\phi Y\psi=0.

(2.133)

Now to conclude the proof it is sufficient to show that
T_{f}Y
,
YT_{f}
,
T_{f}^{\dagger}Y
,
YT_{f}^{\dagger}
are all operator in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. We will start by studying
T_{f}Y
.

We know that
Y
and
T_{f}
are bounded operator which have both finite propagation, so also
T_{f}Y
is a bounded operator with finite propagation.

Moreover if
g
is a function on
M\sqcup N
with compact support than we have that
gT_{f}
is a compact operator since
T_{f}
is in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
and son
gT_{f}Y
is compact.

Now let us consider
T_{f}Yg
: now we know that for all
\alpha
we have that

supp(Yg(\alpha))\subseteq B_{1}(supp(g)).

(2.134)

This means that, if
\phi
is a compactly supported function such that
\phi\cong 1
on
B_{1}(supp(g))
, then we have that

T_{f}Yg=T_{f}\phi Yg.

(2.135)

Then we con observe that
T_{f}\phi
is a compact operator. Then also holds for
T_{f}Yg
. This means that
T_{f}Y
is in
C_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. Exactly in the same way one can check that
YT_{f}
,
T_{f}^{\dagger}Y
,
YT_{f}^{\dagger}
are operator in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.
∎

Remark 44
.

Let us consider the kernel
K
of
T_{f}
. Let us fix some local normal coordinates
\{x,y\}
on
M\times N
. Since Proposition
2.2.6
, we know that outside
im(t_{f})
the kernel is identically
0
. Morever, inside
im(t_{f})
, we have that, the kernel is locally given by

K_{J}^{I}(x,y)=\beta_{IJ^{c}}(x,y),

(2.136)

where the functions
\beta_{IJ^{c}}(x,y)
are the components of the pullback of the Thom form
\omega
along the map
t_{f}^{-1}:im(t_{f})\subset M\times N\longrightarrow f^{*}TN
.

Let us recall that, respect to some fibered coordinates
\{x^{i},y^{j}\}
on
f^{*}T^{\delta}N
, the components of
\omega
are algebraic combinations of pullback of components of the metric
h
on
N
and of Christoffell symbols of the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{E}
along the map
f
and derivatives of
f
(see subsection
1.188
). This means that if
f
is a
C^{k}_{b}
-map the local components of
\omega
and their derivatives of order
k
are uniformly bounded only .

Moreover we also know, beacuse of Proposition
1.4.1
, that if
f
is a
C^{k}_{b}
-map then also
p_{f}
and, in particular,
t_{f}
have uniformly bounded derivatives of order
0,1...,k
.

Let us suppose that
t_{f}
has bounded derivatives of order
k
. Then we know that
t_{f}
is a diffeomorphism with its image. This fact, using also the inverse function Theorem, implies that
t_{f}^{-1}
(up to consider
\delta
small enough) has bounded derivatives of order
0,1,...k
.

The bounds on the components of
\omega
and on the derivatives of
t_{f}^{-1}
imply that if
f
is a
C^{k}_{b}
map, then the components of the kernel
K
of
T_{f}
in normal coordinates have bounded derivatives of order
0,1,...k
.

Proposition 2.2.10
.

Let
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
be two Riemannian manifolds of bounded geometry. Let
f:M\longrightarrow N
be a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence. Then we have that
T_{f}
and
dT_{f}=T_{f}d
have uniformly bounded support (definition in Appendix
D
). Moreover if
f
is a
C^{2}_{b}
-map, then
dT_{f}
is a bounded operator.

Proof.

We will first show that
T_{f}
has uniformly bounded supported kernel
K
. Since
supp(d_{M}K)\subseteq supp(K)
it will follow immediately that also
dT_{f}
has uniformly bounded supported kernel.

We know, since Proposition
2.2.6
that
K(p,q)=0
if
(p,q)
is not in
im(t_{f})
, where
t_{f}=(\pi,p_{f})
.

Fix
p
, a point of
M
. We can observe that if
q
is not in
p_{f}(B^{\delta}_{p})
, then
(p,q)\notin im(t_{f})
and so
K(p,q)=0
. We know, since
p_{f}
is lipschitz and
diam(B^{\delta})=2\delta
, that

diam(supp(K(p,\cdot))\leq 2\delta C_{p_{f}}.

(2.137)

Fix now
q
in
N
. We have that, becuse of Remark
21
, that

\pi(p_{f}^{-1}(q))\subseteq B_{1}(f^{-1}(q))

(2.138)

and if
p
is not in
B_{1}(f^{-1}(q))
then
K(p,q)=0
. Then, since
f
is a lipshitz-homotopy equivalence, then in particular we have that
f
is a uniformly proper map. This means that

diam(f^{-1}(A))\leq\alpha(diam(A))

(2.139)

for a continuous function
\alpha:[0,+\infty]\longrightarrow[0,+\infty)
. Then we have that

diam(supp(K(\cdot,q))\leq\alpha(0)+1.

(2.140)

To conclude the proof we want to apply Proposition
D.0.2
. In order to apply this Proposition we have to show that

|\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}K^{I}_{J}(x,y)|\leq C.

(2.141)

Because of Remark
44
and Remark
62
in Appendix
D
this fact imediatly follows if
f
is a
C^{2}_{b}
-map.
∎

Corollary 2.2.11
.

The operators
dT_{f}=T_{f}d
,
T_{f}^{\dagger}d=dT_{f}^{\dagger}
,
T_{f}yd
,
dyT_{f}
,
T_{f}^{\dagger}yd

dyT_{f}^{\dagger}
are all operators in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

Since
dT_{f}=T_{f}d
is a bounded integral operator with
supp(dT_{f})\subseteq supp(T_{f})
then
dT_{f}
has finite propagation. Moreover, since it has finite propagation we have that for all
g
in
C_{c}(M\sqcup N)
then
dT_{f}g
and
gdT_{f}
are smoothing operator with compact support and so are compact operators (Appendix
D
).

We can observe that

T_{f}^{\dagger}d=-T_{f}^{\dagger}d^{\dagger}=-(dT_{f})^{\dagger}

(2.142)

and since
dT_{f}
is in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
we have that the same holds for
T_{f}^{\dagger}d=dT_{f}^{\dagger}
.

Finally we can observe that

\begin{split}T_{f}yd&=T_{f}(dy-1-T_{f}^{\dagger}T_{f})\\
&=(T_{f}d)y-T_{f}-T_{f}T_{f}^{\dagger}T_{f}\end{split}

(2.143)

which is a combination of operators in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. All the other combinations are in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
for the same argument.
∎

2.3
Lipschitz-homotopy invariance of the Roe Index

2.3.1
The perturbed signature operator

Let us consider now two orientable, connected, Riemannian manifolds
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
.
We will consider
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\bigsqcup N)\cong\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\oplus\mathcal{L}^{2}(-M)
, where
-M
is the manifold
M
with the opposite orientation.

Definition 2.3.1
.

We will denote with
d_{M\sqcup N}
the operator

d_{M\sqcup N}:=\begin{bmatrix}d_{N}&&0\\
0&&-d_{M}\end{bmatrix}

(2.144)

if the dimensions of
M
and
N
are even, and the operator

d_{M\sqcup N}:=i\begin{bmatrix}d_{N}&&0\\
0&&-d_{M}\end{bmatrix}.

(2.145)

if the dimensions are odd.

Moreover we will say

Definition 2.3.2
.

Signature operator
the operator

\mathfrak{D}_{M\sqcup N}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathfrak{D}_{N}&&0\\
0&&-\mathfrak{D}_{M}\end{bmatrix}

(2.146)

In general, the signature operator is not
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-invertible: our next step is to define a perturbation
A
such that
\mathfrak{D}_{M\sqcup N}+A
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-invertible. To do this we will follow the construction given in
[
23
]
.

Let us introduce
\gamma:\mathcal{L}^{2}(\Omega^{*}(M\bigsqcup N))\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(\Omega^{*}(M\bigsqcup N))
the operator defined for all
\alpha
as
\gamma(\alpha):=(-1)^{|\alpha|}
. One can easily check that
\gamma^{\dagger}=-\gamma
.
Moreover
\gamma
commute with
\tau
and with
d_{M\sqcup N}
.

Let us define, now

R_{\beta}:=\begin{bmatrix}1&&0\\
\beta T_{f}&&1\end{bmatrix}.

(2.147)

We have that
R_{\beta}
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-invertible: the inverse is given by

\begin{bmatrix}1&&0\\
-\beta T_{f}\gamma&&1\end{bmatrix}.

(2.148)

We will denote by
\tau
the operator

\tau:=\begin{bmatrix}\tau_{N}&&0\\
0&&-\tau_{M}\end{bmatrix}.

(2.149)

Consider now
\alpha
a real number: we can define

L_{\alpha,\beta}:=\begin{bmatrix}1-T^{\dotplus}_{f}T_{f}&&\beta(\gamma+\alpha y)T_{f}^{\dotplus}\\
\beta T_{f}(-\gamma-\alpha y)&&1\end{bmatrix}.

(2.150)

Moreover, if the dimension of
M
is even, we will define the operator
\delta_{\alpha}
as follow

\delta_{\alpha}:=\begin{bmatrix}d_{N}&&\alpha iT^{\dagger}_{f}\gamma\\
0&&-d_{M}\end{bmatrix},

(2.151)

in the other case we have

\delta_{\alpha}:=\begin{bmatrix}i\cdot d_{N}&&-\alpha T^{\dagger}_{f}\gamma\\
0&&-i\cdot d_{M}\end{bmatrix},

(2.152)

It’s easy to check that
\delta_{\alpha}^{2}=0
. Since
d_{N}\oplus(-d_{M})
is a closed operator and
T^{\dagger}
is bounded, then we have that
\delta_{\alpha}
is a closed operator with the same domain of
d_{N}-d_{M}
.

It’s possible to observe that
L_{\alpha}d_{M\sqcup N}=d_{M\sqcup N}L_{\alpha}
,
L_{\alpha}\delta_{\alpha}=\pm\delta_{\alpha}^{\dagger}L_{\alpha}
(we have a plus if
dim(M)
is odd, a minus otherwise) and that
L_{\alpha}^{\dagger}=L_{\alpha}
.

Morover we have that
R^{\dagger}_{\beta}R_{\beta}=L_{0}
, so
L_{0}
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-invertible and the same holds for
L_{\alpha}
if
|\alpha|
is small enough.

Let us define now

S_{\alpha}:=\frac{\tau L_{\alpha}}{|\tau L_{\alpha}|}.

(2.153)

Again, if
|\alpha|
is small enough
S_{\alpha}
is invertible (it is in particular an involution). We also have that
S_{\alpha}^{\dotplus}=S_{\alpha}
.

Consider, now
U_{\alpha}:=(|\tau L_{\alpha}|)^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Since
|\tau L_{\alpha}|
is invertible, also
U_{\alpha}
is invertible.

Definition 2.3.3
.

If the dimension od
M
is even, we will call
perturbed signature operator
the operator

D_{\alpha,\beta}:=U_{\alpha,\beta}(\delta_{\alpha}-S_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{\alpha}S_{\alpha,\beta})U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}.

(2.154)

if
M
has dimension odd, we have that

D_{\alpha,\beta}:=-iU_{\alpha,\beta}(S_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{\alpha}+\delta_{\alpha}S_{\alpha,\beta})U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}.

(2.155)

2.3.2
L^{2}
-invertibility of
D_{\alpha,1}

Lemma 2.3.1
.

Let us consider two Riemannian manifolds of bounded geometry
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
and let
f
be a lipschitz-homotopic equivalence. Then we have that
ker(\delta_{\alpha})=im(\delta_{\alpha})
in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)
.

Proof.

Since
T_{f}d=dT_{f}
, we have that
T_{f}
gives a map between the complexes

0\xrightarrow{d_{N}}....\xrightarrow{d_{N}}dom(d_{N})^{k-1}\xrightarrow{d_{N}}dom(d_{N})^{k}\xrightarrow{d_{N}}dom(d_{N})^{k+1}\xrightarrow{d_{N}}...

(2.156)

and

0\xrightarrow{-d_{M}}....\xrightarrow{-d_{M}}dom(d_{M})^{k-1}\xrightarrow{-d_{M}}dom(d_{M})^{k}\xrightarrow{-d_{M}}dom(d_{M})^{k+1}\xrightarrow{-d_{M}}...

(2.157)

In particular, we have that
\delta_{\alpha}
is a mapping cone over these chains. Now, since
T_{f}
is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-cohomology, this means that
\delta_{\alpha}
is acyclic, i.e.

ker(\delta_{\alpha})=im(\delta_{\alpha}).

(2.158)

∎

Proposition 2.3.2
.

The operators

D_{\alpha}:dom(d_{N})\oplus dom(d_{M})\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{2}(\Omega^{*}(M\sqcup N))\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(\Omega^{*}(M\sqcup N))

(2.159)

are injective if
|\alpha|
i small enough.

Proof.

Let us consider the operator

\delta_{\alpha}\pm S_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{\alpha}S_{\alpha,\beta},

(2.160)

where we have a plus if
dim(M)
is odd and a minus otherwise.
If we check that this operator is injective, than also the perturbed signature operator is injective (in both cases: even or odd).

Let us consider on
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)
the scalar product

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}:=\langle\cdot,|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}.

(2.161)

We have that
|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|
is self-adjoint respect to this scalar product indeed given
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
two
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-differential forms, we have that

\begin{split}\langle|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}&=\langle|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\omega_{1},|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\overline{\langle|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\omega_{2},|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\omega_{1}\rangle}_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\overline{\langle|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\omega_{2},\omega_{1}\rangle}_{\alpha,\beta}\\
&=\langle\omega_{1},|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\omega_{2}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}.\end{split}

(2.162)

The operators
\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}
and
S_{\alpha,\beta}
are self-adjoint too, indeed

\begin{split}\langle\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}&=\langle\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{1},|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle\omega_{1},\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle\omega_{1},|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}\\
&=\langle\omega_{1},\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}.\end{split}

(2.163)

and

\begin{split}\langle\omega_{1},S_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}&=\langle\omega_{1},|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|S_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle\omega_{1},\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}||\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|^{-1}\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|S_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle S_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}\\
&=\langle\omega_{2},S_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{1}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}.\end{split}

(2.164)

Moreover, respect to this scalar product
\pm S_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{\alpha}S_{\alpha,\beta}
is the adjoint of
\delta_{\alpha}
, indeed

\begin{split}\langle\delta_{\alpha}\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}&=\langle\delta_{\alpha}\omega_{1},|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle\delta_{\alpha}\omega_{1},|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}||\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}||\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|^{-1}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle\delta_{\alpha}\omega_{1},\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}\frac{\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}}{|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle\omega_{1},\tau\delta_{\alpha}^{\dotplus}\tau\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}\frac{\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}}{|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle\omega_{1},\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}(\pm\delta_{\alpha})\frac{\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}}{|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle\omega_{1},|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\pm\frac{\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}}{|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|}\delta_{\alpha}\frac{\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}}{|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle\omega_{1},\pm\frac{\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}}{|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|}\delta_{\alpha}\frac{\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}}{|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}.\end{split}

(2.165)

We can denote with
\delta_{\alpha}^{*}
the operators
\pm S_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{\alpha}S_{\alpha,\beta}
.

It’s important to observe, now, that

ker(\delta_{\alpha}^{*})=im(\delta_{\alpha})^{\perp}=ker(\delta_{\alpha})

(2.166)

We want to prove the injectivity of
\delta_{\alpha}+\delta_{\alpha}^{*}
studying
(\delta_{\alpha}+\delta_{\alpha}^{*})^{2}=\delta_{\alpha}\delta_{\alpha}^{*}+\delta_{\alpha}^{*}\delta_{\alpha}
.

Let us consider
\xi
in
ker(\delta_{\alpha}\delta_{\alpha}^{*}+\delta_{\alpha}^{*}\delta_{\alpha})
: we have that

\begin{split}0&=\langle\xi,\delta_{\alpha}\delta_{\alpha}^{*}+\delta_{\alpha}^{*}\delta_{\alpha}\xi\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}\\
&=\langle\xi,\delta_{\alpha}\delta_{\alpha}^{*}\xi\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}+\langle\xi,\delta_{\alpha}^{*}\delta_{\alpha})\xi)\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}\\
&=\langle\delta_{\alpha}^{*}\xi,\delta_{\alpha}^{*}\xi\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}+\langle\delta_{\alpha}\xi,\delta_{\alpha}\xi\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}\\
&=||\delta_{\alpha}^{*}\xi||_{\alpha,\beta}+||\delta_{\alpha}\xi||_{\alpha,\beta}.\end{split}

(2.167)

Then we have that
\xi
is in
ker(\delta_{\alpha})\cap ker(\delta_{\alpha}^{*})=0
. Then
\delta_{\alpha}\delta_{\alpha}^{*}+\delta_{\alpha}^{*}\delta_{\alpha}
is injective.

Moreover we also have

\langle\xi,\delta_{\alpha}\delta_{\alpha}^{*}+\delta_{\alpha}^{*}\delta_{\alpha}\xi\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}=\langle\delta_{\alpha}+\delta_{\alpha}^{*}\xi,\delta_{\alpha}+\delta_{\alpha}^{*}\xi\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}.

(2.168)

It means that if
\xi
is in
ker(\delta_{\alpha}\delta_{\alpha}^{*}+\delta_{\alpha}^{*}\delta_{\alpha})
then it is in
ker(\delta_{\alpha}+\delta_{\alpha}^{*})
and so the first kernel is contained in the second one. Moreover the second kernel is contained in the first one beacuse
(\delta_{\alpha}+\delta_{\alpha}^{*})^{2}=\delta_{\alpha}\delta_{\alpha}^{*}+\delta_{\alpha}^{*}\delta_{\alpha}
. So the kernels coinde.

This means that
\delta_{\alpha}+\delta_{\alpha}^{*}
is injective.
∎

Remark 45
.

The operators
D_{\alpha,\beta}
are self-adjoint. Let us denote by
\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
the operator

\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}:=\delta_{\alpha}\pm S_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{\alpha}S_{\alpha,\beta},

(2.169)

where we are considering a plus if
dim(M)
is odd and a minus otherwise.

We have that the even perturbed signature operator is

D_{\alpha,\beta}=U_{\alpha,\beta}\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}U^{-1}_{\alpha,\beta}.

(2.170)

We know that
\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
is a self-adjoint operator respect to the scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}
. Then
D_{\alpha,\beta}
is selfadjoint respect the standard scalar product on
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)
.

\begin{split}\langle U_{\alpha,\beta}\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}U^{-1}_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}&=\langle\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}U^{-1}_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{1},U_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}U^{-1}_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{1},|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle U^{-1}_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{1},\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle\omega_{1},U^{-1}_{\alpha,\beta}|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}\\
&=\langle\omega_{1},U_{\alpha,\beta}\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)}.\end{split}

(2.171)

Let us consider now the odd case. If we consider

\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}=(-i)(S_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{\alpha}+\delta_{\alpha}S_{\alpha,\beta})

(2.172)

we can observe then that

D_{\alpha,\beta}=U_{\alpha,\beta}\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}

(2.173)

Now, if we prove that
\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
is selfadjoint respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}
we can conclude exactly how we did in the even case. We have

\begin{split}\langle(-i)(S_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{\alpha}+\delta_{\alpha}S_{\alpha,\beta})\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}&=(-i)\langle\delta_{\alpha}\omega_{1},S_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}\\
&+(-i)\langle S_{\alpha}\omega_{1},(-S_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{\alpha}S_{\alpha,\beta})\omega_{2}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}\\
&=\langle\omega_{1},-iS_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{\alpha}S_{\alpha,\beta}S_{\alpha,\beta}\omega_{2}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}\\
&+\langle\omega_{1},iS_{\alpha,\beta}(-S_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{\alpha}S_{\alpha,\beta})\omega_{2}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}\\
&=\langle\omega_{1},(-i)(S_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{\alpha}+\delta_{\alpha}S_{\alpha,\beta})\omega_{2}\rangle_{\alpha,\beta}.\end{split}

(2.174)

Proposition 2.3.3
.

The operators
D_{\alpha,\beta}
are
L^{2}
-invertible.

Proof.

Since
U_{\alpha,\beta}
and
S_{\alpha,\beta}
are
L^{2}
-invertible, it is sufficient to proof that

\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}:=\delta_{\alpha}+\delta_{\alpha}^{*}
is invertible.

Since
\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
is self-adjoint, its spectrum can be be decomposed in
essential spectrum
given by the subset of
\lambda
s such that

\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}-\lambda Id

(2.175)

is not a Fredholm operator and in
discrete spectrum
which is the subset given by the eigenvalues with finite multiplicity.

Since
\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
is injective, the zero can’t be in in discrete spectrum.

Now, using the Theorem 2.4. of
[
2
]
we have that, since
\delta_{\alpha}
is acyclic, then zero can’t be in the essential spectrum of
\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{2}
. But the essential spectrum of
\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}^{2}
is the same of
\hat{D}_{\alpha}
. Then
\hat{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
is invertible and the same holds for
D_{\alpha,\beta}
.
∎

2.3.3
The perturbation in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}

Proposition 2.3.4
.

The perturbation
D_{\alpha,\beta}-D
is an operator of
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

We can observe that

\begin{split}D_{\alpha,\beta}-D&=U_{\alpha,\beta}(\delta_{\alpha}-S_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{\alpha}S_{\alpha,\beta})U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}-d+\tau d\tau\\
&=U_{\alpha,\beta}(\delta_{\alpha}-d)U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}+(U_{\alpha,\beta}dU_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}-d)\\
&+U_{\alpha,\beta}(S_{\alpha,\beta}\delta_{\alpha}S_{\alpha,\beta}-\tau d\tau)U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}+(U_{\alpha,\beta}(\tau d\tau)U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}-\tau d\tau).\end{split}

(2.176)

We know that
\delta_{\alpha}-\delta=\alpha T_{f}^{\dagger}
is in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

The first step is to show that

S_{\alpha,\beta}=\tau+H_{\alpha,\beta},

(2.177)

U_{\alpha,\beta}=1+G_{\alpha,\beta}

(2.178)

and

U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}=1+K_{\alpha,\beta}

(2.179)

where
H_{\alpha,\beta}
,
G_{\alpha,\beta}
,
K_{\alpha,\beta}
are operators in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

To prove this first step we can follow Proposition 2.1.11. of
[
24
]
. We can observe that
L_{\alpha,\beta}=1+Q_{\alpha,\beta}
, with
Q_{\alpha,\beta}
in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

This means that

|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|=\sqrt{1+R_{\alpha,\beta}},

(2.180)

where
R_{\alpha,\beta}=2Q_{\alpha,\beta}+Q_{\alpha,\beta}^{2}
in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. Now, let us consider the complex function
g(z):=\sqrt{1+z}-1
. Since
g(0)=0
, we have that

g(z)=az+zh(z)z

(2.181)

where
h
is a holomorphic function and
a
is a number. Then we have that

|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|=1+g(R_{\alpha,\beta})=1+V_{\alpha,\beta}

(2.182)

with
V_{\alpha,\beta}
in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. Now, we can observe that

U_{\alpha,\beta}=\sqrt{|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|}

(2.183)

Applying the same argument we can obtain that

U_{\alpha,\beta}=1+G_{\alpha,\beta}.

(2.184)

where
G_{\alpha,\beta}
is in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

Let us observe the operator

Z_{\alpha,\beta}=|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|^{-1}-1

(2.185)

is an operator of
D_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
since
|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|
is in
D-f^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. In particular we have that

\begin{cases}|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\circ|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|^{-1}=(1+V_{\alpha,\beta})(1+Z_{\alpha,\beta})=1\\
|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|^{-1}\circ|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|=(1+Z_{\alpha,\beta})(1+V_{\alpha,\beta})=1.\end{cases}

(2.186)

and so we obtain that

Z_{\alpha,\beta}=-V_{\alpha,\beta}-V_{\alpha,\beta}Z_{\alpha,\beta}=-V_{\alpha,\beta}-Z_{\alpha,\beta}V_{\alpha,\beta}.

(2.187)

Then we have that
Z_{\alpha,\beta}
is in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

Now, since

U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}=\sqrt{|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|^{-1}}

(2.188)

then we have that

U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}=1+K_{\alpha,\beta}.

(2.189)

where
K_{\alpha,\beta}
is in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.
Moreover we also have that

\begin{split}S_{\alpha,\beta}&=\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta}|^{-1}\\
&=\tau(1+Q_{\alpha,\beta})(1+Z_{\alpha,\beta})\\
&=\tau+H_{\alpha,\beta},\end{split}

(2.190)

where
H_{\alpha,\beta}
in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

We’ve completed the first step. In the second step, we have to proof that
G_{\alpha,\beta}d
,
H_{\alpha,\beta}d
,
K_{\alpha,\beta}d
,
dG_{\alpha,\beta}
,
dH_{\alpha,\beta}
,
dK_{\alpha,\beta}
,
G_{\alpha,\beta}dG_{\alpha,\beta}
and
H_{\alpha,\beta}dK_{\alpha,\beta}
are in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

To prove this we have to observe that
L_{\alpha,\beta}=1+Q_{\alpha,\beta}
where

Q_{\alpha,\beta}=\begin{bmatrix}\beta T_{f}^{\dagger}T_{f}&&(1-i\alpha\gamma y)\beta T_{f}^{\dagger}\\
\beta T_{f}(1+i\alpha\gamma y)&&0\end{bmatrix}

(2.191)

is an algebraic combination of
T_{f}
,
T_{f}^{\dagger}
,
T_{f}y
,
yT_{f}
,
T_{f}^{\dagger}y
,
yT_{f}^{\dagger}
. Then we have that
Q_{\alpha,\beta}d
and
dQ_{\alpha,\beta}
is an operator in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
(Corollary
2.2.11
). The same property, obviously, holds for
R_{\alpha,\beta}
.

Now we can observe that if
A
is an operator in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
such that
Ad
and
dA
are operators in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
then also
g(A)d
and
dg(A)
are in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
, indeed

g(A)d=(aA+Ah(A)A)d=a(Ad)+Ah(A)(Ad)

(2.192)

and

dg(A)=d(aA+Ah(A)A)=a(dA)+(dA)h(A)A.

(2.193)

So we have that the compositions of
V_{\alpha,\beta}
and
G_{\alpha,\beta}
with
d
are operators in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. Moreover, since

Z_{\alpha,\beta}=-V_{\alpha,\beta}-V_{\alpha,\beta}Z_{\alpha,\beta}=-V_{\alpha,\beta}-Z_{\alpha,\beta}V_{\alpha,\beta}

(2.194)

then this property also holds for
Z_{\alpha,\beta}
,
K_{\alpha,\beta}
and
H_{\alpha,\beta}
.
Now, since
D_{\alpha,\beta}-D
is an algebraic combination of operators in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
, the perturbation is in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.
∎

2.3.4
Involutions

One can easily check that the operator
W_{\alpha,\beta}:=U_{\alpha,\beta}S_{\alpha,\beta}U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}
is a well-defined, selfadjoint, involution whenever
\alpha\neq 0
and
\beta=1
or when
\alpha=0
and
\beta\in[0,1]
.

We have that
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)
can be split, for any
\alpha
and
\beta
, in

\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)=V_{+,\alpha,\beta}\oplus V_{-,\alpha,\beta}

(2.195)

where
V_{\pm,\alpha,\beta}
are the
\pm 1
-eigenvalues of
W_{\alpha,\beta}:=U_{\alpha,\beta}S_{\alpha,\beta}U_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}
. Respect to this decomposition we have that

D_{\alpha,\beta}=\begin{bmatrix}0&&D_{\alpha,\beta+}\\
D_{\alpha,\beta-}&&0\end{bmatrix}.

(2.196)

Consider a real value
\alpha_{0}
such that
D_{\alpha_{0},1}
is invertible, we can define
\gamma:[0,1]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
as
\gamma(t):=(\alpha(t),\beta(t))
where

\alpha(t)=\begin{cases}0\text{ if }t\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]\\
2t\alpha_{0}\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}\text{ and }\beta(t)=\begin{cases}2t\text{ if }t\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]\\
1\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

(2.197)

Then we can consider the map
W_{\gamma}:[0,1]\longrightarrow D^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
defined as

W_{\gamma(t)}:=U_{\gamma(t)}S_{\gamma(t)}U_{\gamma(t)}^{-1}.

(2.198)

We want to prove that
W_{\gamma}
is a continuous function and that for all
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
we have that

W_{\gamma(t_{1})}-W_{\gamma(t_{2})}\in C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}.

(2.199)

The statement (
2.199
) can easily checked observing that for all
t
in
[0,1]
we have that
W_{\gamma(t)}-\tau
is in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

To check the continuity of
W_{\gamma(t)}
in
t
we have to observe that for all
t
in
[0,1]
we have that

W_{t}:=\sqrt{|\tau L_{\gamma(t)}|}\circ\frac{\tau L_{\gamma(t)}}{|\tau L_{\gamma(t)}|}\circ\sqrt{|\tau L_{\gamma(t)}|^{-1}}.

(2.200)

Now, we know that
\tau L_{\gamma(t)}
is continuous in
t
and the same holds for its square. Now, we know that

|\tau L_{\gamma(t)}|=1+f(1-\tau L_{\gamma(t)}^{2})

(2.201)

where
f(z)=az+zh(z)z
and
h
is an holomorphic function. Now, since the properties of holomorphic functional calculus on bounded operator, we have that if
T_{k}\rightarrow T
then
f(T_{k})\rightarrow f(T)
. In particular we have that
|\tau L_{\gamma(t)}|
is continuous in
t
. Moreover exactly with the same argument we can see that also
\sqrt{|\tau L_{\gamma(t)}|}
is continuous in
t
.

The operator
|\tau L_{\gamma(t)}|^{-1}
is continuous in
t
because for all
t
the operator
|\tau L_{\gamma(t)}|
is invertible with bounded inverse and the function
z\rightarrow\frac{1}{z}
is holomorphic in every bounded open set of
\mathbb{C}
which doesn’t contains the
0
. Finally also
\sqrt{|\tau L_{\gamma(t)}|^{-1}}
is continuous. Then we have that
W_{t}
is continuous in
t
. Moreover, since
[0,1]
is compact, we have that
W_{t}
is uniformly continuous, i.e. exists
C>0
such that

||W_{t}-W_{t+\epsilon}||\leq C\epsilon.

(2.202)

Lemma 2.3.5
.

Let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a lipschitz-homotopic equivalence between two Riemannian manifolds of bounded geometry. Consider the splitting given by

\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)=V_{+}\oplus V_{-}

(2.203)

where
V_{\pm}
is the
\pm 1
-eigenspace of
\tau
. Then if
\alpha_{0}
is such that
D_{\alpha_{0},1}
is invertible, then there is an isometry
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},\pm}:V_{\pm,\alpha_{0},1}\longrightarrow V_{\pm}
(which implies
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},\pm}^{\times}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},\pm}=I
) and
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},\pm}
is the restriction to
V_{\pm,\alpha_{0},1}
of the operator
\frac{I\pm\tau}{2}+P_{\alpha_{0}}
where
P_{\alpha_{0}}
is an operator in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

We will prove the assertion just for the
+
case. The minus case is exactly the same.

Consider the operator
W_{\gamma(t)}
. Since it is uniformly continuous in
t
, we can divide
[0,1]
in
N_{0}
intervals
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
where
t_{0}=0
and
t_{N_{0}}=1
and

||W_{t_{i}}-W_{t_{i+1}}||\leq 1.

(2.204)

Now, we know that for all
t
the operator
W_{t}
is an involution and we have an orthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)=V_{+,\alpha(t),\beta(t)}\oplus V_{-,\alpha(t),\beta(t)}
, where
V_{\pm,\alpha(t),\beta(t)}
is the
\pm 1
-eigenspace of
W_{t}
.

Now the projection on
V_{\pm,\alpha(t),\beta(t)}
can be written as

\frac{I\pm W_{t}}{2}.

(2.205)

Let us now consider
F_{i}
the restriction of
\frac{I+W_{t_{i}}}{2}
to
V_{+,\alpha(t_{i+1}),\beta(t_{i+1})}
.

Our next step is to prove that
F_{i}:V_{+,\alpha(t_{i+1}),\beta(t_{i+1})}\longrightarrow V_{+,\alpha(t_{i}),\beta(t_{i})}
is invertible. To prove this fact we have to consider the operator
G_{i}
given by the restriction of
\frac{I+W_{t_{i+1}}}{2}
to
V_{+,\alpha(t_{i}),\beta(t_{i})}
.

Consider now
H_{i}:=W_{t_{i}}-W_{t_{i+1}}
. Then we have that for all
v
in
V_{+,\alpha(t_{i+1}),\beta(t_{i+1})}

\begin{split}G_{i}\circ F_{i}(v)&=(\frac{I+W_{t_{i+1}}}{2})(\frac{I+W_{t_{i}}}{2})v\\
&=(\frac{I+W_{t_{i+1}}}{2})(\frac{I+W_{t_{i+1}}+H_{i}}{2})v\\
&=\frac{I+W_{t_{i+1}}}{2}v+(\frac{I+W_{t_{i+1}}}{4})H_{i}v\\
&=Iv+(\frac{I+W_{t_{i+1}}}{4})H_{i}v.\end{split}

(2.206)

Now, since

||(\frac{I+W_{t_{i+1}}}{4})H_{i}||\leq||\frac{I+W_{t_{i+1}}}{2}||\cdot||\frac{H_{i}}{2}||\leq 1\cdot\frac{1}{2}||W_{t_{i+1}}-W_{t_{i}}||\leq\frac{1}{2},

(2.207)

we have that
G_{i}\circ F_{i}
is invertible. Then
F_{i}
is injective. With exactly with the same argument one can proof that
F_{i}\circ G_{i}
is invertible and so
F_{i}
is also surjective.

Let us now consider the isometry
U_{i}:V_{+,\alpha(t_{i+1}),\beta(t_{i+1})}\longrightarrow V_{+,\alpha(t_{i}),\beta(t_{i})}
as

U_{i}:=\frac{F_{i}}{|F_{i}|}.

(2.208)

Finally we can define the isometry as the following composition

\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},+}:=U_{0}\circ U_{1}\circ...\circ U_{n}:V_{+,\alpha_{0},1}\longrightarrow V_{+}.

(2.209)

Now we have to prove that
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},+}
is the restriction to
V_{+,\alpha_{0},1}
of an operator
I+P_{\alpha_{0}}
where
P_{\alpha_{0}}
is an operator in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. First we have to observe that
G_{i}=F_{i}^{\times}
, indeed if
v
is a vector of
V_{+,\alpha(t_{i+1}),\beta(t_{i+1})}
and
w
is a vector of
V_{+,\alpha(t_{i}),\beta(t_{i})}
then we can observe that

\langle v,\frac{I+W_{t_{i+1}}}{2}w\rangle=\langle v,w\rangle=\langle\frac{I+W_{t_{i}}}{2}v,w\rangle.

(2.210)

This means that
F_{i}^{\times}F_{i}(v)=Iv+(\frac{I+W_{t_{i+1}}}{4})H_{i}v
. Then, since
H_{i}
is in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
, we have that

F_{i}^{\times}F_{i}=(I+L)_{|_{V_{+,\alpha(t_{i+1}),\beta(t_{i+1})}}},

(2.211)

where
L
is an on operator in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. So, exactly as we did in the last proposition, one can prove that

|F_{i}|^{-1}=(I+Q)_{|_{V_{+,\alpha(t_{i+1}),\beta(t_{i+1})}}},

(2.212)

where
Q
is an operator in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. This means

\begin{split}U_{i}=\frac{F_{i}}{|F_{i}|}&=(\frac{I+W_{t}}{2})(I+Q)\\
&=(\frac{I+\tau}{2}+\frac{1}{2}H_{\alpha(t),\beta(t)})(I+Q)\\
&=\frac{I+\tau}{2}+C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}\end{split}

(2.213)

Then we have that

\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0}}=(\frac{I+\tau}{2})^{n}+C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}=\frac{I+\tau}{2}+C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}.

(2.214)

∎

2.3.5
Invariance of the Roe Index

Consider
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
two manifolds of bounded geometry and let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a
C^{2}_{b}
-map. Our first goal, in this subsection, is to defined a Roe index in
K_{n}(\frac{D_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}}{C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}})
of the signature operator on
M\sqcup N
.

In order to do it, we have to introduce a lemma which relates the Roe and structure algebras of
M
and
N
with the Roe and structure algebras of
M\sqcup N
.

Lemma 2.3.6
.

Consider
M
and
N
two Riemannian manifolds. Then if
X=M,N
there is a
*
-homomorphism

i_{X}:D^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}\longrightarrow D_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}

(2.215)

such that
i_{X}(C^{*}(X)^{\Gamma})\subseteq C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. We will denote by
\iota_{X}
the map

\iota_{X}:\frac{D^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}}{C^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}}\longrightarrow\frac{D^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}}{C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}}

(2.216)

induced by
i_{X}
.

Proof.

We can start observing that
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)=\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\oplus\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
, and so we can define
\pi_{M}:\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
and
\pi_{N}:\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
. Then we have that

D_{c}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}=\bigoplus\limits_{Y,Z=M,N}D_{c}^{*}(M\sqcup N)_{Y,Z}^{\Gamma},

(2.217)

where
D_{c}^{*}(M\sqcup N)_{Y,Z}^{\Gamma}
are the operators
A
of
D_{c,f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
such that

W\neq Z\lor S\neq Y\implies\pi_{W}\circ A\circ\pi_{S}=0.

(2.218)

The same decomposition can be considered for
C^{*}_{c,f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. As consequence we have that

\frac{D_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}}{C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}}=\bigoplus\limits_{Y,Z=M,N}\frac{\overline{D_{c}^{*}(M\sqcup N)_{Y,Z}^{\Gamma}}}{\overline{C^{*}_{c}(M\sqcup N)_{Y,Z}^{\Gamma}}}.

(2.219)

We will first consider the case
X=N
. We can define the map
i_{N}:D^{*}_{c}(N)^{\Gamma}\longrightarrow D^{*}_{c,f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
for all
A
as the operator

i_{N}(A)(\alpha,\beta)=(A\alpha,0).

(2.220)

We have that
i_{N}(A)
is a controlled operator. To prove this fact we consider two function
\phi,\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\sqcup N)
(without loss of generality we can suppose that their supports are connected). Then if
supp(\phi)
and
supp(\psi)
are not contained in
N\subset M\sqcup N
we have
\phi i_{N}(A)\psi=0
. On the other hand if both are contained in
N
we have that

d(f\sqcup id_{N}(supp(\phi)),f\sqcup id_{N}(supp(\psi)))=d(supp(\phi),supp(\psi)).

(2.221)

Then if
A
is a controlled operator, then same is true for
i_{N}(A)
. It is also true that if
A
is pseudo-local or locally compact then also
i_{1}(A)
is pseudo-local or locally compact. It’s easy now to check that

D^{*}_{c}(N)^{\Gamma}\cong D^{*}_{c}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}_{N,N}

(2.222)

and

C^{*}_{c}(N)^{\Gamma}\cong C^{*}_{c}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}_{N,N}.

(2.223)

The isomorphism is
i_{N}
and its inverse map is

i^{-1}(A)=\pi_{N}\circ A_{|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)}}.

(2.224)

Since

\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N))=\bigoplus_{Y,Z=M,N}\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{L}^{2}(Y),\mathcal{L}^{2}(Z))

(2.225)

and

C^{*}_{c}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}_{N,N}\subset\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{L}^{2}(N),\mathcal{L}^{2}(N))=\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{L}^{2}(N))

(2.226)

we have that

D^{*}(N)^{\Gamma}\cong\overline{D^{*}_{c}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}_{N,N}}

(2.227)

and

C^{*}(N)^{\Gamma}\cong\overline{C^{*}_{c}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}_{N,N}}.

(2.228)

Finally we can define the map
\iota_{N}
just as

\iota_{N}([A]):=[i_{N}(A)].

(2.229)

Let us consider the case
X=M
. We will define
i_{M}
and
\iota_{M}
exactly as we defined
i_{N}
and
\iota_{N}
. We just have to prove that if
A
is a controlled operator in
\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{L}^{2}(M))
, then
i_{M}(A)
, which is the operator defined as

i_{M}(A)(\alpha,\beta)=(0,A\beta),

(2.230)

is a controlled operator. Fix then
S>C_{f}\cdot R
where
C_{f}
is the lipschitz constant of
f
and
R
is a constant such that if
\phi,\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(M)

d(supp(\phi),supp(\psi))>R\implies\phi A\psi=0.

(2.231)

We want to show that if
\phi,\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(M\sqcup N)
(with out loss of generality we will consider
\phi
and
\psi
with connected support)

d((f\sqcup id_{N})supp(\phi),(f\sqcup id_{N})supp(\psi))>S\implies\phi i_{M}(A)\psi=0.

(2.232)

We can observe that if
supp(\phi)
and
supp(\psi)
are not contained in
M\subset M\sqcup N
, then
\phi i_{M}(A)\psi=0
so we can consider
\phi,\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(M)
. We have that

d(f(supp(\phi)),f(supp(\psi)))>S.

(2.233)

Consider
x
in
supp(\phi)
and
y
in
supp(\psi)
: then we have

S<d(f(x),f(y))\leq C_{f}d(x,y)

(2.234)

and so

d(x,y)>\frac{d(f(x),f(y))}{C_{f}}>\frac{S}{C_{f}}>R.

(2.235)

Then it means that

\begin{split}&d(f(supp(\phi)),f(supp(\psi)))>S\implies\\
&d(supp(\phi),supp(\psi))>R\implies\phi i_{M}(A)\psi=0.\end{split}

(2.236)

∎

Corollary 2.3.7
.

The previous Lemma also holds considering the Roe and structure algebras defined using the
(M,\Gamma)
-module, the
(N,\Gamma)
-module and the
(M\sqcup N,\Gamma)
-module defined as in Example
2.1.7
.

Corollary 2.3.8
.

Let us consider a Riemannian manifold
X
. and let us denote by
\delta_{X}
the transgression map

\delta_{X}:K_{n}(\frac{D^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}}{C^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}})\longrightarrow K_{n-1}(C^{*}(X)^{\Gamma}).

(2.237)

Let us consider
X=M,N
. Then if
n
is even

\delta_{M\sqcup N}\circ\iota_{X,\star}=i_{X,\star}^{u}\circ\delta_{X},

(2.238)

if
n
is odd

\delta_{M\sqcup N}\circ\iota^{u}_{X,\star}=i_{X,\star}\circ\delta_{X}.

(2.239)

Proof.

It is a consequence of the previous Lemma and Lemma
C.0.5
in Appendix C.
∎

Let us recall that the signature operator in
M\sqcup N
is defined as

D_{M\sqcup N}:=\begin{bmatrix}D_{N}&&0\\
0&&-D_{M}\end{bmatrix}.

(2.240)

Let us consider a chopping function
\chi
. We have that

\chi(D_{M\sqcup N})=\begin{bmatrix}\iota_{N}(\chi(D_{N}))&&0\\
0&&-\iota_{M}\chi(D_{M})\end{bmatrix}

(2.241)

is an operator in
\frac{D^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}}{C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}}
.

Exactly as in the connected case, we have the following definition.

Definition 2.3.4
.

The
fundamental class of
D_{M\sqcup N}
is
[D_{M\sqcup N}]\in K_{n+1}(\frac{D_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}}{C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}})
given by

[D_{M\sqcup N}]:=\begin{cases}[\frac{1}{2}(\chi(D_{M\sqcup N})+1)]\text{if $n$ is odd,}\\
[\chi(D_{M\sqcup N})_{+}]\text{if $n$ is even.}\par\end{cases}

(2.242)

Remark 46
.

Again the definition in the even case is well-given since we are considering
\chi(D_{M\sqcup N})_{+}
in
B(H_{M\sqcup N})
where
H_{M\sqcup N}
is defined as in Example
2.1.7
.

Definition 2.3.5
.

The
Roe index
of
D_{M\sqcup N}
is the class

{Ind_{Roe}(D_{M\sqcup N}):=\delta[D_{M\sqcup N}]}

(2.243)

in
K_{n}(C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma})
, where
\delta
is the connecting homomorphism in K-Theory.

Let us now introduce a result of Higson and Roe, in particular from
[
11
]
. The following paragraph, definitions and Proposition come directly from
[
11
]
.

Let us fix a
C^{*}
-subcategory
A
of the category of all Hilbert spaces and bounded linear maps (this is an additive subcategory whose morphism sets
Hom_{A}(H_{1},H_{2})
are Banach subspaces of the bounded linear operators from
H_{1}
to
H_{2}
, and are closed under the adjoint operation). Let us also fix an ideal
J
in
A
(apart from the fact that we no longer require identity morphisms, this is a
C^{*}
-subcategory with the additional property that any composition of a morphism in
J
with a morphism in
A
is a morphism in
J
).

Definition 2.3.6
.

An unbounded, self-adjoint, Hilbert space operator
D
is analytically controlled over
(A,J)
if

•

the Hilbert space on which it is defined is an object of
J
;

•

the resolvent operators
(D\pm i)^{-1}
are morphisms of
J
; and

•

the operator
\frac{D}{\sqrt{1+D^{2}}}
is a morphism of
A
.

Proposition 2.3.9
.

Let
D
be an analytically controlled self-adjoint operator and let
g:[-\infty,+\infty]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function. If
C
is a bounded self-adjoint operator in
A
then the difference
g(D)-g(D+C)
lies in
J
.

Now, we will use this Proposition of
[
11
]
to prove the following fact.

Proposition 2.3.10
.

Let
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
be two manifolds of bounded geometry and let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a
C^{2}_{b}
-map which is a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence. Then if
n
is odd we have that in
K^{*}(\frac{D_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}}{C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}})

[D_{M\sqcup N}]=[\frac{1}{2}(\chi(D_{M\sqcup N})+1)]=[\frac{1}{2}(\chi(D_{\alpha,\beta})+1)]

(2.244)

and if
n
is even

[D_{M\sqcup N}]=[Q\chi(D_{M\sqcup N})_{+}]=[\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},-}\chi(D_{\alpha_{0},1})\mathcal{U}^{*}_{\alpha_{0},+}].

(2.245)

Proof.

Let us start with the odd case. It’s sufficient to apply Propostion of
[
11
]
in the case
A
is
D_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
,
J
is
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
,
D
is the signature operator,
g=\chi
and
C=D_{\alpha,\beta}-D
. Indeed since

\chi(D_{\alpha,\beta})-\chi(D)\in C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}

(2.246)

then they are the same element in
\frac{D_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}}{C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}}
and so they induce the same element in
K
-theory.

For the even case it is sufficient, because of
2.3.5
, to remind that

\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},-}-\frac{I-\tau}{2}\in C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}

(2.247)

and so

\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},+}^{*}-\frac{I+\tau}{2}\in C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}.

(2.248)

Now, applying
[
11
]
, we also know that

\chi(D_{\alpha_{0},1})-\chi(D)\in C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}

(2.249)

and this means that

\chi(D_{M\sqcup N})_{+}-\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},-}\chi(D_{\alpha_{0},1})\mathcal{U}^{*}_{\alpha_{0},+}

(2.250)

is in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
since all the operators here are in
D^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. Then we can conclude applying Remark
37
, indeed we have that implies that the perturbation (
2.250
) can be seen in

C^{*}_{\rho_{M\sqcup N}}(M\sqcup N,H_{M\sqcup N})^{\Gamma}

(2.251)

where
H_{M\sqcup N}
and
\rho_{M\sqcup N}
are defined as in Example
2.1.7
.
∎

We can observe that since
D_{\alpha_{0},1}
is invertible, one can choose as function
\chi
a function which is
1
on
spec(D)\cap(0,+\infty)
e
\chi\equiv-1
on
spec(D)\cap(-\infty,0)
. This means that the following definition is well-given.

Definition 2.3.7
.

The
\rho_{f}
-class
of
D
is the class of
K_{n+1}(D_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma})

\rho_{f}=\begin{cases}[\frac{1}{2}(\chi(D_{\alpha_{0},1})+1)]\text{if n is odd,}\\
[(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},-})\chi(D_{\alpha_{0},1})(\mathcal{U}^{*}_{\alpha_{0},+})]\text{if n is even.}\end{cases}

(2.252)

Proposition 2.3.11
.

Let
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
be two manifolds of bounded geometry. Let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence which preserves the orientation. Then if
f
is a
C^{2}_{b,u}
-map, then the Roe index of
M\sqcup N
is zero.

Proof.

Consider the long exact sequence in
K
-theory

....\xrightarrow{i_{\star}}K_{n}(D_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma})\xrightarrow{p_{\star}}K_{n}(\frac{D_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}}{C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}})\xrightarrow{\delta}K_{n+1}(C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma})\longrightarrow...

(2.253)

Now, we know that
Ind_{Roe}(D_{M\sqcup N})=\delta([D_{M\sqcup N}]
. But in this case we know that

\delta([D_{M\sqcup N}]=\delta(p_{\star}(\rho_{f}(D_{M\sqcup N})))=0
. Then the Roe index vanishes.
∎

We are ready now to prove the lipschitz-homotopy invariance of the Roe index.

Theorem 2.3.12
.

Let
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
be two manifolds of bounded geometry. Let
\Gamma
be a group acting uniformly proper, discontinuous and free on
M
and
N
by orientation-preserving isometries. Consider
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
a
\Gamma
-equivariant lipschitz-homotopy equivalence which preserves the orientations. Then

f_{\star}(Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{M}))=Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{N}).

(2.254)

Proof.

Suppose that
\Gamma
acts FUPD. Since all
\Gamma
-invariant lipschitz map are coarsely approximable by
\Gamma
-invariant
C^{2}_{b}
-maps (Proposition
1.2.3
), we can consider
f
as a
C^{2}_{b}
-lipschitz-homotopy equivalence.

Consider a
\Gamma
-invariant isometry
A:\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
which covers
f
. We know that it exists by Proposition 4.3.5. of
[
21
]
. Then we know that

f_{\star}([D_{M}])=Ad_{V,\star}^{(u)}[D_{M}]=[VD_{M}V^{*}],

(2.255)

where we have the
u
if
n
is odd and we haven’t it if
n
is even.

Let us consider now
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. We already know the decomposition

C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}=\bigoplus\limits_{X,Y=M,N}C^{*}(M\sqcup N)_{X,Y}^{\Gamma}.

(2.256)

Let us define now the operator
H:C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}\longrightarrow C^{*}(N)^{\Gamma}
defined for all
T
in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)_{M,M}^{\Gamma}
as

Ad_{V}(T)=VTV^{*},

(2.257)

as
i_{N}^{-1}
on
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)_{N,N}^{\Gamma}
and zero for all the others operators. It’s easy to see that
H
is a
*
-homomorphism, and so it means that
H
induce a map between the
K
-groups. In particular we have that if
n
is odd, then

\begin{split}H_{\star}^{u}(\delta_{M\sqcup N}[\mathcal{D}_{M\sqcup N}])&=H_{\star}\circ i_{M,\star}^{u}(\delta_{M}[\mathcal{D}_{M}])-H_{\star}^{u}\circ i_{N,\star}^{u}(\delta_{N}[\mathcal{D}_{N}])\\
&=(H\circ i_{M})^{u}_{\star}(Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{M}))-(H\circ i_{N})^{u}_{\star}(Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{N}))\\
&=Ad_{V,\star}^{u}(Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{M}))-(id_{N})^{u}_{\star}(Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{N}))\\
&=f_{\star}(Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{M}))-Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{N}).\end{split}

(2.258)

and if
n
is even we have that

\begin{split}H_{\star}(\delta[\mathcal{D}_{M\sqcup N}])&=H_{\star}(\delta_{M\sqcup N}(i_{M,\star}^{u}[\mathcal{D}_{M}]))-H_{\star}(\delta_{M\sqcup N}(i_{N,\star}^{u}[\mathcal{D}_{N}]))\\
&=H_{\star}\circ i_{M,\star}(\delta_{M}[\mathcal{D}_{M}])-H_{\star}\circ i_{N,\star}(\delta_{N}[\mathcal{D}_{N}])\\
&=(H\circ i_{M})_{\star}(Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{M}))-(H\circ i_{N})_{\star}(Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{N}))\\
&=Ad_{V,\star}(Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{M}))-(id_{N})_{\star}(Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{N}))\\
&=f_{\star}(Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{M}))-Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{N}).\end{split}

(2.259)

Finally we know that
\delta_{M\sqcup N}[\mathcal{D}_{M\sqcup N}]=0
which implies

H^{(u)}_{\star}(\delta_{M\sqcup N}[\mathcal{D}_{M\sqcup N}])=0,

(2.260)

and so

f_{\star}(Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{M}))=Ind_{Roe}(\mathcal{D}_{N}).

(2.261)

∎

2.4
Lipschitz-homotopy stability of
\rho_{f}

2.4.1
Lipschitz-homotopy stability of
D^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
and
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}

In the previous sections we saw that given a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence, one can define a coarse structure on
M\sqcup N
and a class
\rho_{f}
in the K-theory of
D_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. Starting by now, we would like to study lipschitz-homotopy equivalence which are lipschitz-homotopic.

The main goal of this section is to prove that if
f\sim_{\Gamma}f^{\prime}
then they induce the same coarse structure on
M\sqcup N
and, in particular,

f\sim_{\Gamma}f^{\prime}\implies\rho_{f}=\rho_{f^{\prime}},

(2.262)

Lemma 2.4.1
.

Let us consider
f
and
f^{\prime}:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
two lipschitz map. Then if
f\sim_{\Gamma}f^{\prime}
for some
\Gamma
, then they are close and so they induce the same coarse structure on
M\sqcup N
.

Proof.

We will say that two maps
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}:S\longrightarrow M\sqcup N
. Given a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence
g:M\longrightarrow N
, we will say that
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
are
g
-close if they are close respect to the coarse structure induced by
g
. We will denote this relation as
\phi_{1}\sim_{f}\phi_{2}
.

In particular we know that

\phi_{1}\sim_{g}\phi_{2}\iff\exists R\geq 0\mbox{ such that }d(g\sqcup id_{N}\circ\phi_{1}(x),g\sqcup id_{N}\circ\phi_{2}(x))\leq R.

(2.263)

If we are able to show that
\phi_{1}\sim_{f}\phi_{2}
implies
\phi_{1}\sim_{f^{\prime}}\phi_{2}
then we conclude the proof.

Consider
H:(M\times[0,1],g\times g_{eucl})\longrightarrow(N,h)
the lipschitz homotopy between
f
and
f^{\prime}
. Then we have that
H\sqcup id_{N}
is a lipschitz-homotopic equivalence between
f\sqcup id_{N}
and
f^{\prime}\sqcup id_{N}
. Then we have that for every
y
in
M\sqcup N

\begin{split}d(f\sqcup id_{N}(y),f^{\prime}\sqcup id_{N}(y))&=d(H\sqcup id_{N}(y,0),H\sqcup id_{N}(y,1))\\
&\leq C_{H}d((y,0),(y,1))=C_{H},\end{split}

(2.264)

where
C_{H}
is the lipschitz constant of
H
. Let us suppose now
\phi_{1}\sim_{f}\phi_{2}
: we have that

\begin{split}d(f^{\prime}\sqcup id_{N}(\phi_{1}(x)),f^{\prime}\sqcup id_{N}(\phi_{2}(x)))&\leq d(f^{\prime}\sqcup id_{N}(\phi_{1}(x)),f\sqcup id_{N}(\phi_{1}(x)))\\
&+d(f\sqcup id_{N}(\phi_{1}(x)),f\sqcup id_{N}(\phi_{2}(x)))\\
&+d(f\sqcup id_{N}(\phi_{2}(x)),f^{\prime}\sqcup id_{N}(\phi_{2}(x)))\\
&\leq C_{H}+R+C_{H}\leq K\end{split}

(2.265)

and so it means that
\phi_{1}\sim_{f^{\prime}}\phi_{2}
.
∎

Corollary 2.4.2
.

Given two lipschitz-homotopy equivalences
f,f^{\prime}:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
, if
f\sim_{\Gamma}f^{\prime}
then

C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}=C^{*}_{f^{\prime}}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}

(2.266)

and

D^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}=D^{*}_{f^{\prime}}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}.

(2.267)

Obviously the same also holds considering the Roe and structure algebras defined using the
(M\sqcup N,\Gamma)
-module defined in Example
2.1.7
.

2.4.2
The curves
T_{s}
and
dT_{s}

Proposition 2.4.3
.

Let us consider two manifolds of bounded geometry
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
. Consider, moreover, a group
\Gamma
which acts FUPD on
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
. Let
f
and
f^{\prime}:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be two
C^{2}_{b}
-maps which are lipschitz-homotopy equivalences. Let us suppose, moreover, that
f\sim_{\Gamma}f^{\prime}
. Then there is a homotopy
h
such that the curve in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
defined as

\begin{split}T:[0,1]&\longrightarrow C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}\\
s&\longrightarrow T_{s}:=T_{h(\cdot,s)}\end{split}

(2.268)

and the curve
dT
defined for each
s
as
dT(s):=dT_{s}
are continuous.

Proof.

We already know that for each homotopy
h
and for all
s\in[0,1]
we have that
T_{s}\in C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. Then we just have to prove that

\lim\limits_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}||T_{s}-T_{s+\epsilon}||=0.

(2.269)

To show this fact we consider a homotopy
h
between
f
and
f^{\prime}
such that its first and second derivatives in normal coordinates are uniformly bounded: we know that it exists since Lemma
1.2.5
.

Consider the submersion
p_{h}
defined as in Proposition
1.4.1
. Because of Remark
28
,
p_{H}^{*}
is a R.-N.-lipschitz map. Let us consider the operator
T_{h}
: following the proof we give for
T_{f}
(Proposition
2.2.6
) one can check that
T_{h}
is a smoothing operator.

Then if we check that for each
s\in[0,1]
the operators
T_{s}
,
T_{s+\delta}
and
T_{h}
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
D.0.5
, and the same happens to
dT_{s}
,
dT_{s+\delta}
and
dT_{h}
then we can conclude.
Consider
\omega
a Thom form of
TN
defined as in Subsection
1.5.2
. Let us denote by
H:h^{*}TN\longrightarrow TN
and by
F_{s}:f^{*}_{s}TN\longrightarrow TN
the bundle maps induced by
h,f_{s}
. Then the operators
T_{h}
and
T_{s}
are defined using
H^{*}\omega
and
F_{s}^{*}\omega
. Define, moreover, the maps

t_{h}=(id_{M}\times[0,1],p_{h})_{:}h^{*}TN\longrightarrow M\times[0,1]\times N

(2.270)

and for each
w
in
[0,1]
consider

t_{s}=(id_{M},p_{f_{s}}):f^{*}_{s}TN\longrightarrow M\times N

(2.271)

We have that the kernel of
T_{h}
, around
im(t_{h})
, is locally given by

K(x,y,s)=\beta_{I,J}(x,y,s)dx^{I}\boxtimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{J}}+\beta_{IJ0}(x,y,s)dx^{I}\wedge ds\boxtimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{J}}

(2.272)

where
\beta_{IJ}
and
\beta_{IJ0}
are the components of
(t^{-1}_{h})^{*}H^{*}\omega
. Observe that
F_{s}=H\circ I_{s}
where
I_{s}:f^{*}_{s}TN\longrightarrow h^{*}TN
is the map induced by
i_{s}(p):=(p,s)
. Then we have that the kernel
of
T_{s}
, around
im(t_{s})
, is given by

K_{s}(x,y)=\alpha_{IJ}(x,y)dx^{I}\boxtimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{J}}

(2.273)

where
\alpha_{IJ}
are the components of

(t_{s}^{-1})^{*}F_{s}^{*}\omega=(t_{s}^{-1})^{*}I_{s}^{*}H^{*}\omega=i_{s}^{*}(t_{h}^{-1})^{*}H^{*}\omega.

(2.274)

This means that

\alpha_{IJ}=i_{s}^{*}(\beta_{I,J}).

(2.275)

This means that the first condition of Lemma
D.0.5
on the kernels of
T_{s}
,
T_{s+\delta}
and
T_{h}
is satisfied.

We know that
T_{h}
and
dT_{h}
have uniformly bounded support (which is the first condition), because of Proposition
2.2.10
.

Moreover, the second condition, which is the bound on the derivative of the kernel of
T_{h}
and
dT_{h}

|\frac{\partial}{\partial s}K^{I}_{J}(x,s)|\leq C,

(2.276)

is satisfied for both
T_{h}
and
dT_{h}
since the first and second derivatives in normal coordinates of
h
uniformly bounded.

Consider, indeed, the map
t_{h}:=(\pi,p_{h})
: we know, because of Proposition
2.2.6
, that the components of the kernel of
T_{H}
in local normal coordinates are given by an algebraic combination of the pullback along
t_{h}^{-1}
of the components of the Thom form
\omega
written in fibered coordinates. Then, since
h
is a lipschitz map, the inequality (
2.276
) is satisfied for
T_{h}
.

Moreover, applying Lemma
D.0.4
, we also have that the inequality (
2.276
) is satisfied for
dT_{h}
if the second derivatives of
h
are uniformly bounded in normal coordinates.
∎

Corollary 2.4.4
.

Under the same assumptions, also the operator
dT^{\dagger}_{s}=T^{\dagger}_{s}d
define a curve in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

We can observe that

T_{s}^{\dagger}d=(dT_{s})^{\dagger}=\tau\circ(dT_{s})^{*}\circ\tau

(2.277)

Then, since
dT_{f_{s}}
is a continuous curve in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
, that
\tau
is an operator in
D^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
and that the adjoint

\begin{split}*:C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}&\longrightarrow C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}\\
A&\longrightarrow A^{*}\end{split}

(2.278)

is continuous we conclude that
T_{s}^{\dagger}d=dT_{s}^{\dagger}
defines a continuous curve in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.
∎

2.4.3
The curves
T_{s}y_{s}
and
dT_{s}y_{s}

Let us consider for each

a lipschitz-homotopy equivalence

such that the map
h:(M\times[0,1],g+g_{eucl})\longrightarrow(N,h)
is a lipschitz map. Then we can denote by

the
y
operator defined for each
f_{s}
. Our next goal is to prove that
T_{s}y_{s}
and
dT_{s}y_{s}
define two continuous curves in
\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{L}^{2}(N))
.

We can start observing that

y_{s}:=\frac{Y_{s}+Y^{\dagger}_{s}}{2}

(2.279)

where

Y_{s}:=(p_{f_{s},2})_{\star}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}\circ pr_{2\star}\circ e_{\omega_{1}}\circ\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}\circ C\circ p_{f_{s},1},

(2.280)

and
3
3

3

In what follows we identify the bundles
f_{s}^{*}(TN)
. Actually, the only
difference between
f_{s}^{*}(TN)
and
f_{s+\delta}^{*}TN
concern the Sasaki metrics on them, but it is irrelevant in this
Subsection since Proposition
1.5.7
.

•

p_{f_{s},i}:f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)_{i}\longrightarrow N
is the map
p_{f_{s}}
,

•

pr_{i}:\mathcal{B}=f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)_{1}\oplus f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)_{2}\longrightarrow f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)_{i}
, is the projection on the
i
th component,

•

e_{\omega,i}
is the operator of wedging by
pr_{i}^{*}F_{s}^{*}\omega
where

is the projection on the
i
component,

•

C:f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)_{1}\oplus f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)_{2}\times[0,1]\longrightarrow f^{*}(T^{\delta}N)_{1}
is the map defined as

C(v_{f(p)1},v_{f(p)2},w):=wv_{f(p)1}+(1-w)v_{f(p)2}.

(2.281)

We can observe that
\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}
commutes with
e_{\omega_{i}}
,
pr_{2,\star}
. This happens because
\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}
, up to signs, is an integration along fibers and so we can apply the Proposition VII of Chapter 9 of
[
9
]
. Then we can see
Y_{s}
, up to signs, as

Y_{s}=\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}\circ(p_{f_{s},2},id_{[0,1]})_{\star}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}\circ(pr_{2},id_{[0,1]})_{\star}\circ e_{\omega_{1}}\circ\tilde{P}_{f_{s}}^{*}

(2.282)

where

is the standard projection and

.

Lemma 2.4.5
.

Let
p_{H}:(M\times[0,1],g+g_{[0,1]})\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a lipschitz submersion. Suppose, moreover that

\begin{split}(p_{H},id_{[}0,1]):(M\times[0,1],g+g_{[0,1]})&\longrightarrow(N\times[0,1],h+g_{[0,1]})\\
(p,s)&\longrightarrow(p_{H}(p,s),s)\end{split}

(2.283)

is a R,-N.-lipschitz submersion. Let us denote, for each
t\in[0,1]
, by
p_{t}

the map

p_{t}:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)

(2.284)

defined as
p_{t}(p):=p_{H}(p,t)
. We have that
p_{t}
is a R.-N.-lipschitz submersion for each
t
. Moreover if
\omega\in\Omega^{*}_{cv}(M)
is a closed form such that
|\omega|_{p}
is uniformly bounded, then for each smooth
\alpha
in
dom(d_{M})
we have that

p_{t\star}\circ e_{\omega}-p_{t+\delta\star}\circ e_{\omega}=A_{\delta}d\alpha,

(2.285)

where
A_{\delta}
is a bounded operator with norm less or equal to
\delta\cdot C
for some
C
in
\mathbb{R}
. This, in particular implies that

||p_{t}\circ e_{\omega}-p_{t+\delta}\circ e_{\omega}||\leq\delta C||d\alpha||.

(2.286)

Moreover we also have that

e_{\omega}\circ p_{t}^{*}-e_{\omega}\circ p_{t+\delta}^{*}(\alpha)=\pm A\dagger d\alpha.

(2.287)

and so

||e_{\omega}\circ p_{t}^{*}-e_{\omega}\circ p_{t+\delta}^{*}(\alpha)||\leq\delta\cdot C\cdot d\alpha.

(2.288)

Proof.

Observe that if
(p_{h},id_{[0,1]})
is a lipschitz submersion then also
p_{t}
is a lipschitz submersion. Moreover the Fiber Volume of
p_{t}
is the Fiber Volume of
(p_{h},id_{[0,1]})
in
(p,t)
. Then we have that
p_{t}
is a R.-N.-lipschitz submersion.
Consider
s\in[t,t+\delta]
. We denote by
F_{s}
the fiber of
p_{s}
. Observe that, since the Stokes Theorem, we have that

\begin{split}p_{t}\circ e_{\omega}-p_{t+\delta}\circ e_{\omega}(\alpha)&=\int_{F_{t}}\alpha\wedge\omega-\int_{F_{t+\delta}}\alpha\wedge\omega\\
&=\int_{F_{t}\times\{t\}}\alpha\wedge\omega-\int_{F_{t+\delta}\times\{t\}}\alpha\wedge\omega\\
&=\int_{t}^{t+\delta}\int_{\partial F_{s}\times\{s\}}\alpha\wedge\omega+d\int_{t}^{t+\delta}\int_{F_{s}\times\{s\}}\alpha\wedge\omega\\
&+\int_{t}^{t+\delta}\int_{F_{s}\times\{s\}}d\alpha\wedge\omega.\end{split}

(2.289)

Observe that the first integral is null since
\omega
is null on
\partial F
. The second integral is also null
since
\alpha
is
0
-form respect to
[t,t+\delta]
. Then we obtain that

p_{t}\circ e_{\omega}-p_{t+\delta}\circ e_{\omega}(\alpha)=pr_{M\star}\circ(p_{h},id_{[t,t+\delta]})_{\star}\circ e_{\omega}(d\alpha),

(2.290)

where
pr_{M}:M\times[t,t+\delta]\longrightarrow M
is the projection on the first component. Then
||pr_{M\star}||\leq\delta\cdot C_{1}
. Moreover, since
|\omega_{p}|
is uniformly bounded then
e_{\omega}
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-bounded. This means that

||p_{t}\circ e_{\omega}-p_{t+\delta}\circ e_{\omega}(\alpha)||\leq\delta\cdot C_{1}\cdot C_{2}\cdot C_{3}\cdot||d\alpha||.

(2.291)

Then, observing that

(p_{t}\circ e_{\omega}-p_{t+\delta}\circ e_{\omega})^{\dagger}=\pm e_{\omega}\circ p_{t}^{*}\mp e_{\omega}\circ p_{t+\delta}^{*}

(2.292)

We have, since (
2.290
)

\begin{split}e_{\omega}\circ p_{t}^{*}-e_{\omega}\circ p_{t+\delta}^{*}\alpha&=\pm(d\circ e_{\omega}\circ(p_{h},id_{[t,t+\delta]})^{*}\circ pr_{M}^{*})\alpha\\
&=\pm(e_{\omega}\circ(p_{h},id_{[t,t+\delta]})^{*}\circ pr_{M}^{*})d\alpha\end{split}

(2.293)

Then we also have (
2.288
).
∎

Proposition 2.4.6
.

The curves
y_{s}T^{\dagger}_{s}
,
T_{s}y_{s}
,
dT_{s}y_{s}
,
dy_{s}T^{\dagger}_{s}
,
y_{s}T^{\dagger}_{s}d
and
T_{s}y_{s}d
are continuous in
s
.

Proof.

Observe that for each
s
in
[0,1]
we have that

||Y_{s+\delta}T_{s+\delta}^{\dagger}-Y_{s}T^{\dagger}_{s}||\leq||Y_{s+\delta}||\cdot||T^{\dagger}_{s+\delta}-T^{\dagger}_{s}||+||(Y_{s+\delta}-Y_{s})T^{\dagger}_{s}||.

(2.294)

Observe that the norm of
Y_{s}
is uniformly bounded because the norm of
p_{f_{s}}
is uniformly bounded in
s
. Indeed the norm of
p_{f_{s}}
is bounded by a constant which continuously depends on the lipschitz constant and the Fiber Volume of
p_{f_{s}}
. However, these are both controlled by the lipschitz constant and the Fiber Volume of
p(h,id_{[0,1]}
. This means that, in order to show that
Y_{s}T^{\dagger}_{s}
is continuous in
s
we have to prove that

||(Y_{s+\delta}-Y_{s})T^{\dagger}_{s}||\leq C\cdot\delta.

(2.295)

Observe that

\begin{split}Y_{s+\delta}-Y_{s}&=\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}\circ[(p_{f_{s+\delta},2},id_{[0,1]})_{\star}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}-(p_{f_{s},2},id_{[0,1]})_{\star}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}]\circ(pr_{2},id_{[0,1]})^{*}\circ e_{\omega_{1}}\circ\tilde{P}_{f_{s}}^{*}\\
&+\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}\circ(p_{f_{s},2},id_{[0,1]})_{\star}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}(pr_{2},id_{[0,1]})^{*}\circ[e_{\omega_{1}}\circ\tilde{P}_{f_{s+\delta}}^{*}-e_{\omega_{1}}\circ\tilde{P}_{f_{s}}^{*}].\end{split}

(2.296)

Let us denote by
A
the operator
(pr_{2},id_{[0,1]})*\circ e_{\omega_{1}}\circ\tilde{P}_{f_{s}}^{*}
. Observe that

Ad\alpha=dA\alpha

(2.297)

for each smooth form
\alpha
. Observe that, applying Lemma
2.4.5
,

\begin{split}&||\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}\circ[(p_{f_{s+\delta},2},id_{[0,1]})_{\star}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}-(p_{f_{s},2},id_{[0,1]})_{\star}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}]\circ A(\alpha)||\\
&\leq C_{1}\cdot||[(p_{f_{s+\delta},2},id_{[0,1]})_{\star}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}-(p_{f_{s},2},id_{[0,1]})_{\star}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}]\circ A(\alpha)||\\
&\leq C_{1}\cdot C\cdot||dA\alpha||\\
&\leq C_{1}\cdot C\cdot||Ad\alpha||\\
&\leq C_{1}\cdot C\cdot K\cdot||d\alpha||.\end{split}

(2.298)

Moreover we also have that

\begin{split}&||\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}\circ(p_{f_{s},2},id_{[0,1]})_{\star}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}(pr_{2},id_{[0,1]})^{*}\circ[e_{\omega_{1}}\circ\tilde{P}_{f_{s+\delta}}^{*}-e_{\omega_{1}}\circ\tilde{P}_{f_{s}}^{*}](\alpha)||\\
&\leq C\cdot||e_{\omega_{1}}\circ\tilde{P}_{f_{s+\delta}}^{*}-e_{\omega_{1}}\circ\tilde{P}_{f_{s}}^{*}(\alpha)||\\
&\leq\delta\cdot C\cdot||d\alpha||.\end{split}

(2.299)

This means that

||(Y{s+\delta}-Y_{s})\alpha||\leq\delta\cdot R\cdot||d\alpha||.

(2.300)

and so

\begin{split}||(Y{s+\delta}-Y_{s})T^{\dagger}_{s}\alpha||&\leq\delta\cdot K_{1}\cdot||dT^{\dagger}_{s}\alpha||\\
&\leq\delta\cdot K_{2}\cdot||\alpha||\end{split}

(2.301)

Then we have that

||(Y{s+\delta}-Y_{s})T^{\dagger}_{s}||\leq\delta\cdot K_{2}

(2.302)

and the curve
Y_{s}T^{\dagger}_{s}
is continuous in
s
. Observe that, up to signs, we have that

\begin{split}Y_{s}^{\dagger}&=\tilde{P}_{f_{s}\star}\circ e_{\omega_{1}}\circ(pr_{2},id_{[0,1]})^{*}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}\circ(p_{f_{s},2},id_{[0,1]})^{*}\circ[\int_{0,\mathcal{L}}^{1}]^{\dagger}\\
&=\tilde{P}_{f_{s}\star}\circ e_{\omega_{1}}\circ(pr_{2},id_{[0,1]})^{*}\circ e_{\omega_{2}}\circ(p_{f_{s},2},id_{[0,1]})^{*}\circ pr_{[0,1]}^{*}\end{split}

(2.303)

where
pr_{[0,1]}N\times[0,1]\longrightarrow N
is the projection on the first component. Then, observe
that, using the same arguments we used for
Y_{s}
, we obtain that

||Y^{\dagger}_{s+\delta}-Y^{\dagger}_{s}(\alpha)||=\delta\cdot K_{3}||d\alpha||

(2.304)

and so

||(Y^{\dagger}_{s+\delta}-Y^{\dagger}_{s})T^{\dagger}_{s}\alpha||=\delta\cdot C||\alpha||

(2.305)

This means that

||(Y^{\dagger}_{s+\delta}-Y^{\dagger}_{s})T^{\dagger}_{s}||=\delta\cdot C

(2.306)

and so
Y_{s}^{\dagger}T^{\dagger}_{s}
,
y_{s}T^{\dagger}_{s}
and
T_{s}y_{s}
are continuous in
s
.

Finally, observe that

(y_{s}-y_{s+\delta})dT^{\dagger}_{s}=0

(2.307)

indeed for each smooth
\alpha
we have that

||(y_{s}-y_{s+\delta})dT^{\dagger}_{s}\alpha||\leq\delta\cdot C\cdot||d^{2}T^{\dagger}_{s}\alpha||=0.

(2.308)

Then we obtain that also
y_{s}dT^{\dagger}_{s}
and
T_{s}dy_{s}
are continuous on
s
. Moreover also

dy_{s}T^{\dagger}_{s}=(1-T^{\dagger}_{s}T_{s}+y_{s}d)T^{\dagger}_{s}

(2.309)

and

T_{s}y_{s}d=T_{s}(1-T^{\dagger}_{s}T_{s}+dy_{s})

(2.310)

are continuous in s.
∎

2.4.4
Lipschitz-homotopy stability of
\rho_{f}

Starting by now we will denote by
L_{\alpha,\beta,s}
the operator that we called
L_{\alpha,\beta}
in subsection
2.3.3
, but defined using
T_{s}
. We know that if
\alpha=0
, then
L_{0,\beta,s}
is invertible. It follows by the the definition of
L_{\alpha,\beta,s}
that
L_{0,\beta,s}
can be seen as a compact continuous curve in
\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N))
. Then there is an
\alpha_{0}>0
such that
L_{\alpha_{0},\beta,s}
are invertible for all
s
.

Then we will denote by
\delta_{\alpha_{0},s}
,
U_{\alpha_{0},\beta,s}
and
S_{\alpha_{0},\beta,s}
the operators
\delta_{\alpha_{0}}
,
U_{\alpha_{0},\beta}
and
S_{\alpha_{0},\beta}
introduced in subsection
2.3.3
defined using
T_{s}
.

Finally we denote by
D_{\alpha_{0},\beta,s}
the perturbed signature operator defined using
T_{s}
.

Proposition 2.4.7
.

Let us consider two orientated Riemannian manifolds of bounded geometry
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
. Consider a group
\Gamma
acting by isometries on
(M,g)
and on
(N,h)
that preserve the orientations. Let
f_{0},f_{1}:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
two lipschitz-homotopy equivalence which preserves the orientation and let us suppose that
f_{0}\sim_{\Gamma}f_{1}
. Then the difference between

D_{\alpha_{0},\beta,s}-D

(2.311)

defines a continue curve in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

We know, beacause of Proposition 14.5, that for all
s\in[0,1]

D_{\alpha_{0},\beta,s}-D\in C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma},

(2.312)

then we just have to prove that

D_{\alpha_{0},\beta,s}=D+\gamma(s),

(2.313)

where
\gamma:[0,1]\longrightarrow C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. We know that

S_{\alpha,\beta,s}=\frac{\tau L_{\alpha,\beta,s}}{|\tau L_{\alpha,\beta,s}|}.

(2.314)

It’s easy to check that since
T_{s}
is continuous in
s
, then the same happens to
\tau L_{\alpha,\beta,s}
. Then, using the boundedness of
f(x)=\frac{x}{|x|}
and the uniformly boundedness of the operators
\tau L_{\alpha,\beta,s}
, then we have that
S_{\alpha,\beta,s}
is continuous in
S
. This means that if we consider as in Proposition 14.5

S_{\alpha,\beta,s}=\tau+H_{\alpha,\beta,s},

(2.315)

where
H_{\alpha,\beta,s}
is a continuous curve on
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. Using the same arguments one can easily prove that

U_{\alpha,\beta,s}=1+G_{\alpha,\beta,s}

(2.316)

and

U_{\alpha,\beta,s}^{-1}=1+K_{\alpha,\beta,s}

(2.317)

where
G_{\alpha,\beta,s}
and
K_{\alpha,\beta,s}
are operators in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
continuous in
s
.

The next step is to prove that
G_{\alpha,\beta,s}d
,
H_{\alpha,\beta,s}d
,
K_{\alpha,\beta,s}d
,
dG_{\alpha,\beta,s}
,
dH_{\alpha,\beta,s}
,
dK_{\alpha,\beta,s}
are continuous operators in
s
.

Let us now consider the operators
y_{s}
,
T_{s}
and
dT_{s}
: we proved that they give a curve in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. The same obviously holds also for
dT_{s}y_{s}
. Moreover it is also true for
T_{s}y_{s}d
, indeed

T_{s}y_{s}d=-T_{s}dy_{s}+T_{s}+T_{s}T_{s}^{\dagger}T_{s}

(2.318)

We can observe that

L_{\alpha,\beta,s}=1+Q_{\alpha,\beta,s},

(2.319)

where
Q_{\alpha,\beta,s}
is an algebraic combination of
T_{s}
,
T^{\dagger}_{s}
,
T_{s}y
,
yT_{s}
,
T^{\dagger}_{s}y
and
yT^{\dagger}_{s}
. Then we have that
Q_{\alpha,\beta,s}
,
Q_{\alpha,\beta,s}d
and
dQ_{\alpha,\beta,s}
give a curve in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. The same property holds for
R_{\alpha,\beta,s}=2Q_{\alpha,\beta,s}+Q_{\alpha,\beta,s}^{2}
.

Now we can observe that if
A_{s}
is a curve in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
such that
Ad
and
dA
are also two curves in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
then also
g(A)d
and
dg(A)
are curves in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
, indeed

g(A_{s})d=(aA_{s}+A_{s}h(A_{s})A_{s})d=a(A_{s}d)+A_{s}h(A_{s})(A_{s}d)

(2.320)

and

dg(A_{s})=d(aA:s+A_{s}h(A_{s})A_{s})=a(dA_{s})+(dA_{s})h(A_{s})A_{s}.

(2.321)

and since the continuity of the functional calculus on bounded operators we have that the compositions of
V_{\alpha,\beta}
and
G_{\alpha,\beta}
with
d
are operators in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. Moreover, since

Z_{\alpha,\beta,s}=-V_{\alpha,\beta,s}-V_{\alpha,\beta,s}Z_{\alpha,\beta,s}=-V_{\alpha,\beta,s}-Z_{\alpha,\beta,s}V_{\alpha,\beta,s}

(2.322)

then this property also holds for
Z_{\alpha,\beta,s}
,
K_{\alpha,\beta,s}
and
H_{\alpha,\beta,s}
.
Now, since
D_{\alpha,\beta,s}-D
is an algebraic combination of operators in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
, the perturbation is a continuous curve in
C^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.
∎

Lemma 2.4.8
.

Let
f_{0},f_{1}:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
twolipschitz homotopy-equivalences between two riemannian manifolds with bounded geometry. Consider the splitting given by

\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)=V_{+}\oplus V_{-}

(2.323)

where
V_{\pm}
is the
\pm 1
-eigenspace of
\tau
. Then if
\alpha_{0}
is such that
D_{\alpha_{0},1,s}
is invertible for each
s
, then the isometry
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},s,\pm}:V_{\pm,\alpha_{0},1}\longrightarrow V_{\pm}
of Lemma 14.6 is the restriction to
V_{\pm,\alpha_{0},1,s}
of the operator
\frac{I\pm\tau}{2}+P_{\alpha_{0},s}
where
P_{\alpha_{0},s}
is continuous curve in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

Let us define the operator

W_{\alpha,\beta,s}=U_{\alpha,\beta,s}S_{\alpha,\beta,s}U_{\alpha,\beta,s}^{-1}.

(2.324)

In the previous proposition we proved that
U_{\alpha,\beta,s}
,
S_{\alpha,\beta,s}
and
U_{\alpha,\beta,s}^{-1}
are continuous in
s
and so the same holds also for
W_{\alpha,\beta,s}
. Then, exactly as we did in Remark 48, we can define
W_{\gamma(t),s}
that will be continuous in
s
and in
t
. This means that there is a
C
for each
s
and for each
\epsilon
such that

||W_{t,s}-W_{t+\epsilon,s}||\leq C\epsilon.

(2.325)

Since
W_{t,s}
is uniformly continuous on
t
, we can divide
[0,1]
in
N_{0}
intervals
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
where
t_{0}=0
and
t_{N_{0}}=1
and

||W_{t_{i},s}-W_{t_{i}+1,s}||\leq 1.

(2.326)

Then we can define the curves
H_{i,s}:=W_{t_{i},s}-W_{t_{i}+1,s}
and

L_{i,s}:=\frac{I+W_{t_{i},s}}{4}H_{i,s}.

(2.327)

As we proved in Lemma 14.6, one can easily check that
I+L_{i,s}
is invertible and if we define
Q_{i,s}
as the operator such that

I+Q_{i,s}=(I+L_{i,s})^{-1}

(2.328)

and, using the continuity of the functional calculus, we have that
Q_{i,s}
is continuous in
s
. Then the operator

\begin{split}U_{i,s}&=\frac{I+W_{t_{i},s}}{2}(I+Q_{i,s})\\
&=\frac{I+W_{0,s}-W_{0,s}+W_{t_{i},s}}{2}(I+Q_{i,s})\\
&=[\frac{I+\tau}{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\sum\limits_{j=0}^{i}H_{i,s})](I+Q_{i,s})\\
&=\frac{I+\tau}{2}+S_{i,s}\end{split}

(2.329)

where
S_{i_{s}}
is a continuous curve in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. Then we can conclude observing that

\begin{split}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},s}&=U_{0,s}\circ...\circ U_{N_{0},s}\\
