&=\frac{I+\tau}{2}^{N_{0}}+P_{\alpha_{0},s}\\
&=\frac{I+\tau}{2}+P_{\alpha_{0},s},\end{split}

(2.330)

where
P_{\alpha_{0},s}
is a continuous curve in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.
∎

Proposition 2.4.9
.

Let us consider
D
an operator which is analytically controlled over
(D^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma},C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma})
on
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N)
. Suppose that
D
has bounded inverse and let
S
be a continuous map
S:[0,1]\longrightarrow C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
such that for all
t\in[0,1]
the operator
D+S(t)
has a bounded inverse.

Then if
\chi:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a bounded continuous function, we have that

\chi(D+S):[0,1]\longrightarrow D^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}

(2.331)

is a continuous map.

Proof.

We know, because Lemma 5.8 of
[
11
]
, that for all
t\in[0,1]
the operator
\chi(D+S)(t)
is in
C^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. This means that we just have to prove that for all
\epsilon>0
and for all
t_{0}
in
[0,1]
there is a
\delta>0
such that for all
t
such that
|t-t_{0}|\leq\delta

||\chi(D+S(t))-\chi(D+S(t_{0}))||\leq\epsilon.

(2.332)

It’s easy to see that if the Proposition holds for
\chi(x)=\arctan(x)
then it also holds for every bounded continuous
f
. Indeeed we can observe that

\bigcup\limits_{t\in[0,1]}Spec(arctan(D+S(t)))\subseteq[-b,-a]\sqcup[a,b]

(2.333)

for some
a,b>0
and so, since
f=\frac{f}{\arctan}\arctan
on
[-b,-a]\sqcup[a,b]
, we have that

f(D+S(t))=\frac{f}{\arctan}(\arctan(D+S(t)))

(2.334)

and since
\arctan(D+S(t))
are bounded, then

\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}f(D+S(t))=f(D+S(t_{0})).

(2.335)

Let us suppose
\chi(x)=\arctan(x)
. We will use the complex logarithm form

\arctan(x)=\frac{i}{2}\log(\frac{i+x}{i-x}).

(2.336)

In particular, since
\arctan
is an odd function, we will use

\arctan(x)=-\frac{i}{2}\log(-\frac{i+x}{i-x}).

(2.337)

We know, since Proposition 5.9 of
[
11
]
, that for all
t
the operator
D+S(t)
is analitically controlled and so
(D+S(t)\pm i)
has bounded inverse. We can observe then

\begin{split}\frac{i+D+S(t)}{i-D-S(t)}&=\frac{i+D+S(t)}{i-D-S(t)}\cdot\frac{i+D+S(t)}{i+D+S(t)}\\
&=[-1+2i(D+S(t))+(D+S(t))^{2}]((D+S(t))^{2}+1)^{-1}\\
&=[-1+2i(D+S(t))^{-1}+(D+S(t))^{-2}]\frac{(D+S(t))^{2}}{(D+S(t))^{2}}((D+S(t))^{-2}+1)^{-1}\\
&=[-1+2i(D+S(t))^{-1}+(D+S(t))^{-2}]((D+S(t))^{-2}+1)^{-1}.\end{split}

(2.338)

Then for all
t
we have that
-1+2i(D+S(t))^{-1}+(D+S(t))^{-2}
is bounded since it is a sum of bounded operators. In particular, applying the Theorem 1.16 of
[
14
]
, we obtain that

-1+2i(D+S(t))^{-1}+(D+S(t))^{-2}
is a continuous curve in
\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{L}^{2}(M\sqcup N))
and its norm is a continuous function
\alpha:[0,1]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

Moreover also
((D+S(t))^{-2}+1)^{-1}
is bounded, indeed we know that

Spec((D+S(t))^{-2})\subseteq[\epsilon,+\infty)

(2.339)

and so

Spec((D+S(t))^{-2}+1)\subseteq[1+\epsilon,+\infty)

(2.340)

Then, using that
\lambda
is in the spectrum of
((D+S(t))^{-2})^{-1}
if and only if
\lambda+1
is in the spectrum of
Spec((D+S(t))^{-2}+1)
, we have that the spectral radius (i.e. the norm) of
((D+S(t))^{-2}+1)^{-1}
is strictly less of
1
.

Then we have that

||[-1+2i(D+S(t))^{-1}+(D+S(t))^{-2}]((D+S(t))^{-2}+1)^{-1}||\leq\alpha(t)\leq M_{0}.

(2.341)

Observe that the spectral radius of
\frac{i-D-S(t)}{i+D+S(t)}
is less or equal to its norm. Then we have that

Spec(\frac{i+D+S(t)}{i-D-S(t)})\subseteq B_{M_{0}}(0),

(2.342)

where
B_{M_{0}}(0)
is the ball centered in
0
with radius
M_{0}
in
\mathbb{C}
.
Moreover, exactly as we proved that the spectrum of
\frac{i+D+S(t)}{i-D-S(t)}
is uniformly bounded in
t
, then we can check the same for

\frac{i-D-S(t)}{i+D+S(t)}=(\frac{i+D+S(t)}{i-D-S(t)})^{-1}.

(2.343)

This means that there is a disk of radius
M_{1}
centered in zero, such that

Spec(\frac{i+D+S(t)}{i-D-S(t)})\cap B_{M_{1}}(0)=\emptyset.

(2.344)

Consider the corona
C_{M_{0},M_{1}}
given by the intersection of
B_{M_{1}}(0)^{c}
and
B_{M_{0}}(0)
.
We obtain that

Spec(-\frac{i+D+S(t)}{i-D-S(t)})\subset C_{M_{0},M_{1}}.

(2.345)

Fix the polar coordinates
(r,\theta)
in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
where
\theta=(-\pi,+\pi)
. Since the spectrum of
D+S(t)
is real and there is a gap around
0
which doesn’t depends on
t
, then there is an angle
\theta_{0}
such that

Spec(-\frac{i+D+S(t)}{i-D-S(t)})\subset F_{\theta_{0}}

(2.346)

where

F_{\theta_{0}}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}|\theta(z)\in[-\pi+\theta_{0},\pi-\theta_{0}]\}.

(2.347)

Indeed we have that

Spec(D+S(t))\subset(-\infty,-r_{0})\times\{-\pi\}\sqcup(r_{0},+\infty)\times\{0\}=A_{-}\sqcup A_{+}

(2.348)

for some
r_{0}>0
. Then, if
z
is in
A_{-}
, then

\theta(i+z)=\pi-\alpha

(2.349)

where

\alpha=\arccos(\frac{z}{\sqrt{z^{2}+1}})

(2.350)

is contained in
[0,\theta_{1}]\subset[0,\frac{\pi}{2})
, where

\theta_{1}=\arccos(\frac{r_{0}}{\sqrt{r_{0}^{2}+1}}).

(2.351)

Moreover we also have that

\theta(i-z)=\alpha>0.

(2.352)

Then we have that

\theta(\frac{i+z}{i-z})=\pi-2\alpha\in[\pi-2\theta_{1},\pi)\subset(0,\pi)

(2.353)

and so

\theta(-\frac{i+z}{i-z})\in[-\pi+2\theta_{1},0)\subset(-\pi,0)

(2.354)

If
z
is in
A_{+}
, then
\theta(i+z)=\alpha
and
\theta(i-z)=\pi-\alpha
. This means that if
z
is in
A_{+}

\theta(\frac{i+z}{i-z})=-\pi+2\alpha\in(-\pi,-2\theta_{1}]\subset(-\pi,0)

(2.355)

and so

\theta(-\frac{i+z}{i-z})\in(0,\pi-2\theta_{1}]\subset(0,\pi)

(2.356)

We proved (
2.346
): it is sufficient consider
\theta_{0}:=2\theta_{1}
.

Since (
2.346
) and (
2.345
) we obtain that

Spec(-\frac{i+D+S(t)}{i-D-S(t)})\subset F_{\theta_{0}}\cap C_{M_{0},M_{1}}.

(2.357)

Observe that
K:=F_{\theta_{0}}\cap C_{M_{0},M_{1}}
is a compact set where the logarithm is a well-defined, continuous function. This implies that for all
\epsilon
there is a polinomial
p_{\epsilon}
which is an
\epsilon
-approximation of logarithm on
K
. Then if we denote by
a:=\frac{i+D+S(t)}{i-D+S(t)}
and
b:=\frac{i+D+S(t_{0})}{i-D+S(t_{0})}
we have that

\begin{split}||\log(a)-\log(b)||&=||\log(a)-p_{\epsilon}(a)||+||p_{\epsilon}(a)-p_{\epsilon}(b)||+||\log(b)-p_{\epsilon}(b)||\\
&\leq\epsilon+||p_{\epsilon}(a)-p_{\epsilon}(b)||+\epsilon.\end{split}

(2.358)

Observe that if
||a-b||
is small enough, then also
||p_{\epsilon}(a)-p_{\epsilon}(b)||
is small. This means that to conclude the proof we just have to show that for each
\epsilon
there is a
\delta
such that if
|t-t_{0}|\leq\delta
, then

||\frac{i+D+S(t)}{i-D-S(t)}-\frac{i+D+S(t_{0})}{i-D-S(t_{0})}||\leq\epsilon.

(2.359)

Let us denote by
\tilde{D}:=D+S(t_{0})
and by
S_{t}:=S(t)-S(t_{0})
. Consider, then

\begin{split}\frac{i+\tilde{D}+S_{t}}{i-\tilde{D}-S_{t}}&=\frac{i+\tilde{D}}{i-\tilde{D}-S_{t}}+\frac{S_{t}}{i-\tilde{D}-S_{t}}\\
&=[\frac{i-\tilde{D}}{i+\tilde{D}}+\frac{S_{t}}{i+\tilde{D}}]^{-1}+\frac{S_{t}}{i-\tilde{D}-S}_{t}.\end{split}

(2.360)

We know that

\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}S_{t}=0.

(2.361)

Moreover, applying the Theorem 1.16 of
[
14
]
we have that

||(i-\tilde{D}-S_{t})^{-1}-(i-\tilde{D})^{-1}||\leq\frac{||(i-\tilde{D})^{-1}||^{2}\cdot||S_{t}||}{1-||(i-\tilde{D})^{-1}||\cdot||S_{t}||}

(2.362)

and so

\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}(i-\tilde{D}-S_{t})^{-1}=(i-\tilde{D})^{-1}.

(2.363)

Finally we have that (always applying
[
14
]
)

||[\frac{i-\tilde{D}}{i+\tilde{D}}+\frac{S_{t}}{i+\tilde{D}}]^{-1}-[\frac{i-\tilde{D}}{i+\tilde{D}}^{-1}]^{-1}||\leq\frac{||\frac{i+\tilde{D}}{i-\tilde{D}}^{-1}||^{2}\cdot||(i+\tilde{D})^{-1}\cdot||S_{t}||}{1-||\frac{i+\tilde{D}}{i-\tilde{D}}^{-1}||\cdot||(i+\tilde{D})^{-1}\cdot||S_{t}||}

(2.364)

and so

\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}[\frac{i-\tilde{D}}{i+\tilde{D}}+\frac{S_{t}}{i+\tilde{D}}]^{-1}=\frac{i-\tilde{D}}{i+\tilde{D}}.

(2.365)

Then, using (
2.361
), (
2.363
) and (
2.365
) we have that

\begin{split}\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}\frac{i+D+S(t)}{i-D+S(t)}&=\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}\frac{i+\tilde{D}+S_{t}}{i-\tilde{D}+S_{t}}\\
&=\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}\frac{i+\tilde{D}}{i-\tilde{D}}\\
&=\frac{i+D+S(t_{0})}{i-D+S(t_{0})}.\end{split}

(2.366)

And so we can conclude that

\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}\arctan(D+S(t))=\arctan(D+S(t_{0})).

(2.367)

∎

Theorem 2.4.10
.

Let us consider two manifolds of bounded geometry
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
and a group
\Gamma
which acts uniformly proper and free by isometries on
M
and
N
. Let
f_{0},f_{1}:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
two
C^{k}_{b}
-map which are
\Gamma
-equivariant lipschitz-homotopy equivalences. Suppose, moreover, that
f_{0}\sim_{\Gamma}f_{1}
. Then we have

[\rho_{f_{0}}]=[\rho_{f_{1}}]\in D^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}.

(2.368)

As consequence of this fact we have that for each lipschitz-homotopy equivalence
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
we can define a class in
D^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
which only depends on the lipschitz-homotopy class of
f
.

Proof.

Consider the odd case: if we are able to find a continuous curve
\gamma
in
D^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
such that for all
t\in[0,1]
is a projection,

\gamma(0)=\frac{1}{2}(\chi(D_{\alpha_{0},1,0})+1\mbox{ and }\gamma(1)=\frac{1}{2}(\chi(D_{\alpha_{0},1,1})+1.

(2.369)

Then obviously we can define

\gamma(t)=\frac{1}{2}(\chi(D_{\alpha_{0},1,t})+1

(2.370)

and the continuity of
\gamma
is given by the continuity of
\chi(D_{\alpha_{0},1,t}
.

Consider the even case: we have to find a continuous curve in
D^{*}_{\rho}(M\sqcup N,H_{M\sqcup N})^{\Gamma}
, where
H_{M\sqcup N}
and
\rho_{M\sqcup N}
are defined as in Example
2.1.7
such that for all
t\in[0,1]
is a unitary,

\gamma(0)=\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},0,-}\chi(D_{\alpha_{0},1,0})\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},0,+}^{*}\mbox{ and }\gamma(1)=\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},1,-}\chi(D_{\alpha_{0},1,1})\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},1,+}^{*}.

(2.371)

Let us consider the for each
t\in[0,1]

\gamma(t)=\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},t,-}\chi(D_{\alpha_{0},1,t})\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},t,+}^{*}

(2.372)

This is a curve in
D^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
. The continuity of
\gamma
is given by the continuity of
\chi(D_{\alpha_{0},1,t})
and
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha_{0},t,-}
. Let us recall Remark
37
: we have that, since
\gamma
is continuous on
D^{*}_{f}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
and unitary for each
t
in
[0,1]
, then it is also continuous in
D^{*}_{\rho}(M\sqcup N,H_{M\sqcup N})^{\Gamma}
and it is still unitary for each
t
in
[0,1]
.
∎

Definition 2.4.1
.

Let
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
be two manifolds of bounded geometry. Consider
\Gamma
a group that acts uniformly proper and free on
M
and on
N
by isometries. Suppose that
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
is a
\Gamma
- invariants lipschitz-homotopy equivalence. Then, if
dim(N)=n
we can define the
projected secondary index of

(M,f)
as the class in
K_{n+1}(D^{*}(N)^{\Gamma})
defined as

\rho(M,f):=f_{\star}(\rho_{\tilde{f}}),

(2.373)

where
\tilde{f}
is a
C^{k}_{b}
-approximation of
f
, where
k\geq 2
.

Observe that, if
\tilde{f}_{1}
and
\tilde{f}_{2}
are two
C^{k}_{b}
approximations of
f
, then
\tilde{f}_{1}\sim_{\Gamma}\tilde{f}_{2}
. Then applying Theorem
2.4.10
we have that the definition of
\rho(M,f)
doesn’t depend on the choice of the
C^{k}_{b}
approximation. Moreover we also have the following Corollary.

Corollary 2.4.11
.

Consider
f
,
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
as above. Then the projected secondary index
\rho(M,f)
only depends on
M
and on the lipschitz-homotopy class of
f
.

Appendix A
Inequalities between norms of differential forms

Lemma A.0.1
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold. Consider
p
in
M
: we have that on
T_{p}^{*}M
the norm
||\cdot||_{T^{*}_{p}(M)}
given by
g
and the operatorial norm

||\alpha_{p}||_{Op}=\sup\limits_{v_{p}\in B_{1}(0)\subset T_{p}M}|\alpha_{p}(v_{p})|,

(A.1)

are the same norm.

Proof.

Let
e_{i}
be an orthonormal basis of
T_{p}M
and let
\epsilon^{i}=g(e_{i},\cdot)
be the dual basis of
T_{p}^{*}M
. The basis
\{\epsilon^{i}\}
is an orthonormal basis. Indeed if
G
is the matrix of
g
related to
\{e_{i}\}
then

\begin{split}g(\epsilon^{i},\epsilon^{j})&=(Ge_{i})^{\tau}G^{-1}Ge_{j}\\
&=e_{i}^{\tau}G^{\tau}e_{j}=e_{i}^{\tau}Ge_{j}.\end{split}

(A.2)

So, if
\omega=\alpha_{j}\epsilon^{j}
then its
g
-norm is
\sqrt{\sum\limits_{j}\alpha_{j}^{2}}
.

Let
w=a^{i}e_{i}
be an unitary tangent vector (i.e.
\sqrt{\sum\limits_{i}(a^{i})^{2}}=1
), then

\begin{split}||\omega||_{Op}&=(\sup\limits_{w\in B_{1}(0)\subset T_{p}M}|\omega(w)|)\\
&=\sup\limits_{w\in B_{1}(0)\subset T_{p}M}|\alpha_{j}a^{i}\epsilon^{j}(e_{i})|\\
&=\sup\limits_{w\in B_{1}(0)\subset T_{p}M}|\alpha_{i}a^{i}|\\
&=\sup\limits_{w\in B_{1}(0)\subset T_{p}M}(\alpha,a),\end{split}

(A.3)

where
(\alpha,a)
is the euclidean scalar product in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
between
\alpha=(\alpha_{i})
amd
a=(a^{j})
. Following now the proof of Cauchy-Schwartz we have that if
a
is proportional to
\alpha
(i.e.
a=\frac{\alpha}{||\alpha||}
), then

\sup\limits_{w\in B_{1}(0)\subset T_{p}M}(\alpha,a)=(\alpha,\frac{\alpha}{||\alpha||})

(A.4)

So

\begin{split}||\omega||_{Op}&=||\alpha||_{eucl}\cdot||\frac{\alpha}{||\alpha||}||_{eucl}\\
&=||\alpha||_{eucl}1=||\omega||_{g}.\end{split}

(A.5)

∎

Lemma A.0.2
.

Consider two differential forms
\alpha
and
\beta
on a Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
. Then there is a constant
C
such that

|\alpha\wedge\beta|_{p}\leq C|\alpha|_{p}\cdot|\beta|_{p}

(A.6)

Proof.

It’s a direct consequence of Hadamard-Schwartz inequality
[
19
]
, which states that given
\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{k}
arbitrary exterior forms in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of degree
l_{1},...,l_{k}
, then there is a constant
C
that depends only by
n
such that

|\alpha_{1}\wedge...\wedge\alpha_{k}|\leq C|\alpha_{1}|\cdot...\cdot|\alpha_{k}|.

(A.7)

∎

Lemma A.0.3
.

Let
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
be a lipschitz map between Riemannian manifolds. Then for all
\alpha_{p}
in
T^{*}_{p}(N)
and for all
q
in
f^{-1}(p)
we have that there is a number
L

||f^{*}\alpha_{p}|^{2}_{\Lambda^{k}_{q}M}\leq L||\alpha_{p}||^{2}_{\Lambda^{k}_{p}N},

(A.8)

where
||\cdot||^{2}_{\Lambda^{k}_{x}X}
is the norm induced by the metric of
X
on
\Lambda^{k}_{x}X
.

Proof.

Let us suppose that
\alpha
is a
1
-form. We have that

\begin{split}||f^{*}\alpha_{p}||_{T^{*}_{q}M}&=\sup_{||v||=1}f^{*}\alpha_{p}(v)\\
&=\sup_{||v||=1}\alpha_{p}(f_{\star}v)\\
&\leq||\alpha_{p}||_{T^{*}_{p}N}\sup_{||v||=1}||f_{\star}v||\\
&\leq C_{f}||\alpha_{p}||_{T^{*}_{p}N}.\end{split}

(A.9)

Let us recall that a
k
-form in a point
q
can be always seen as sum of wedges of
k
orthogonal
1
-forms.
Then consider
\alpha=a^{i_{1}...i_{k}}\epsilon_{i_{1}}\wedge...\wedge\epsilon_{i_{k}}
an element of
\Lambda^{k}_{p}(N)
. We have that

||\alpha||_{\Lambda^{k}_{p}(N)}=\sum\limits_{i_{1}<...<i_{k}}|a^{i_{1}...i_{k}}|.

(A.10)

Observe that

\begin{split}||f^{*}\alpha||_{\Lambda^{k}_{q}(M)}&\leq\sum\limits_{i_{1}<...<i_{k}}|a^{i_{1}...i_{k}}|\cdot||f^{*}(\epsilon_{i_{1}})\wedge...\wedge f^{*}(\epsilon_{i_{k}})||\\
&\leq\sum\limits_{i_{1}<...<i_{k}}|a^{i_{1}...i_{k}}|\cdot C\cdot||f^{*}(\epsilon_{i_{1}})||\cdot...\cdot||f^{*}(\epsilon_{i_{k}})||\\
&\leq C\cdot C_{f}^{k}\sum\limits_{i_{1}<...<i_{k}}|a^{i_{1}...i_{k}}|=C\cdot C_{f}^{k}||\alpha||_{\Lambda^{k}_{p}(N)}.\end{split}

(A.11)

∎

Corollary A.0.4
.

Given a differential form
\alpha
on
N
and a lipshitz map
f:(M,g)\longrightarrow(N,h)
. Consider the function

\begin{split}N&\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\\
q&\longrightarrow||f^{*}\alpha||_{\Lambda_{q}^{*}M}.\end{split}

(A.12)

In the same way
||\alpha||_{\Lambda_{p}^{*}(N)}
can be seen as a function
M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
.
Then we have that

||f^{*}\alpha||_{\Lambda_{q}^{*}M}\leq K_{f}f^{*}(||\alpha||_{\Lambda_{p}^{*}(N)})(q).

(A.13)

as functions on
M
to
\mathbb{R}
, where
K_{f}
is the maximum between
1
,
C_{f}
,
C_{f}^{n}
, where
n=dim(N)
.

Appendix B
Orientation and integration along the fibers

In this Appendix we will introduce the notion of orientation for a fiber bundle and the integration along fibers. We are interested in this notions because we have the following result of Ehresmann:

Theorem B.0.1

(Ehresmann lemma)
.

Given a smooth map
p:E\longrightarrow N
between smooth manifolds, if
p
is

•

a surjective submersion

•

a proper map

then there is a smooth manifold
F
such that
(E,p,N,F)
is a fiber bundle.

The following part is taken from
[
9
]
. Let
\mathcal{B}=(E,\pi,B,F)
be a smooth fiber bundle with
dimB=n
,
dimF=r
. Recall that the fibre
F_{x}
, at
x\in B
is a submanifold of
E
and denote the inclusion by
j_{x}:F\longrightarrow E
.

Definition B.0.1
.

The bundle
B
is called orientable if there exists an
r
-form
\psi
on
E
such that
j_{x}^{*}\psi
orients
F
, for every
x\in B
. An equivalence class of such
r
-forms is called an
orientation
for the bundle and a member of the equivalence class is said to represent the orientation.

Let now
f:X\longrightarrow Y
be an orientable fiber bundle with fiber
F
. If
\partial X=\emptyset
, then we will denote by
\Omega^{*}_{vc}(X)
the space of differential forms
\alpha
such that if
K
is a compact set of
Y
then
supp(\alpha)\cap f^{-1}(K)
is a compact set. If
X
has a boundary, then we define
\Omega^{*}_{vc}(X)
as
\Omega^{*}_{vc}(X\setminus\partial X)
.

Consider
\alpha_{p}\in\Lambda^{*}(X)
and let
v_{p}
be a tangent vector in
p\in X
. We denote by
i_{v_{p}}\alpha_{p}
the contraction of
\alpha_{p}
with
v_{p}
.

Definition B.0.2
.

Consider a submersion between differentiable manifolds
p:X\longrightarrow Y
. We denote by
\Omega_{vc}^{*}(X)
the space of
vertically compactly supported smooth forms
the set of differential form
\alpha
on
X
such that, for each compact set
K
, we have that
p^{-1}(K)\cap supp(\alpha)
is a compact set.

Definition B.0.3
.

Consider a fiber bundle
f:X\longrightarrow Y
and let
F
be a generic fiber over a point
p
in
Y
. The
integration along the fibers
is the map
f_{\star}:\Omega^{*}_{vc}(X)\longrightarrow\Omega^{*-r}(Y)
defined for all
\alpha
in
\Omega^{*}_{vc}(X)
as

f_{\star}(\alpha)_{p}(v_{1},...v_{*-r}):=\int_{F_{p}}j_{p}^{*}(i_{\tilde{v_{1}}}...i_{\tilde{v}_{*-r}}\alpha),

(B.1)

where
\tilde{v}_{j}
are some vectors such that
f_{\star}(\tilde{v}_{j})=v_{j}
. We will denote
f_{\star}(\alpha)
also as

\int_{F}\alpha.

(B.2)

Remark 47
.

The definition above doesn’t require a choice for the lifts of
v_{j}
s. Indeed if we use
\tilde{v_{j}}+w_{j}
where
w_{j}\in ker(f_{\star})
instead of
v_{j}
, one can easily check that

j_{p}^{*}((\tilde{v_{1}}+w_{1})\dashv...(\tilde{v}_{*-r}+w_{*-r})\dashv\alpha)=j_{p}^{*}(\tilde{v_{1}}\dashv...\tilde{v}_{*-r}\dashv\alpha)+j_{p}^{*}(\beta)

(B.3)

where the differential form
j_{p}^{*}(\beta)
is a form in
\Omega^{*}(F)
which is not a top-degree form. This means that

\int_{F}j_{p}^{*}(\beta)=0.

(B.4)

Remark 48
.

If
f:X\longrightarrow Y
is a proper surjective submersion, then
f_{\star}
is well defined for each
\alpha
in
\Omega^{*}(X)
even if
\partial X\neq\emptyset
.

Now we introduce some important properties of the integration along fibers. One can find all the proofs in
[
9
]
, in particular in chapter 7, section 5.

Proposition B.0.2

(Projection Formula and Fubini Theorem)
.

The map
f_{\star}
is surjective and, in particular if
\alpha
is in
\Omega^{*}(Y)
and
\beta
in
\Omega^{*}_{vc}(X)
then

\int_{F}f^{*}\alpha\wedge\beta=\alpha\wedge\int_{F}\beta,

(B.5)

and

\int_{X}\beta=\int_{Y}(\int_{F}\beta).

(B.6)

Remark 49
.

The proof of the Projection Formula for a vector bundle
(E,\pi M)
given
[
1
]
holds even if
(E,\pi,M)
is a fiber bundle. In particular, following the proof, it’s clear that if
\beta
is in
\Omega^{*}(X)\setminus\Omega^{*}_{vc}(X)
, but
f_{\star}\beta
is well-defined, then Projection Formula still holds.

Proposition B.0.3
.

The integration along fibers
f_{\star}:\Omega^{*}_{vc}(X)\longrightarrow\Omega^{*-r}(Y)
satisfies the following equalities for all vector field
W
in
Y
and for all vector field
Z
in
X
which is
f
-related to
W

•

i_{W}\circ q_{F}=q_{F}\circ i_{Z}

•

\Theta_{W}\circ q_{F}=q_{F}\circ\Theta_{Z}

•

d\circ q_{F}=q_{F}\circ d

where
i_{W}
(and
i_{Z}
) is the inner product with
W
(resp.
Z
),
\Theta_{W}
(or
\Theta_{Z}
) is the Lie derivative along
W
(resp.
Z
) and
d
is the exterior derivative.

Appendix C
K-Theory

Consider a
*
-algebra
A
.

Definition C.0.1
.

A
C^{*}
-algebra is a Banach algebra such that

||x^{*}x||=||x||^{2}

(C.1)

for each
x
in
A
.

There is an equivalent definition of
C^{*}
-algebra.

Definition C.0.2
.

A
C^{*}
-algebra
is a
*
-algebra which is isometrically
*
-isomorphic to a norm-closed
*
-subalgebra of
B(H)
where
H
is an Hilbert space.

Our goal, in this Appendix, is to introduce the notion of K-theory groups of a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
and to show the main properties.

Definition C.0.3
.

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Consider
A\subseteq B(H)
for some Hilbert space
H
. A
C^{*}
-algebra
A
is said to be
unital
if it contains the identity operator.

Definition C.0.4
.

We define the
unitalization of

A
as the
C^{*}
-algebra
\tilde{A}
generated by
A
and the identity operator of
B(H)
.

Remark 50
.

Let
A
and
B
be two
C^{*}
-algebras, then a
*
-morphism
\phi:A\longrightarrow B
can be extended in a unique way to a
*
-morphism
\Phi:\tilde{A}\longrightarrow\tilde{B}
imposing
\Phi(1)=1
.

Observe that if
A
already contains the identity operator, then
\tilde{A}=A
.

As shown in
[
10
]
, we have a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow\tilde{A}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow 0.

(C.2)

We use this sequence later, to define the K-theory groups of a non-unital
C^{*}
-algebra.

Definition C.0.5
.

Let us consider a ring
A
and the module
M_{n}(A)
given by the matrices with coefficient in
A
. An element
p
of
M_{n}(A)
is a
projection
if
p=p^{2}
.

Definition C.0.6
.

Let us consider a unital
C^{*}
-algebra. The
K-Theory of
A
is the group
K_{0}(A)
whose generators are
[p]
where
p
is a projection of
M_{n}(A)
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

•

if
p
and
q
are projections in
M_{n}(A)
and if there is a continuous path
\alpha:[0,1]\longrightarrow M_{n}(A)
such that
\alpha(0)=p
,
\alpha(1)=q
and
\alpha(t)
is a projection for every
t
in
(0,1)
then
[p]=[q]
.

•

[0_{n}]=0_{K_{0}(A)}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
0_{n}
is the null matrix of
M_{n}(A)
and
0_{K_{0}(A)}
is the identity element of
K_{0}(A)
.

•

if
[p]\in M_{n}(A)
and
[q]\in M_{m}(A)
then
[p]+[q]=[p\oplus q]
where
[p\oplus q]
is the matrix in
M_{n+m}(A)
given by

{\begin{pmatrix}p&0\\
0&q\end{pmatrix}}.

(C.3)

Given a
*
-homomorphism
\phi:A\longrightarrow B
between
C^{*}
-algebras, there is a map
\phi_{\star}:K_{0}(A)\longrightarrow K_{0}(B)
such that for all
a
in
M_{n}(A)
we have that

\phi_{\star}[a]=[\phi_{n}(a)],

(C.4)

where
\phi_{n}(a)
is the
n\times n
matrix defined as
\phi_{n}(a)_{i,j}:=\phi(a_{i,j})
. This map induces a morphism
\phi_{\star}
between the
K_{0}
groups of
A
and
B
.

Definition C.0.7
.

Let
A
a non-unital
C^{*}
-algebra. Then we define the
K-Theory of
A
as the group
K_{0}(A)
defined as the kernel of

\phi_{\star}:K_{0}(\tilde{A})\longrightarrow K_{0}(\mathbb{C})

(C.5)

where
\phi:\tilde{A}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is the map in (
C.2
)

The proof of the following proposition can be find in Remark 4.1.2 of
[
10
]
.

Proposition C.0.1
.

In particular we have a covariant functor
\mathcal{K}
between the category
\mathring{A}
which has the unital
C^{*}
-algebras as objects and unital
*
-homomorphism as arrows and the category
Grp
which has groups has objects and morphisms of groups as arrows. This covariant functor is defined as

\begin{cases}\mathcal{K}(A):=K_{0}(A)\\
\mathcal{K}(A\xrightarrow{\phi}B):=K_{0}(A)\xrightarrow{\phi_{\star}}K_{0}(B).\end{cases}

(C.6)

Remark 51
.

The functor can be extend to non-unital
C^{*}
-algebras defining for each
*
-morphism
\phi:A\longrightarrow B
, the group morphism

\Phi_{\star|_{K_{0}(A)}}:K_{0}(A)\longrightarrow K_{0}(B),

(C.7)

where
\Phi:\tilde{A}\longrightarrow\tilde{B}
is the
*
-morphism induced by
\phi
.

Consider an unital
C^{*}
-algebra
A
and
J
an ideal of
A
. If we have the short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow J\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow\frac{A}{J}\longrightarrow 0

(C.8)

Then by Proposition 4.3.15 of
[
10
]
also the sequence

K_{0}(J)\longrightarrow K_{0}(A)\longrightarrow K_{0}(\frac{A}{B})

(C.9)

is exact.

Definition C.0.8
.

Let us consider a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
. The
suspension of
A
is the set
S(A)
of continuous functions
f:[0,1]\longrightarrow A
such that
f(0)=f(1)=0
and the structure of
C^{*}
-algebra induced by
A
.

Definition C.0.9
.

Let us consider a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
and
p\in\mathbb{N}
. The
p
-th group of K-Theory
is the group

K_{p}(A)=K_{0}(S^{p}(A)),

(C.10)

where
S^{1}(A)=S(A)
and
S^{p}(A)=S(S^{p-1}(A))
.

Remark 52
.

Consider two
C^{*}
-algebras
A
and
B
and let
\psi:A\longrightarrow B
a
*
-homomorphism. Then we can define the map

\begin{split}S(\psi):S(A)&\longrightarrow S(B)\\
\gamma&\longrightarrow\phi\circ\gamma.\end{split}

(C.11)

Moreover, we define
S^{n}(\psi)
as
S(S^{n-1}(\psi))
.

We can observe that if
\psi
is injective, then also
S(\psi)
is injective. Moreover, if
J
is an ideal of
A
and
i:J\rightarrow A
is the inclusion, then also
S(i):S(J)\longrightarrow S(A)
is the inclusion of
S(J)
in
S(A)
.

Let us denote by
\pi:A\longrightarrow\frac{A}{J}
be the quotient map.

Then we have that

\frac{S(A)}{S(J)}\cong S(\frac{A}{J}).

(C.12)

To prove this fact we can consider the map
\phi:\frac{S(A)}{S(J)}\longrightarrow S(\frac{A}{J})
as follow: given a class of path
[\alpha]
in
\frac{S(A)}{S(J)}
we have that

\phi([\alpha]):=\pi\circ\alpha.

(C.13)

The map
\phi
is well defined, indeed if

[\beta]=[\alpha]\implies\alpha=\beta+\eta

(C.14)

where
\eta\in S(I)
but this means that
\pi\circ\alpha=\pi\circ\beta+\pi\circ\eta=\pi\circ\beta
.

Moreover
\phi
is also injective: if
[\alpha]\neq[\beta]
then it means that there is a
t_{0}\in[0,1]
such that

\alpha(t_{0})-\beta(t_{0})\notin J.

(C.15)

This implies that

\pi\circ\alpha(t_{0})-\beta(t_{0})\neq 0

(C.16)

and so

\phi([\alpha])\neq\psi([\beta]).

(C.17)

Finally
\phi
is surjective. Consider a curve

\gamma:[0,1]\longrightarrow\frac{A}{J}

(C.18)

such that
\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)=[0]
: by Lemma 4.3.13. of
[
10
]
we have that it can be lifted to a path

\tilde{\gamma}:[0,1]\longrightarrow A

(C.19)

such that
\tilde{\gamma}(0)=0
. We also know, since
\pi\circ\tilde{\gamma}=\gamma
that
\tilde{\gamma}(1)\in J
. Let us define the curve
\sigma:[0,1]\longrightarrow J
as

\sigma(t)=t\cdot\tilde{\gamma}(1).

(C.20)

Then we can observe that

\eta=\tilde{\gamma}-\sigma

(C.21)

is a curve in
A
such that
\eta(0)=\eta(1)=0
and
\pi(\eta)=\pi(\tilde{\gamma})=\gamma
. Then we have that

\phi([\eta])=\gamma.

(C.22)

Finally, if
P:S(A)\longrightarrow\frac{S(A)}{S(J)}
is the quotient map, then one can easily check that

S(\pi)=\psi\circ P

(C.23)

This means that, identifying
\frac{S^{n}(A)}{S^{n}(J)}
with
S^{n}\frac{A}{J}
, then we can also identify
S^{n}(\pi)
and the projection
S^{n}(A)\longrightarrow\frac{S^{n}(A)}{S^{n}(J)}
. Then if

0\rightarrow J\xrightarrow{i}A\xrightarrow{\pi}\frac{A}{J}\rightarrow 0

(C.24)

is an exact sequence, then also the sequence

0\rightarrow S^{n}(J)\xrightarrow{S^{n}(i)}S^{n}(A)\xrightarrow{S^{n}(\pi)}S^{n}(\frac{A}{J})\rightarrow 0

(C.25)

is exact. Then using (
C.9
) we have that

K_{n}(J)\xrightarrow{S^{n}(i)_{\star}}K_{n}(A)\xrightarrow{S^{n}(\pi)_{\star}}K_{n}(\frac{A}{J})

(C.26)

is exact.

There is another way to define
K_{1}(A)
.

Definition C.0.10
.

Given a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, a matrix
u
in
M_{n}(A)
we define the
adjoint
of
u
as the matrix
u^{*}
given by the transposition
u^{\tau}
of
u
and applying the involution of
A
in each component of
u^{\tau}
.

We have that the matrix
u
is
unitary
if it is invertible and

u^{-1}=u^{*}.

(C.27)

Definition C.0.11
.

Let
A
be an unital
C^{*}
-algebra. Then
K_{1}^{u}(A)
is the group whose generators are
[u]
where
u
is a unitary matrix of
M_{n}(A)
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

•

if there is a continuous path
\alpha:[0,1]\longrightarrow M_{n}(A)
such that
\alpha(0)=u
,
\alpha(1)=v
and for all
t
in
[0,1]
,
\alpha(t)
is a unitary matrix then
[u]=[v]
.

•

the identity matrices are all in the class which is the identity element of
K_{1}^{u}(A)
.

•

if
[u]\in M_{n}(A)
and
[v]\in M_{m}(A)
then
[u]+[v]=[u\oplus v]
where
[u\oplus v]
is the matrix in
M_{n+m}(A)
given by

{\begin{pmatrix}u&0\\
0&v\end{pmatrix}}

(C.28)

Remark 53
.

As the authors say on pg.107 in
[
10
]
, we have that also the
K_{1}
groups of non unital
C^{*}
-algebras can be descripted by unitaries. In particular one have to consider the unitary matrixes over
\tilde{A}
which are equal to the identity matrix modulo
A
.

Remark 54
.

If
\phi:A\longrightarrow B
is a
*
-homomorphism, then one can define for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
the map between the space of unitary
n\times n
matrices

\begin{split}\phi^{u}:U(A)_{n}&\longrightarrow U(B)_{n}\\
v=(v_{i,j})&\longrightarrow(\phi(v_{i,j}))\end{split}

(C.29)

where
(\phi(v_{i,j}))
is defined applying
\phi
to each entry of
v
.

This maps induce a morphism

\phi^{u}_{\star}:K_{1}^{u}(A)\longrightarrow K_{1}^{u}(B)

(C.30)

defined on the generators
[v]
of
K_{1}^{u}(A)
as
[u_{\phi}(v)]
. Obviously the functorial properties

\phi^{u}_{\star}\circ\sigma^{u}_{\star}=(\phi\circ\sigma)^{u}_{\star}

(C.31)

and

id^{u}_{A,\star}=id_{K_{0}^{u}(A)}

(C.32)

hold.

Proposition C.0.2
.

Let
A
be an unitary
C^{*}
-algebra. The groups
K_{1}(A)
and
K_{1}^{u}(A)
are isomorphic.

One can find the proof of this fact in
[
10
]
. The isomorphism, in particular, is in Proposition 4.8.2. In this Proposition they consider, without loss of generality, as generators of
K_{0}(S(A))
the classes
[p]
where
p
is a loop of projections in
M_{2m}(A)
based in
1_{m}\oplus 0_{m}
. Then they prove that any such loop
p(t)
can be written as

p(t)=u(t)p(0)u^{*}(t)

(C.33)

where
u:[0,1]\longrightarrow M_{n}(A)
is, for all
t\in[0,1]
unitary.
Since
u(0)
commute with
p(0)=1_{m}\oplus 0_{m}
, then
u(0)
has the form

u(0)=\begin{bmatrix}v&0\\
0&w\end{bmatrix}.

(C.34)

At this point they define the map
\mathcal{A}_{A}:K_{0}(S(A))\longrightarrow K_{0}^{u}(A)
posing

\mathcal{A}_{A}[p(t)]=[v].

(C.35)

Lemma C.0.3
.

Consider a
*
-homomorphism
\phi:A\longrightarrow B
. Then we have that

\phi^{u}_{\star}=\mathcal{A}_{B}\circ(S\phi)_{\star}\circ\mathcal{A}_{A}^{-1}.

(C.36)

Proof.

It is enough to observe that

\phi^{u}_{\star}[v]=[\phi^{u}(v)]=[(\phi(v_{i,j})_{i,j})]

(C.37)

and

\mathcal{A}_{B}\circ(S\phi)_{\star}\circ\mathcal{A}_{A}^{-1}[v]=\mathcal{A}_{B}\circ(S\phi)_{\star}[V]

(C.38)

where
V
is the constant curve based on the matrix

\begin{bmatrix}vv^{*}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}v&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}v^{*}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}.

(C.39)

Then we can observe that

(S\phi)_{\star}[V]=[\phi\circ V]=\begin{bmatrix}\phi(v)\phi(v)^{*}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\phi(v)&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\phi(v)^{*}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}

(C.40)

and so we can conclude

\mathcal{A}_{B}\circ(S\phi)_{\star}\circ\mathcal{A}_{A}^{-1}[v]=\mathcal{A}_{B}\circ(S\phi)_{\star}[V]=[(\phi(v_{i,j})_{i,j})]=\phi^{u}_{\star}[v].

(C.41)

∎

Similarly to the singular homology case, there is a connecting homomorphism
\delta:K_{n}(\frac{A}{B})\longrightarrow K_{n-1}(J)
which given a long exact sequence associated to the short exact sequence (
C.9
).

To define the connecting homomorphism we have to introduce a particular
C^{*}
-algebra.

Definition C.0.12
.

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. Then the
mapping cone of the projection
\pi:A\longrightarrow\frac{A}{J}
is the set

C(A,A/J):=\{(a,f)|a\in A,f:[0,1]\longrightarrow\frac{A}{J}\mbox{ is continuous, }f(0)=0,f(1)=\pi(a)\}

(C.42)

with the
C^{*}
-algebra structure induced by
A
.

Remark 55
.

The Proposition 4.5.3. of
[
10
]
states

K_{0}(J)\cong K_{0}(C(A,A/J)).

(C.43)

The isomorphism between the
K_{0}
-groups is induced by the
*
-homomorphisms

\begin{split}\psi_{J}:J&\longrightarrow C(A,\frac{A}{J})\\
b&\longrightarrow(b,0).\end{split}

(C.44)

Consider now another
C^{*}
-algebra
B
, an ideal
J
of
B
and a
*
-homomorphism

\phi:B\longrightarrow A

(C.45)

such that
\phi(I)\subseteq J
and denote by
\Phi:\frac{B}{I}\longrightarrow\frac{A}{J}
the
*
-homomorphism induced by
\phi
.

We can define the map

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{\phi}:C(B,\frac{B}{I})&\longrightarrow C(A,\frac{A}{J})\\
(a,\gamma)&\longrightarrow(\phi(a),\Phi\circ\gamma).\end{split}

(C.46)

Then it’s easy to see that

\psi_{J}\circ\phi=\mathcal{I}_{\Phi}\circ\psi_{I}.

(C.47)

This means that in K-Theory, considering
\phi_{\star}:K_{0}(I)\longrightarrow K_{0}(J)
, we have that

\phi_{\star}=\psi_{J,\star}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\Phi,\star}\circ\psi_{I,\star}.

(C.48)

Remark 56
.

Let us define the
*
-homomorphism
\Delta
given by

\begin{split}\Delta:S(\frac{A}{J})&\longrightarrow C(A,A/J)\\
f&\longrightarrow(0,f).\end{split}

(C.49)

Then we can define the transgression map
\delta
as the map

\delta:=\Delta_{\star}:K_{1}(\frac{A}{J})=K_{0}(S(\frac{A}{J}))\longrightarrow K_{0}(C(A,A/J))\cong K_{0}(J).

(C.50)

Moreover, using that
\frac{S(A)}{S(J)}\cong S(\frac{A}{J})
, finally we define
\delta
as

\delta:=\Delta_{\star}:K_{n+1}(\frac{A}{J})\longrightarrow K_{n}(J).

(C.51)

Now, following Proposition 4.5.9 of
[
10
]
, one can prove that

...\rightarrow K_{n}(A)\rightarrow K_{n}(\frac{A}{J})\rightarrow K_{n-1}(J)\rightarrow K_{n-1}(A)\rightarrow...\rightarrow K_{0}(\frac{A}{J})

(C.52)

is a long exact sequence. Moreover we also have the following classical result

Proposition C.0.4

(Bott periodicity)
.

Given a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, then
K_{0}(A)\cong K_{2}(A)
.

This means that the long exact sequence above can be seen as the sequence

\begin{matrix}K_{0}(A)&&\longrightarrow K_{0}(B)\longrightarrow&&K_{0}(C)\\
\uparrow&&&&\downarrow\\
K_{1}(C)&&\longleftarrow K_{1}(B)\longleftarrow&&K_{1}(A)\end{matrix}

(C.53)

where the vertical arrows are the connecting homomorphisms.

Remark 57
.

Starting by now, given a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, we will consider
K_{1}(A)=K_{1}^{u}(A)
and not as
K_{0}(S(\frac{A}{J}))
. This means that the transgression map
\delta:K_{1}(\frac{A}{J})\longrightarrow K_{0}(J)
, for us, will be

\delta=\psi_{J,\star}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{\star}\circ\mathcal{A}^{-1}_{\frac{A}{J},\star},

(C.54)

where
\mathcal{A}_{\frac{A}{J},\star}
is the isomorphism between
K_{0}(S(\frac{A}{J}))\longrightarrow K_{0}^{u}(\frac{A}{J})
and
\psi_{\star}
is the isomorphism
\psi_{J,\star}:K_{0}(J)\longrightarrow K_{0}(C(A,A/J))
.

Moreover, following pg. 110 of
[
10
]
, the map which gives Bott periodicity

\beta:K_{0}(\frac{A}{J})\longrightarrow K_{0}^{u}(S(\frac{A}{J}))=K_{1}(S(\frac{A}{J}))

(C.55)

may be defined sending a projection
p\in M_{n}(A)
in the unitary loop given by

\beta([p]):=[p\overline{z}+1-p],

(C.56)

where
\overline{z}:S^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is the identity function on the unit circle.

This means that, for us, the transgression map
\delta:K_{0}(\frac{A}{J})\longrightarrow K_{1}(J)
will be

\delta=\mathcal{A}_{J,\star}\circ\psi_{S(A),S(J),\star}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{S(A),S(J),\star}\circ\mathcal{A}_{S(\frac{A}{J}),\star}^{-1}\beta

(C.57)

Indeed we have that

\mathcal{A}_{S(\frac{A}{J}),\star}^{-1}:K_{1}^{u}(S(\frac{A}{J}))=K_{1}(S(\frac{A}{J}))\longrightarrow K_{0}(SS\frac{A}{J}),

(C.58)

\Delta_{S(A),S(J),\star}:K_{0}(SS\frac{A}{J})=K_{0}(S(\frac{SA}{SJ}))\longrightarrow K_{0}(C(S(A),S(A)/S(J)))

(C.59)

and

\psi_{S(A),S(J),\star}:K_{0}(C(S(A),S(A)/S(J)))\longrightarrow K_{0}(S(J)).

(C.60)

Then applying
\mathcal{A}_{J,\star}
we obtain a class in
K_{1}(J)=K_{0}^{u}(J)
.

Finally if
i:B\longrightarrow A
is such that
i(I)\subseteq J
and there is a map
\iota:\frac{B}{I}\longrightarrow\frac{A}{J}
induced by
i
which is injective, then we have

\beta_{A}\circ\iota_{\star}=\iota^{u}_{\star}\circ\beta_{B}.

(C.61)

Lemma C.0.5
.

Consider two
C^{*}
-algebras
A
and
B
and let
J
be an ideal of
A
and
I
be an ideal of
B
. Then consider a
*
-homomorphism
i:B\longrightarrow A
such that
i(I)\subseteq J
. Then, if
\iota:\frac{B}{I}\longrightarrow\frac{A}{J}
is the map induced by
i
and we denote by
\delta_{A}
and
\delta_{B}
the transgression maps, we have that

\delta_{A}\circ\iota^{u}_{\star}=i_{\star}\circ\delta_{B},

(C.62)

if we consider
\delta_{A}:K_{1}(\frac{A}{J})\longrightarrow K_{0}(J)
and

\delta_{A}\circ\iota_{\star}=i^{u}_{\star}\circ\delta_{B}

(C.63)

if we consider
\delta_{A}:K_{0}(\frac{A}{J})\longrightarrow K_{1}(J)
.

Proof.

First we will prove (
C.62
). Recall that because of (
C.54
), that

\delta_{A}=\psi_{J,\star}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{A,\star}\circ\mathcal{A}^{-1}_{\frac{A}{J},\star}.

(C.64)

Moreover we can observe that, because of Lemma
C.0.3
, we have that

\iota^{u}_{\star}=\mathcal{A}_{\frac{A}{J},\star}\circ(S\iota)_{\star}\circ\mathcal{A}^{-1}_{\frac{B}{I},\star}.

(C.65)

Finally, applying the equality (
C.48
) we obtain

i_{\star}=\psi^{-1}_{J,\star}\circ\mathcal{I}_{i,\star}\circ\psi_{I,\star}.

(C.66)

Then we have that

\begin{split}\delta_{A}\circ\iota^{u}_{\star}&=\psi_{J,\star}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{A,\star}\circ\mathcal{A}^{-1}_{\frac{A}{J},\star}\circ\mathcal{A}_{\frac{A}{J},\star}\circ(S\iota)_{\star}\circ\mathcal{A}^{-1}_{\frac{B}{I},\star}\\
&=\psi_{J,\star}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{A,\star}\circ(S\iota)_{\star}\circ\mathcal{A}^{-1}_{\frac{B}{I},\star}\end{split}

(C.67)

and

\begin{split}i_{\star}\circ\delta_{B}&=\psi^{-1}_{J,\star}\circ\mathcal{I}_{i,\star}\circ\psi_{I,\star}\circ\psi_{I,\star}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{B,\star}\circ\mathcal{A}^{-1}_{\frac{B}{I},\star}\\
&=\psi^{-1}_{J,\star}\circ\mathcal{I}_{i,\star}\circ\Delta_{B,\star}\circ\mathcal{A}^{-1}_{\frac{B}{I},\star}\end{split}

(C.68)

Then we have that

\begin{split}&\delta_{A}\circ\iota^{u}_{\star}=i_{\star}\circ\delta_{B}\iff\\
&\psi_{J,\star}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{A,\star}\circ(S\iota)_{\star}\circ\mathcal{A}^{-1}_{\frac{B}{I},\star}=\psi^{-1}_{J,\star}\circ\mathcal{I}_{i,\star}\circ\Delta_{B,\star}\circ\mathcal{A}^{-1}_{\frac{B}{I},\star}\iff\\
&\Delta_{A,\star}\circ(S\iota)_{\star}=\mathcal{I}_{i,\star}\circ\Delta_{B,\star}.\end{split}

(C.69)

Consider
\alpha
in
S(\frac{B}{I})
: we have that

\Delta_{A,\star}\circ(S\iota)_{\star}(\alpha)=(0,\iota\circ\alpha)=\mathcal{I}_{i,\star}\circ\Delta_{B,\star}\alpha.

(C.70)

This means that all the identities in (
C.69
) hold and we proved (
C.62
).

Let us prove (
C.63
). Since (
C.57
) we have that

\delta_{A}=\mathcal{A}_{J,\star}\circ\psi_{S(A),S(J),\star}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{A,\star}\circ\mathcal{A}_{S(\frac{A}{J}),\star}^{-1}\circ\beta_{A},

(C.71)

where
\beta
is the Bott isomorphism. We know, since (
C.61
), that

\beta_{A}\circ\iota_{\star}=\iota^{u}_{\star}\circ\beta_{B}.

(C.72)

This means that

\begin{split}\delta_{A}\circ\iota_{\star}&=\mathcal{A}_{J,\star}\circ\psi_{S(A),S(J),\star}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{A,\star}\circ\mathcal{A}_{S(\frac{A}{J}),\star}^{-1}\circ\beta_{A}\circ\iota_{\star}\\
&=\mathcal{A}_{J,\star}\circ\psi_{S(A),S(J),\star}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{A,\star}\circ\mathcal{A}_{S(\frac{A}{J}),\star}^{-1}\circ\iota^{u}_{\star}\circ\beta_{B}\\
&=\mathcal{A}_{J,\star}\circ\psi_{S(A),S(J),\star}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{A,\star}\circ S(\iota)_{\star}\circ\mathcal{A}_{S(\frac{B}{I}),\star}^{-1}\circ\beta_{B}.\end{split}

(C.73)

and

\begin{split}i^{u}_{\star}\circ\delta_{B}&=\mathcal{A}_{J,\star}\circ(Si)_{\star}\circ\mathcal{A}_{I,\star}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{A}_{I,\star}\circ\psi_{S(B),S(I),\star}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{B,\star}\circ\mathcal{A}_{S(\frac{B}{I}),\star}^{-1}\circ\beta_{B}\\
&=\mathcal{A}_{J,\star}\circ S(i)_{\star}\circ\psi_{S(B),S(I),\star}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{B,\star}\circ\mathcal{A}_{S(\frac{B}{I}),\star}^{-1}\circ\beta_{B}.\end{split}

(C.74)

We can observe that
S(i)(S(I))\subseteq S(J)
. This means that we can apply the Remark 6 and we have that

S(i)_{\star}=\psi^{-1}_{S(A),S(J),\star}\circ\mathcal{I}_{S(i),\star}\circ\psi_{S(B),S(I),\star}.

(C.75)

This means that

\begin{split}i^{u}_{\star}\circ\delta_{B}&=\mathcal{A}_{J,\star}\circ\psi^{-1}_{S(A),S(J),\star}\circ\mathcal{I}_{S(i),\star}\circ\psi_{S(B),S(I),\star}\circ\psi_{S(B),S(I),\star}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{B,\star}\circ\beta_{B}\\
&=\mathcal{A}_{J,\star}\circ\psi^{-1}_{S(A),S(J),\star}\circ\mathcal{I}_{S(i),\star}\circ\Delta_{B,\star}\circ\mathcal{A}_{S(\frac{B}{I}),\star}^{-1}\circ\beta_{B}.\end{split}

(C.76)

Then we have that

\begin{split}&\delta_{A}\circ\iota_{\star}=i^{u}_{\star}\circ\delta_{B}\iff\\
&\Delta_{A,\star}\circ S(i)_{\star}=\mathcal{I}_{S(i),\star}\circ\Delta_{B,\star}.\end{split}

(C.77)

Using then a simple computation, as we did proving (
C.62
), one can check that the last equality holds. So we proved (
C.63
).
∎

Appendix D
Smoothing operators

In this section we will focus on integral operators. In particular, given two complete Riemannian manifolds
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
and give an integral operator
A:\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
we introduce conditions on its kernel that make
A
a bounded operator. Moreover we will study the kernel of the operator
dA
and
Ad
, where
d
is the closed extension of the exterior derivative. We have used this tools in section
2.3
to prove that
T_{f}
,
dT_{f}
and
T_{f}y
and other combinations of these operators are in
C_{f}^{*}(M\sqcup N)^{\Gamma}
.

In this section we will denote by

\int_{N}f(x)d\mu_{X}

(D.1)

the Bochner integral of a measurable function
f:(X,\mu_{X})\longrightarrow B
, where
(X,\mu_{X})
is a measured space and
B
is a Banach space, and we will denote by
a_{\star}
the operator of integration along the fibers of a submersion
a
.

Definition D.0.1
.

Consider two complete Riemannian manifolds
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
. Let us denote by
pr_{N}
the projection
pr_{N}:M\times N\longrightarrow N
and by
pr_{M}
the projection on the first component. Let us define the bundle on
M\times N
given by

\Lambda^{*}(M)\boxtimes\Lambda(N):=pr_{M}^{*}(\Lambda^{*}(M))\otimes pr_{N}^{*}(\Lambda(N)),

(D.2)

where
\Lambda(N):=\Lambda(TN)
is the dual bundle of
\Lambda^{*}(N)
.

Remark 58
.

Consider two differential manifolds
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
and consider for
i=1,2
the projections
pr_{i}:X_{1}\times X_{2}\longrightarrow X_{i}
. Then we have that

pr_{1}^{*}(\Lambda^{*}(X_{1}))\cong\Lambda^{*}_{\star,0}(X_{1}\times X_{2})\subset\Lambda^{*}(X_{1}\times X_{2})

(D.3)

where
\Lambda^{*}_{\star,0}(X_{1}\times X_{2})
is the subbundle of
\Lambda^{*}(X_{1}\times X_{2})
given by the forms

\alpha_{(p,q)}\in\Lambda^{*}_{(p,q)}(X_{1}\times X_{2})

(D.4)

such that

ker(\alpha_{(p,q)})\supseteq(\{0\}\oplus T_{q}X_{2})\times...\times(\{0\}\oplus T_{q}X_{2})

(D.5)

The isomorphism is the following: for all
(s,q)
in
X_{1}\times X_{2}
if

\alpha_{s,q}:T_{s}X_{1}\times...\times T_{s}X_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

(D.6)

is an element of
pr_{1}^{*}(\Lambda^{*}(X_{1}))
, the we can define
\overline{\alpha}
as the application

\overline{\alpha}_{s,q}:(T_{s}X_{1}\oplus T_{q}X_{2})\times...\times(T_{s}X_{1}\oplus T_{q}X_{2})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

(D.7)

defined as

\overline{\alpha}_{s,q}((v_{1,1}+v_{1,2}),...,(v_{k,1}+v_{k,2}))=\begin{cases}\alpha_{s,q}(v_{1,1},...,v_{k,1})\mbox{ if all }v_{j,2}=0\\
0\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(D.8)

The inverse map is given by the restriction of
\overline{\alpha}_{s,q}
to
(T_{s}X_{1}\oplus\{0\})\times...\times(T_{s}X_{1}\oplus\{0\})\cong T_{s}X_{1}\times...\times T_{s}X_{1}
.

This means that for all
k
in
\mathbb{N}
the sections of
pr_{1}^{*}(\Lambda^{k}(X_{1}))
can be seen as
(k,0)
-differential forms on
X_{1}\times X_{2}
and vice-versa. In particular we have that we can see the pullback using the projection
pr_{i}
as

pr_{1}^{*}:\Omega^{*}(X_{1})\longrightarrow\Gamma(pr_{1}^{*}(\Lambda^{*}(X_{1})))\subseteq\Omega^{*}(X_{1}\times X_{2}).

(D.9)

The same holds also for
pr_{2}
.

Definition D.0.2
.

An operator
A:dom(A)\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
is an
integral operator
if there is a section
K
of the fiber bundle
\Lambda^{*}(M)\boxtimes\Lambda(N)
such that, for
\alpha\in dom(A)\cap\Omega^{*}(N)
and for almost all
p\in M
we have that

A(\alpha)(p):=\int_{N}K(p,q)\alpha(q)d\mu_{N}.

(D.10)

where
K(p,q)\alpha(q)
is the inner product
i_{pr_{N}^{*}\alpha}K
valued in
(p,q)
. Moreover, if the kernel
K
is a smooth section of
\Lambda^{*}(M)\boxtimes\Lambda(N)
then
A
is a
smoothing operator
.

Remark 59
.

The integral operators are well-defined. Indeed we have that
K(p,q)\alpha(q)
is a section of
pr_{M}^{*}(\Lambda^{*}(M))
, and so it can also be seen for all
p
in
M
as a function

\begin{split}N&\longrightarrow\Lambda^{*}_{p}(M)\\
q&\longrightarrow K(p,q)\alpha(q).\end{split}

(D.11)

Then, for all
p\in M
, the Bochner integration respect to the measure on
N
is well-defined.

It is also possible to see the integral over
N
as an integration along the fibers. Indeed
K(p,q)\alpha(q)=i_{pr_{N}^{*}\alpha}K(p,q)\in pr_{M}^{*}(\Lambda^{*}(M))_{(p,q)}
can be seen as sum of
(k_{i},0)
-differential form on
M\times N
. This means that

\begin{split}A(\alpha)(p)&=\int_{N}K(p,q)\alpha(q)d\mu_{N}\\
&=pr_{M\star}(i_{pr_{N}^{*}\alpha}K\wedge pr_{N}^{*}Vol_{N})(p).\end{split}

(D.12)

To show this equality it is sufficient to compute the integrals using local coordinates on
M
.

Remark 60
.

Given two coordinate charts
\{U,x^{s}\}
on
M
and
\{V,y^{l}\}
on
N
, we have that a smooth section of
\Lambda^{*}(M)\boxtimes\Lambda(N)_{(p,q)}
is locally given by

f(p,q)_{S}^{L}dx^{S}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{L}},

(D.13)

where
S=(s_{1},...,s_{m})
and
L=(l_{1},...,l_{n})
are multi-index,
dx^{S}=dx^{s_{1}}\wedge...\wedge dx^{s_{m}}
,
\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{L}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{l_{1}}}\wedge...\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{l_{n}}}
and
f(p,q)_{S}^{L}
is a function in
C^{\infty}(U\times V)
.

Remark 61
.

We know that for all
p
in
M
and for all
q
in
N
we have that
\Lambda_{p}^{*}(M)
and
\Lambda_{q}(N)
have a norm induced by their metrics (one can easily prove that the norm on
\Lambda_{q}(N)
is equal to the dual norm of
\Lambda_{q}^{*}(N)
). We have that for all
k\in\Lambda^{*}(M)_{p}\boxtimes\Lambda(N)_{q}
there are some
\beta_{i,p}\in\Lambda^{*}(M)
and some
\gamma_{i,q}\in\Lambda(N)
such that

k=\sum_{i}\beta_{i,p}\otimes\gamma_{i,q}.

(D.14)

Then, imposing for all
\beta_{p}\in\Lambda_{p}^{*}(M)
and for all
\gamma_{q}\in\Lambda_{q}(N)
that

|\beta_{p}\otimes\gamma_{q}|:=|\beta_{p}|_{\Lambda_{p}^{*}(M)}\cdot|\gamma_{q}|_{\Lambda_{q}(N)},

(D.15)

we can induce a norm on
\Lambda^{*}(M)\boxtimes\Lambda(N)_{(p,q)}
. Moreover, if the
\beta_{i,p}
and the
\gamma_{i,q}
are choosen such that for all
i\neq j

\langle\beta_{i,p},\beta_{j,p}\rangle_{\Lambda_{p}^{*}(M)}=\langle\gamma_{i,p},\gamma_{j,p}\rangle_{\Lambda_{q}(N)}=0,

(D.16)

then we have that

|k|^{2}=\sum_{i}|\beta_{i,p}|^{2}\cdot|\gamma_{i,q}|^{2}.

(D.17)

Consider a smooth kernel of an integral operator
K
and a differential form
\alpha
in
\Omega^{*}(N)
. Then, for each point
(p,q)
we have that

K(p,q)\alpha(q)=\sum_{i}\gamma_{i,q}(\alpha(q))\beta_{i,p}

(D.18)

and so

\begin{split}|K(p,q)\alpha(q)|^{2}&=\sum_{i}|\gamma_{i,q}(\alpha(q))|^{2}|\beta_{i,p}|^{2}\\
&\leq\sum_{i}|\beta_{i,p}|^{2}|\gamma_{i,q}|^{2})\alpha(q)|^{2}\\
&\leq(\sum_{i}|\beta_{i,p}|^{2}|\gamma_{i,q}|^{2})|\alpha(q)|^{2}\\
&=|K(p,q)|^{2}|\alpha(q)|^{2}.\end{split}

(D.19)

Definition D.0.3
.

Let us consider two Riemannian manifolds
(N,h)
and
(M,g)
and let
A:dom(A)\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
be an integral operator with kernel
K
. We have that
A
is an in integral operator with
compactly supported
kernel if the support of
K
as section of
\Lambda^{*}(M)\boxtimes\Lambda(N)
is compact.

Definition D.0.4
.

Consider
N
and
M
two Riemannian manifolds. Let
A:\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
be an integral operator with kernel
K
. We say that

•

A
has
left-uniformly bounded support
if there is
R\geq 0
such that for all
q\in N

diam(supp(K(\cdot,q)))\leq R,

(D.20)

•

A
has
right-uniformly bounded support
if there is
S\geq 0
such that for all
p\in M
for all
q\in N
we have that

diam(supp(K(p,\cdot)))\leq S.

(D.21)

Finally we will say that
A
has
uniformly bounded support
if it has both right and left uniformly bounded support.

Lemma D.0.1

(Jensen inequality)
.

Consider a probability space
(X,\sigma_{X},\mu_{X})
and consider a measurable function
f:X\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Let
\phi:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a convex function. Then

\phi(\int_{X}f(x)d\mu_{X})\leq\int_{X}\phi(f(x))dx.

(D.22)

Proposition D.0.2
.

Let
(N,n)
and
(M,m)
be two Riemannian manifolds of bounded geometry. Let
A:dom(A)\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
be an integral operator with kernel
K
. If
A
has uniformly bounded support with constant
Q
and

\sup_{(p,q)\in M\times N}|K(p,q)|<L,

(D.23)

then the operator
A
is bounded and its norm satisfies

||A||\leq W,

(D.24)

where
W
is a constant which depends on
L
on curvature of
M
.

Proof.

Consider a smooth form
\alpha
in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
. We have that

||A(\alpha)||^{2}=\int_{M}|\int_{N}K(p,q)\alpha(q)d\mu_{N}|^{2}d\mu_{M}.

(D.25)

Step 1.
Fix
p
in
M
, fix an orthonormal basis
\{\epsilon^{I}\}
on
\Lambda^{*}_{p}(M)
and consider the coordinates
\{y_{I}\}
related to
\{\epsilon^{I}\}
. Consider, moreover, the maps
y_{I,r}:\Lambda^{*}_{p}(M)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
y_{I,i}:\Lambda^{*}_{p}(M)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

y_{I}=y_{I,r}+i\cdot y_{I,i}.

(D.26)

We have that

\begin{split}|\int_{N}K(p,q)\alpha(q)d\mu_{N}|^{2}&=\sum\limits_{I,r}(\int_{N}y_{I,r}[K(p,q)\alpha(q)]d\mu_{N})^{2}\\
&+\sum\limits_{I,i}(\int_{N}y_{I,i}[K(p,q)\alpha(q)]d\mu_{N})^{2}.\end{split}

(D.27)

Since
A
is right-uniformly bounded, then there is a
S\geq 0
such that, for each fixed
p
in
M
, there is a ball
B_{S}(q_{p})\subset N
such that

K(\tilde{p},q)\alpha(q)=0

(D.28)

if
\tilde{p}
is not in
B_{S}(q_{p})
.

For each fixed
p
consider the measure
\mu_{p}
on
B_{S}(q_{p})
defined for each
\mu_{N}
-measurable set as

\mu_{p}(B):=\frac{\mu_{N}(B)}{\mu_{N}(B_{S}(q_{p}))}.

(D.29)

Observe
B_{S}(q_{p})
is a probability space. Let us define the map

\begin{split}F_{p}:B_{S}(q_{p})&\longrightarrow\Lambda^{*}_{p}(M)\\
q&\longrightarrow\mu_{N}(B_{S}(q_{p}))\cdot K(p,q)\alpha(q)\end{split}

(D.30)

Then, for each
I
, define the maps

y_{I,r}\circ F_{p}:B_{S}(q_{p})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}.

(D.31)

and

y_{I,i}\circ F_{p}:B_{S}(q_{p})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}.

(D.32)

Consider the function
\phi:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined as
\phi(x)=x^{2}
. Then we can apply the Jensen inequality to (
D.31
) and (
D.32
) considering on
B_{S}(q_{p})
the measure
\mu_{p}
. We obtain

\begin{split}\phi(\int_{B_{S}(q_{p})}y_{I,r}\circ F_{p}d\mu_{p})\leq\int_{B_{S}(q_{p})}\phi(y_{I,r}\circ F_{p})d\mu_{p}\end{split}

(D.33)

and

\begin{split}\phi(\int_{B_{S}(q_{p})}y_{I,i}\circ F_{p}d\mu_{p})\leq\int_{B_{S}(q_{p})}\phi(y_{I,i}\circ F_{p})d\mu_{p}.\end{split}

(D.34)

Observe that the left-hand part of (
D.33
) is

\begin{split}\phi(\int_{B_{S}(q_{p})}y_{I,r}\circ F_{p}d\mu_{p})&=(\int_{B_{S}(q_{p})}y_{I,r}(K(p,q)\alpha(q))\frac{\mu_{N}(B_{S}(q_{p}))}{\mu_{N}(B_{S}(q_{p}))}d\mu_{N})^{2}\\
&(\int_{N}y_{I,r}(K(p,q)\alpha(q))d\mu_{N})^{2}\end{split}

(D.35)

and the left-hand part of (
D.34
) is

\phi(\int_{B_{S}(q_{p})}y_{I,i}\circ F_{p}d\mu_{p})=(\int_{N}y_{I,i}(K(p,q)\alpha(q))d\mu_{N})^{2}.

(D.36)

The right-hand of (
D.33
) is

\begin{split}\int_{B_{S}(q_{p})}\phi(y_{I,r}\circ F_{p})d\mu_{p}&=\int_{B_{S}(q_{p})}\frac{\mu_{N}(B_{S}(q_{p}))^{2}}{\mu_{N}(B_{S}(q_{p}))}(y_{I,r}[K(p,q)\alpha(q)])^{2}d\mu_{N}\\
&=\mu_{N}(B_{S}(q_{p}))\int_{B_{S}(q_{p})}(y_{I,r}[K(p,q)\alpha(q)])^{2}d\mu_{N}\\
&\leq\mu_{N}(B_{S}(q_{p}))\int_{N}(y_{I,r}[K(p,q)\alpha(q)])^{2}d\mu_{N}\end{split}

(D.37)

while the right-hand of (
D.34
) is

\int_{B_{S}(q_{p})}\phi(y_{I,i}\circ F_{p})d\mu_{p}=\mu_{N}(B_{S}(q_{p}))\int_{N}(y_{I,i}[K(p,q)\alpha(q)])^{2}d\mu_{N}

(D.38)

Then, since (
D.27
), we have that

\begin{split}|\int_{N}K(p,q)\alpha(q)d\mu_{N}|^{2}&=\sum\limits_{I,r}(\int_{N}y_{I,r}[K(p,q)\alpha(q)]d\mu_{N})^{2}\\
&+\sum\limits_{I,i}(\int_{N}y_{I,i}[K(p,q)\alpha(q)]d\mu_{N})^{2}\\
&\leq\mu_{N}(B_{S}(q_{p}))\sum\limits_{I,r}\int_{N}(y_{I,r}[K(p,q)\alpha(q)])^{2}d\mu_{N}\\
&+\mu_{N}(B_{S}(q_{p}))\sum\limits_{I,i}\int_{N}(y_{I,i}[K(p,q)\alpha(q)])^{2}d\mu_{N}\\
&\leq\mu_{N}(B_{S}(q_{p}))\int_{N}|K(p,q)\alpha(q)|^{2}d\mu_{N}.\end{split}

(D.39)

Observe that since
N
has buonded geometry, then there is a constant
C
such that

\mu_{N}(B_{S}(q_{p}))\leq C.

(D.40)

Then we have that

|\int_{N}K(p,q)\alpha(q)d\mu_{N}|^{2}\leq C\cdot\int_{N}|K(p,q)\alpha(q)|^{2}d\mu_{N}.

(D.41)

And so we conclude the first step.

Step 2.

Observe that

|K(p,q)\alpha(q)|^{2}\leq|K(p,q)|^{2}|\alpha(q)|^{2}.

(D.42)

We proved in Remark
61
. Then we have that

\begin{split}||A(\alpha)||^{2}&=\int_{M}|\int_{N}K(p,q)\alpha(q)d\mu_{N}|^{2}d\mu_{M}\\
&\leq C\cdot\int_{M}\int_{N}|K(p,q)\alpha(q)|^{2}d\mu_{N}d\mu_{M}\\
&\leq C\cdot\int_{M}\int_{N}|K(p,q)|^{2}\cdot|\alpha(q)|^{2}d\mu_{N}d\mu_{M}\\
&\leq C\cdot\int_{N}\int_{M}|K(p,q)|^{2}\cdot|\alpha(q)|^{2}d\mu_{M}d\mu_{N}\\
&\leq C\cdot\int_{N}(\int_{M}|K(p,q)|^{2}d\mu_{M}|\alpha(q)|^{2}d\mu_{N}.\end{split}

(D.43)

We can observe that, since
A
has uniformly bounded support and since
M
has bounded geometry, then exists a real number
L

\mu_{M}(supp(K(\cdot,q)))\leq Vol(B_{L}(q))\leq R

(D.44)

Moreover if
|K(p,q)|^{2}\leq L
, we have that for all
q
in
N

\int_{M}|K(p,q)|^{2}d\mu_{M}\leq C_{S}L^{2}\leq R

(D.45)

Then we have that

\begin{split}||A(\alpha)||^{2}&\leq C\cdot R\int_{N}|\alpha(q)|^{2}d\mu_{N}\\
\leq CR||\alpha||.\end{split}

(D.46)

∎

An obvious consequence of this Proposition is that an integral operator with compactly supported kernel is bounded.

Lemma D.0.3
.

Smoothing operators with compactly supported kernel are compact operators

Proof.

Let us consider two Riemannian manifolds
(M,g)
and
(N,h)
and consider an integral operator
A:\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
with a compactly supported kernel
K
. We can consider a system of coordinates
\{U_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}^{i}\}
and a partition of unity
\{\rho_{\alpha}\}
related to this system. We will consider, moreover, for each
\alpha
,
\mu_{M}(pr_{M}(U_{\alpha}))<+\infty
. Then we have that

A=\sum_{\alpha}\rho_{\alpha}A

(D.47)

and it’s easy to observe that for any
\alpha
the operator
\rho_{\alpha}A
is an integral operator with compactly supported kernel
\rho_{\alpha}K
. Since just a finite number of
\rho_{\alpha}K\neq 0
, if we are able to show that
\rho_{\alpha}A
is compact for all
\alpha
, then
A
is compact.

This means that we can consider the support of
K\subset U_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha
, without loss of generality.

Consider now an orthonormal frame
\{\epsilon^{j}\}
of
\Lambda^{*}(M)
and
\{w_{i}\}
an orthonormal frame of
\Lambda(N)
, then

\begin{split}A\alpha(p)&=\int_{N}K(p,q)\alpha(q)d\mu_{N}\\
&=\int_{N}\sum_{I,J}K^{I}_{J}(p,q)\epsilon^{J}\otimes w_{I}\alpha(q)d\mu_{N}\\
&=\int_{N}i_{pr^{*}_{N}\alpha}\sum_{I,J}K^{I}_{J}\epsilon^{J}\otimes w_{I}d\mu_{N}\\
&=\int_{N}\sum_{I,J}i_{pr^{*}_{N}\alpha}K^{I}_{J}\epsilon^{J}\otimes w_{I}d\mu_{N}\\
&=\sum_{I,J}\int_{N}K^{I}_{J}(p,q)\epsilon^{J}\otimes w_{I}\alpha(q)d\mu_{N}\\
&=\sum_{I,J}A_{I,J},\end{split}

(D.48)

where
A_{I,J}
are the integral operators with kernel
K^{I}_{J}\epsilon^{J}\otimes w_{I}
. If for any
I,J
we have that
A_{I,J}
are compact operators, then also
A
is a compact operator.

To show that
A_{I,J}
is a compact operator we will show that
A_{I,J}(B_{1}(0))
is relatively compact i.e.
\overline{A_{I,J}(B_{1}(0))}
is a compact subset of
\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
. In particular we have that since
A_{I,J}
has compactly supported kernel, then it is a continuous operator and so
\overline{A_{I,J}(B_{1}(0))}=\overline{A_{I,J}(B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{*}(N))}
.

Let us consider a
\beta\in B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{*}(N)
: we have that
\beta=f_{I}W^{I}
, where
\{W^{i}\}
is the dual frame of
\{w_{i}\}
. Then we have that

A_{I,J}\beta=(\int_{N}K^{I}_{J}f_{I}d\mu_{N})\epsilon^{J}=:G_{J}\epsilon^{J}.

(D.49)

Consider
\{e_{J}\}
is the dual frame of
\{\epsilon^{J}\}
. We can define the operator

i_{e_{J}}:\Omega^{*}_{c}(pr_{M}(U_{\alpha}))\longrightarrow C^{\infty}_{c}(pr_{M}(U_{\alpha}))

(D.50)

defined as

i_{e_{K}}(g_{J}\epsilon^{J})=\begin{cases}g_{K}\mbox{ if }J=K\\
0\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(D.51)

Then we have that

i_{e_{J}}A_{I,J}\beta(p)=\int_{N}K^{I}_{J}(p,q)f_{I}(q)d\mu_{N}=G_{J}(p)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(pr_{M}(U_{\alpha})).

(D.52)

Let us consider on
C^{\infty}_{c}(pr_{M}(U_{\alpha}))
the uniform norm defined as

|f|_{\infty}=\sup\limits_{p\in pr_{M}(U_{\alpha})}|f(p)|

(D.53)

Let us consider a sequence of compactly supported smooth functions
\{f_{n}\}\subset C^{\infty}_{c}(pr_{M}(U_{\alpha}))
. If there is a
f:pr_{M}(U_{\alpha})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that

\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow+\infty}|f_{n}-f|_{\infty}=0,

(D.54)

then, since
\mu_{M}(pr_{M}(U_{\alpha}))<+\infty
, we have that

\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow+\infty}||f_{n}-f||_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(pr_{N}(U_{\alpha}))}=0.

(D.55)

Let us consider a sequence

A_{I,J}\beta_{n}=(\int_{N}K^{I}_{J}f_{I,n}d\mu_{N})\epsilon^{J}=:G_{J,n}\epsilon^{J}

(D.56)

in
A_{I,J}(B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{*}(M))
. Then if the sequence
\{G_{J,n}\}_{n}
uniformly converges to a function
g:pr_{M}(U_{\alpha})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, then
A_{I,J}\beta_{n}
converges in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
. Indeed, since
\epsilon^{I}
is an orthonormal frame, we have that

\begin{split}\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow+\infty}||A_{I,J}\beta_{n}-g\epsilon^{J}||_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)}&=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow+\infty}||(G_{J,n}-g)\epsilon^{J}||_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)}\\
&=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow+\infty}||G_{J,n}-g||_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(pr_{M}(U_{\alpha}))}=0.\end{split}

(D.57)

The next step is to prove that
i_{e_{J}}\circ A_{I,J}(B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{*}(N))
has compact closure respect to the uniform norm on
C^{\infty}_{c}(pr_{M}(U_{\alpha}))
.

We can use the Ascoli-Arzelà theorem: to show that
i_{e_{I}}\circ A_{I,J}(B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{*}(N))
has compact closure we have to proof that it is equicontinuous and pointwise bounded.

Remember that a subset of
C^{\infty}_{c}(pr_{M}(U_{\alpha}))
is equicontinuous if
\forall p\in U_{\alpha}\forall\epsilon>0
there is a neighborhood
U_{x}
of
x
such that for all

\forall y\in U_{x}\forall f\in i_{e_{J}}\circ A_{I,J}(B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{*}(N))\implies|f(y)-f(x)|<\epsilon

(D.58)

and that
i_{e_{J}}\circ A_{I,J}(B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{*}(N))
is pointwise bounded, i.e for all
x
in
U_{\alpha}

\sup\{|f(x)||f\in i_{e_{J}}\circ A_{I,J}(B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{*}(N))\}<+\infty.

(D.59)

First we show the equicontinuity (
D.58
): let us fix
\epsilon>0
and
p
in
U_{\alpha}
. Consider
i_{e_{J}}\circ A_{I,J}\beta
where
\beta=f_{I}W^{I}\in B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{*}(N)\subset\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
, we have that

\begin{split}|i_{e_{J}}\circ A_{I,J}\beta(y)-i_{e_{J}}\circ A_{I,J}\beta(p)|^{2}&=|\int_{N}K^{I}_{J}(y,q)f_{I}(q)d\mu_{N}-\int_{N}K^{I}_{J}(p,q)f_{I}(q)d\mu_{N}|^{2}\\
&\leq\int_{N}|K^{I}_{J}(y,q)-K^{I}_{J}(p,q)|^{2}|f_{I}(q)|^{2}d\mu_{N}.\end{split}

(D.60)

Now we can observe that
K^{I}_{J}
is continuous and with compact support. Then in particular it is uniformly continuous, i.e there is a
\delta>0
, which doesn’t depend by
(p,q)
, such that for all
(y,q)
in
B_{\delta}((p,q))
we have

|K^{I}_{J}(y,q)-K^{I}_{J}(p,q)|^{2}\leq\epsilon.

(D.61)

So we obtain that

\begin{split}|i_{e_{J}}\circ A_{I,J}\beta(y)-i_{e_{J}}\circ A_{I,J}\beta(p)|^{2}&\leq\int_{N}|K^{I}_{J}(y,q)-K^{I}_{J}(p,q)|^{2}|f_{I}|^{2}d\mu_{N}\\
&\leq\epsilon\int_{N}|\beta|^{2}d\mu_{N}=\epsilon.\end{split}

(D.62)

Finally to prove the pointwise boundedness (
D.59
) it is sufficient to observe that

\begin{split}|i_{e_{J}}\circ A_{I,J}\beta(p)|^{2}&=|\int_{N}K^{I}_{J}(p,q)f_{I}(q)d\mu_{N}|^{2}\\
&\leq\int_{N}|K^{I}_{J}(p,q)|^{2}|f_{I}|^{2}d\mu_{N}\\
&\leq M\int_{N}|\beta(q)|^{2}d\mu_{N}=M,\end{split}

(D.63)

where
M
is the maximum of
K^{I}_{J}
on
U_{\alpha}
.

We can conclude that
A_{I,J}(B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{*}(N))
has compact closure. Indeed for every sequence
\beta_{n}
in
B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{*}(N)
we have that
A_{I,J}\beta_{n}=G_{J,n}\epsilon^{J}
for some
G_{J,n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(pr_{M}(U_{\alpha}))
. Then, since
i_{e_{J}}\circ A_{I,J}(B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{*}(N))
has compact closure, we know that there is a uniformly converging subsequence
\{G_{J,n_{k}}\}
of
\{G_{J,n}\}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(pr_{M}(U_{\alpha}))
. This, using (
D.57
), this means that the sequence
\{G_{J,n_{k}}\epsilon^{J}\}
is a
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-converging subsequence of
\{G_{J,n}\epsilon^{J}\}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
.

This means that
A_{I,J}
is a compact operator ans so also
A
is a compact operator and, in general, all integral operators with compactly supported kernel are compact.
∎

Lemma D.0.4
.

Let
A:dom(A)\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
be a smoothing operator. Then given a multi-index
I
and given an index
l
, we will denote by
Jl
the multi-index defined as
Jl:=(j_{1},...,j_{n},l)
.

We have that
dA
is also a smoothing operator and if the kernel of
A
is locally given by

K(x,y)=K^{I}_{J}(x,y)dx^{J}\boxtimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{I}}

(D.64)

then
dA
is an integral operator and its kernel is locally given by

d_{M}K^{I}_{S}:=_{|_{loc}}(\sum\limits_{Jl=S}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{l}}K^{I}_{J}(x,y))dx^{S}\boxtimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{I}}

(D.65)

Proof.

Let
p
be a point in
M
and
q
in
N
. Then we have that

\begin{split}dA(\alpha)&=d\int_{N}K(p,q)\alpha(q)d\mu_{N}\\
&=d\int_{N}K(p,q)\alpha(q)\wedge Vol_{N}\\
&=\int_{N}d(K(p,q)\alpha(q)\wedge Vol_{N}).\end{split}

(D.66)

Now, we can observe that
K(p,q)\alpha(q)\wedge Vol_{N}
is top-degree form on
N
, so
d=d_{M}
in this case. Let us consider now a coordinate system
\{x^{i},y^{j}\}
on
M\times N
. Then we have that

\begin{split}dA(\alpha)(x)&=\int_{N}d_{M}[(\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{J}}\dashv\alpha(y))K^{I}_{J}(x,y)dx^{J}]\wedge Vol_{N}\\
&=\int_{N}(\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{J}}\dashv\alpha(y))\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{l}}(K^{I}_{J}(x,y))dx^{l}\wedge dx^{J}\wedge Vol_{N}\\
&=\int_{N}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{l}}(K^{I}_{J}(x,y))dx^{l}\wedge dx^{J}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{I}}(\alpha(y))Vol_{N}\\
&=\int_{N}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{l}}(K^{I}_{J}(x,y))dx^{l}\wedge dx^{J}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{I}}(\alpha(y))d\mu_{N}.\end{split}

(D.67)

and so we have that

d_{M}K^{I}_{S}:=_{|_{loc}}(\sum\limits_{Jl=S}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{l}}K^{I}_{J}(x,y))dx^{S}\boxtimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{I}}.

(D.68)

To conclude the proof we have to check that the local sections

\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{l}}(K^{I}_{J}(x,y))dx^{l}\wedge dx^{J}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{I}}

(D.69)

are the local forms of a global section of
\Lambda^{*}(M)\boxtimes\Lambda(N)
. To do this we can define an operator

Q:\Omega^{*}(M\times N)\longrightarrow\Gamma(pr_{M}^{*}(\Lambda(M)))\subseteq\Omega^{*}(M\times N)

(D.70)

where

Q(\alpha)=\begin{cases}\frac{\alpha}{pr_{N}^{*}Vol_{N}}\mbox{ if }\alpha\mbox{ is a }(q,n)\mbox{-form where }n=dim(N)\\
0\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(D.71)

Observe that

\Gamma(pr_{M}^{*}(\Lambda^{*}(M)))\otimes_{C^{\infty}(M\times N)}\Gamma(pr_{N}^{*}(\Lambda(N)))\cong\Gamma(\Lambda^{*}(M)\boxtimes\Lambda(N)).

(D.72)

This means that we can consider the operator

Q\otimes 1:\Omega^{*}(M\times N)\otimes_{C^{\infty}(M\times N)}\Gamma(pr_{N}^{*}(\Lambda(N)))\longrightarrow\Gamma(\Lambda^{*}(M)\boxtimes\Lambda(N))

(D.73)

Let us now observe that

\Lambda^{*}(M)\boxtimes\Lambda(N)=pr_{M}^{*}(\Lambda^{*}(M))\otimes pr_{N}^{*}(\Lambda(N))\subset\Lambda^{*}(M\times N)\otimes pr_{N}^{*}(\Lambda(N))

(D.74)

and so we have that

\Gamma(\Lambda^{*}(M)\boxtimes\Lambda(N))\subseteq\Omega^{*}(M\times N)\otimes_{C^{\infty}(M\times N)}\Gamma(pr_{N}^{*}(\Lambda(N)))

(D.75)

The vector space on the right can be see as the space of
\Gamma(pr_{N}^{*}(\Lambda(N)))
-valued differential forms on
M\times N
. This means that we can apply to
K\in\Gamma(\Lambda^{*}(M)\boxtimes\Lambda(N))
the exterior derivative
d_{vec}
of a
\Gamma(pr_{N}^{*}(\Lambda(N)))
-valued differential form. Then it’s easy to check that

Q\otimes 1(d_{vec}K)_{|_{loc}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{l}}(K^{I}_{J}(x,y))dx^{l}\wedge dx^{J}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{I}}

(D.76)

∎

Remark 62
.

One can observe that the support of the kernel of
dA
is strictly contained in the support of the kernel of
A
. This means that if
A
has right-uniformly bounded support, then also
dA
ha uniformly bounded support.

Moreover if
A
is a smoothing operator which kernel
K
has uniformly bounded support and if in normal coordinates there is a uniform bound

|\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}K^{I}_{J}(x,y)|\leq C,

(D.77)

then
dA
is a bounded operator.

Lemma D.0.5
.

Let
H:\mathcal{L}^{2}(N\times[0,1])\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
be a smoothing operator between manifolds of bounded geometry and denote by
K
its kernel. Fix, locally, some normal coordinates
\{x^{i}\}
on
M
and
\{y^{j}\}
on
N
.

K_{|_{loc}}:=K^{J}_{I}(x,t,y)dx^{I}\boxtimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{J}}+K^{J}_{I,+}(x,t,y)dx^{I}\wedge dt\boxtimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{J}}

(D.78)

Consider
s
and
s+\delta
in
[0,1]
for some
\delta>0
. Let us define the operators
A_{s}
and
A_{s+\delta}:\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)
as the smoothing operators which kernel is given by

K_{s}(x,y):=i^{*}_{s,x,y}\circ K=K^{J}_{I}(x,s,y)dx^{I}\boxtimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{J}}

(D.79)

and

K_{s+\delta}(x,y):=i^{*}_{s+\delta,x,y}\circ K=K^{J}_{I}(x,s+\delta,y)dx^{I}\boxtimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{J}}

(D.80)

where
i^{*}_{t,p,q}:\Lambda^{*}(M\times[0,1])_{(p,t,q)}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{*}(M)_{(p,q)}
is the operator induced by the pullback of the immersion
i_{t}:M\longrightarrow M\times[0,1]
defined as
i_{t}(p)=(p,t)
.

So, if

1.

there is a number
Q
such that for each
q
in
N
and for each
p
in
M

diam(K(\cdot,\cdot,q))\leq Q,

(D.81)

and

diam(K(p,\cdot,\cdot))\leq Q,

(D.82)

2.

there is a globally bound for all
t\in[0,1]

|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}K_{J}^{I}(p,t,q)|\leq C,

(D.83)

then there is a constant
L\geq 0
such that

||A_{s+\delta}-A_{s}||\leq L\delta,

(D.84)

where
L
depends by
C
and by the scalar curvature of
M
.

Proof.

We have that the kernel of
A_{s+\delta}-A_{s}
is locally given by

\begin{split}K_{s+\delta}(x,y)-K_{s}(x,y)&=[K^{J}_{I}(x,s+\delta,y)-K^{J}_{I}(x,s,y)]dx^{I}\boxtimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{J}}\\
&=[\int_{0}^{\delta}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}K^{J}_{I}(x,t,y)dt]dx^{I}\boxtimes\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{J}}\end{split}

(D.85)

and so

||K_{s+\delta}(x,y)-K_{s}(x,y)||^{2}\leq C^{2}\delta.

(D.86)

Moreover, since

diam(supp(K(\cdot,\cdot,q)))\leq Q

(D.87)

then

diam(supp(K_{s+\delta}-K_{s}(\cdot,\cdot,q)))\leq diam(pr_{M}(supp(K(\cdot,\cdot,q))))\leq Q.

(D.88)

This means that

\mu_{M}(supp(K_{s+\delta}-K_{s}(\cdot,\cdot,q)))\leq R

(D.89)

Then it is sufficient to apply the Proposition
D.0.2
and we have that

||A_{s+\delta}-A_{s}||\leq RC^{2}\delta=L\delta.

(D.90)

∎

Lemma D.0.6
.

Consider
A:\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
a smoothing operator with kernel
K
and
A_{\delta}:\mathcal{L}^{2}(M)\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}(N)
is another smoothing operator with kernel
K_{\delta}
such that

||[K-K_{\delta}](p,q)||^{2}_{(p,q)}\leq\delta.

(D.91)

then if their support are left-uniformly bounded by a constant
Q
, we have that

||A-A_{\delta}||\leq L\delta,

(D.92)

where
L
just depends by
Q
and the infimum of the scalar curvature of
M

Proof.

It is a direct consequence of Proposition
D.0.2
.
∎
